# COPYRIGHT STATEMENT

The copyright law of the United States — Title 17, United States Code — concerns the making of photocopies or other reproductions of copyrighted material. Under certain conditions specified in the law, libraries and archives are authorized to furnish a photocopy or other reproduction. One of these specified conditions is that the photocopy or reproduction is not to be "used for any purpose other than private study, scholarship, or research." If a user makes a request for, or later uses, a photocopy or reproduction for purposes in excess of "fair use," <note>Fair use is a legal doctrine that permits limited use of copyrighted material without acquiring permission from the rights holder.</note> that user may be liable for copyright infringement.

Columbia University Library reserves the right to refuse to accept a copy order if, in its judgement, fulfillment of the order would involve violation of the copyright law.

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the formal title page and dedication into the "Preface to Kind Readers" (Lectoribus Benivolis Salutem). The author, Richard Volkmann, begins by defining the ancient scope of the word "Music" to set the historical stage for the technical treatise that follows. He establishes that for the Greeks, music was inextricably linked to philosophy and general education.</meta>

## Greetings to Kind Readers.

It is well known today among everyone that the term **Music** <note>original: Musicae</note> had a dual meaning among the ancients. First, all the arts were included under this name which it was fitting for a free-born man to know—whatever pertained in some way to the more liberal cultivation of the mind. Therefore, in the works of **Plato**, music is philosophy itself <note>original: musica est ipsa philosophia</note>; see Wyttenbach on Plato’s *Phaedo*, page 127. The first traces of this expression may be traced back to **Socrates** himself, to whom, on the day before Plato was accepted into the circle of his disciples, a musical swan was seen in a dream to fly into his lap; see Pausanias, Book I, 30, 3. <note>This famous legend suggests that Plato was the "musical swan" destined to harmonize the teachings of Socrates.</note> And in general, those roles which the gymnastic arts of the Greeks claimed for strengthening and refining the health of the body, music held for guarding the health of the mind. On this matter, it is sufficient to consult Bernhardy in the *History of Greek Literature*, Volume I, page 73. Examples of this usage were long ago gathered by Wallis in his commentary on Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*, page 281, and by Locella in his work on Xenophon of Ephesus, page 125 and following.

Thus, <gloss>the music of the encyclical studies</gloss> <note>original: ἡ τῶν ἐγκυκλίων μουσική; this refers to the "circle" of general education that a well-rounded citizen was expected to complete.</note> in the writings of **Philo** (*On Drunkenness*, III, page 190) is the same as <gloss>general education</gloss>. Very close to this are the words of **Achilles Tatius** (Book I, 8): "if indeed you were an amateur in musical knowledge," that is, if you shrank from the study of the more humane letters <note>In this context, "humane letters" or humanities refers to literature, rhetoric, and philosophy.</note>. The same is found in **Xenophon of Ephesus** (Book I, 1): "for he practiced every kind of education and cultivated a varied music." Similarly **Dio Chrysostom** (*Oration* LIII, 7), where he recounts that Homeric poetry spread as far as the shores of the Indians, exclaims: "so great was the power of one man's music." **Philemon** settles the matter in **Plutarch** (*On Listening to the Poets*, 13, page 35):

> Nothing is more pleasant, nor more musical, <note>original: μουσικώτερον; here used to mean "more refined" or "more cultured."</note>
> than to be able to endure being insulted.

And the same meaning is found in the words of **Aelian** (*Historical Miscellany*, XII, 50):

<summary>This preface explains that in antiquity, "Music" referred not just to sound, but to the entire spectrum of liberal arts, philosophy, and mental cultivation. The author cites various classical authorities like Plato, Socrates, and Philo to illustrate that a "musical" person was a refined, educated person.</summary>
<keywords>Music, Philosophy, Education, Plato, Socrates, Philo, Philemon, Greek Literature, Enkuklios Paideia, liberal arts</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>Following the previous page’s discussion on the broad definition of "music" as general education, this page narrows the focus to technical music theory (Harmonics) and begins a scholarly defense of the authorship of Plutarch's "On Music," addressing historical skepticism regarding its style and presence in ancient catalogs.</meta>

# PREFACE

That the **Spartans** <note>original: Λακεδαιμόνιοι; also known as Lacedaemonians</note> **were inexperienced in music** <note>original: μουσικῆς ἀπείρως εἶχον</note> is a certain fact. To settle the matter in a few words: this broader meaning of music became so prevalent that what we call "music" and "musical theory" was usually called **Harmonics** <note>original: ἁρμονική; the science of pitch and intervals</note> by the ancients. Conversely, there were those who retained the proper and stricter meaning of the word, among whom was **Plutarch** himself. Furthermore, if we look at derived words, a **musical man** <note>original: ἀνὴρ μουσικός</note> is rarely someone who pursues music through practice, but—referring to the knowledge and theory of music—he is rather one who is skilled not only in Harmonics and Rhythmics, but also in Metrics, Organics <note>the study of musical instruments</note>, and the remaining parts of music (see Meibomius's *Aristoxenus*, page 76); such a person is what we call: **a professional musician.** <note>original: ein musiker von fach</note> Or, to use the words of **Boethius** from *On Music, Book I, 34, page 1085*: "He is a musician who, after weighing the matter with reason, takes up the science of singing—not as a servant to the work, but through the command of contemplation"—and a little later: "a musician is one who possesses the faculty of judging according to a proposed speculation or reason... concerning modes and rhythms, and the types of songs and their mixtures—and concerning the poems of the poets." By contrast, a **music-maker** <note>original: ἀνὴρ μουσουργός</note> is one who elicits charming sounds from one or another musical instrument, either by plucking or blowing, having prepared this ability through practice. Thus, in **Heliodorus** (II, 24), the Athenian girl **Thisbe**, who learned to pluck the lyre just a little, is called **"excellent at music-making."** <note>original: μουσουργίαν ἀρίστην</note> Finally, a **lover of music** <note>original: φιλόμουσος</note> sometimes signifies a friend of the Muses and the arts, and sometimes a patron of music, as in Plutarch’s *Precepts of Marriage* (chapter 17) and **Dio Chrysostom** (Orations XIX, 3): "I am a lover of hearing and a lover of music <note>original: φιλήκοος καὶ φιλόμουσος</note> purely and simply, though I am not at all experienced in music."

But enough on these matters. I had to discuss these words first so that I would not seem to have omitted anything, and secondly, to settle once and for all things that might cause annoyance if repeated throughout the book. Now, to approach the dialogue **On Music** <note>original: de Musica</note> by Plutarch, which I have undertaken to treat: there remain many commentaries by ancient writers who recorded the harmonic treatment of music in literature. On the other hand, it is from this single book by Plutarch that we learn from what beginnings music proceeded and to what successes and degrees of perfection it reached. From this source,

<column-break/>

those things finally receive their light which **Athenaeus** recorded in a scattered fashion on this subject in the fourth and fourteenth books of **The Banquet of the Learned** <note>original: Deipnosophistarum</note>. I call this book "Plutarch’s," although I am well aware there have been and are learned men who classify it among the forged works. But if you look for the reasons for this opinion, none are brought forward, except for one by **Amyot** which has come to my attention. He claimed the style and eloquence of the book seemed foreign to Plutarch in these very brief words: **"the style does not seem at all to be Plutarch's."** <note>original: le style ne semble point estre de Plutarque</note> However, **Burette** long ago exploded this opinion in his book titled *Memoirs of Literature taken from the registers of the Royal Academy of Inscriptions and Belles-Lettres* (Volume VIII, page 27 of the large edition; Volume XI, page 44 and following of the small edition).

For if we start with the manuscripts—though I deeply regret that I could not obtain a more accurate knowledge of them than Burette did—I have never heard of a book of Plutarch's that lacked the treatise *On Music*, provided they intended to present the complete body of the so-called **Moralia** <note>Plutarch's "Moral Essays," a collection of 78 essays on various customs and philosophies</note>. Indeed, all of them place it at the end of the collection; and that there are or were books that inserted it in the middle of the collection is evidenced by the **Aldine edition** of the *Moralia* from the year 1509. It is true that in the so-called **Catalog of Lamprias** <note>An ancient list of Plutarch's works, purportedly written by his son</note>, the dialogue *On Music* is not listed among Plutarch’s writings. However, **Fabricius** already warned in his *Library* that this catalog is mutilated at the end, so that if any of Plutarch’s writings were missing from it, this would not yet suffice for their condemnation. Furthermore, there is another, larger catalog which **Siebenkees** published from a Venetian manuscript, followed by **Harless**, in which the dialogue *On Music* is named—though I do not think even this catalog should be granted great authority. And if anyone wished to solve this question—whether it is genuine or forged—solely from the language or composition of the dialogue, the matter would be extremely difficult and an arduous task. For Plutarch follows no fixed rule in his style, but depends on the reading of those writers whom he followed as authors while composing a particular dialogue. I see clear evidence of this not only in other works but also in the fact that some words are found in individual dialogues...

<summary>This page defines the distinction between a theoretical musician (scholar) and a music-maker (performer) in antiquity. It also begins a defense of the authenticity of Plutarch's "On Music," arguing against critics who claim its style is uncharacteristic.</summary>
<keywords>Plutarch, Boethius, Harmonics, music theory, musicology, authorship, Moralia, manuscript history, Greek music, professional musicians</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Following the previous page’s discussion on the technical definition of "Harmonics," this page evaluates the stylistic quirks of Plutarch’s prose and provides internal evidence to support his authorship of the treatise "On Music," arguing that its moral tone and musical metaphors are characteristic of his genuine works.</meta>

# PREFACE

...words used ten or twenty times in one place, which are then sought in vain in others. Plutarch is even less consistent in how he forms his sentences; at times he builds elaborate, artistic periods, while at others he breaks his thoughts into tiny clauses and phrases <note>original: *cola et commata*</note>, as if he wished to render some commentary of **Seneca** into the Greek language. As a result, an even "color" or tone of speech is often lacking, at least in his *Moralia* <note>Plutarch's "Moral Essays"</note>. This does not surprise me in an author who was certainly shaped by reading the best books, but who cared little for the refinements of the **Atticists** <note>writers who imitated the "pure" style of ancient Athens</note>, such as those we admire in **Lucian** and **Dio Chrysostom**—to say nothing of the admittedly affected writing style of the **Philostrati**. Therefore, if there are some slightly careless expressions in this *Dialogue on Music*, whether regarding syntax or choice of words, which might seem unworthy of Plutarch—who, though not perfect in everything, is a highly regarded writer—these must be tolerated in this work as they are in his other booklets. There remain many other features, appearing in almost all his writings, which clearly prove he is the author.

The core of the dialogue is an exhortation to musicians: they should cast aside "makeup" <note>false ornamentation</note> and the neglected, soft "delights" of a corrupted art form to return to the original simplicity and the strict discipline of the ancients. It is clear how well this writing agrees with Plutarch’s entire character and genius; everywhere we find him to be a **praiser of the past** <note>original: *temporis acti laudatorem*</note> and a stern censor of loose morals. Furthermore, many passages in his books testify that Plutarch himself was **a great lover of music** <note>original: φιλομουσότατον</note> and was more than moderately skilled in musical disciplines. Not only do musical questions arise in conversation throughout his works, but it was Plutarch's habit—which became almost a custom—to illustrate his speech with comparisons and images drawn from musical matters for the sake of ornament. (See *On Flattery and Friendship*, p. 67, 3 in **Duebner’s** edition; *On Progress in Virtue*, p. 90, 1 and p. 100, 10; *On the Utility of Enemies*, p. 104, 43; *On Having Many Friends*, p. 115, 20; *On Fortune*, p. 118, 39 and p. 119, 19, and many others collected by **Burette** in the work cited above, p. 61 ff.) Indeed, I have not found a single passage that contradicts or opposes what we read in this *Dialogue on Music*.

# PREFACE

Therefore, having considered and weighed all these arguments, whether external or internal, I see no reason not to judge the booklet as genuine and written by Plutarch himself. There are, however, some reasons why I attribute it to a **young Plutarch**. Besides the great similarity in composition between this dialogue and certain other booklets known to have been written in his youth, I hold this reason to be the weightiest: because **Soterichus of Alexandria** is mentioned among the guests of **Onesicrates**, I am led to believe this is the same Onesicrates mentioned in *Table Talk* <note>original: *Quaestiones Convivales*</note> V, 5—the most famous Alexandrian physician, in whose social circles learned discussions were held. Plutarch visited Alexandria with his grandfather **Lamprias** when he was quite a young man. Now, if Onesicrates is referred to as being among the living in the *Dialogue on Music*, the dialogue could only have been composed during that period when Plutarch began to produce his first displays of learning. At that time, he devoted himself primarily to physical science and medical matters; these studies, perhaps following a transition through the interpretation of **Nicander**, were followed by grammar and poetry, and finally history mixed with philosophy—though I do not dare to assert these claims about higher disputes as certainties.

Now that we have reached the point of inquiring into the reliability and authority of the dialogue—which, as things stand, is almost the unique source for the history of Greek music—we must speak of the authors whom Plutarch followed, or perhaps, in his usual manner, copied word-for-word. Since he himself named very few, only four in total come into consideration. The first is **Heraclides Ponticus**, whom he followed in those matters regarding the mythical age of music throughout the third chapter. But that Heraclides was a man, as everyone knows, both "most ready to lie and most dull-witted in sniffing out the frauds of others," to use the words of **Lobeck** from *Aglaophamus*, page 328; he deserves little, or rather minimal, credit. Even all those things discussed regarding the *War of the Titans* by **Thamyris**, and the songs of **Demodocus** and **Phemius** and their form, are fictitious and foolish; although most of these are not presented as certainties, they should not have been presented at all. The second who comes into...

<summary>The author defends the authenticity of Plutarch's "On Music," arguing that its inconsistent style matches Plutarch's other works and that it likely dates to his youth in Alexandria. He begins a critical evaluation of Plutarch's sources, starting with a harsh dismissal of the unreliable Heraclides Ponticus.</summary>
<keywords>Plutarch, De Musica, authorship defense, prose style, music education, Soterichus of Alexandria, Onesicrates, Heraclides Ponticus, Greek music history, literary criticism</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion from the previous page regarding Plutarch’s authorship and style, the text now transitions into an analysis of the specific historical sources Plutarch consulted for his dialogue on music, beginning with the Italian scholar Glaucus of Rhegium.</meta>

<page-num>XII</page-num>
# PREFACE

...first comes **Glaucus the Italian**. Plutarch mentions him in chapter four, chapter seven, twice in chapter ten, and in three separate places while explaining chronological descriptions. It appears to me that Plutarch placed great value on Glaucus’s authority when establishing the era of ancient musicians and poets. This Glaucus was from Rhegium <note>a Greek colony in Southern Italy</note> and, according to **Diogenes Laertius** (Book IX, 38), was a contemporary of Democritus around the 63rd Olympiad <note>approximately 528–525 BC</note>. He wrote a book *On Ancient Poets and Musicians*, which some have mistakenly attributed to the orator **Antiphon** <note>original: *Plut. X Rhett. p. 833 D.*</note>. (Cf. Junsius, *Writers of Philosophical History* I, 4 p. 26; Voss, *Greek History* IV p. 437; Westerm. C. Mueller, *Fragments of Greek Historians* II p. 23; Sengebusch, *Homeric Dissertations* I p. 208). The few surviving fragments of this writer reveal a learned and accurate man; considering his great antiquity, everything cited from him seems highly worthy of a more diligent examination. Nor was he unskilled in music theory, for he is the same Glaucus who became a household name <note>original: *in proverbium abiit*, literally "passed into a proverb"</note> due to certain subtle acoustic devices he invented alongside **Hippasus of Metapontum** <note>a Pythagorean philosopher</note>. *See Zenobius II, 91 and Leutsch’s notes on this passage.* 

Regarding the reliability of **Alexander Polyhistor**, a more recent writer from whose book on Phrygia Plutarch seems to have drawn most of his information about Phrygian musicians, I cannot offer a definitive opinion. Finally, the fourth writer Plutarch followed was **Aristoxenus of Tarentum**, surnamed "The Musician." He was the most famous restorer of music theory after the Pythagoreans, and his authority on musical matters remained supreme among the Greeks until the era of **Claudius Didymus** and **Ptolemy**. Nearly everything presented from the fifteenth chapter onward concerning music theory seems to have flowed from Aristoxenus, even if the author’s name is not praised in every instance. However, regarding the fragments of Aristoxenus that relate to music history, one must exercise cautious judgment, regardless of the man's great learning. He occasionally erred, as shown by other evidence and specifically by the clear testimony of **Proclus** in his commentary on Plato’s *Timaeus* (III p. 192). Plutarch did not mention other writers on the subject of music, with the possible exception of **Lysis the Pythagorean**, who is cited once in chapter 16. Nevertheless, it is probable—indeed, certain—that he consulted many more, given his own learning and his almost incredible diligence in reading,

<column-break/>

<page-num>XIII</page-num>
# PREFACE

as evidenced by certain chapters, especially the twenty-eighth, which were clearly compiled from three or four different writers.

Because of this, Plutarch is certainly to be praised for his diligence in consulting sources. But since he exercised little critical judgment in evaluating the reliability of those sources, this *Dialogue on Music*—more than Plutarch's other writings—deserves to be clarified with an extensive commentary. By collecting all the surviving testimonies of antiquity regarding music history into one place, each individual point may receive its proper light. Therefore, my first and almost only intention in writing this small book was to gather as diligently as possible everything from both ancient and modern writers that might serve to illustrate Plutarch’s words. Once gathered, I aimed to organize and evaluate them. If I seem to some to have made little progress in certain parts of this work, let the readers remember that it is the very nature of music history to teem with fables <note>original: *figmentis*, meaning fabrications or fictions</note>; it almost never allows for a certain and firm tradition. For music, more than the other arts, looks toward the amusement of the common people. Its inventions please the ear, but a long series of new developments quickly replaces previous ones; once heard, the old forms are suddenly neglected, and the names of the inventors—celebrated by their contemporaries for only a short time—are wiped away. 

Thus, in music history, everything is in flux and uncertain. This surely escapes no one who is involved in these studies. For instance, what thick clouds of uncertainty surround even those things that have come down to our memory regarding **Guido of Arezzo** <note>original: *Vito Aretinus*</note>, the inventor of more recent music, and his "hand" <note>the Guidonian Hand, a mnemonic tool for singers</note>? How arduous and difficult it would be if someone wished to accurately track the history of the **Dithyramb** <note>a wild, choral hymn</note> among moderns—I mean that genre of songs called by the Italian word ***Opera***—through the course of centuries and continue it right up to the present day? And what were those few things that could even be known in the time of Aristoxenus regarding the most ancient music of the Greeks? Nothing, I think, except a few names, and even those are uncertain. Of the music itself—I mean the songs and the scales <note>original: *modorum*</note>—scarcely one or two fragments remained. I have not avoided harmonic questions, but I have only touched upon them when they were necessary for understanding Plutarch’s own words...

<page-num>XIV</page-num>
<header>XIV PREFACE.</header>

...it seemed necessary to say these words. On the other hand, if I have proposed things here and there in the commentary which, because they depart from the common consensus of learned men, cause the reader wonder, I ask that they accurately examine the reasons why I have so decided. In these matters, I could easily have erred, and perhaps I have erred quite often. However, I have never conducted myself as one who, moved by a little glory for saying something new, wished to deliberately mix the highest things with the lowest and attack the words of the authors.

Regarding the rest of the work’s plan, a few things must be added. I neither wished, nor was I able, to produce a new "recension" <note>a critical revision of a text based on all available evidence</note> of the text conformed to the rules of critical discipline that prevail today. Therefore, I have seen to it that the edition of **Dübner** <note>Jean Frédéric Dübner, a 19th-century German scholar who worked in Paris</note> be reprinted, which was based on a new comparison <note>term: collation; the line-by-line comparison of different manuscripts to find errors</note> of the Paris manuscripts; it is certainly superior and more corrected in many places than that of **Wyttenbach** <note>Daniel Wyttenbach, an earlier 18th-century scholar famous for his edition of Plutarch's essays</note>. I have, however, made use of a new comparison of the excellent Vatican manuscript 192, performed some time ago by **Franz**, a professor in Berlin. A copy of this was sent for my use by the very kind **Bellermann**, at the request of the distinguished **Poethko**—a teacher in the upper classes at the Gymnasium in Grimma—who today holds all of Franz’s notes which he himself had collected to prepare a new edition of the Greek musical writers. To these men, who have served my edition so well, I cannot help but offer my greatest public thanks. 

Furthermore, I have changed Dübner’s wording from the "Roman book" <note>referring to a specific manuscript or edition source</note> in the places written below: In Chapter III, I enclosed the words **cithara-singing and** <note>original: τὴν κιθαρῳδίαν καὶ</note> in brackets, as they are missing in the manuscript. After **Thamyris, a Thracian by race,** <note>original: Θάμυριν δὲ τὸ γένος Θρᾷκα</note> I added **and** <note>original: καί</note>. In Chapter VI: **those things regarding the gods** <note>original: τὰ γὰρ πρὸς τοὺς θεοὺς</note> in the Roman source is used instead of Dübner's phrasing. In Chapter XII: **at their age** <note>original: κατ' αὐτοὺς τὴν ἡλικίαν</note> instead of **at that age**. In Chapter XVII: **that also in those** <note>original: ὅτι καὶ ἐν ἐκείναις</note> following the Roman source and Wyttenbach, instead of **and in those**. — **but truly also that [there are] processional songs** <note>original: ἀλλὰ μὴν καὶ ὅτι προσῴδια</note> (this is also in the Paris manuscript 3, according to Burette) instead of **still**. In Chapter XXV: **appearing** <note>original: παραγιγνόμεναι</note> using a different spelling for "arriving." In Chapter XXVII: **difference** <note>original: διαφορᾶς</note> instead of **destruction** <note>original: διαφθορᾶς</note>. In Chapter XXIX: **he used in the [Songs] of the Mother** <note>original: κέχρηται ἐν τοῖς Μητρῴοις</note> (see Wyttenbach’s note on this place) instead of the plural **they used**. In Chapter XXXIII, I added **to be** <note>original: εἶναι</note> after **cause** <note>original: αἰτίαν</note>, and in the same place **and** <note>original: καί</note> before **the character**. In Chapter XLIII, I wrote **took up at the [right] times** <note>original: τοῖς καιροῖς παρέλαβε</note>; later, **sufficiently** <note>original: ἄδην</note>. In Chapter XLIV: **to happen** <note>original: γίγνεσθαι</note> using an alternative spelling. At the end, I added **with** <note>original: σύν</note> before **children**. Furthermore, in Chapter XVII, I removed an unpleasant vocal gap <note>term: hiatus; a break between two vowel sounds in adjacent syllables</note> by conjecture...

<page-num>XV</page-num>
<header>PREFACE. XV</header>

...**by Zeus, without knowing** <note>original: μὰ Δία ἀγνοήσας</note>. In Chapter XXVIII, I wrote **into dithyrambs** <note>original: εἰς διθυράμβων</note> (plural) instead of **into a dithyramb** (singular). At the beginning of Chapter XLIV, I retained **to you** <note>plural</note> from Wyttenbach's edition. In addition, I have changed not a few things based on conjecture <note>educated guesses where the manuscript is broken</note>, whether they were the work of other learned men or my own modest emendations accepted into the text, though the reason for each is given in the commentaries. I decided this was all the more necessary because all surviving manuscripts of Plutarch’s *Moralia* <note>Plutarch's "Moral Essays," the collection containing this dialogue</note> flowed from a single original source <note>term: archetype</note> that was, in many places, depraved, mutilated, and tampered with—as Dübner mentioned in his own preface. 

The dialogue *On Music* itself was corrupted in that original source even more than the others by ignorant scribes who were little versed in musical matters; as a result, one can hope for very little safety for the text from the manuscripts alone. This opinion was also confirmed by samples of various readings from other Italian manuscripts, which **R. Hercher** kindly shared with me via letter. Therefore, what good would it have done if I had anxiously retained everything that is read as corrupt in the manuscripts, leaving readers to find the truth in the notes? Nevertheless, I imposed a strict law upon myself in this area: to receive no conjectures except those that seemed either most certain or showed the highest probability, even if others may judge them differently. To corrupt words for which I did not wish to apply a more forceful cure, I placed a single asterisk (*). I marked gaps in the text with two or three asterisks (** / ***). Additions that seemed to be the work of later editors <note>term: interpolations</note> were enclosed in brackets [ ].

I have added the Latin translation of Wyttenbach, corrected and emended in many places following Dübner. I certainly would have provided another, older version made from a good manuscript—which contains no small amount of critical help—if only it had been possible to compare it. I am speaking of the version by **Carlo Valgulio**, an Italian man, which was published separately with a dissertation on Plutarch’s *Dialogue on Music* dedicated to Titus Pyrrhinus; it first appeared in Brescia in the year 1507 from the press of Angelus Britannicus. It was reprinted along with Valgulio’s version of *Precepts on Marriage* and the dialogue *On Moral Virtue* in Basel by Cratander. These same small works, with the addition of the booklet *On the Opinions of Philosophers* as translated by the same Valgulio, along with certain other writings of Plutarch, Censorinus, and the *Table of Cebes*, were produced in Paris...

<page-num>XVI</page-num>
<header>### PREFACE.</header>

...issued from the press of **Ascensius** <note>Josse Bade, known as Ascensius, a famous 16th-century printer</note> in the year 1514 in the largest format <note>original: "forma maxima"</note>; see **Maittaire**, *Annals of Typography*, Volume II, page 225. I know of a fourth edition from **Venice** in the year 1532 in an octavo format <note>original: "octonis foliis"; a book size where each sheet is folded into eight leaves</note> from **Graesse’s** *Literary History*. I have not seen this version myself; therefore, I can only offer readers what **Burette** <note>Jean-Pierre Burette, an 18th-century French scholar of ancient music</note> transcribed from it in his own commentaries. However, so that my edition would not be deprived of all its splendor, I wished to add the complete French translation by **Jacques Amyot** from the Paris edition of 1582. For Amyot not only expressed the author's words accurately, using a charming and most pleasant style of speech—so that his version is very pleasing to all who have some knowledge of French literature—but he also corrected the very words of **Plutarch**, aided by good critical resources. The extent of his care and diligence in comparing the various readings of both French and Italian manuscripts and old editions, and in consulting and collecting the conjectures of learned men, was recently explained by **Auguste de Blignières** (following **Fabricius** in the *Greek Library*) in the book titled: *Essay on Amyot and the French Translators of the 16th Century*, Paris, 1851, page 208. Soon, however, after my plan changed for other reasons, I thought I should be content with noting the various readings from Amyot’s version.

It remains for me to speak of **Burette**, a man who deserves the highest credit for a more accurate knowledge of ancient Greek music, who illustrated this *Dialogue on Music* with singular diligence and provided it with an extensive commentary. Since it contains many things which, given our current knowledge of ancient literature, might seem either obsolete or false—and many things which no one today would argue over in a commentary—the whole work could not be presented to readers. However, whatever of "good fruit" <note>original: "bonae frugis"; meaning useful or valuable material</note> I judged to be in his notes, I have accurately excerpted, adding the author's name, as it would be wrong to deprive him of his due honor. By **Burette’s** commentary, I mean the individual papers of that most learned man inserted into the *Memoirs of the French Academy of Inscriptions* between the years 1728 and 1743, which I have cited from the smaller edition <note>a multi-volume collection of academic papers</note>...

The edition of **Burette** itself, which appeared in Paris in 1735...

<page-num>XVII</page-num>

...in quarto format <note>original: "foliis quaternis"; a book size where sheets are folded into four leaves</note> with this title: *Plutarch's Dialogue on Music, translated from the Greek by J. P. Burette with remarks* <note>original French: "Dialogue de Plutarque sur la musique..."</note>—is an extremely rare book, of which only twelve copies are said to have been printed; I have not read it. However, I have profited greatly in interpreting Plutarch's words from that excellent and copious exposition on Greek lyric poetry <note>original: "poesi melica"</note> which the most distinguished **Bernhardy** gave in his *History of Greek Literature*, which I have turned to my own use with due respect. These are the main things I had to advise readers by way of a preface. I earnestly pray and hope that they will receive my labor kindly and with some benefit.

I had written this far some months ago. But now, having looked over the printer's proofs <note>original: "typothetarum plagulis"</note> again, I see first that on page 9, line 19, by some chance, the words of Plutarch were published as "and finally Pericleitus" <note>original Greek: "τελευταιόν τε Περίκλειτον"</note> instead of "but finally Pericleitus" <note>original Greek: "τελευταῖον δὲ Περίκλειτον"</note>. On page 49, line 20, read "Lacedaemonians" <note>original Greek: "Λακεδαιμονίους"</note>. Other things are missing from the commentary by my own fault, which, since they are still found complete in my notes, I have decided to write below. Thus, readers should be advised that on page 22, line 20, I omitted the word "upon" <note>original Greek: "ἐπὶ"</note> before the phrase "the Dorian mode," because it seemed to have been repeated without cause by a scribe's error from the preceding line and was entirely useless. Furthermore, let these things be added:

Page 56, line 1: A "guide" <note>term: kathegetes; a mentor or leader of one's life</note> is he according to whose example we shape ourselves, whom we follow as a leader and moderator of life; a "teacher" <note>term: didaskalos</note> is he from whom we learn something. Thus we should understand the words of the wife in Plutarch's *Conjugal Precepts*, chapter 48: "But it is no less dignified to hear a wife saying: 'Husband, you are to me a **guide**, a **philosopher**, and a **teacher** of the most beautiful and divine things.'" <note>original Greek: "ἀνερ, ἀτὰρ σύ μοι ἐσσὶ καθηγητὴς καὶ φιλόσοφος καὶ διδάσκαλος..."</note> **Himerius** called **Aristotle** the "guide" <note>original Greek: "καθηγεμόνα"</note> of **Hermias** in Oration VI, 6.

Page 58, line 37: What I said about the **Platonists** <note>followers of Plato's philosophy</note> receives light from **Sextus Empiricus**, *Against the Musicians*, page 753, line 27: "And indeed, if neither the followers of Plato accepted music, etc." <note>original Greek: "καὶ μὴν εἰ οὔτε οἱ περὶ τὸν Πλάτωνα μουσικὴν ἀπεδέξαντο..."</note> Regarding the musical studies of the **Peripatetics** <note>followers of Aristotle</note> and even of **Theophrastus** himself, the words of Plutarch in *It is Not Possible to Live Pleasantly [according to Epicurus]*, page 1095 E, ought to be mentioned above all: "What are you saying, **Epicurus**? In the morning you walk into the theater to listen to singers and flute-players, but at a banquet, when **Theophrastus** is discussing harmonies, and **Aristoxenus** is discussing transitions, and **Aristotle**..." <meta>This page continues the editorial preface, transitioning from a discussion of past editions to a detailed list of scholarly corrections and supplementary notes (corrigenda and addenda) based on recent philological research into ancient Greek music and history.</meta>

<page-num>XVIII</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

Nauck, in the *Rhenish Museum* <note>original: "Mus. Rhen." — a major German journal for classical studies</note>, Vol. VI, p. 432, proposed for the phrase "Aristophanes on Homer, you will cover your ears with your hands," showing displeasure and loathing; concerning which words Nauck wrote on p. 433: "the words 'concerning changes' <note>original: "περὶ μεταβολῶν"</note> are dark to me in the above. Should physical questions (weather changes and the like) perhaps be hinted at here? If so, one would expect 'Aristoxenus speaking on harmonies and Theophrastus on changes, etc.'" — It is clear to everyone that this is full of errors. For "changes" <note>original: "μεταβολαί"</note> must be understood as **musical modulations**. From the same passage of Plutarch, the following should also be transcribed, which relate to music: "For what sort of flute <note>original: "αὐλός" (aulos) — a double-reed wind instrument</note>, or lyre tuned for song, or what chorus — so delighted **Epicurus** and **Metrodorus**, as much as the discourses and teachings on choruses, and the problems concerning flutes, rhythms, and harmonies, delighted **Aristotle**, **Theophrastus**, **Hieronymus**, and **Dicaearchus**? Such as: why the narrower of equal flutes sounds deeper; and why, when the panpipe <note>original: "σύριγγος"</note> is pulled up, it becomes sharp in all its tones, but when tilted, it becomes deep again; and when brought close to the other it sounds deeper, but when moved away it sounds sharper."

p. 61, 19: Moreover, Plutarch himself had inspected the records <note>original: "ἀναγραφάς" — official registers or chronicles</note> which were preserved in **Sparta** in his age. In addition to the passage cited from the book *Against Colotes*, the *Life of Agesilaus*, chapter 19, is relevant here: "But we found in the **Spartan records** the wife of **Agesilaus** named as Cleora, and his daughters as Eupolia and Prolyta." Cf. Hertzberg, *Life of King Agesilaus*, p. 226. Also below, in the eighth chapter, the records of the **Panathenaean games** <note>original: "fasti Panathenaeorum"</note> are cited, if I am not mistaken, as if they had been inspected by Plutarch himself. It is strange that **Pausanias** reported nothing by name from these records.

p. 64, 22: I have furthermore found the word "destruction" <note>original: "πόρθησις"</note> in **Josephus**, *Jewish Antiquities*, Book X, 10, 3, Vol. II, p. 327, 1 (Bekk. edition) and in **Alciphron**, III, 45.

p. 65, 31: Concerning the signs of punctuation, if I see correctly, my most learned friend **Karl Schmidt** of Stettin recently settled the matter in the congratulatory booklet with which the masters of the Stettin Gymnasium honored the four-hundredth anniversary of the **University of Greifswald**.

p. 68, 20: However, **Sextus Empiricus**, in *Against the Musicians*, p. 752, 15, seems to look toward another **nome** <note>original: "nomum" — a traditional melodic pattern or "law" of composition in ancient music</note>, and perhaps not even a nome at all:

<column-break/>

<page-num>XIX</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

"at any rate, the same melody is a stimulant for horses, but for humans listening in theaters, it is not at all so. And perhaps for horses it is not stimulating, but rather disturbing."

p. 69, 14: In the same word, and indeed in a very similar manner, the scribes made an error in **Athenagoras**, *A Plea for the Christians*, chapter 1, where Gesner's most correct conjecture restored "Tennes the Tenedian" for "Tennes the divine."

p. 73, 47: Nor is **Glauca of Chios**, a most noble **cithara player** <note>original: "citharistria"</note> (cf. Bergk, *Rhenish Museum*, Vol. I, p. 357 ff.), called a "composer of tunes" <note>original: "κρουματοποιός"</note> by the commentator on **Theocritus** (IV, 31) for any other reason than that she also sang to the flutes, which an epigram by **Hedylus** in **Athenaeus** (IV, p. 176 C) seems to show.

p. 78, 22: after "words" add "as they wish."

p. 83, 6: Concerning the same "Many-headed" <note>original: "Polycephalo"</note> nome, let us hear **Nonnus** in the *Dionysiaca*, XL, 27:

> The Berecyntian — double flutes
> bellowed the terrible Libyan wail, which formerly both
> Stheno and Euryale, with a single many-necked voice,
> shrieked out in mourning for the newly-slain Medusa,
> while two hundred dragons hissed from their heads;
> from whose lamenting, winding whistling of their hair,
> they named the 'many-headed' dirge of Medusa.

p. 85, 33: **Nonnus**, in *Dionysiaca* XIII, 360, pointed out by Meineke on Theocritus I, 24 (p. 176, third edition), attributes the invention of the **Libyan flute** to **Minerva** <note>Athena</note>, as does XXIV, 36:

> lest ever your music-loving Athena should blame you,
> she who once found the Libyan mold of the twin-joined flutes
> sounding as a grim imitation of the Gorgon's heads.

p. 95, 33: Concerning the six divisions of the **tetrachord** <note>a series of four notes forming the basis of Greek scales</note>, **Sextus Empiricus** teaches thus in *Against the Musicians*, p. 758, 14: "but then again, the harmonic melody of those being sung is indivisible, while the diatonic and the chromatic had certain more specific differences: the diatonic had two, called the 'soft' diatonic and the 'intense'; while the chromatic had three: for one part of it is called 'tonic,' another 'hemiolic' <note>original: "ἡμιόλιον"</note>, and another 'soft'." In this passage, it is strange—and it escaped the editors—that instead of "melody" <note>original: "μέλος"</note> one should read "genus" <note>original: "γένος"</note>, and instead of "hemitonion" one should read "hemiolion."

<meta>This page continues the editorial preface, which serves as a detailed commentary and correction list (addenda et corrigenda) for a primary text on ancient music. The author is cross-referencing historical accounts of musical performance in Alexandria, Sparta, and Athens with various classical sources like Nonnus, Plutarch, and Sextus Empiricus.</meta>

<page-num>XX</page-num>
<header>XX&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;PREFACE.</header>

p. 100, 25: Regarding the great zeal of the Alexandrians for music in a later age, **Herodian** <note>original: "Herodian. IV, 8, 8" — a 3rd-century historian of the Roman Empire</note> also seems to testify in the passage where a reception was prepared for the Emperor **Antoninus Caracalla** as he turned aside toward Alexandria, "the likes of which they say had never happened for any king; for instruments of every kind of music <note>original: "πάσης μούσης ὄργανα" — literally 'instruments of every Muse'</note> were placed everywhere and produced a varied sound."

p. 102, 16: Regarding the spelling **Torebus** <note>original: "Τόρηβος"</note> instead of Torebus, I am now in doubt following a recent reading of **Nonnus** <note>original: "Nonni... Dionys. XIII, 465" — refers to the *Dionysiaca*, a massive epic poem</note>:
->“those who [dwell in] wide Torebus, and those who were the nurses of Plutus —”<-

p. 108, 35: The correction of **flute-players** <note>original: "αὐλητάς" (auletas)</note> for **flute-makers** <note>original: "αὐλοποιούς" (aulopoious)</note> now seems probable to me. **Spanheim** <note>Ezekiel Spanheim, a 17th-century scholar</note> on Callimachus’ *Hymn to Diana*, verse 243, page 293, says: "these same pipes <note>original: "fistulae" — likely panpipes or simple whistles</note>, after the <term>tibia</term> <note>a double-reed woodwind instrument, similar to an oboe, often called a 'flute' in older translations</note> had later been introduced, were simultaneously retained in ancient times at the Pythian games; although they were afterwards rejected by **Telephanes of Megara**, as Plutarch teaches in *On Music*, page 1138."

p. 111, 39: Perhaps the writer intended to name **Timotheus**. In any case, unless we wish to assign the first place among the "Long-Lived" <note>original: "Macrobios" — referring to a list of famous long-lived men</note> to **Antigenidas**, a distinction seems necessary between the younger Antigenidas, a contemporary of **Alexander**, and the elder, a very famous flute-player in the time of **Philoxenus**. This opinion seems supported by the fact that different names are reported for his father. Compare O. Jahn on Cicero’s *Brutus*, 187.

p. 115, 10: To this pertains **Sextus Empiricus** in *Against the Musicians*, page 749, 28: "The leaders of Greece and those famous for bravery, the **Spartans**, always went to war with music leading them. And those who followed the advice of **Solon** <note>the famous Athenian lawgiver</note> drew up for battle to the sound of the flute and the lyre, making their armed movement rhythmic."

p. 116, 11: Perhaps it will seem strange to someone that I have attributed any authority to the testimony of **Iamblichus** <note>a Neoplatonist philosopher often viewed as more mystical than historical</note>. But Iamblichus was not unskilled in musical matters, as is proven even by those things he set forth regarding the musical studies of **Pythagoras**, and he had proposed that a book be written on the treatment of harmony; compare his *Life of Pythagoras*, chapter 120, along with Nicomachus' *Introduction to Arithmetic*, pages 171–172.

p. 117, 40: However, **Bernhardy** <note>Gottfried Bernhardy, a 19th-century German philologist</note> concludes otherwise in his *History of Greek Literature*, Vol. II, part 1, page 427: "even the <term>paracataloge</term> <note>a technique in Greek drama where a performer spoke over instrumental music, similar to modern melodrama</note> will have been nothing other than the insertion of a related rhythm with musical notation or an instrumental movement." — I do not fully understand another passage by this great man regarding the *paracataloge* in Vol. I, page 314, which I deeply regret.

<column-break/>

<page-num>XXI</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;XXI</header>

p. 122, 8: Since it has often happened in this commentary that clans are mentioned in whom musical skill was passed down from father to son through a kind of inheritance, lest anyone think this is contrary to ancient custom, it is worthwhile to mention the **Eunidae**, an Attic clan. They traced their origin back to **Euneus of Lemnos** <note>the son of the hero Jason and Hypsipyle</note>, about whom **Bergk** <note>Theodor Bergk, a German philologist</note> remarked in his *Commentary on Ancient Comedy*, page 70: "this clan, like many others among the Athenians, was joined by strict necessity with the worship and religion of the gods: for they played the strings and danced in the sacred rites." **Hesychius** <note>an ancient lexicographer</note> says: "Eunidae: a clan named after Euneus, son of Jason; a clan of dancers and lyre-players. Others say a certain clan of lyre-players in Athens." **Harpocration** <note>another 2nd-century lexicographer</note>, page 89 of Bekker’s edition: "Eunidae — there is a clan among the Athenians named the Eunidae, and they were singers-to-the-lyre who provided their services for the sacred rites." Add **Photius**, page 36, 16. Hence they were especially involved in processions; see **Pollux**, Book VIII, 103, who says that even the heralds in the solemn sacred rites were chosen from this clan. It is not necessary to report on other clans of poets and singers.

p. 140, 29: In **Nonnus**, however, the **rhomptra** <note>original: "ῥόπτρα" — often interpreted as a bullroarer or a type of bronze clapper</note> seem to be distinct from the drums (*tympana*) and rather agree with the **platage** <note>clappers or rattles</note>. For they are listed among the playthings of boys in *Dionysiaca* XLVIII, 779, and furthermore he said "he struck the *rhomptra*" in XLVII, 731. You may also gather that the *rhomptra* were different from cymbals from XXXIII, 228. They were made of bronze (XIV, 348; XLVII, 722; XLVIII, 779). They are called "Lydian" in XLV, 18; and "clattering" <note>original: "περίκροτα"</note> in XLIII, 102. He spoke of "shaking the *rhomptra*" (IX, 116; XLVII, 722), and "brandishing" or "whirling" them (XIV, 348; XVII, 344; XX, 302). The instrument itself "shouts" or "rings" in XXVIII, 42. I have not understood what is read in XLVI, 121.

It is also worthwhile to consult Nonnus regarding the remaining Bacchic instruments. Thus he mentioned **krotala** <note>castanets or clappers</note> in XVI, 400, and their "crash" in XVIII, 107. The goddess **Rhea** is called the "clapper-loving goddess" in XX, 37 — and you may refer the following to the striking of the instrument in XXVII, 226:
->“and by the alternating hands of clapper-loving women<-
->a double-striking sound was beaten”<-
(for it is incorrectly read as "Echo" in Graefe's edition). In a strange manner, one of the Bacchantes in XIV, 401:

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary and correction of a text on ancient music, specifically focusing on the descriptions of musical instruments found in the works of the 5th-century epic poet Nonnus of Panopolis.</meta>

<page-num>XXII</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

->— he chased the race of enemies with a hero-breaking <term>rhombus</term> <note>original: "ῥόμβῳ" (rhombō). This was a bullroarer or a whirling instrument used in religious mysteries to create a roaring sound.</note> —<-

and I refer the "Berecyntian clatter" <note>original: "πάταγον Βερέκυντα" — likely referring to the noisy worship of the goddess Cybele on Mount Berecynthus</note> to the same rhombus in book XLIV, 140. The drum <note>original: "Tympanum"</note> sacred to Rhea (XIV, 214, where I am not sure whether "cymbals" <note>original: "κύμβαλα"</note> should be read instead of "drums" <note>original: "τύμπανα"</note> compared with X, 387; see however XII, 121) he very often called "ox-hide" <note>original: "βοείην"</note> ("the loud-crashing circle of ox-hide" XI, 125) and "double-striking ox-hide" (XV, 54; XLV, 16; cf. XXIX, 285), because it was beaten with the hand from both sides; and the "twin-beaten circles of ox-hide" in XX, 307 (cf. XVIII, 105; XLIII, 73) refer to the same thing. Because it was bound by a bronze hoop, it is called the "resounding ox-hide heavy with bronze" (X, 388). But it is a new detail, which we learn from XV, 53 and XLIII, 116, that these drums were suspended from the neck by a leather strap. Finally, cymbals are called "deep-roaring with twin bronze" (XIV, 402 and XXIX, 238), where they are shaken by Terpsichore <note>the Muse of dance</note>. Notable is the passage in XLVIII, 781:

->and quickly he gave the cymbals, which the nurses shake<-
->as remedies for infants against grief-loving pains.<-

p. 141, 14: The panpipe <note>original: "Fistula" or "Syrinx"</note>, whose reeds are joined by well-compacted wax, is said to be made of wax in Theocritus I, 126, if we listen to the interpreter **Meinecke** <note>August Meinecke, a 19th-century classical scholar</note> on page 197 of his third edition. If we grant this, the "wax-bound breath" mentioned in the same Theocritus, Epigram V, 4, regarding the breath of a panpipe joined with wax, will not bother us much. — **Colluthus** <note>a Greek epic poet, c. 500 AD</note> in verse 124 called the panpipe of Paris "a choir of sweet-sounding reeds," perhaps remembering Nonnus, *Dionysiaca* II, 21. Nonnus himself, if we look through his poems, knows Pan as the inventor of the panpipe (XLI, 373) and called the instrument itself "the thing of Pan" <note>original: "Πανιάδα"</note> (VII, 49; XVIII, 104; XXIII, 212; XXIV, 40) — "pastoral" (XLIII, 293) — "herding" (XXVII, 227 and also 294):

->and the god of the country-dwellers, ruling the herding trumpet<-
->— — — — goat-pasturing Pan,<-

where it seems certain that "panpipe" <note>original: "σύριγγος" (syringos)</note> should be read instead of "trumpet" <note>original: "σάλπιγγος" (salpingos)</note>. Furthermore, the <term>syrinx</term> <note>panpipe</note> is called "shifting-songed" (XL, 223), "sweet-toned" (XXIX, 287), "sweet-speaking" (X, 387), and "abundant," which "shrills piercingly" (XX, 4). But Nonnus also knows a warlike use of the syrinx (cf. XIII, 510; cf. Herodotus I, 17), and for that reason he called it "battle-rousing" (XIV, 403) and also "army-leading" (XXVII, 227; XXXIX,

<column-break/>

<page-num>XXIII</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

128). Therefore the syrinx "booms" and "shrieks" several times, and its "clanging" is mentioned in XX, 306.

p. 145, 40: This is that <term>tibia</term> <note>a reed instrument like an oboe</note> about which Bacchus speaks in Nonnus, *Dionysiaca* XLIII, 71:

->my horned pipe, striking a warlike sound,<-
->might utter a responsive melody to the bellowing conch<-

compared with VIII, 26: "the herding din of the horned pipe." Furthermore, Nonnus called **Hyagnis** the inventor of the double pipe with finger-holes (p. 143, 36) in XLI, 374; and this is that "double-voiced pipe of Athena," which **Marsyas**, the son of Hyagnis, blows in the contest established with Apollo (X, 233). In passing, I advise that in the same book, verse 335:

->but the double-voiced pipe of the clear-sounding Loves<-

one should read "clear-sounding" <note>changing the Greek case from genitive plural to nominative singular</note>; on the other hand, "pipe of the Loves" is correct, just as "panpipes of the Loves" in XIII, 507. Finally, the "Acharnian reed" is notable, which is mentioned in XLVII, 23.

p. 148, 31: It was the opinion of the ancients that in olden times shells were used in place of trumpets, as among the barbarians (see **Sextus Empiricus**, *Against the Musicians*, p. 753, 11). Therefore **Triton** is praised as the inventor of the trumpet, whom **Forkel** <note>Johann Nikolaus Forkel, an 18th-century music historian</note>, following a poor author and deceived by a poor spelling, called "Arichonda," a name I have sought in vain in any ancient writer. Cf. Nonnus, *Dionysiaca* I, 61; XXXVI, 93. The same author called the shell <note>original: "κόχλον"</note> a "sea-trumpet" (XLIII, 299; cf. XVII, 92). Furthermore, the Scholion on Nicander's *Alexipharmaca* 393 applies here: "the <term>strombos</term> <note>a spiral shell</note>, which the ancients called an 'ostrakon,' one of the shellfish which they used instead of a trumpet." Regarding another musical use of shells, see Athenaeus XIV, p. 636 E.

p. 155, 32: The story about the gift of the lyre made to Apollo by Mercury was well illustrated by **Preller** in his *Greek Mythology* (I, p. 245): <note>original German: "bei welcher dichtung wohl die betrachtung zu grunde liegt..."</note> "in which poem the underlying view is likely that the god of the herds and rain, the grazing shepherd (the 'Nomios'), is indeed very inventive, so that he ingeniously forms the lyre from the tortoise shell; but only the god of the sun and light knows how to treat the magnificent instrument rightly and how to find the divine tones and songs of joyful devotion upon it."

# PREFACE XXII.

->— he chased the race of enemies with a hero-breaking <term>rhombus</term> <note>original: "ῥόμβῳ" (rhombō). This was a bullroarer or a whirling instrument used in religious mysteries to create a roaring sound.</note> —<-

And I refer the "Berecyntian clatter" <note>original: "πάταγον Βερέκυντα"</note> to the same rhombus in book XLIV, 140. The drum <note>original: "Tympanum"</note> sacred to Rhea (XIV, 214, where I am not sure whether "cymbals" <note>original: "κύμβαλα"</note> should be read instead of "drums" <note>original: "τύμπανα"</note> compared with X, 387; see however XII, 121) he very often called "ox-hide" <note>original: "βοείην"</note> ("the loud-crashing circle of ox-hide" XI, 125) and "double-beaten ox-hide" (XV, 54; XLV, 16; cf. XXIX, 285), because it was beaten with the hand from both sides; and the "twin-beaten circles of ox-hide" in XX, 307 (cf. XVIII, 105; XLIII, 73) refer to the same thing. Because it was bound by a bronze hoop, it is called the "resounding ox-hide heavy with bronze" (X, 388). But it is a new detail, which we learn from XV, 53 and XLIII, 116, that these drums were suspended from the neck by a leather strap. Finally, cymbals are called "deep-roaring with twin bronze" (XIV, 402 and XXIX, 238), where they are shaken by Terpsichore <note>the Muse of dance</note>. Notable is the passage in XLVIII, 781:

> and quickly he gave the cymbals, which the nurses shake
> as remedies for infants against grief-loving pains.

p. 141, 14: The panpipe <note>original: "Fistula" or "Syrinx"</note>, whose reeds are joined by well-compacted wax, is said to be made of wax in Theocritus I, 126, if we listen to the interpreter Meineke (p. 197 of the third edition). If we concede this, the phrase "wax-bound breath" <note>original: "κηρόδετον πνεῦμα"</note> in the same Theocritus, Epigram V, 4, said regarding the breath of a panpipe joined with wax, will not offend us much. — Colluthus (v. 124) called the panpipe of Paris "a choir of sweet-sounding reeds" <note>original: "χορὸν εὐκελάδων δονάκων"</note>, perhaps remembering Nonnus's *Dionysiaca* II, 21. Nonnus himself, if we look through his poems, recognizes Pan as the inventor of the panpipe (XLI, 373) and called it the "Pan-pipe" <note>original: "Πανιάδα"</note> (VII, 49; XVIII, 104; XXIII, 212; XXIV, 40), the "shepherd’s pipe" <note>original: "ποιμενίην"</note> (XLIII, 293), and the "pastoral pipe" <note>original: "νομίην"</note> (XXVII, 227 and 294):

> and the god of the country-dwellers, ruling over the pastoral trumpet,
> goat-tended Pan,

where it seems certain that "panpipe" <note>original: "σύριγγος" (syringos)</note> should be read instead of "trumpet" <note>original: "σάλπιγγος" (salpingos)</note>. Furthermore, the <term>syrinx</term> <note>panpipe</note> is called "varied-singing" (XL, 223), "sweet-toned" (XXIX, 287), "sweet-speaking" (X, 387), and "abundant, which sounds shrilly" (XX, 4). But Nonnus also knew of a warlike use for the syrinx (cf. XIII, 510; compared with Herodotus I, 17), and for that reason he called it "battle-rousing" (XIV, 403) and "army-leading" (XXVII, 227; XXXIX,

<column-break/>

# PREFACE XXIII.
<page-num>XXIII</page-num>

128). Thus the syrinx "buzzes" and "shrieks" several times, and its "clatter" <note>original: "καναχή"</note> is mentioned in XX, 306.

p. 145, 40: This is that <term>tibia</term> <note>original: "aulos"; a reed instrument often translated as pipe or flute</note> about which Bacchus speaks in Nonnus's *Dionysiaca* XLIII, 71:

> my horned pipe, striking a warlike sound,
> might utter a responsive melody to the bellowing shell

compared with VIII, 26: "the pastoral sound of the horned pipe." Furthermore, Nonnus called Hyagnis the inventor of the double perforated pipe (p. 143, 36) in XLI, 374; and this is that "twin-voiced pipe of Athena" which Marsyas, the son of Hyagnis, blows in the contest established with Apollo (X, 233). I remark in passing that in the same book, v. 335:

---

-> but the twin-voiced pipe of the clear-sounding Loves <-

---

"clear-sounding" <note>original: "λιγύφθογγος" (ligyphthongos)</note> should be read; on the other hand, "pipe of the Loves" <note>original: "αὐλὸς Ἐρώτων"</note> is correct, just as "panpipes of the Loves" (XIII, 507). Finally, the Acharnian reed is notable, which is mentioned in XLVII, 23.

p. 148, 31: It was the opinion of the ancients that in olden times shells were used in place of trumpets, as among barbarians (see Sextus Empiricus, *Against the Musicians*, p. 753, 11). Thus Triton is praised as the inventor of the trumpet, whom Forkel—having followed a faulty author and being deceived by a faulty writing—called Arichondas; I have sought his name in vain in ancient writers. Cf. Nonnus's *Dionysiaca* I, 61; XXXVI, 93. The same author called the "shell" <note>original: "κόχλον"</note> a "sea-trumpet" (XLIII, 299; cf. XVII, 92). Furthermore, a Commentator on Nicander’s *Alexipharmaca* (393) relates to this: "the spiral shell, which the ancients called a tile, among the shellfish which they used instead of a trumpet." Regarding another musical use of shells, see Athenaeus XIV, p. 636 E.

p. 155, 32: The story of the gift of the lyre to Apollo by Mercury has been well illustrated by Preller in *Greek Mythology* I, p. 245:

> <note>original German quote</note> "In which poetry the underlying observation is that the god of herds and rain, the grazing shepherd <note>pastoral</note>, is indeed very inventive, so that he cleverly forms the lyre from the tortoise shell, but that only the god of the sun and light knows how to handle the glorious instrument correctly and to find the divine tones and songs of jubilant devotion upon it."

<page-num>XXIV, 1</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

p. 160, 17: I have not been able to determine exactly what form of the <term>pectis</term> <note>original: "pectidis". The *pectis* was an ancient Lydian stringed instrument, typically described as a small harp played with the fingers without a plectrum.</note> Nonnus imagined for himself, although he mentions it quite frequently. It is called "clear-sounding" <note>original: "Λιγυηχής" (ligyēchēs)</note> in Book VIII, 377; and in Book XLIII, 217, someone "pipes a song of war on the *pectis*" <note>original: "πηκτίδι συρίζει" (pēktidi syrizei). This is a strange phrasing, as the verb *syrizei* usually refers to the sound of a pipe or flute, not a harp.</note>. In the passage in Book XX, 93:

->"the *pectides* touch well after a victory at sea"<-
<note>original: "πηκτίδες εὖ ψαύουσιν ἐναλίην μετὰ νίκην" (pēktides eu psauousin enaliēn meta nikēn)</note>

I would prefer to read "are touched" <note>original: "ψαύονται" (psauontai), suggesting a passive voice—that the instruments are being played.</note>.

->Written at STETTIN on November 20, 1856.<-
<note>original: "SEDINI". Sedinum is the Latin name for Stettin, now Szczecin.</note>

---

# PLUTARCH ON MUSIC.

->THE CHARACTERS OF THE DIALOGUE<-
->ONESICRATES. SOTERICHUS. LYSIAS.<-

# PLUTARCH ON MUSIC.

->THE CHARACTERS OF THE DIALOGUE<-
->ONESICRATES. SOTERICHUS. LYSIAS.<-

<summary>The editor concludes his philological notes on the musical instruments in Nonnus's poetry before introducing the main text of Plutarch's "On Music" and its cast of speakers.</summary>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the actual dialogue of Plutarch's "On Music" (De Musica), moving from the editor’s technical preface into the literary text. The translator maintains the parallel structure of the Greek source and its Latin translation, both of which are presented here in English.</meta>

### CHAPTER I.

<margin>p. 1131 B</margin>
The wife of Phocion "the Good" <note>original: "Chrestos". This was a nickname given to the Athenian statesman Phocion (c. 402–318 BC) for his legendary integrity and austerity.</note> used to say that her own "ornament" <note>original: "kosmos". This term refers to jewelry or finery, but here it is used metaphorically for her source of pride.</note> was the military successes of Phocion; but I consider my own ornament—not only for myself but indeed shared by all my kin—to be my teacher's devotion to learning and discourse.
5 For we know that the most illustrious successes of generals are the causes of safety only from immediate dangers for a few soldiers, or for a single city, or at most for one nation; yet they in no way make these soldiers, citizens, or kinsmen better people. But education <term>paideia</term> <note>original: "παιδεία". In the Greek world, this referred to the total system of education and cultural training intended to create a well-rounded citizen.</note>, being the very essence of happiness <margin>C</margin>
and the cause of good judgment, is found to be useful not only to a household, a city, or a nation, but to the entire human race.
Therefore, just as the benefit of education is greater than all military achievements, so also is the memory of it more worthy of our devotion.

### CHAPTER II.

15 On the second day of the festival of Cronus <note>original: "Kronia". An Athenian festival in honor of the god Cronus, characterized by feasting and a temporary social equality between masters and slaves.</note>, the noble Onesicrates invited to a banquet men who were skilled in music;

---

### CHAPTER I. The wife of Phocion, who was called "The Just,"

said that her own finery consisted of the noble deeds of her husband: but I
judge as my own ornament—not only as my personal property but as something shared with all my
friends—the diligence of my teacher in the study of the liberal
5 arts. For we have known the most beautiful deeds of commanders
to have brought safety only from present danger to a few
soldiers, or to one city or nation: but they have by no means
made those soldiers, citizens, or kinsmen better. But a liberal
education <note>original: "liberalem disciplinam"</note>, which is both the material of happiness and
10 the cause of good counsel, you will find to be useful not only to a family, a city, or a people, but to the whole
human race. Therefore, by as much as this is more useful than all
warlike matters, by so much more should its memory be
cherished.

### CHAPTER II. Truly, on the second day of the Saturnalia, that distinguished

15 Onesicrates had called to a banquet men skilled in music: there were

<summary>This page introduces the dialogue by arguing that education (paideia) and the liberal arts are superior to military glory because they benefit all of humanity and improve the soul. The setting is established at a banquet hosted by Onesicrates during the festival of the Saturnalia (or Cronia).</summary>

<page-num>4</page-num>
->PLUTARCH’S ON MUSIC CH. II.<-

...indeed, Soterichus of Alexandria and Lysias, one of those who received a stipend from him. When the customary rites had been completed, the host spoke: "The question of what the cause of the human
<margin>D</margin> voice might be, my friends, is not suitable to investigate right now; for such a theory 5 requires a more sober leisure. But since the best grammarians <note>In the ancient world, "Grammar" (grammatike) encompassed not just syntax, but the study of literature, phonetics, and the correct preservation of language.</note> define the voice as 'struck air perceptible to the hearing,' and since we happened
yesterday to inquire into Grammar—as the art capable of crafting voices through letters and storing them in the 
memory—let us see what second science, following after it, is appropriate to the voice. 10 I believe it is Music. For it is a pious and primary duty for men to sing hymns to the gods, who alone granted them articulate speech. This very thing Homer also 
signified, in the verses where he says:
<margin>E</margin> > All day long with song they appeased the god, 15
> singing a beautiful paean <note>original: "paieona". A song of praise or thanksgiving, specifically dedicated to Apollo.</note>, the sons of the Achaeans,
> celebrating the Far-Shooter <note>original: "hekaergon". A common epithet for the god Apollo.</note>. And he, hearing them, was glad in his heart.
Come then, O members of the musical guild, remind our companions: who was the first to use music, 20 and what has time added to its growth, and who were the famous practitioners of the

### CHAPTER III.

<margin>F</margin> 5 Lysias then took up the word and said: "You seek the answer to a problem that has been investigated by many, good Onesicrates. For most of the Platonists and the best of the Peripatetic <note>original: "Peripatou". Followers of Aristotle's school of philosophy, so named because Aristotle used to walk (peripatein) while teaching.</note> philosophers have been eager to write both about ancient music and about the corruption that has 10 befallen it. Furthermore, those grammarians and music theorists who have reached the peak of education have devoted much effort to this. Indeed, there is great disagreement among those who have written on the subject.
Heraclides <note>Heraclides Ponticus (c. 387–312 BC), a philosopher and student of Plato who wrote extensively on music.</note>, in his *Collection of Musical Matters*, says that Amphion, the son of 15 Zeus and Antiope, first invented the singing and composition associated with the cithara <term>citharody</term> <note>original: "kitharoidia". The art of singing to the accompaniment of the cithara, a professional version of the lyre.</note>, his father clearly having taught him. He confirms this from the Registry <note>original: "Anagraphe". A public record or chronological list often used by ancient historians to verify dates and successions.</note> kept in Sicyon, through which he names the priestesses at Argos, the poets, and the musicians. At the same period, he says that Linus of 20 Euboea composed laments <note>original: "threnoi". Ritual songs of mourning.</note>, and Anthes of Anthedon in Boeotia composed hymns, and Pierus of Pie-

### CHAPTER V.

And Alexander <note>Likely Alexander Polyhistor, a 1st-century BCE scholar known for his encyclopedic compilations.</note> in his *Collection on Phrygia* said that Olympus first brought instrumental melodies <term>kroumata: music produced by striking or plucking an instrument, rather than singing.</term> to the Greeks, as did the Idaean Dactyls <note>Mythical master-craftsmen and magicians associated with Mount Ida.</note>. He says that Hyagnis was the first to
<margin>5</margin> play the flute, then his son Marsyas, and then Olympus. Terpander is said to have emulated the epic verses of Homer and the melodies of Orpheus. But Orpheus appears to have imitated no one; for no one had existed before him except for the flute-singing
<margin>10</margin> <margin>p. 1133</margin> poets, and the work of Orpheus is in no way similar to theirs. Clonas, the creator of flute-singing modes <note>original: "nomoi". These were standardized "laws" or melodic frameworks for performance.</note>, who lived shortly after Terpander, was a Tegean according to the Arcadians, but a Theban according to the Boeotians. After Terpander and Clonas, it is recorded that Archilochus lived. Some
<margin>15</margin> other writers say that Ardalus of Troezen established the flute-singing Muse before Clonas. There was also the poet Polymnestus, son of Meletes of Colophon, who composed the "Polymnestian" modes. Regarding Clonas, those who have written on the subject mention that he created the "Apothetos" <note>original: "Apotheton". Likely meaning "the set-aside" mode.</note> and

### CHAPTER VI.

In general, the art of lyre-singing <term>citharoedia: the practice of singing while playing the kithara, a professional version of the lyre.</term> established by Terpander remained entirely simple until the time of Phrynis. For in the old days, it was not permitted to compose lyre-songs as they do now, nor to change the harmonies and
<margin>10</margin> rhythms. In the "modes" <note>original: "nomoi".</note> they maintained the appropriate pitch <note>original: "tasis". The specific tension or tuning of the strings.</note> for each;
<margin>C</margin> for this reason they were given that name. They were called "laws" <note>The Greek word "nomoi" means both "musical modes" and "laws." Plutarch is explaining the linguistic connection: music was once strictly regulated by law.</note> because it was not permitted to deviate from the established form of pitch for each. For once they had performed their religious duties toward the gods, they immediately proceeded to the poetry of
<margin>15</margin> Homer and others. This is clear through the "Preludes" <note>original: "prooemia". Short musical introductions used to lead into epic recitations.</note> of Terpander. The shape of the lyre was also first changed according to Cepion, the student of Terpander. It was called "Asiatic" because the Lesbian lyre-singers, who lived near Asia, used it. Finally, they say Periclitus the lyre-singer won a victory in Lacedaemon [Sparta]...

### Chapter VII.

Now that we have presented the ancient modes for both the flute-singers and the lyre-singers, we shall move on to those intended for solo flute-playing <term>auletics: instrumental performance on the aulos (double-reed pipe) without vocal accompaniment.</term>. For it is said that the aforementioned Olympus, being a flute-player from Phrygia, composed a
<margin>10</margin> flute mode in honor of Apollo called the "Many-headed" mode <note>original: "Polykephalos". This famous piece was said to imitate the hissing of the serpents on the head of the Gorgon Medusa as she was slain by Perseus.</note>. They say this Olympus was a descendant of the first Olympus, the son of Marsyas, who composed
<margin>12</margin> modes in honor of the gods. For this elder Olympus, having become a favorite of Marsyas and having learned the art of the flute from him,
<margin>14</margin> introduced the harmonic modes to Greece, which the Greeks now use in the festivals of the gods. Others say that the "Many-headed" mode belongs to Crates, who was a
<margin>17</margin> student of Olympus. But Pratinas <note>A 5th-century BCE poet and dramatist from Phlius, known for his satyr plays and musical innovations.</note> says this mode belongs to Olympus the Younger. The so-called "Chariot" mode <note>original: "Harmatios nomos". This was a fast-paced, spirited melody, perhaps mimicking the motion of a chariot.</note> is said to have been
<margin>20</margin> composed by the first Olympus, the student of Marsyas. And regarding Mar-

...[Periclitus the citha]roedus <note>A professional singer of the lyre.</note> won the Carnea at Lacedaemon <note>Sparta</note>, being a native of Lesbos; after his death, the continuous succession of lyre-singers among the Lesbians ceased. Certain people falsely believe that Hipponax lived in the time of Terpander, although it appears that even Periclitus preceded Hipponax in age.

### Chapter VII.

<margin>5</margin> But since we have related the ancient modes for singing with the flute and the lyre together, we shall now pass to solo flute modes. For it is handed down that Olympus, whom we mentioned, when he was a piper from Phrygia, created a flute mode for Apollo, which was called "Many-headed." They say this Olympus was one of those
<margin>10</margin> who descended from the first Olympus, the disciple of Marsyas, who created modes for the gods. For he, since he had been the favorite of Marsyas and had learned the art of piping from him, brought the harmonic modes into Greece, which the Greeks now use in the festivals of the gods. Others
<margin>14</margin> make Crates the author of the "Many-headed" mode, who was a disciple of Olympus. Pratinas attributes it to Olympus the Younger. Moreover, the "Chariot" mode which they speak of is said to have been created by Olympus the Elder, the
<margin>17</margin> disciple of Marsyas. Some say that Marsyas was called Massen;

<margin>b</margin> In each of the aforementioned modes, they say that Sacadas composed a strophe <note>A structural unit of a choral song.</note> and taught the choir to sing: the first in the Doric mode, the second in Phrygian, and the third in Lydian. ### CHAPTER IX.

<margin>c</margin> Now, the first establishment of musical culture in Sparta was organized by Terpander. For the second establishment, the following men are credited as the primary <margin>10</margin> leaders: Thaletas of Gortyn, Xenodamus of Cythera, Xenocritus of Locri, Polymnestus of Colophon, and Sacadas of Argos. It is said that after these men introduced their innovations, the "Festival of the Naked Youths" <note>original: "Gymnopaediae"; an annual Spartan festival involving choral competitions.</note> in Lacedaemon was organized, as were the "Displays" <note>original: "Apodixes"</note> in Arcadia, and the so-called "Garment Festival" <note>original: "Endymatia"</note> in <margin>15</margin> Argos. Thaletas, Xenodamus, and Xenocritus were poets of Paeans <note>Hymns of praise or thanksgiving, typically addressed to Apollo.</note>; Polymnestus was a composer of the so-called "Orthian" songs <note>High-pitched, vigorous, or "upright" melodies.</note>, and Sacadas composed Elegies. Others say that Xenodamus was a composer of "Dance-songs" <term>hyporchemata: a lively choral song accompanied by mimetic dancing.</term> and not of Paeans, as Pratinas also claims. Indeed, a song by Xenodamus himself is remembered, <margin>20</margin>

<column-break/>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. IX—X.</header>

<margin>D</margin> which is clearly a dance-song. Pindar also made use of this genre of poetry. That the Paean differs from the dance-song will be made clear by the poems of Pindar himself, for he wrote both Paeans and dance-songs.

<margin>5</margin> ### CHAPTER X.

Polymnestus also composed flute-song modes <term>auloedic: a genre where a singer is accompanied by the aulos, a double-reed pipe.</term>. Whether he used the "Orthian" mode in his melodic composition, as the music theorists <note>original: "harmonikoi"</note> claim, we cannot state with certainty; for the ancient writers have said nothing on this matter. Regarding Thaletas of Crete, it is also disputed whether he was a poet of Paeans. For Glaucus, <margin>10</margin> asserting that Thaletas lived after Archilochus, says that he imitated the songs of Archilochus but extended them to a greater length, and introduced the "Paeonic" and "Cretic" rhythms into melodic composition. These rhythms were used neither by Archilochus, nor by Orpheus, nor by Terpander. They say that Thaletas <margin>15</margin> developed these from the flute-playing of Olympus and thus gained the reputation of being a great poet. Concerning Xenocritus, who was by birth from the Locrians in Italy <note>Specifically Locri Epizephyrii, a Greek colony in southern Italy.</note>, it is disputed whether he was a composer of Paeans; for they say he was a composer of heroic subjects involving dramatic actions <note>original: "pragmata echouson"; suggesting his poems had a narrative or theatrical quality.</note>. For this reason, some [consider his works] to be Dithyrambs...
