...to call his compositions. Glaucus states that Thaletas was older than Xenocritus in age.

### CHAPTER XI.

According to Aristoxenus, Olympus is believed by musicians to have been the inventor of the Enharmonic genus <note>The Enharmonic was one of the three "genera" or families of scales in ancient Greek music, characterized by the use of very small intervals called quarter-tones.</note>. For before him, all music was either Diatonic or Chromatic <note>The Diatonic (tones and semitones) and Chromatic (semitones and minor thirds) were the other two primary musical systems.</note>. It is suspected that the discovery happened something like this: while Olympus was moving within the Diatonic genus and frequently shifting the melody to the Diatonic *parhypate* <note>original: "παρυπάτη"; the second-lowest note in a four-note grouping or tetrachord.</note>, sometimes from the *paramese* <note>original: "παραμέση"; the note just above the middle note.</note> and sometimes from the *mese* <note>original: "μέση"; the middle note and central anchor of the Greek scale.</note>, he passed over the Diatonic *lichanos* <note>original: "λιχανός"; the "index-finger" note. By skipping this note, he created a larger melodic leap.</note> and realized the beauty of the <margin>p. 1135</margin> character <note>original: "ethos"; the emotional or moral quality of the music.</note>. Thus, admiring the system that resulted from this proportion, he adopted it and composed within it using the Dorian mode <note>A traditional Greek musical scale often associated with solemnity and bravery.</note>. For in this system, he touched upon neither the specific notes of the Diatonic nor those of the Chromatic, but rather those of the Harmonic. The first of his Enharmonic compositions were of this kind. For among these, they place the Spondean style <term>Spondiasmus: a type of melody used for libations (sponde) that omitted certain notes to create a specific sacred character.</term> first, in which none of the divisions of the tetrachord show their specific character—unless one looks at the "tense" Spondean style and imagines it to be Diatonic. It is clear, however, that anyone who assumes this would be putting forth something both false and unmusical.

<column-break/>

...named by some as Dithyrambs <note>Wild, passionate choral hymns typically dedicated to Dionysus.</note>. Glaucus writes that this same Xenocritus lived after Thaletas.

### CHAPTER XI.

Furthermore, Olympus—as Aristoxenus says—is thought by musicians to have been the discoverer of the Enharmonic genus. For before him, all systems were Diatonic and Chromatic. They suspect that Olympus made the discovery in this way: while he was occupied with the Diatonic genus and often transferred the modulation to the Diatonic *parhypate*—sometimes from the *paramese*, sometimes from the *mese*—and skipped over the Diatonic *lichanos*, he grasped the beauty of the <term>ethos</term> <note>original: "ἤθους"; that is, the beauty of the musical character.</note>. Thus, marvelling at and embracing the system established by this proportion, he employed it in the Dorian mode. For in this system, he did not touch upon those elements peculiar to the Diatonic or the Chromatic, but rather those belonging to the Harmonic genus. His first Enharmonic works were of this nature. Indeed, they identify the Spondean style as the first of these, in which none of the tetrachord divisions reveals itself as distinct—unless someone, observing the more "tense" Spondean style, should think it to be the Diatonic genus itself. But he who assumes this is clearly...

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XI—XII. 15</header>

...setting down something both false and out of tune. It is false because it is a quarter-tone <note>original: "diesis"; the smallest interval in Greek music.</note> lower than the tone placed at the leading position. It is out of tune because, even if one were to place the characteristic of the "tense" Spondean style into the power of the Diatonic tone, it would result in two <margin>B</margin> consecutive major thirds <note>original: "ditones"; an interval of two whole tones.</note> being placed together, one simple and one compound. For the "dense" <note>original: "pyknon"; a grouping of small intervals in the lower part of a tetrachord.</note> enharmonic style <margin>5</margin> placed around the middle notes, which is in use today, does not seem to belong to that original poet. This can easily be observed if one listens to someone playing the flute <note>original: "aulos"; a double-reed wind instrument, the primary instrument of Greek music.</note> in the ancient style; for he intends even the semitone in the middle notes to be uncomposite <note>original: "asyntheton"; an interval that is not further divided into smaller parts.</note>. <margin>10</margin> Such, then, were the first forms of the Enharmonic. Later, the semitone was divided in both the Lydian and the Phrygian modes. It appears that Olympus advanced music by introducing something that was previously unborn and unknown by those before him, and became the leader of beautiful Greek music.

### CHAPTER XII.

<margin>15</margin> There is also a discussion to be had regarding Rhythms. For certain types and species of rhythms were further discovered, as well as forms of melody-making and rhythm-making. First, the innovation of Terpander introduced a certain beautiful style into music...

<column-break/>

...stating something both false and discordant with the song. It is false because it will be lower by a quarter-tone than the tone placed in the primary position. It is foreign to the song because it would happen that, if one were to place the nature of the "tense" Spondean style into the function of a Diatonic tone, <margin>5</margin> two consecutive major thirds would be placed together, one compound and the other simple. For that "dense" enharmonic style which is located around the middle notes and is in use today does not seem to belong to that poet. This can easily be noticed if one hears someone singing with the pipe <note>original: "tibia"; the Latin term for the Greek aulos.</note> in the ancient manner; <margin>10</margin> for he wishes even the semitone in the middle notes to be uncomposite. And such was the Enharmonic genus in the beginning; later in time, the semitone was divided in the Lydian and Phrygian modes. It seems that Olympus, having increased the range of music, introduced something not yet born and hitherto unknown, and was the leader of beautiful Greek music.

<margin>15</margin> One must also speak concerning Rhythms. For certain further genera and species of rhythms were discovered, and even the composition of melodies and rhythms. For first, Terpander's striving for new things in that art brought a certain charming...

Thus, music is in every respect a venerable thing, being the invention of the gods.

### CHAPTER XV.

The ancients used music in a manner worthy of its dignity, just as they did with all their other pursuits. But people today, having cast aside its solemnity, introduce into the theaters a broken <note>original: "κατεαγυῖαν"; suggesting a rhythm that is fragmented or lacks the "straight" strength of older styles.</note> and chattering <note>original: "κοτίλην"; literally "twittering" or "garrulous."</note> kind of music in place of that manly, divine, and god-pleasing style of old. For this reason, Plato, in the third book of his *Republic*, expresses displeasure with such music. He rejects the Lydian mode <term>Harmonia: a "mode" or "tuning" associated with specific moods and regions.</term>, since it is high-pitched <note>original: "ὀξεῖα"; literally "sharp" or "acute."</note> and suitable for mourning; indeed, they say that its very first composition was of a mournful nature. For Aristoxenus

<column-break/>

...in the books they wrote concerning the *Apparitions* of the gods, they have recorded these things. And that statue is so ancient that it is said to have been made by the Meropes, who lived in the age of Heracles. Moreover, a piper accompanies the boy who carries the Lydian laurel to Delphi. Indeed, they say that from ancient times the sacred rites of the Hyperboreans were accustomed to be sent to Delos with the use of pipes, lyres, and pan-pipes. There are those who assert that the god himself sang with the pipe, as the best composer of songs, Alcman, relates. Corinna even says that Apollo learned to play the pipes from Minerva <note>Roman name for Athena.</note>. Therefore, music is altogether venerable as an invention of the gods.

### CHAPTER XV.

Furthermore, the ancients used it, as was fitting, just as they did all other disciplines. But in this age, its majesty having been abandoned, they bring into the theaters a broken and talkative music instead of that masculine and divine style, pleasing to the immortals, which Plato censures in the third book of his *Republic*, repudiating the Lydian mode as being shrill and adapted to lamentations: from which they say its first institution was mournful. For Aristoxenus 

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XV—XVI.</header>

writes in the first book of *On Music* that Olympus first played a funeral song <note>original: "ἐπικήδειον"; a song performed over a corpse.</note> on the flute in the Lydian mode upon the death of Python <note>The great serpent slain by Apollo.</note>. Some, however, say that Melanippides began this type of melody. But Pindar, in his *Paeans*, says that the Lydian mode was first taught by Anthippus for the wedding of Niobe. Others record that Tyrrhebus was the first to use this mode, as Dionysius Iambus relates.

### CHAPTER XVI.

The Mixolydian mode is also a passionate <note>original: "παθητική"; referring to the "pathos" or emotional suffering depicted in art.</note> one, suitable for tragedies. Aristoxenus says that Sappho was the first to discover the Mixolydian, and from her the tragic poets learned it. At any rate, they took it and joined it with the Dorian mode <note>The Dorian mode was considered the most "Greek," characterized by stability and "manliness."</note>, since the latter provides magnificence and dignity, while the former provides emotion; and through the mixture of these two, tragedy is composed. But in his *Historical Commentaries on Music*, he says that Pythoclides the flute-player was its inventor. Lysis, however, claims that Lamprocles the Athenian, having realized that the disjunction <term>Diazeuxis: a technical term in Greek music theory referring to the gap between two tetrachords in a scale.</term> did not occur where almost everyone thought it did, but rather at the high end, arranged its form to be like the interval from the *paramese* to the *hypate hypaton* <note>Technical names for specific strings/notes on the lyre; this indicates a specific octave species.</note>. Furthermore, they say that the "relaxed" Lydian mode, which is the opposite of the Mixolydian, is similar

writes in the first book of *On Music* that Olympus sang a mournful song with the pipes in the Lydian mode concerning the death of Python. There are those who make Melanippides the author of that song. Pindar in his *Paeans* says the Lydian mode was first used by Anthippus for the marriage of Niobe. Others say Turrebus was the first to use it, about which Dionysius Iambus tells.

### CHAPTER XVI.

The Mixolydian also, striking the soul, is fit for tragedies. Aristoxenus writes that this was first discovered by Sappho: from her the tragic poets learned it, who joined it with the Dorian; since the latter procures magnificence and majesty, while the former disturbs the mind, and from the mixture of both tragedy is truly composed. In the *Historical Commentaries on Harmony*, it is read that it was devised by Pythoclides the piper. Lysis maintains that Lamprocles the Athenian, when he noticed the disjunction was not where almost everyone had thought, but toward the acute [notes], established such a mode for it, as exists from the *paramesa* to the *hypate hypaton*. ### CHAPTER XVII.

Of these musical modes <note>original: "ἁρμονιῶν" / "harmonias"; refers to specific melodic scales or tunings associated with different Greek regions.</note>, since one of them is suited for lamentation and the other is somewhat loose and relaxed, Plato rightly rejected them. He chose the Dorian mode instead, as it was fitting for warlike and temperate men. And by Zeus, he did not do this out of ignorance—as Aristoxenus says in the second book of his *Musical Matters*—regarding the fact that even in those other modes there was something useful for the preservation of the state. For Plato paid great attention to the science of music, having been a student of Draco of Athens and Metallus of Acragas <note>Modern Agrigento in Sicily.</note>. But because, as we have already said, there is a great deal of majesty in the Dorian mode, he preferred it. He was not unaware that many Dorian *Parthenia* <note>original: "Παρθένια"; songs composed specifically for choruses of young maidens.</note> were written by Alcman, Pindar, Simonides, and Bacchylides, nor that Processionals and Paeans <note>Hymns usually dedicated to Apollo or for victory.</note> were composed in it.
<margin>p. 1137</margin>Furthermore, he knew that even tragic laments and some love songs were once set to the Dorian style. However, the songs dedicated to Ares <note>Mars</note> and Athena <note>Minerva</note>, along with the Spondaic <note>original: "σπονδεῖα"; solemn music played during libations or sacrifices.</note> melodies, were sufficient for him; for these are enough to strengthen the soul of a temperate man. And regarding the Lydian...

---

...resembling the Ionian, they claim it was found by Damon of Athens.

### CHAPTER XVII.

Of these musical modes, since one of them is prepared for mourning and the other is dissolute, Plato rightly rejected them and selected the Dorian, as being suitable for warlike and self-controlled men. By Hercules, he did not do this through ignorance—as I might say with Aristoxenus in the second book of *Musical Matters*—as if there were not something in those others that might be profitable for maintaining the constitution of public affairs. For Plato was most studious of music, as one who had heard Draco the Athenian and Metallus of Agrigentum. But because, as I have warned, he recognized a great majesty in the Dorian, he preferred that mode. He was not ignorant that many Dorian songs for maidens were made by Alcman, Pindar, Simonides, and Bacchylides; indeed, even Processionals, Paeans, and even tragic lamentations and certain love songs were included in the Dorian mode. But those things which were sung to Mars and Minerva and the Spondaic chants were sufficient for him: for those were powerful enough to strengthen the mind of an honest man. Likewise, regarding the use of the Lydian and Ionian...

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XVII—XIX.</header>

...and Ionian modes, he was not ignorant; for he knew that tragedy makes use of this kind of melody.

### CHAPTER XVIII.

Indeed, all the ancients, though they were not unacquainted with all the modes, used only some of them. For it was not ignorance that caused such narrowness and few-stringedness <note>original: "ὀλιγοχορδίας" / "paucitatem chordarum"; refers to instruments with a limited number of strings, contrasting with the "many-stringed" innovations of later periods.</note> among them; nor was it through a lack of skill that Olympus, Terpander, and those who followed their choice did away with the use of many strings and variety. At any rate, the poems of Olympus, Terpander, and all those of similar character bear witness to this. For though they have few strings and are simple, they surpass the varied and many-stringed works, so that no one can imitate the style <note>original: "τρόπον" / "modum"; the specific melodic and rhythmic character of a composer.</note> of Olympus, and those who occupy themselves with many-stringed and many-styled music are left behind by him.

### CHAPTER XIX.

That the ancients did not refrain from the "third" <note>Referring to the third note or string of a scale.</note> in the Spondaic style out of ignorance is made clear by its use in the "striking" <note>original: "κρούσει" / "pulsatione"; the technique of playing the strings, likely with a plectrum or fingers.</note> which occurred then. For they would never have used it in harmony with the *parhypate* <note>original: "παρυπάτην"; the "next-to-the-highest" note in the ancient Greek tetrachord system.</note> if they had not recognized its use. But it is manifest...

---

...harmonies, he was not ignorant, knowing that tragedy uses that kind of modulation.

### CHAPTER XVIII.

But even all the ancients, though they were unskilled in no harmony, nevertheless used only certain ones. For ignorance did not produce such constraints and fewness of strings among them; nor was it by lack of knowledge that Terpander, Olympus, and their followers rejected a multitude and variety of strings. And the songs of Olympus, Terpander, and others who held the same style argue this for all: which, although they are content with few strings and are simple, they stand above all the varied ones that require many strings. So much so, indeed, that no one can express the style of Olympus, and all are inferior to him when they turn themselves to complex modulation provided with many strings.

### CHAPTER XIX.

That the ancients in the Spondaic style did not ignore the use of the third string is sufficiently demonstrated by its employment in the striking of the strings that existed then. For they would not have used it in consonance with the *parhypate* if they had been ignorant of its use. But it is manifest...

It defends the technical skill of the "ancients" against the perceived superiority of modern, complex music.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the technical and ethical choices made by ancient Greek musicians, specifically explaining why certain notes and scales were intentionally omitted to preserve the "ethos" or character of a musical mode.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XIX.</header>

<margin>c</margin> ...Indeed, it is clear that the sense of beauty which is established in the Spondiac style <note>original: "σπονδειακῷ τρόπῳ"; a solemn, slow-paced musical style used for religious libations.</note> by the removal of the third note was what led their musical sense to shift the melody to the *paranete* <note>original: "παρανήτην"; literally "next to the highest note." In Greek music theory, notes were named based on their position on the lyre.</note>. The same logic applies to the *nete* <note>original: "νήτης"; the highest note of a four-note segment (tetrachord).</note>. For they used this note in instrumental playing <note>original: "κροῦσιν"; the striking of strings with a plectrum.</note> both as a dissonance against the *paranete* and as a consonance <margin>5</margin> with the *mese* <note>original: "μέσην"; the central note of the Greek scale system, acting as a tonal anchor.</note>. However, in the vocal melody, it did not seem to them to be appropriate for the Spondiac style. And it was not only with these notes, but also with the "highest of the conjunct" <note>original: "συνημμένων νήτῃ" / "synemmenon neta"; a specific note in the Greek system of overlapping four-note scales.</note> that <margin>D</margin> everyone behaved in this way. For in the instrumental playing itself, they created a dissonance with the *paranete* and *paramese* <note>original: "παραμέσην"; the note just above the middle note.</note>, while creating a consonance <margin>10</margin> with the *mese* and the *lichanos* <note>original: "λιχανόν"; the "forefinger" note, usually the third note from the bottom of a tetrachord.</note>. But in the vocal melody, the performer would have felt a sense of shame because of the specific character <note>original: "ἤθει"; the moral or emotional quality produced by music.</note> that this note creates. It is clear even from Phrygian music that this note was not unknown to Olympus <note>A legendary figure credited with inventing many Greek musical forms.</note> and his followers; for they used it not only in <margin>15</margin> instrumental playing, but also in vocal melodies in the songs for the Mother of the Gods and in certain Phrygian pieces. It is also clear regarding the *hypate* <note>original: "ὑπατῶν"; the lowest notes/strings of the scale.</note> that it was not through ignorance that they avoided this tetrachord <note>A scale of four notes.</note> in the Dorian mode; for they clearly used it in the other modes, proving they knew it. But to preserve the character of the mode, they <margin>E</margin> removed it from the Dorian scale, honoring its inherent beauty. <margin>20</margin>

<column-break/>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XX—XXI.</header>

### CHAPTER XX.

A similar situation exists among the tragic poets. For even to this day, tragedy does not use the chromatic or enharmonic scales <note>original: "χρωματικῷ γένει καὶ τῷ ἐναρμονίῳ"; the three "genera" or types of scales were Diatonic, Chromatic (using half-steps), and Enharmonic (using quarter-tones).</note>. Yet the cithara <note>A professional version of the lyre.</note>, which is many generations older <margin>5</margin> than tragedy, used them from the beginning. It is obvious that the chromatic scale is older than the enharmonic. Of course, one must define "older" according to human encounter and usage; for according to the nature of the scale-types themselves, one is not older than another. If, then, someone were to say that Aeschylus <margin>F</margin> or Phrynichus <note>Early masters of Greek tragedy.</note> avoided the chromatic scale out of ignorance, <margin>10</margin> would he not be speaking absurdly? For that same person would have to say that Pancrates <note>A later musician who favored old styles.</note> was ignorant of the chromatic scale; for he also mostly avoided it, though he did use it in some instances. Therefore, he clearly avoided it not out of ignorance, but by choice. Indeed, he strove to imitate, as he himself said, the style of Pindar and Simonides <note>Famous lyric poets of the 5th century BCE.</note>, and in general the style <margin>15</margin> that is now called "the ancient style."

### CHAPTER XXI.

The same argument holds true for Tyrtaeus of Mantinea, Andreas of Corinth, Thrasyllus of Phlius, and many others, all of whom we know avoided the chromatic scale, modulation, and the use of many strings <note>Referring to the trend of adding more strings to the lyre to allow for more complex and "corrupt" music.</note> by choice...

<summary>This page discusses how ancient Greek musicians and tragic poets intentionally avoided complex notes and chromatic scales not out of ignorance, but to preserve the traditional beauty and moral character (ethos) of their music. It cites masters like Olympus, Aeschylus, and Pindar as examples of those who preferred simplicity over technical complexity.</summary>
<keywords>Spondiac mode, Ethos, Nete, Paranete, Dorian mode, Olympus, Phrygian music, Tragedy, Chromatic scale, Enharmonic scale, Aeschylus, Pindar, intentional simplicity, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of the "ancients," arguing that their choice of musical simplicity was an intentional aesthetic and moral decision rather than a result of technical ignorance. It shifts from discussing specific flute-players to the philosophical underpinnings of music in Plato's works.</meta>

# PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXI.

<margin>p. 1138</margin> ...abtaining from chromaticism <note>original: "χρώματός" / "chromatos"; the use of semitones or "colored" notes, which was seen as more sensual and less stable than the older diatonic style.</note>, modulation, the use of many strings, and many other types of rhythms, harmonies, vocabularies, melodies, and modes of expression that were readily available to them. For example, Telephanes of Megara <note>A famous flute player of the 4th century BCE known for his conservative style.</note> was so hostile to the use of stops <margin>5</margin> <note>original: "σύριγξιν" / "syringxin"; referring to the sliding rings or finger-hole mechanisms on the *aulos* (flute) that allowed for easier chromatic modulation.</note> that he never even allowed flute-makers to add them to his instruments; indeed, it was chiefly for this reason that he withdrew from the Pythian Games. 

In general, if anyone concludes that those who do not use a particular technique are ignorant of it, they will find themselves condemning many people even today. For instance, we see the <margin>10 b</margin> followers of Dorion <note>A celebrated flute player and gourmet of the late 5th century BCE.</note> looking down on the style of Antigenidas <note>A rival innovator in flute music who popularized a more complex, emotional style.</note> because they do not use it; conversely, the followers of Antigenidas reject the Dorion style for the same reason. Similarly, cithara-players <note>original: "κιθαρῳδῶν"; professional musicians who sang while playing the *kithara*, a large, concert-grade lyre.</note> avoid the style of Timotheus <note>A revolutionary musician who added more strings to the lyre and introduced "New Music."</note>; for they have almost entirely wandered off into "patched-up" melodies <note>original: "καττύματα"; literally "cobbled-together" or "re-soled" bits of song.</note> and the poems of Polyidus. 

<margin>15</margin> Furthermore, if someone were to examine the concept of "variety" correctly and with expertise, comparing the past with the present, they would find that variety was indeed in use back then. The ancients employed a variety of rhythmic compositions that were actually more complex than what we have now; they certainly held rhythmic variety in high honor. <margin>20 c</margin> Also, the "dialects" of instrumental striking <note>original: "κρουματικὰς διαλέκτους"; the specific ways strings were plucked or struck to create different textures.</note> were more varied in those days. The difference is that modern musicians are lovers of stories <note>original: "φιλόμυθοι" / "philomythoi"; perhaps implying a focus on theatrical or program music.</note>, while the ancients were lovers of rhythm. It is clear, 

<column-break/>

# PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXI—XXII.

therefore, that the ancients did not abstain from "broken" melodies <note>original: "κεκλασμένων μελῶν"; melodies with frequent jumps or complex ornamentation, often viewed as effeminate or decadent by conservatives.</note> because of ignorance, but by deliberate choice. And why should this be surprising? For many other practices in life are not ignored by those who avoid them; rather, they are set aside as <margin>5</margin> inappropriate for certain uses because they are considered unseemly.

### CHAPTER XXII.

Having demonstrated that Plato did not reject certain musical elements out of ignorance or lack of experience, but because he judged them improper for his ideal state <note>Refers to Plato's *Republic*, where he banishes "soft" or "plaintive" musical modes.</note>, we shall next show that he was indeed an expert in harmony. In his account of the "generation of the soul" <note>original: "ψυχογονίᾳ" / "psychogonia"; the passage in Plato's *Timaeus* (35b-36b) where the Creator fashions the World Soul according to musical-mathematical ratios.</note> <margin>10 D</margin> in the *Timaeus*, he demonstrated his diligence in mathematics and music in this way: "And after these things, he filled up the double and triple intervals, cutting off portions from the whole and placing them in the space between them, so that in each interval there were two means." This introduction was a product of his expertise in harmony, <margin>15</margin> as we shall soon show. 

There are three primary "means" from which every other mean is derived: the arithmetic, the harmonic, and the geometric. In the first <note>Arithmetic</note>, the middle term exceeds and is exceeded by an equal number; in the third <note>Geometric</note>, the terms are related by an equal ratio; in the second <note>Harmonic</note>, it is neither by ratio nor by number. Therefore, Plato...

<summary>This page argues that the simplicity of ancient Greek music was a deliberate aesthetic choice rather than a sign of technical incompetence. It cites the flute-player Telephanes as an example and begins a technical proof of Plato's musical expertise based on the mathematical ratios found in his Timaeus.</summary>
<keywords>Telephanes of Megara, music theory, musical variety, rhythmic variety, Plato, Timaeus, World Soul, musical means, arithmetic mean, harmonic mean, geometric mean, ancient vs modern music</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical philosophy, moving from historical anecdotes about flute players to the mathematical and cosmic theories of Plato and Aristotle. It explains how music was seen not just as entertainment, but as a reflection of the numerical laws governing the soul and the universe.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXII.</header>

Plato, wishing to demonstrate through music the harmony of the soul and the four elements, <margin>e</margin> and the cause of the agreement between things that are unlike one another, revealed that in every interval there are two "soul-means" <note>original: "μεσότητας ψυχικὰς"; mid-points or averages that connect two extremes, here used to describe how the soul balances different parts of the universe.</note> according to musical ratio. <margin>5</margin> For it happens that in the musical concord of the octave <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν" / "diapason"; literally "through all [strings]," the interval where the pitch frequency doubles.</note>, there are two middle intervals, the proportion of which we shall demonstrate. For the octave is perceived in a double ratio <note>A 2:1 ratio (e.g., 12:6).</note>. For the sake of illustration, we shall represent this double ratio with the numbers six and <margin>F</margin> twelve. This interval extends from the *hypata meson* <margin>10</margin> <note>original: "ὑπάτης μέσων"; the lowest note of the "middle" tetrachord in the Greek scale, roughly equivalent to a modern E.</note> to the *neta diezeugmenon* <note>original: "νήτην διεζευγμένων"; the highest note of the "disjunct" tetrachord, an octave above the hypata meson.</note>. Since six and twelve are the extremes, the *hypata meson* takes the number six, and the *neta diezeugmenon* takes the number twelve. It is then necessary to take the numbers falling between these, which will appear as a "four-to-three" ratio <note>original: "ἐπίτριτος" / "sesquitertius"; the ratio 4:3, which creates the musical interval of a Fourth.</note> to one extreme and a "three-to-two" ratio <margin>15</margin> <note>original: "ἡμιόλιος" / "sesquialter"; the ratio 3:2, which creates the musical interval of a Fifth.</note> to the other. These numbers are eight and nine; for relative to six, eight is a "four-to-three" ratio, and nine is a "three-to-two" ratio. Such is the relationship to one extreme. Regarding the other extreme—the twelve—it is a "four-to-three" ratio to the nine, and a "three-to-two" ratio <margin>p. 1139</margin> to the eight. Since these numbers (eight and nine) lie between six and twelve, and since the interval of the octave <margin>20</margin> is composed of the Fourth <note>original: "διὰ τεττάρων" / "dia tettaron".</note> and the Fifth <note>original: "διὰ πέντε" / "dia pente".</note>, it is clear that the *mese* <note>original: "μέση"; the "middle" string, the central pitch of the Greek system.</note> will have the number eight, while the *paramese* <note>original: "παραμέση"; the string next to the middle.</note> will have

---

<insert>[For the concord which in musical matters we call the Octave, receives two means between its extreme terms, the proportion of which we shall show.]</insert>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXII—XXIII.</header>

the number nine. This being the case, the *hypata* will have the same ratio to the *mese* as the *paramese* has to the *neta diezeugmenon*; for from the *hypata meson* to the *mese* is a Fourth, and from the *paramese* to the *neta diezeugmenon* is also a Fourth. This same proportion <margin>5</margin> is found in the numbers: for just as six is to <margin>B</margin> eight, so is nine to twelve; and as six is to nine, so is eight to twelve. For eight is the "four-to-three" ratio of six, as is twelve of nine; and nine is the "three-to-two" ratio of six, as is twelve of eight. What has been said will suffice to demonstrate the diligence and expertise Plato possessed regarding mathematical matters.

### CH. XXIII.

As for the fact that harmony is something venerable, divine, and great, Aristotle—the student of Plato—says the following: "Harmony is celestial in its nature, possessing a divine, beautiful, and <margin>15</margin> marvelous character. Being four-fold in its power by nature, it possesses two means, the arithmetic and the harmonic; its parts, its magnitudes, and its excesses appear according to number and equal measurement; for melodies are regulated within two tetrachords <note>original: "τετραχόρδοις"; four-stringed units that were the building blocks of Greek scales.</note>." These are his literal words. Its body... <margin>C</margin>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and philosophical exploration of music, moving from general concepts of harmony to the specific Pythagorean ratios that define musical intervals. It explains how specific notes in the Greek scale—the neta, hypata, mese, and paramese—correspond to the numbers 12, 6, 8, and 9 to create the perfect mathematical order of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXIII.</header>

He said that the body of music consists of unequal parts that nevertheless harmonize <note>original: "συμφωνούντων"; sounding together in agreement.</note> with one another. Furthermore, its middle points agree according to arithmetic proportion. For the highest note <note>original: "νήατον" / "neta"; the note with the highest pitch, though often physically the "lowest" string on a lyre.</note>, tuned to a double ratio compared to the lowest note <note>original: "ὕπατον" / "hypata"; the note with the lowest pitch.</note>, <margin>5</margin> produces the octave harmony <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν" / "diapason"; literally "through all," the interval where the frequency doubles (ratio 2:1).</note>. As we said before, the highest note consists of twelve units, while the lowest note is six. The *paramese* <note>The note "next to the middle."</note>, harmonizing with the lowest note at a three-to-two ratio <note>original: "ἡμιόλιον" / "sesquialtera"; the ratio 3:2, which creates the interval of a perfect fifth.</note>, is nine units; and we said the middle note <note>original: "μέσης" / "mese"; the central note of the scale, the reference point for tuning.</note> was eight units. From these, the most important intervals of music <margin>D</margin> <margin>10</margin> are formed: the fourth <note>original: "διὰ τεσσάρων" / "diatessaron"; the interval produced by a 4:3 ratio.</note>, which is a four-to-three ratio <note>original: "ἐπίτριτον" / "sesquitertia".</note>; the fifth <note>original: "διὰ πέντε" / "diapente"; the interval produced by a 3:2 ratio.</note>, which is a three-to-two ratio; and the octave, which is a double ratio. Moreover, the nine-to-eight ratio <note>original: "ἐπόγδοον" / "sesquioctava".</note> is preserved, which is the ratio of a whole tone. In the same way, the parts and <margin>15</margin> means of the harmony exceed or are exceeded by each other according to numerical excess and geometric power. Aristotle points out these powers as follows: the highest note exceeds the middle note by a third of its own value, and the lowest note is similarly <margin>E</margin> <margin>20</margin> exceeded by the *paramese*...

---

<note>The following is the Latin translation of the Greek text above.</note>

Furthermore, he said it consists of unlike parts, but parts which harmonize among themselves; indeed, even their middle points agree in arithmetic proportion. For the lowest string <note>original: "ima chorda"; referring to the neta, the highest in pitch but lowest in physical position.</note> and the highest string <note>original: "summam"; referring to the hypata, the lowest in pitch.</note> combined in a double ratio create the Octave consonance. For as we said, <margin>5</margin> the *hypata* is compared to the number six, the *neta* to twelve. The *paramese* corresponds to the *hypata* in a three-to-two ratio, and we said the number nine fits it, while eight fits the middle note. It is certain that the principal intervals of music consist of these ratios: the Fourth in a four-to-three ratio, the Fifth in a three-to-two, and the Octave in a double ratio. Meanwhile, the <margin>10</margin> nine-to-eight ratio is also preserved, as in the ratio of a whole tone. Now it also happens that the parts and middle points of Harmony are exceeded or exceed one another according to Arithmetic excess and Geometric power. And Aristotle declares these properties thus: that the *neta* is greater than the *mese* by a third of itself, and by the same proportion the <margin>15</margin> *paramese* exceeds the *hypata*. Thus, a certain relationship of intervals or distinctions is created when the terms exceed the *mese* and *para-*...

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXIII—XXIV.</header>

...so that the differences become relative; for they exceed and are exceeded by the same proportions. By these same ratios, the outer notes exceed and are exceeded by the *mese* and *paramese*—by the four-to-three and three-to-two ratios. Such is harmonic <margin>5</margin> progression. But the difference between the highest note and the middle note shows an equal part according to arithmetic ratio. Likewise for the <margin>F</margin> *paramese* and the lowest note; for the *paramese* exceeds the middle note by the nine-to-eight ratio. Again, the highest note is double the lowest; the *paramese* is one-and-a-half times the lowest; and the <margin>10</margin> middle note is tuned to a four-to-three ratio with the lowest. According to Aristotle, harmony is naturally formed by these parts and quantities in this way.

### CH. XXIV.

<margin>15</margin> Music is composed most naturally of the even and the odd, and of the "even-odd" nature <note>original: "ἀρτιοπερίσσου" / "pariter-impar"; a number that is even but becomes odd when divided by two, such as 6 or 10.</note>—both the harmony itself and all its parts. The whole itself is "even," as it is composed of four terms; but its parts and ratios are even, <margin>p. 1140</margin> odd, and even-odd. For it has the highest note <margin>20</margin> as even with twelve units, the *paramese* as odd with nine units, the middle note as even with eight units, and the lowest note as even-odd, being six units. Being so naturally formed, it and its parts relate to each other...

---

<note>The following is the Latin translation of the Greek text above.</note>

...*mese*, and are exceeded by them, with the three-to-two and four-to-three ratios governing the matter in both cases. And this is what they call the Harmonic increase, that is, the Progression. But the *neta* from the *mese*, and the *paramese* from the *hypata*, are equally distant by arithmetic <margin>5</margin> differences of the intervals: for the *paramese* exceeds the *mese* by a nine-to-eight ratio. Again, the *neta* is double the *hypata*; the *paramese* is worth the *hypata* once plus its half; and the *mese* is a four-to-three ratio to the *hypata*. By these parts and numbers, therefore, harmony is composed according to the opinion of Aristotle.

### CH. XXIV.

The same is properly established according to nature <margin>10</margin> from the nature of the even, the odd, and the even-odd, both in the harmony itself and in all its parts. For the whole itself is even, since it consists of four terms; its parts and proportions are even, odd, and even-odd. For the *neta* is even, because it consists of the <margin>15</margin> number twelve; the *paramese* is odd, because it is nine; the *mese* is even, because it is eight; and the *hypata* is even-odd, because it is six. By these...

by their excesses <note>original: "ὑπεροχαῖς"; the mathematical difference between two terms in a ratio.</note> and their ratios, a harmony is forged where the whole is in accord with its parts and the parts with the whole.

## CHAPTER XXV.

<margin>B</margin> Furthermore, among the senses that are innate to our bodies, some are celestial and divine; these were provided to mankind by God through harmony—namely sight and <margin>5</margin> hearing. They reveal harmony through sound <note>original: "φωνῆς"; voice or sound.</note> and light. Even the other senses that follow them, insofar as they are senses, are constituted according to harmony. For they perform all their functions not without harmony, although they are lesser than <margin>10</margin> the first two, they are not detached from them. For sight and hearing, appearing in our bodies alongside the presence of God, possess a nature that is both powerful and beautiful, according to reason <note>original: "λογισμὸν"; referring here to the divine logic or proportion of the universe.</note>.

## CHAPTER XXVI.

<margin>C</margin> It is clear from these things that the ancient Greeks, with good reason, took the greatest care in educating the young <margin>15</margin> in music. For they believed they should shape and regulate the souls of the youth through music toward what is graceful and decorous. Music is clearly useful for every occasion and every serious endeavor, but primarily for the dangers of war. To meet these dangers, some used the <term>aulos<gloss>A double-reed wind instrument, often translated as 'flute' but closer to an oboe.</gloss></term>, <margin>20</margin> such as the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans.</note>, among whom the so-called *Castor’s Air* <note>original: "Καστόρειον μέλος"; a traditional Spartan marching tune named after the hero Castor.</note> was played on the flute whenever they advanced in order to fight the enemy. Others made their approach against their opponents to the sound of the lyre; it is recorded that the Cretans used this <margin>5</margin> method of going out to face the dangers of war for a long time. Others even in our own day continue to use trumpets. The Argives <note>People of Argos.</note> used the flute for the wrestling matches at the festival called the *Sthenia*. They say this contest <margin>D 10</margin> was originally established in honor of Danaus, but was later dedicated to Zeus Sthenius <note>Zeus "the Mighty."</note>. Indeed, even now it is the custom to play the flute for the <term>pentathlon<gloss>An athletic contest involving five events: long jump, javelin, disco, sprint, and wrestling.</gloss></term>, though the music played now is neither refined nor ancient, nor is it like the music used by those men of old, such as the tune composed by Hierax for this contest, which was called <margin>15</margin> *Endrome* <note>original: "Ἐνδρομή"; literally 'The Sprint' or 'Running-tune'.</note>. Nevertheless, even if the music played now is weak and unrefined, the flute is still played.

## CHAPTER XXVII.

However, they say that among the even more ancient Greeks, <margin>20</margin> they did not even know the "theatrical" Muse <note>Music intended for spectacle or performance in a theater.</note>. Instead, they employed the entire science of music for the honor of the gods and the education of the youth, as there was not yet any theater <margin>E</margin> at all among...

### CAP. XXVIII.

Someone might say: "My good man, was nothing then further discovered or innovated by the ancients?" I myself say that discoveries were made, but with dignity and decorum. For those who <margin>15</margin> recorded such things attribute to Terpander the addition of the *nete* <gloss>the highest string or note of a scale</gloss> of the Doric mode, which those before him had not used in melody. And the whole Mixolydian tone is said to have been further discovered, as well as the style of the "upright" melody <note>original: "ὀρθίου μελῳδίας"; a high-pitched, stirring style of song.</note> consisting of upright rhythms, and in addition to the upright, also the "marked" trochee. <margin>20</margin> Furthermore, as Pindar says, Terpander was also the inventor of *scolia* <gloss>drinking songs sung by guests at banquets</gloss>. Moreover, Archilochus further discovered the rhythmic composition of trimeters and

...built for those men, music was still found in temples, serving the veneration of the gods and the praises of good men. Moreover, it is probable that the word "theater" came later, and the word "to view" <note>original: "θεωρεῖν"</note> much earlier was derived from the word "God" <note>original: "θεός"</note>. But in our <margin>5</margin> times, so much dissolution has been accepted that no memory of that music which serves the education of boys now remains, and there is no care for it; instead, all who apply their minds to music embrace the Muse who serves the theaters.

CAP. XXVIII. But someone might say: "Hey you, was nothing <margin>10</margin> invented by the ancients, nothing innovated?" Truly I admit certain things were invented, but with majesty and decorum. For those who have written the history of these matters attribute the *nete* of the Doric mode to Terpander, since those before him did not use it. Indeed, they say the whole Mixolydian tone was added, and the mode of the "Upright" song <margin>15</margin> which consists of upright rhythms, and besides the upright, also the "marked" trochee. Furthermore, as Pindar says, Terpander also found the *Scolia* songs. But also Archilochus thought out the rhythms of trimeters and...

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXVIII—XXIX.</header>

<margin>p. 1141</margin> the stretching [tuning] into non-homogenous rhythms, and the *parakataloge* <note>original: "παρακαταλογή"; a style of melodic speech or recitation accompanied by an instrument, similar to recitative in opera.</note> and the striking [of strings] related to these. To him first are attributed the epodes, the tetrameters, the cretic, and the prosodiac rhythms, and the expansion of the heroic meter; <margin>5</margin> by some even the elegiac meter. Besides these, he is credited with the tension of the iambic toward the *paeon epibatos*, and the expanded heroic into both the prosodiac and the cretic. Furthermore, they say Archilochus demonstrated that among iambics, some are spoken to the accompaniment of the striking [of strings] and others are <margin>10</margin> sung—a practice later used by the tragic poets, <margin>B</margin> and which Crexus took and adapted for the use of dithyrambs <gloss>wild, choral hymns to Dionysus</gloss>. They also think he first invented the striking [of strings] beneath the song, whereas all the ancients struck the strings in unison with the notes of the voice <note>original: "πρόσχορδα" (proschorda); playing the exact same notes as the singer without accompaniment or harmony.</note>.

### CAP. XXIX.

And that famous Olympus, to whom they attribute the beginning of Greek and "nomic" <note>original: "νομικῆς"; music following the *nomoi*, or traditional melodic laws.</note> music, they say discovered the "Enharmonic" genus, and among rhythms, the prosodiac, in which the [song] of Mars...

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXIX—XXX.</header>

<margin>C</margin> ...is the "Law of Ares" <note>original: "Ἄρεως νόμος" (Areos nomos); a "nomos" was a traditional, standardized melody or "musical law."</note>, as well as the *Choreios* rhythm, which he used extensively in songs for the Mother of the Gods <note>original: "Μητρῴοις" (Metriois); hymns dedicated to Cybele.</note>. Some also believe that Olympus discovered the *Bacchic* foot. Every one of the ancient melodies demonstrates that these things are so. However, Lasus of Hermione <note>A 6th-century BC poet and musician, often credited with being the teacher of Pindar and an early theorist.</note>, by shifting the rhythms into the style of the dithyramb <gloss>wild, passionate choral hymns to Dionysus</gloss> and following the "many-voicedness" <note>original: "πολυφωνίᾳ" (polyphonia); here referring to the complex, overlapping sounds of the pipes.</note> of the flutes, used more notes and scattered them about, leading music into its first great transformation. For while the lyre had possessed seven strings [up until the time of Terpander of Antissa], Lasus scattered the sounds into a greater number of notes.

### Cap. XXX.

Similarly, Melanippides the song-writer, coming after him, did not remain with the pre-existing music, nor did Philoxenus, nor Timotheus. Indeed, flute-playing also transitioned from a simpler form of music into one more "variegated" <note>original: "ποικιλωτέραν" (poikiloteran); implying complex, ornate, or even fussy ornamentation.</note>. For in ancient times [up until the time of Melanippides the 
<margin>D</margin> dithyrambic poet], it was the custom for the flute-players to receive their pay from the poets—since the poetry clearly held the leading role while the flute-players served the teachers <gloss>the poets who "taught" the chorus</gloss>. But later, even this custom was corrupted, to such an extent that Pherecrates the comic poet introduced Music on stage in the form of a woman,

her entire body having been brutally maltreated. He depicts Justice questioning her about the cause of this outrage, and Music replying:

> who took me and was the first to let me go

> <margin>10</margin> compared to the evils I suffer now...

> holding twelve harmonies on only nine strings.

### CAP. XXXI.

Aristoxenus <note>A famous Greek music theorist and student of Aristotle.</note> has made it clear that through training and education,
either a correction or a distortion of the art occurs. For...

...any person might judge his right to be his left.
Even he, however, was tolerable to me.

JUSTICE: Which Timotheus? MUSIC: Someone from Miletus, a redhead. <margin>5</margin>

**CAP. XXXI.** Aristoxenus shows that education and discipline can produce
either a correct or a depraved handling of the arts.

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXXI—XXXII.</header>

...among those of his own age, he says it happened to Telesias of Thebes
that as a youth he was raised in the most beautiful music and
learned, among other things by famous men, the works of
Pindar, and those of Dionysius the Theban, and of Lamprus, and
of Pratinas, and of the rest—all those lyric poets who <margin>5</margin>
were excellent composers of instrumental music <note>original: "κρουμάτων" (kroumaton); specifically referring to music played on stringed instruments.</note>. He also played the flute

he was so thoroughly deceived by the theatrical and
variegated music <note>original: "ποικίλης μουσικῆς" (poikiles mousikes); "variegated" implies complex, ornate, or overly decorative musical styles.</note> that he looked down upon those beautiful things in

and among these, specifically those that were the most "variegated" and
possessed the greatest amount of innovation. Yet when he set out to compose songs and
attempted both styles—the Pindaric and the Philoxenian—
he was unable to achieve success in the Philoxenian
style. The cause of this was his excellent <margin>15</margin>
upbringing from childhood.

### CAP. XXXII.

If, therefore, anyone wishes to use music in a beautiful and discerning
way, let him imitate the ancient manner. Moreover,
let him supplement it with other studies and let him <margin>D</margin> use
philosophy...

<column-break/>

...For he relates that Telesias the Theban, who lived in his own time,
was educated as a youth in the most beautiful music, and had learned the
poems of other noble poets, such as Pindar, Dionysius the
Theban, Lamprus, Pratinas, and the rest who excelled in lyric poetry and
musical modes. He was also skilled at playing the flute, and had been <margin>5</margin>
engaged with enough industrious success in the remaining parts of all
disciplines. But when he reached a mature age, he was so deceived by the theatrical
and "varied" music of the stage that, having come to despise those beautiful things in
which he had been nurtured, he thoroughly learned the songs of Timotheus and Philoxenus—
and of these, specifically those that were the most varied and <margin>10</margin>
contained the most novelty. But when he applied himself to composing a lyric
poem, and attempted both the style of Philoxenus and that of Pindar,
he did not succeed in the Philoxenian manner; the cause of which was
that most beautiful training of his early years.

**CAP. XXXII.** Therefore, he who wishes to use music rightly and with judgment <margin>15</margin>
should emulate the ancient style. But furthermore, let him also
perfect it with other disciplines, and place philosophy over it as a pedagogue <gloss>a guide or teacher</gloss>.

<summary>This page critiques the radical "modern" innovations of Timotheus and Philoxenus through satirical comedy and the biography of Telesias, arguing that complex, theatrical music is a corruption of the "ancient way." It concludes that a proper musical education must be grounded in traditional standards and guided by philosophy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical and moral discussion of music. Having previously discussed the "corrupting" innovations of the new musicians, the author now pivots to the importance of philosophy as a guide for musical education and the limits of technical music theory (Harmonics) in understanding the emotional and moral character (ethos) of a composition.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>38 PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXXII—XXXIII.</header>

[Philoso]phy <note>The Greek text begins mid-word with -φίαν (-phian), clearly the end of "philosophian."</note> should be established as the guide; for she is capable of judging the proper measure and the usefulness of music. For since there are three genera <term>genus|In Greek music theory, a "genus" refers to the specific tuning of the four-note scale called a tetrachord—the basic building block of their musical system</term> into which all music is divided by general classification—the Diatonic, the Chromatic, and the Enharmonic—the person approaching music must be knowledgeable in the poetry that uses these and must be capable of the interpretation that delivers the composed works.

<margin>5</margin> First, then, it must be understood that all musical learning is a habituation <note>original: "ἐθισμός" (ethismos). This suggests that early training is about developing an instinctive "feel" for music before the student understands the underlying reasons.</note> which does not yet include the reason why each of the things taught must be learned by the student. After this, it should be considered that the enumeration of modes <term>mode|Also called "tonoi" or "tropoi," these were the different scales or keys, such as Dorian or Phrygian, each associated with a different emotional character</term> is not immediately introduced into such an education and training. Instead, most people learn randomly, whatever pleases the teacher or the student. 

<margin>10</margin> However, the wise rejected what was random, just as the Lacedaemonians <gloss>Spartans</gloss> did in ancient times, as well as the Mantineans and Pelleneans. For they chose a single mode, or at most a very few, which they thought were fitting for the correction of character, and thus they practiced true music.

### CHAPTER XXXIII.

This would become clear if one were to examine each of the sciences to see what it investigates. For it is evident that Harmonic science <term>Harmonics|The technical, mathematical study of musical intervals and scales, distinct from the art of composing or performing melodies</term> is the knowledge of the genera of the tuned scale, of intervals, 

---

...[philoso]phy as the guide: for she is able to judge what is fitting for music and what is beneficial for it. For since all music is divided into three genera: the Diatonic, the Chromatic, and the Enharmonic; he who applies himself to music must be skilled in the poetry that uses these things, and he must be a master of the art <margin>5</margin> of expressing the melodies underlying the poems correctly and appropriately. 

Therefore, in the beginning, it must be held that all musical discipline is a process of habituation, which does not yet show the learner the purpose for which each precept is learned. Next, it must be considered that the enumeration of modes is not immediately added to this kind of instruction. Instead, most people teach or learn randomly <margin>10</margin> whatever pleases the pupil or the teacher: but the prudent reject useless things, as did the Lacedaemonians, Mantineans, and Pelleneans of old. For these people, choosing some single mode, or at least very few, which they judged suitable for forming character, made use of true music.

### CHAPTER XXXIII.

This will become manifest if anyone, examining the individual <margin>15</margin> sciences, looks into what subject each one treats. For it is clear that Harmonics treats the substance of har-

<meta>catchword: har-</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XXXIII. 39</header>

systems, notes, pitches, and systematic modulations. But it is not possible for it to proceed any further than this. Consequently, one should not seek from it the ability to judge whether the poet has taken the Hypodorian mode appropriately—one might say, musically—<margin>5</margin> for the beginning, or the Mixolydian and Dorian for the conclusion, or the Hypophrygian and Phrygian for the middle. 

For the study of Harmonics does not extend to such matters, but requires many other things besides; for it is ignorant of the power of propriety <note>original: "οἰκειότητος" (oikeiotetos). This is the "fitness" or "appropriateness" of a musical choice to the subject matter or mood.</note>. <margin>p. 1143</margin> Neither the Chromatic genus nor the Enharmonic will ever <margin>10</margin> possess the power of propriety perfectly, through which the character of the composed melody is revealed; rather, this is the work of the artist. 

It is clear, then, that the sound of the scale is one thing, and the melodic composition constructed within the scale is another, which the study of Harmonics cannot investigate. <margin>15</margin> The same logic applies to rhythms. For no rhythm will possess the power of perfect propriety within itself. <margin>B</margin> For when we say something is said "appropriately," we say it with a view toward some moral character; and we say the cause of this is a certain composition, or a mixture, or both. For instance, Olympus <note>A legendary flute player and composer credited with inventing the Enharmonic genus.</note> used the Enharmonic genus <margin>20</margin> set in the Phrygian mode, mixed with the *paeon epibatos* rhythm: this 

<meta>catchword: this</meta>

---

monic genera, intervals, systems, sounds, pitches, and systematic changes: it cannot proceed further. Therefore, it does not undertake questions of this kind: whether the poet has taken the Hypodorian appropriately and musically, so to speak, at the beginning <margin>5</margin> of the poem, the Mixolydian and Dorian at the end, and the Hypophrygian and Phrygian in the middle. 

For Harmonics does not reach that far, but has need of many other things besides, for it is ignorant of the force of appropriateness. Indeed, neither the Chromatic nor the Enharmonic genus will ever advance to that perfect force of appropriateness <margin>10</margin> by which the character of a poem is judged, for this is the duty of the artist. 

It is manifest, therefore, that the sound of the system is different from the modulation of the melody in the system, which it is not the business of the harmonic treatise to consider. The same is the case with rhythms. <margin>15</margin> For no rhythm will have the perfect force of appropriateness in itself. For when we affirm that something is said "appropriately" or "suitably," we are looking at its moral character: and we say that the cause of this is a certain composition, or mixture, or both. As when Olympus, having placed the Enharmonic genus in the Phrygian mode, with

<meta>This page continues the technical and moral discussion of music. ...for the character of the beginning gave rise to the Athena *nomos* <term>nomos|A traditional, highly structured melody or "law" of composition associated with a particular deity; the "Athena Nomos" was a specific ancient style for the flute</term>. When melody and rhythm-making were technically added, and the rhythm itself was skillfully transformed—with the trochaic <term>trochaic|A rhythmic foot consisting of a long syllable followed by a short one</term> replacing the paeon <term>paeon|A rhythmic foot usually consisting of one long and three short syllables, often associated with hymns to Apollo or Athena</term>—the enharmonic genus <note>original: "ἐναρμόνιον γένος" (enarmonion genos). The most complex of the three Greek musical scales, featuring intervals as small as quarter-tones.</note> of Olympus was established. 

Yet even while the enharmonic genus and the Phrygian mode remained, and indeed the whole system stayed the same, the **ethos** <term>ethos|The moral and emotional "flavor" or character that a piece of music conveys to the listener; the Greeks believed music could directly shape the soul</term> underwent a great change. 
<margin>C</margin> For the so-called "harmony" in the Athena *nomos* differs greatly in its character from the introductory version <note>original: "ἀναπείρας" (anapeiras), referring to the opening trial or warm-up section of a flute performance.</note>. 

Therefore, if the faculty of judgment were added to musical experience, it is clear that such a person would be a master of the art. For someone who knows the Dorian mode <note>original: "Δωριστὶ" (Dōristi). The Dorian mode was considered by the ancient Greeks to be the most noble, stable, and educational scale, associated with Spartan discipline.</note> without having the judgment to understand how to use it appropriately will not know what they are doing, nor will they preserve its character. It is even debated regarding the Dorian composers themselves whether the technical study of harmonics <term>harmonics|The mathematical and structural study of musical intervals and scales</term> is capable of identifying these Dorian qualities, as some believe, or not. 

<margin>D</margin> The same logic applies to the whole of rhythmic science. For one who knows the paeon will not know how to apply it appropriately simply by knowing the structure of the paeon itself. For there is doubt even about the paeonic rhythm-composers, whether the technical study of rhythm can distinguish these things,

<column-break/>

as some say, or if its reach does not extend that far. Therefore, it is necessary for someone who intends to distinguish between what is appropriate and what is foreign to possess at least two types of knowledge: first, of the **ethos** (character) for which the composition was made, and second, of the technical elements from which the composition is built. 

What has been said should suffice to show that neither harmonics, nor rhythmics, nor any other of the specific musical sciences is self-sufficient on its own to understand character or to judge the other aspects of music.
<margin>E</margin> 

### CAP. XXXIV.

[Although there are three genera <term>genus|A "genus" refers to the specific tuning of the four-note scale (tetrachord). The three types were Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic</term> into which musical tuning is divided—equal in terms of the range of their scales, the acoustic power of their notes, and their tetrachords—the ancients focused their efforts on only one. For those before our time did not investigate the Chromatic or the Diatonic, but only the Enharmonic, and specifically the system of one range called the "octave" <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν" (dia pasōn), literally "through all [notes]," the standard Greek term for the octave interval.</note>. 

While they disagreed about the specific shading <term>shading|Also called "chroai," these were slight variations in the tuning of the inner notes of a scale to create different "colors" of sound</term>, almost everyone agreed that the "harmony" was essentially one]. 
<margin>F</margin> Therefore, no one who stops at this technical knowledge alone would ever truly grasp the matters of harmonic science...

<summary>This page argues that technical music theory (Harmonics and Rhythmics) is insufficient for a true musician; one must also possess the "faculty of judgment" to understand the moral character (ethos) and appropriate use of different styles. It notes that older musicians focused almost exclusively on the Enharmonic genus and the octave, neglecting other scales.</summary>
<keywords>Athena nomos, Olympus, Ethos, Dorian mode, Phrygian mode, Enharmonic genus, Paeon, Trochee, Harmonics, Rhythmics, Octave, Musical Judgment</keywords>
