<meta>This page continues the discussion on the requirements for a true judge of music. The previous page established that technical experience must be paired with the faculty of judgment. Here, Plutarch explains that a music critic must look beyond individual notes and rhythms to understand the "whole body" of the art, balancing sensory perception with intellectual reasoning.</meta>

...having progressed, but clearly he must follow both the individual sciences and the whole body of music, as well as the mixtures and compositions of the parts. For the man who is only a harmonicist <term>harmonicist|A specialist in "harmonics" (harmonikos), the technical study of intervals and scales, rather than the broader art and ethics of music</term> is limited in a certain way. 

To speak generally, sensation <note>original: "αἴσθησιν" (aisthesis); the physical act of hearing</note> and intellect <note>original: "διάνοιαν" (dianoian); the mental capacity for understanding and reasoning</note> must run together in the judgment of the parts of music. The senses should neither run ahead—which is a fault of the "rash" or overly impulsive senses—nor should they lag behind—which is a fault of the "sluggish" or slow-moving senses. 

<margin>p. 1144</margin> Sometimes, in certain sensory perceptions, both faults occur at once: the same senses might both lag behind and run ahead due to some natural inconsistency. Therefore, these defects must be stripped away from any sensation that is intended to "run alongside" the intellect.

---

<note>The following Latin section is a contemporary scholarly translation of the Greek text above, provided in the original manuscript for reference. It reinforces the need for a balance between the "sensum" (sense) and "intelligentiam" (intelligence).</note>

...one who has progressed even to the knowledge of that single thing. But it will be necessary, as is clear, for him to pursue also the particular sciences (as they call them), the whole body of music, and the mixtures and compositions of the parts. For he who is only a Harmonicist is restricted in a certain way. To speak universally, it is necessary for sense and intelligence to concur in judging the parts of music, so that they neither go before—which happens to headlong senses—nor are left behind—which happens to the slow. It happens in certain senses that both occur, so that due to an inequality of nature they both run ahead and come more slowly than is right. This must therefore be taken away from the sense, so that it can accompany the intellect with an equal pace.

---

### CHAPTER XXXV.

It is always necessary that at least three things fall upon the hearing at once: the tone <note>original: "φθόγγον" (phthongon)</note>, the time <note>original: "χρόνον" (chronon)</note>, and the syllable or letter. 

It will follow then that from the progression of the tone, the harmony is recognized; from the progression of the time, the rhythm <term>rhythm|Greek: rhythmos; the "shape" or "flow" of time in music</term> is known; and from the letter or syllable, the spoken word <note>original: "τὸ λεγόμενον" (to legomenon)</note> is understood. As these things proceed together, the senses must necessarily receive them all at the same time. 

<margin>B</margin> Furthermore, this much is evident: if the sense is unable to separate <note>original: "χωρίζειν" (chorizein)</note> each of the things mentioned, it will be impossible to follow the details or to discern what is mistaken or correct in each of them. 

First, then, one must understand continuity. For the power of judgment must possess the concept of continuity. The "well-performed" or its opposite does not occur in isolated, separate tones or times or letters, but in continuous sequences; for there is a certain "mixing" that occurs in the practical use of these individual parts. 

<margin>C</margin> So much for the matter of following the music.

---

### CHAPTER XXXVI.

The next point to consider is that those who are simply experts in music <note>original: "μουσικῆς ἐπιστήμονες" (mousikes epistemones)</note> are not self-sufficient when it comes to the business of judgment. For it is impossible for a person to become a perfect musician and critic <term>critic|Greek: kritikon; one capable of discerning and judging the quality and moral character of a work</term> solely from those parts which are usually thought to comprise the whole of music. 

I refer to things such as:
* Experience with instruments
* Mastery of song
* Training the ear <note>original: "αἴσθησιν συγγυμνασίας" (aisthesis syngymnasias); literally "joint-exercise of the senses"</note> to perceive harmony and rhythm
* Theoretical study of rhythm and scales <note>original: "ἁρμονικῆς πραγματείας" (harmonikes pragmateias)</note>
* The study of string-striking <note>original: "ψαλμοῦ" (psalmou)</note> or poetic delivery.

<margin>D</margin> Even if one adds all other such technical skills, they are not enough for judgment...

---

<note>The Latin text below continues the parallel translation of the Greek Chapter 35 and 36 above.</note>

**CHAPTER XXXV.** For it is always necessary that three things at minimum fall upon the hearing at once: sound, time, and letter or syllable. It will happen that from the sound and its entry you understand the harmony; from the time, the rhythm; and from the letter or syllable, that which is being said. Since these proceed together, the sense must also receive them together. At the same time, this becomes evident: where the sense cannot separate each of those things mentioned from the rest, it can neither weigh each thing separately nor discern in each one the faulty from the correct. Truly, in the first place, one must learn about continuity. For continuity must fall under the critical faculty. Whether a song is well-composed or otherwise is not discerned in sounds, times, or letters that are disconnected from one another, but in those that are continuous, since a certain mixing of the parts—which are not properly composed on their own—takes place in use. And so much for the continuity and consequence of the parts.

**CHAPTER XXXVI.** Following these things, it must be considered that those skilled in music are not sufficiently instructed by themselves for the science of judging. For it is not possible for a musician and critic to be absolute in all measures if he has only grasped the knowledge of those parts which are judged to be the whole of music: I mean the skill in instruments, and songs, and the perception of joint exercise, insofar as it pertains to the perception of harmony and rhythm; then the rhythmic and harmonic treatment, and the consideration of the striking of strings and elocution, and whatever other things there are...

<vocab>Plutarch, Harmonics, Rhythm, Sensation, Intellect, Criticism, Continuity</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the necessity for a music critic to balance sensory perception (hearing the notes) with intellectual reasoning to understand the "whole body" of a composition. It identifies tone, time, and lyrics as the three simultaneous elements of music and argues that technical skill alone is insufficient for true musical judgment.</summary>

<keywords>Plutarch, music theory, harmonics, rhythm, aesthetics, perception, continuity, musical judgment, harmony, technical skill</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical criticism. Having established that a critic needs both sensory perception and intellectual reasoning, Plutarch now distinguishes between the technical components of a performance and its ultimate artistic and ethical purpose. He transitions into a historical critique, contrasting the disciplined, "majestic" music of the ancients with the perceived decadence of modern styles.</meta>

# PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CHAPTER XXXVI.

...the study of both instrumentation <note>original: "κροῦσίν" (krousin); literally the striking of strings or percussion</note> and diction, and whatever other parts may remain. We must attempt to understand the reasons why it is not possible to become a critic based on these technical elements alone. First, this is because we assume that some things under judgment are complete <note>original: "τέλεια" (teleia); finished or perfected works</note>, while others are incomplete. The "complete" things are each of the poems themselves—such as a piece sung or played on the lyre—or the interpretation <term>interpretation|the Greek 'hermeneia' refers to the actual performance or delivery of the work</term> of each of them, such as flute-playing, singing, and the like. The "incomplete" things are those that contribute toward these and exist for their sake—such as the various technical parts of the interpretation.

<margin>10 E</margin> Second, this is clear from the composition itself: for it follows the same rule. For instance, someone listening to a singer accompanied by a flute <term>singer accompanied by a flute|an 'auloedos,' a performer who sings while another plays the aulos (a double-reed pipe)</term> might judge whether the flutes are in harmony or not, and whether the pronunciation is clear or the opposite. Each of these is a part of the flute-sung interpretation, but it is not the goal <note>original: "τέλος" (telos); the end or purpose</note>; rather, it exists for the sake of the goal. Beyond these things, the character <note>original: "ἦθος" (ethos); the moral or emotional quality</note> of the interpretation will be judged based on all such factors: whether it provides a style appropriate to the poem being performed, which the performer intends to handle and interpret. The same reasoning applies to the emotions <note>original: "παθῶν" (pathon); the passions or feelings</note> signified by the poetic art within the poems.

---

The following pertains to this: we shall endeavor to explain why all these technical skills alone cannot produce a perfect critic. The first cause lies in the fact that among those things subjected to our judgment, some are perfect, others imperfect. The "perfect" include every poem that is sung by voice, flute, or lyre, as well as the expression of each of these, such as the singing of the flute and the voice and others of that kind. The "imperfect" are those which lead to this and are done for its sake, such as the parts of that expression or interpretation. 

The second cause is found in the poetry itself, for it is held in the same way. Indeed, someone listening to a person singing to the flute might pass judgment on whether the flutes are in harmony or dissonance, and whether the dialect is clear or not. Any of these is a part, so to speak, of the flute-singing expression—not the goal itself, but undertaken for the sake of the goal. For according to these and all other such things, judgment ought to be made concerning the nature of the expression or modulation: whether it is suited to the poem to which the music is set, which the artist has decided to treat and express appropriately. The same logic applies to the stirrings of the soul <note>original: "animi commotionum"; the Latin equivalent of 'pathe' or emotions</note> which poetry imitates in its works.

<column-break/>

<page-num>45</page-num>
# PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CHAPTERS XXXVII—XXXVIII.

### CHAPTER XXXVII.

<margin>F</margin> Since the ancients placed the greatest care upon moral character <note>original: "ἠθῶν" (ethon)</note>, they preferred the majesty and simplicity <note>original: "ἀπερίεργον" (aperiergon); literally "not over-wrought" or "un-fussy"</note> of ancient music. For they say that the citizens of Argos once even imposed a penalty for breaking the laws of music, fining the man who first attempted to use more than the seven strings they recognized and who tried to "mix-Lydianize" <note>original: "παραμιξολυδιάζειν"; to play in or imitate the Mixolydian mode, which was seen as overly emotional or mournful</note> among them. 

And Pythagoras the revered rejected the judgment of music through the senses; for he claimed that the excellence of music was grasped by the mind <note>original: "νῷ" (no)</note>. Therefore, he did not judge music by the sense of hearing, but by harmonic proportion. He considered it sufficient to stop the knowledge of music at the octave <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν" (dia pason); the interval of an octave</note> ****

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

<margin>15</margin> But people today have entirely rejected the most beautiful of the genera <term>genera|the three types of Greek scales: Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic</term>, which the ancients especially cultivated because of its majesty. As a result, most people do not possess even a casual perception of enharmonic intervals <term>enharmonic intervals|The most sophisticated ancient scale, using quarter-tones (dieses). It was considered the most difficult to hear and play</term>. They are so lazy and indifferent that they do not even believe that a quarter-tone <note>original: "ἔμφασιν... τῶν ὑπὸ τὴν αἴσθησιν πιπτόντων"; literally 'an appearance among things falling under the senses'</note> provides any sensory impression at all...

---

### CHAPTER XXXVII.
Truly, the ancients, who looked primarily to a character-molded nature, honored first the majesty and simplicity of early music. Even the Argives are said to have defined a punishment for those who violated music, demanding it from him who first dared to use more strings than the seven they had received and to play in the Mixolydian tone. But also Pythagoras, that most grave philosopher, disapproved of the judgment of music proceeding from the senses; he wished it to be examined only by the sharp edge of the mind and by harmonic proportion, not by hearing. He likewise judged it sufficient to establish the knowledge of music within the Octave ***

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.
But the men of our age have so completely repudiated that most beautiful genus, which the ancients studied most for its majesty, that many have not the slightest regard for enharmonic intervals. Indeed, they have proceeded to such a point of laziness that some think an enharmonic quarter-tone <note>original: "diesin enharmonicam"</note> provides no evidence of itself to the sense, and they [reject] it...

<summary>Plutarch discusses the distinction between the technical parts of music and its moral "ethos," highlighting how the ancients used music to shape character. He praises Pythagoras for using reason over the senses and laments that modern listeners have lost the ability to appreciate the complex "enharmonic" genus of music.</summary>
<keywords>Plutarch, music criticism, Pythagoras, Argive laws, musical ethics, enharmonic genus, quarter-tones, Mixolydian mode, musical decline</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical defense of ancient musical structures. Previously, the text established that a music critic needs both sensory perception and intellectual understanding. Here, Plutarch (or the author) defends the "enharmonic" genus and the use of microtonal intervals against contemporary critics who claim that if an interval cannot be easily heard or measured, it should be discarded.</meta>

# PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CHAPTER XXXVIII.

...the enharmonic quarter-tone <term>diesis|The smallest interval in ancient Greek music, roughly a quarter of a whole tone, used in the sophisticated "enharmonic" scale</term>, and they seek to banish it from melodies, claiming that those who held any opinion on this matter or made use of this genus were merely talking nonsense. They believe they bring the strongest proof for their claim primarily from <margin>B</margin> their own lack of perception; they assume that anything that escapes their hearing is entirely non-existent and useless. Furthermore, they argue that its size cannot be grasped through musical consonance <note>original: "συμφωνίας" (symphonias); refers to the mathematical or acoustic agreement of sounds</note> in the same way that the semitone, the whole tone, and other such intervals can be. But they are unaware that, by this same logic, the third, the fifth, and the seventh magnitudes would also be cast out—those which consist of three, five, and seven quarter-tones. In general, all intervals that appear as "odd" numbers would be rejected as useless, insofar as none of them can be grasped through consonance. These would be any intervals measured by an odd number of the smallest quarter-tones. From this, it necessarily follows that none of the divisions of the tetrachord <term>tetrachord|A four-note scale segment, the building block of Greek musical systems</term> would be useful, except for the one which happens to use only "even" intervals—namely, the "intense diatonic" <note>original: "συντόνου διατόνου" (syntonou diatonou)</note> and the "tonal chromatic" <note>original: "τονιαίου χρώματος" (toniaiou chromatos)</note> scales.

---

...they should banish it from songs, and they say that those who perceived anything in it or approved of that genus of music were triflers. They hold this to be the strongest support for their opinion: the dullness of their own senses. They imagine that unless they themselves perceive something, it is already nothing and has no utility. They follow this second argument: that its quantity cannot be perceived through the work of harmony, as can the semitone, the tone, and other such intervals. But those men are ignorant that by the same reasoning, the third, fifth, and seventh intervals must also be driven out, which contain three, five, and seven quarter-tones. Finally, it would be necessary for all intervals in general that are marked by an odd number to be rejected as useless; for none of them can be perceived with the aid of harmony—all those, I say, which the smallest quarter-tone measures in an odd number. From this it will follow that no division of the tetrachords is useful, except that one which defines all intervals by an even number, such as the intense diatonic and the tonal chromatic.

<page-num>47</page-num>
# PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CHAPTERS XXXIX—XL.

### CHAPTER XXXIX.

To say and assume such things is the mark of men who not only oppose the clear facts but are even at war with themselves. For they themselves are seen to use those divisions of the <margin>D</margin> tetrachords most of all in which many of the intervals are either "odd" or "irrational" <term>irrational|In music theory, an 'alogon' or irrational interval is one that cannot be expressed as a simple mathematical ratio of whole numbers</term>. Indeed, they are always "softening" <note>original: "μαλάττουσι" (malattousi); a technical term for slightly lowering the pitch of a note to make it more expressive or "colored"</note> the index-finger notes <note>original: "λιχανοὺς" (lichanous)</note> and the next-to-last notes <note>original: "παρανήτας" (paranetas)</note>. And now they even relax <note>original: "παριανιᾶσι" (parianiasi)</note> some of the fixed notes <note>original: "ἑστώτων φθόγγων" (hestōtōn phthongōn); notes that traditionally define the framework of the scale and should not change pitch</note>, lowering the thirds and the next-to-last notes toward them by some irrational interval. They somehow think that this use of musical systems is most highly esteemed—the one in which most of the intervals are irrational—and they do this not only by relaxing the notes that are naturally movable, but even some of the immovable ones, as is clear to those capable of perceiving such things.

### CHAPTER XL.

The noble Homer taught the use of music that is appropriate for a man. <margin>E</margin> For by showing that music is useful in many circumstances, he depicted Achilles "digesting" <note>original: "πέττοντα" (pettonta); literally 'cooking' or 'ripening,' used metaphorically for processing or soothing his bitter anger</note> his anger against Agamemnon through music, which he had learned from the most wise Chiron <note>The centaur Chiron was the legendary tutor of Achilles, known for teaching medicine, hunting, and music</note>.

---

CHAPTER XXXIX. And yet, this kind of opinion belongs to those men who not only contradict those things which strike the senses, but also fight against their own selves. Since it is well known that they themselves chiefly use those divisions of the tetrachords which are contained by either odd or irrational intervals. For they frequently "soften" the index-finger notes and the next-to-last notes, and even by relaxing some of the standing sounds by a certain irrational interval, they simultaneously relax the thirds and the next-to-last notes. And they think this use of the systems is especially approved, in which most intervals are irrational, and the relaxation is made not only of the movable sounds, but even of some of the immovable ones—a fact which does not escape those who are trained in the perception of such matters.

CHAPTER XL. Truly, Homer taught the use of music suitable for a man. For showing that music is useful in many things, he introduces Achilles, who might digest his anger toward Agamemnon by the work of music, which he had learned from the most wise Chiron.

<summary>This page defends the use of microtonal and "irrational" intervals in Greek music, arguing that critics who reject them based on poor hearing are self-contradictory. It concludes by citing Homer's Achilles as an example of how music serves a moral and psychological purpose, specifically in tempering anger.</summary>

<keywords>enharmonic diesis, music theory, tetrachord, irrational intervals, lichanos, paranete, Achilles, Chiron, Homer, musical ethics, ancient Greek music</keywords>

<header>48 PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CHAPTER XL.</header>
<page-num>48</page-num>

> “And they found him,” he says, “delighting his heart with a clear-toned lyre,
> beautiful and ornate, and upon it was a silver bridge;
> which he had taken from the spoils when he destroyed the city of Eetion.
> With this he was delighting his soul, and he sang the glorious deeds of heroes.”
> <note>original: "κλέα ἀνδρῶν" (klea andron) — literally "the glories of men," a standard term for the heroic epics sung by bards.</note>

<margin>5</margin> Learn, says Homer, how music should be used: for it was fitting for Achilles—the son of Peleus, <margin>F</margin> the most just of men—to sing the glorious deeds and actions of the demigods. Furthermore, Homer teaches the proper time for its use; he discovered a useful and pleasant exercise for someone at rest. For Achilles, being a man of war and <margin>10</margin> action, was not participating in the dangers of the war because of the wrath he felt toward Agamemnon. Therefore, Homer thought it was appropriate for the hero to sharpen his soul <note>original: "παραθήγειν" (parathēgein); suggests whetting a blade, implying music keeps the warrior's spirit sharp even in idleness.</note> with the most beautiful melodies, so that he might be prepared for the departure into battle that would happen a short time later. <margin>15</margin> Clearly, he did this by remembering the deeds of old.
<margin>p. 1146</margin> Such was the nature of ancient music, and it was useful for this purpose. For we hear that both Hercules and Achilles <note>In Greek mythology, Achilles is the archetypal warrior, and Hercules the archetypal strongman; both are here presented as being refined by musical education.</note> made use of music, as did many others whose teacher is handed down as the most wise Chiron, <margin>20</margin> who was a teacher of music alongside justice and medicine.

---

> They found him delighting his mind with a lyre
> skillfully made, its bridge was of solid silver;
> which he had taken from the spoils of the city of Eetion.
> Soothing his soul with this, he sang the famous
<margin>5</margin>> and noble deeds of men.

Learn, says Homer, how music is to be used. For it was worthy of Achilles, the son of the most just Peleus, to sing the excellent deeds of men and demigods. And besides, teaching the fit time for its use, Homer found a useful and pleasant training for one living in leisure. For Achilles, a warlike man born for action, was away from the battles because of the anger conceived against Agamemnon. The poet therefore judged that the hero's mind should be sharpened by the most beautiful songs, so that he would be ready for the sally <note>original: "eruptionem" (Latin); "ἔξοδον" (Greek); referring to his eventual return to the battlefield.</note> which he was to make a little while later; and this he did by commemorating the ancient deeds of the past. Such then was ancient music, and such advantages did it bring. For we hear that both Hercules used music, and Achilles, and many others, whose master was the most wise Chiron, a teacher of music as well as of justice and medicine.

<column-break/>

<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CHAPTER XLI—XLII. 49</header>
<page-num>49</page-num>

### CHAPTER XLI.

In general, anyone with sense would surely not blame the sciences if someone were to use them improperly; rather, he would consider this the specific fault of those who use them. Therefore, if someone has mastered the educational mode of music and <margin>5</margin> has obtained the appropriate care during his childhood years, he will praise and welcome what is noble, <margin>B</margin> and he will criticize the opposite—both in other matters and in those regarding music. Such a person will be pure from every ignoble action. Having reaped the greatest benefit through music, <margin>10</margin> he would become a great asset to himself and the city, employing no unharmonious act or word, always and everywhere preserving what is fitting, temperate, and orderly.

### CHAPTER XLII.

<margin>15</margin> That even the most well-governed of cities have made it a point to take care for noble music, one can cite many other testimonies. One might mention Terpander, <note>Terpander was a semi-legendary 7th-century BCE poet-musician credited with reconciling the Spartans during a period of civil unrest.</note> who once quelled the civil strife <margin>C</margin> that had arisen among the Spartans. And also Thaletas <margin>20</margin> the Cretan, of whom they say that, having arrived among the Spartans according to a certain mandate of the Pythian oracle, <note>original: "πυθόχρηστον" (pythochrēston); refers to an instruction given by the Oracle at Delphi.</note> he healed them through music and delivered them from...

<margin>CHAPTER XLI.</margin> Finally, to speak in summary, no sane person will blame a science if someone does not use it as is right, but will judge it the proper fault of the one using it. Accordingly, if anyone has attained through labor and industry that mode of music which leads to the proper instruction of youth, and has been formed in early life with suitable education, he will praise and embrace beautiful things, but will find fault with their opposites, not only in music but in other matters as well; and such a man will be free from every base action. Having attained this greatest fruit from music, he will benefit both himself and the state, using no discordant word or deed, but everywhere preserving decorum and moderation.

<margin>CHAPTER XLII.</margin> Furthermore, it can be shown by many testimonies that those states which used the best laws took the greatest care for noble music. Let it suffice for us to have brought forward Terpander, who pacified the sedition that had arisen at Sparta, and Thaletas the Cretan, who was summoned by the Spartans by the oracle of the Pythian Apollo...

<summary>This page discusses the moral and pedagogical value of music, using the example of Achilles' education by Chiron to show how music prepares the soul for virtuous action. It further argues that music is essential for civil order, citing historical figures like Terpander and Thaletas who used music to heal social strife in Sparta.</summary>

<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XLII—XLIII.</header>

Pratinas <note>A 5th-century BC Greek dramatist and lyric poet from Phlius.</note> says that music once stayed a plague that had seized Sparta. But indeed, even Homer says that the plague which seized the Greeks ceased through the power of music; for he said:

<margin>5</margin>
> So all day long they sought to appease the god with song,
> The sons of the Achaeans singing a beautiful paean,
> Hymning the Far-Darter; and his heart was glad as he listened.
<note>original: "παιήονα" (paeona) — a paean, a hymn typically addressed to Apollo, often for healing or after victory.</note>

"These verses, good teacher, I have made the capstone <note>original: "κολοφῶνα" (kolophona) — a finishing touch or crowning stroke.</note> of my discourse on music, since you previously declared to us the power of music through these very lines. For in <margin>D</margin> <margin>10</margin> truth, its first and most beautiful work is the grateful offering of thanks to the gods; and following this, its second work is the purification, the melodic and harmonious organization of the soul." Having said these things, Soterichus said: "You have here, good teacher, the general <note>original: "ἐγκυκλίους" (enkyklious) — literally 'circular' or 'rounded' education, the origin of our word 'encyclopedic.'</note> discourses regarding music."

### CHAPTER XLIII.

<margin>15</margin> Soterichus was thus admired for what he had said; for he showed through his countenance and his voice his true zeal for music. And my teacher <note>Referring to the host/narrator who moderates the discussion.</note> said: "Among other things, I accept this from each of you: that both of you have preserved

---

<note>The following is a translation of the Latin parallel text provided in the source manuscript, which mirrors the Greek text above.</note>

Pratinas writes that music calmed the plague then raging in Sparta. But Homer also writes that the plague which had invaded the Greeks ceased by the help of music. For thus he sings:

> Therefore, the whole day long they appeased Phoebus with song,
<margin>5</margin> > the Achaean host singing a beautiful paean
> and modulating the praises of the great Bow-Bearer.
> Joy touched his heart at the hearing of it.

I have brought these verses, good master, as the crowning point of my talk on music, since you had previously <margin>10</margin> cited them to indicate the force of music. For in truth, this is its first and most beautiful duty: a grateful reward toward the gods; the next is the purification of the mind and a harmonious, self-consistent arrangement. Having spoken these things, Soterichus said: "You have, good master, the so-called general discussions about music."

### CHAPTER XLIII.

<margin>15</margin> Soterichus excited admiration with his oration, having demonstrated his study of music with both his face and his voice. And my master said: "Along with the rest, this is approved by me in both of you: that each of you has maintained his own order."

<column-break/>

<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>PLUTARCH ON MUSIC CH. XLIII.</header>

his own proper rank. For Lysias has entertained us with only as much as a performing cithara-player <note>A professional singer who accompanies themselves on the lyre (cithara).</note> ought to know. But Soterichus has shared his abundance with us by teaching as much as <margin>E</margin> pertains to the utility, the theory, and even the power <margin>5</margin> and use of music. But I think they have intentionally left one thing for me; for I will not accuse them of cowardice, as if they were ashamed to drag music down into the banquet-hall. For if it is useful anywhere, it is certainly during drinking, as the noble Homer declared. For song, <margin>F</margin> <margin>10</margin> he says somewhere,

> and dance—for these are the ornaments of a feast.
<note>Homer, Odyssey 1.152.</note>

And let no one suppose that Homer thought music was useful only for pleasure because of these words. For there is a deeper <margin>15</margin> meaning hidden in the verses. For he brought music in at the most opportune moments to provide the greatest utility and help—I mean at the dinners and gatherings of the ancients. For it happened that music was introduced as being capable of counteracting and soothing the "over-heated" power of wine; <margin>20</margin> just as your friend Aristoxenus <note>A Greek philosopher and music theorist, student of Aristotle.</note> somewhere says: for he used to say that music was introduced because while wine

---

<note>The following is a translation of the Latin parallel text provided in the source manuscript, which mirrors the Greek text above.</note>

Lysias proposed to us only those things which it is fitting for a cithara-player performing with his hands to know. Soterichus, however, quite liberally brought to us what pertains to the utility, contemplation, power, and even the use of music. I think they have left what I am about to say to me willingly and on purpose. <margin>5</margin> For I will not fix upon them this crime of timidity, so as to declare they were afraid to bring music down into the banquets. For if anywhere, certainly in drinking, Homer clearly taught that music is useful, saying thus:

> song and dance, the sweet gifts of a dinner.

Nor would I wish anyone to suspect that Homer intended to signify <margin>10</margin> that music conduces only to delight; rather, a deeper sense lies hidden in those words. For he claimed the utility and help of music in the most opportune place—I mean in the dinners and banquets of the ancients. Therefore, it was accepted that music be applied to these, because <margin>15</margin> it was able to call back the mind and mitigate the hot force of wine. In which sentiment your Aristoxenus also somewhere said that music was introduced into dinners because, just as wine undermines both the minds and bodies of those using it to excess, so music by its own order and symmetry leads them back to the contrary state and calms them. Therefore...

<meta>This page concludes Plutarch’s "On Music." The previous page ended with Soterichus finishing his speech on the moral and educational value of music. Here, the host of the banquet (the "Master") provides the final philosophical synthesis, linking music to the order of the cosmos before the gathering concludes.</meta>

tends to cause those who have indulged to excess <note>original: "ἄδην" (aden) — to satiety or excess, referring to the food and wine at the banquet.</note> to stumble in both their bodies and their minds; but music, through its inherent order and symmetry, leads them back to a balanced state and calms them. It is for this reason, Homer says, that the ancients used music as a remedy <note>original: "βοηθήματι" (boethemati) — a help, remedy, or medical aid.</note> at such opportune moments.

<margin>p. 1147</margin>

### CHAPTER XLIV.

"But indeed, my friends, the greatest point of all—and that which most demonstrates the supreme dignity and majesty of music—has been overlooked by you. For Pythagoras, Archytas, Plato, and the rest of the ancient philosophers asserted that the motion of the universe and the revolution of the stars could neither occur nor be sustained without music. They claim that all things were fashioned by God according to **harmony** <note>original: "ἁρμονίαν" (harmonian) — here referring to the "Harmony of the Spheres," the idea that the movement of celestial bodies creates a divine sort of music based on mathematical proportions.</note>. However, it would be inappropriate to prolong this discussion now; for the highest and most 'musical' task of all is to apply the proper **measure** <note>original: "μέτρον" (metron) — the principle of balance or moderation, a key Greek virtue.</note> to everything."

Having said these things, he sang a **paean** <note>A song of praise or thanksgiving, typically dedicated to Apollo.</note>, and after pouring a libation to **Cronus** <note>The Latin text uses "Saturno" (Saturn). Cronus was the father of Zeus and leader of the Titans.</note> and his children, and to all the gods and the Muses, he dismissed the guests from the banquet.

<meta>This page transitions from the general discussion of music in the previous section to a detailed scholarly commentary on Plutarch’s "Life of Phocion." The focus shifts to historical epithets (nicknames) and the personal life of the Athenian statesman Phocion.</meta>

# CHAPTER I.

"Of Phocion the Good"] This same epithet <term>cognomen; an additional name or nickname given to a person based on their character or achievements</term> of Phocion appears again in Plutarch’s *Life of Phocion*, chapter 10: <note>original: "ὥστε θαυμάζειν, ὅπως καὶ ὁπόθεν τραχὺς οὕτως ἀνὴρ καὶ σκυθρωπὸς ἐκτήσατο τὴν τοῦ Χρηστοῦ προσηγορίαν" — "so as to wonder how and from where such a harsh and somber man acquired the title of 'the Good.'"</note>
<margin>5</margin> Aelian, *Various History* XII, 43: "Phocion, who was called 'the Good'"; Dio Chrysostom, *Orations* LXXIII, 7: "and called 'Good' by those very people"; Themistius, *Orations* XVIII, p. 218 a; the Suda <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note> under "Phrynon"; Cornelius Nepos, *Phocion* 1, at the beginning: "from which epithet he was called *bonus* [good]"; Valerius Maximus III, 8.
Indeed, epithets of this kind indicating some excellence of character are very common among more modern people—for we have not only "Good"
<margin>10</margin> rulers, but also "Great," "Pious," "Just," "Steadfast," "Magnanimous," and "Peaceful"—but they very rarely came into use among the Greeks. Therefore, besides Aristides, who was surnamed "the Just," I currently have no one else to mention. I do not recall Alexander, the son of Philip, being
<margin>15</margin> called "the Great" by any Greek writer; regarding Antiochus, it is not clear. This was a Roman custom, about which a notable passage exists in Plutarch’s *Life of Pompey*, at the end of chapter 13: <note>original: "ἤρξατο γράφειν ἑαυτὸν ἐν ταῖς ἐπιστολαῖς καὶ τοῖς διατάγμασι Μάγνον Πομπήϊον· οὐκέτι γὰρ ἦν ἐπίφθονον τοὔνομα σύνηθες γενόμενον. ὅθεν εἰκότως ἂν ἀγασθείη καὶ θαυμάσειεν ἄν τις τοὺς πάλαι Ῥωμαίους, οἳ ταῖς τοιαύταις ἐπικλήσεσι καὶ προσωνυμίαις οὐ τὰς πολεμικὰς ἠμείβοντο καὶ στρατιωτικὰς κατορθώσεις μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὰς πολιτικὰς πράξεις καὶ ἀρετὰς ἐκόσμουν."</note> "He began to write himself in his letters and edicts as 'Pompey the Great'; for the name was no longer a cause of envy once it had become common. Hence one might reasonably admire and
<margin>20</margin> wonder at the Romans of old, who with such titles and surnames rewarded not only military and martial successes, but also adorned political deeds and virtues." And as far as Alexander is concerned, the words of Quirinus <note>The deified name of Romulus, the legendary founder of Rome.</note> delivered in the council of the gods in Julian’s *The Caesars* (p. 316 D) are relevant here:
<margin>25</margin> <note>original: "οὕτω δὲ αὐτὸν οἱ ἐμοὶ τεθαυμάκασιν ἔκγονοι, ὥστε μόνον αὐτὸν ἐκ πάντων, ὅσοι γεγόνασιν ἡγεμόνες ξένοι, ὀνομάζουσι καὶ νομίζουσι μέγαν."</note> "My descendants have so admired him that he alone of all foreign leaders who have ever lived, they name and consider 'Great'." Indeed, it is one thing to say "the great Alexander" <note>original: "ὁ μέγας Ἀλέξανδρος" — using 'great' as a simple description</note> and another to say "Alexander the Great" <note>original: "Ἀλέξανδρος ὁ Μέγας" — using 'Great' as a formal title</note>. If Cyrus, the son of
<margin>30</margin> Cambyses, ever bears the epithet "the Great," it was done to distinguish him from the younger Cyrus, the son of Darius.
— "[she] called [him] her ornament"] Plutarch relates the same in his *Life of Phocion*, end of chapter 19: "But his wife, when a certain Ionian guest-friend displayed her gold and jewel-studded ornament in her braids and necklaces,
<margin>35</margin> said: 'But my ornament is Phocion, who is now in his twentieth year of serving as general for the Athenians'"; and Stobaeus, Title 74, 54, p. 443. One might compare, besides the mother of the Gracchi <note>A reference to Cornelia, who famously pointed to her sons and said, "These are my jewels."</note>, the saying of a certain noble Spartan woman in Plutarch’s *Sayings of Spartan Women* (VIII, p. 241 D).
"that [the wife] of my teacher"] By what right Plutarch calls Onesicrates his "teacher" <note>original: "praeceptorem"</note> here and in chapter
<margin>40</margin> 43, I do not know. However, I think this was said out of honor; otherwise, Plutarch, along with the other Platonists of that age, calls him a "guide" <note>original: "καθηγεμόνα" (kathegemona)</note> or "instructor" <note>original: "καθηγητὴν" (kathegeten)</note>...

It also features a famous anecdote about Phocion’s wife valuing her husband’s character over physical jewelry.</summary>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS I—II.

...had called him, with which name he usually honors his teacher Ammonius. Furthermore, because of Soterichus of Alexandria, who was one of the guests of Onesicrates, I believe this is the same Onesicrates mentioned in the *Table Talk* <note>Plutarch’s *Quaestiones Convivales*, a collection of dinner-table discussions on various topics.</note> V, 5. He was a learned physician of Alexandria, at whose banquets scholarly questions were solved. Plutarch visited him with his grandfather Lamprias when he was quite young. Now, as to what can be gathered regarding the date the dialogue was composed from the fact that Onesicrates is counted among the living here, I have discussed this in the preface and in the German journal titled *Pedagogical Review* <note>original: "Paedag. Revue"</note> XLII, 1856, p. 198.
<margin>10</margin> **Worthy of attention]** <note>original Greek: "ἀξία σπουδῆς"</note> One ought to have said "more worthy" <note>original: "μᾶλλον ἀξία" or "ἀξιωτέρα"</note>. The positive form of the adjective is used here instead of the comparative; on this point, there is a learned note by Bernhardy in his *Syntax* p. 437, citing Dorville’s notes on Chariton p. 528, and Wyttenbach on Plutarch Vol. VI p. 950.
<margin>15</margin>

## CHAPTER II.

**Had summoned]** <note>original Greek: "παρακεκλήκει"</note> It is well known that the "syllabic augment" <term>a prefix, usually the letter 'e', added to the beginning of Greek verbs to indicate a past tense</term> is often omitted in the pluperfect tense by later writers. This occurs more frequently in the passive pluperfect than the active, and in compound verbs rather than simple ones. The form "had happened" <note>original: "συμβεβήκει"</note> is almost exclusively used without the prefix. However, Josephus uses the prefixed form in *Antiquities* VIII, 13, 4; Lucian uses "had eaten" <note>original: "βεβρώκει"</note> in *The Cock* ch. 4; Achilles
<margin>20</margin> Tatius uses "had been silenced" <note>original: "μεμωθήκει"</note> in II, 14, and "had spent" <note>original: "δεδαπανήκει"</note> in III, 16; Dio Chrysostom uses "had plundered" <note>original: "πεπορθήκει"</note> in XI, 56, "they had died" <note>original: "τεθνήκεσαν"</note> in XI, 71, and "they had become" <note>original: "γεγόνεσαν"</note> in XIII, 26. Occasionally, the manuscripts vary, as in "had sent" <note>original: "πεπόμφει"</note> in the same author at XIII, 8, or "had grown" <note>original: "πεφύκει"</note> in XXX, 5.
<margin>25</margin> In Josephus, I found the forms for "you had learned," "had shown," "they had been," and "had spoken." Strabo and Arrian also provide many examples of this kind. Regarding the Attic writers <note>Authors from the Golden Age of Athens, whose style was often imitated by later scholars.</note>, see Matthiae’s *Greek Grammar* § 165; for sacred scripture, see Winer’s *Grammar of the New Testament* § 9. A little
<margin>30</margin> further down, Plutarch used "had been completed" <note>original: "συντετέλεστο"</note>, and "had become" <note>original: "γεγένητο"</note> in Chapter V. The augment was omitted in these forms to avoid the harsh sound resulting from a repeated 'e'.
**Stipend]** <note>original Greek: "σύνταξις"</note> Among later writers, this word signifies a wage or salary, which we call an "annual allowance" <note>original: "Jahrgeld"</note>, or in French a "pension"—similar to the *sportula* <term>a gift or payment given by a patron to a client</term> of the Romans. See Schaefer on Dionysius Vol. V
<margin>35</margin> p. 365, and Jacobs in his notes on the *Anthology* II, 3 p. 223. Thus Plutarch, in his *Life of Alexander* ch. 21, says of Darius's wives: "they enjoyed even greater stipends <note>original: "συντάξεις"</note> than before." See also the *Life of Lucullus* ch. 2: "he provided him with a double expense and stipend, not just as much as he gave the others, but fourfold." See also Lucian's *The Eunuch* ch. 3: "a certain not insignificant salary <note>original: "μισθοφορά"</note> has been arranged <note>original: "συντέτακται"</note> by the king for philosophers according to their sects." In Julian’s *Misopogon* p. 367 A, "public stipends" <note>original: "δημόσιαι συντάξεις"</note> refer to the
<margin>40</margin> annual donations which emperors received from individual cities.
**They define the voice]** <note>original Greek: "ὁρίζονται τὴν φωνήν"</note> Voice is the cause and principle of that which is perceived by hearing; it is defined by Plutarch in *On Fortune* p. 98 B as "the power of perceiving a stroke of air through the ear and brain," or

<column-break/>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER II.</header>

more briefly in *Platonic Questions* VII, 9: "a stroke upon the sensing organ through the ears by the air," following Plato himself in the *Timaeus* p. 541 H: "In general, let us define voice as a stroke transmitted by the air through the ears, through the brain and the blood, as far as the soul; and the motion caused by it, beginning from the head and ending near the region of the
<margin>5</margin> liver, as hearing." See also Alcinous, *Introduction* 19: "Voice is a stroke emitted through the ears from the brain and blood, transmitted as far as the soul; a high sound is one that moves quickly, a low sound moves slowly, and a loud sound is great in quantity, while a soft sound is small." See Plutarch’s *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*
<margin>10</margin> IV, 16 and Stobaeus’s *Physical Selections* p. 153; I owe these references to the diligence of Wyttenbach’s *Notes* p. 671. Roman writers also interpret the voice as a "stroke" or "percussion of the air," and they apply this to any sound whatsoever. Seneca, in *Natural Questions* II, 6, asks: "What is voice but a tension of the air, shaped by the stroke of the tongue so that it may be heard?"
<margin>15</margin> and in II, 29: "voice is nothing other than struck air." Likewise, Vitruvius in V, 3: "voice is a flowing breath, made sensible to the hearing by the stroke of the air. It moves in infinite circular rounds, just as when a stone is thrown into standing water, innumerable circles of waves are born, growing from the center." See the interpreters of Cicero’s *On the Orator* § 149.
<margin>20</margin> I believe "the Grammarians," to whom Plutarch attributes this definition, are the Stoics <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy that emphasized logic and physics as the basis for ethics.</note>, for the *Art of the Voice* by Diogenes of Babylon proves that they treated this subject. However, Aristoxenus explicitly stated in *Harmonics* II p. 44 that the actual doctrine concerning the voice and the percussion of air is foreign to the study of Music, since
<margin>25</margin> Music only looks at the modulations of the voice (ibid. I, 3). On the other hand, Xenocrates set out from the consideration of the voice as a foundation when explaining Dialectic <term>the art of investigating the truth of opinions through logical discussion</term>, as seen in Porphyry's commentary on Ptolemy’s *Music* p. 193. An anonymous author in Menagius mentions a treatise by Aristotle *On the Voice*. Furthermore, there is a difference between "voice" <note>original: "φωνή"</note>, "noise" <note>original: "ψόφος"</note>, and "musical tone" <note>original: "φθόγγος"</note>;
<margin>30</margin> for according to Ptolemy’s *Harmonics* I, 1, "noise" is an effect of the air being struck, while a "musical tone" is a noise that holds one and the same pitch <note>original: "τόνος"</note>.
**On Grammar]** <note>original Greek: "περὶ γραμματικῆς"</note> Besides Plutarch, I have no one who defines grammar as the art of expressing the voice through letters and storing it in the memory as if in a pantry, although similar ideas appear
<margin>35</margin> scattered among the grammarians. Thus Theodosius p. 56, 3: "Grammar is a theoretical and logical art that teaches us to speak well and to write well." See also Bekker’s *Anecdotes* p. 658: "And this is the art concerning the writing and reading of the recorded voice,"
<margin>40</margin> cf. p. 667, 729; Plato’s *Cratylus* p. 431 E; *Sophist* p. 253 A. Moreover, this refers to "Lesser Grammar," for "Greater Grammar" is what we call "Philology" <term>the study of literary texts and language history</term>.
**That Music...]** <note>original Greek: "ὅτι μουσική"</note> Asclepiades also teaches that a certain closer bond exists between grammar, music, and philosophy,
<margin>45</margin> as seen in Sextus Empiricus’s *Against the Grammarians* I, 3 p. 232. The ancients often joined music with wisdom and philosophy. Quintilian, in *Institutes* I, 10, 9, writes: "Who is ignorant that music once held such veneration that the same men were judged to be musicians, prophets, and sages? I will pass over others..."

<summary>This page provides a scholarly analysis of the term "voice" (phone) as defined by ancient Greek and Roman philosophers and grammarians, while also clarifying technical terms regarding salaries (syntaxis) and the scope of grammar.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the history of ancient music, specifically focusing on the intersection of grammar and music, the philosophical schools (Platonists and Peripatetics), and the legendary origins of the art.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS II—III.

"Orpheus and Linus" — a view which Lobeck <note>Christian August Lobeck was a 19th-century scholar known for *Aglaophamus*, a foundational study of Greek mystery religions.</note> circumscribed within its own limits in *Aglaophamus* p. 270. No less was grammar joined with music. Quintilian <note>A famous Roman rhetorician.</note> I, 10, 17: "let us also pass over the fact that grammar and music were once joined; indeed Archytas and Aristoxenus even thought grammar was subordinate to music, and Sophron shows that the same people were teachers of both—likewise Eupolis, in whose work Prodamus teaches both music and letters, etc." Add Bekker's *Anecdota* p. 1168: "that the discovery of the elements <note>meaning the alphabet</note> was the work of a man of both poetry and music is signaled by the fact that anciently the same men were teachers of both music and grammar," compared with what Bergk discussed regarding these passages in his *Commentary on the Remains of Attic Comedy* p. 332.
<margin>10</margin> **A primary duty for men]** <note>original Greek: "προηγούμενον ἀνθρώποις"</note> This is the primary duty of humans: a phrase borrowed from the doctrine of the dialecticians or logicians, who call the major premise the "antecedent" <note>original Greek: "προηγούμενον"</note>; from there it was transferred to mean anything primary or chief. See Wyttenbach's *Observations* p. 779. The adverb "primarily" <note>original Greek: "προηγουμένως"</note>, the use of which is very frequent in Sextus Empiricus, returns below in chapter 26.
<margin>15</margin> **O initiates of music]** <note>original Greek: "ὦ μουσικῆς θιασῶται"</note> Thus Themistius p. 33 c: "the young initiates of Plato." Regarding the initiates of Epicurus, see Bake on Cleomedes p. 443.

## CHAPTER III.

**Both of the Platonists]** <note>original Greek: "τῶν τε γὰρ Πλατωνικῶν"</note> Although in this passage—where it is surprising that no mention of the Pythagoreans is made—it is handed down in distinct words that very many Platonic philosophers devoted effort to the treatment of ancient music, nevertheless hardly one or two can be found who are described as musicians, or whose musical writings are named. Therefore, since we can hardly include Dionysius of Halicarnassus the Younger <note>A music theorist of the 2nd century AD.</note> among the Platonists, only Thrasyllus belongs here, the friend of the Emperor Tiberius, who wrote *On Music and Harmonics* and is praised by Theo and Porphyry in their commentaries on Ptolemy. See Bullialdus on Theo p. 248, and Gelder’s prolegomena to Theo’s *Arithmetic* p. II. 
<margin>30</margin> Furthermore, Theo of Smyrna himself, who is called a Platonist around the time of Hadrian, was a little older than Ptolemy, by whom he is praised. Learned men think he is the same person as the interlocutor in Plutarch's booklet *On the E at Delphi*; see Gelder in the place cited, p. IV onwards. His commentaries survive: *On Mathematical Matters Useful for Reading Plato* (edited by Ismael Bullialdus, Paris 1644), in the second book of which certain learned and not-to-be-despised things regarding ancient music are handed down.
**Those from the Peripatos]** <note>The Peripatetics, or followers of Aristotle.</note> Following the lead of Aristotle, whom Diogenes Laertius (V, 26) and the Anonymous of Menagius record as having written *On Music*, very many of the Peripatetic philosophers studied the history of poetry and music. Thus the writings of many are cited *On Poets*, in which there was also discussion of lyre-players and flute-players: for one might expect less actual treatment of harmonics or musical theory to have occupied them than was the case after the example of the great Aristoxenus. Theophrastus leads the group, whom Diogenes Laertius

<column-break/>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER III.

(V, 46 onwards) records as having written *Harmonics*, *On Musicians*, and three books *On Music*, which are mentioned by Plutarch in *Table Talk* I, 5, Porphyry in *Ptolemy's Harmonics* p. 241, Censorinus chapter 12, Syrianus on the *Metaphysics* XIII, and Martianus Capella book X. Athenaeus used a book by the same author titled *On Comedy*, in which certain things about musicians were also handed down. Also Chamaeleon of Heraclea wrote *On Comedy*, and I believe the same author wrote *On Poets*, of which individual parts—*On Aeschylus*, *On Anacreon*, *On Lasus*—are cited by Athenaeus. Heraclides Ponticus, whose *Collection of Famous Men in Music* is commemorated in this very dialogue of Plutarch, wrote two books *On Poetics and Poets* and *On Music*, according to Diogenes; for Athenaeus (X p. 455 C) praised a third book *On Music*, and he commented in three other books on the condition of music as it was in the age of Euripides and Sophocles, titled *Musical Matters in Euripides and Sophocles*, Diogenes Laertius V, 87. An *Introduction to Music* by the same author is named by Porphyry in *Ptolemy's Harmonics* p. 213. Phanias of Eresus wrote a book *On Poets* from which Athenaeus preserved certain fragments regarding Terpander and Stratonicus. Dicaearchus of Messene touched upon many things pertaining to literary and musical history in the *Life of Greece*; see the argument to Euripides' *Medea* and Athenaeus XIV p. 636 C. 
<margin>20</margin> Although only a single book by him is reported in the Suda <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note>, it is likely that only parts of this book, and not separate books, are to be understood when Dicaearchus's *On Musical Contests* or *On Music* is cited, as occurs several times in the scholia <note>ancient marginal notes</note> to Aristophanes, as well as the arguments to the plays of Sophocles and Euripides in Sextus Empiricus's *Against the Geometers* p. 310, and a commentary on Alcaeus; see Naeke in the *Rhenish Museum* I p. 40 onwards, and Meineke’s *Critical History of the Comedians* p. 8. We know of Hieronymus of Cardia’s commentaries on the poets, in the fifth book of which lyre-players were listed, from Athenaeus XIV p. 635 F and Zenobius II, 55. It is also probable that Hermippus of Smyrna wrote on the poets, whose book on Hipponax is praised by Athenaeus. Let Adrastus of Aphrodisias close the line, a Peripatetic philosopher at the beginning of the second century after Christ, whose book *On Harmonics*, mentioned by Theo (chapters 6, 13, and elsewhere), rumor holds is still preserved today in the libraries of Italy.
<margin>35</margin> **Corruption]** <note>original Greek: "παραφθορά"</note> "Corruption" and "to corrupt" are words used by more recent writers (see Jacobs on Philostratus's *Images* p. 426), technical terms among the grammarians (referring to a "corrupted word") about which you may consult Schmidt in *Fragments of Didymus* p. 15 onwards.
<margin>40</margin> **Amphion]** Heraclides rightly derived the beginnings of the musical art from Thrace, from where the origins of other arts also reached the rest of the Greeks, along with the sacred festivals of the Muses. Strabo (X p. 722) nods to the same idea, where he contends that all music is of Thracian and Asiatic (that is, Phrygian) origin. 
<margin>45</margin> See Bernhardy's *History of Greek Literature* I p. 198—201. These things are therefore certain; however, one cannot proceed further: for whatever computations of years or names of bards are reported, they are all vain and of no reliability, since writers do not agree among themselves about them.

<meta>The previous page discussed the historical link between grammar and music in ancient education, referencing Quintilian and Attic comedy. This page continues the commentary on Chapter 3, examining the legendary musicians Amphion, Linus, and the reliability of ancient Greek records.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER 3.

evidence, but among learned men it is agreed that these have only a "symbolic" force and nature, as they call it. Such is the case of *Amphion*, son of Jupiter and Antiope, whose legendary youth was recorded by Apollodorus <note>A 2nd-century BCE scholar who compiled Greek myths.</note> (III, 5, 5). The Greeks regarded him as the inventor of the lyre and of ancient music in general; see the *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note> under the entry for his name.
<margin>5</margin> Together with his brother Zethus, he surrounded Thebes with walls to ward off the attacks of the Phlegyans; it is said the stones of these walls came together of their own accord at the sound of his lyre (see Apollodorus, as above; and the Scholia <note>Ancient explanatory comments written in the margins of manuscripts.</note> on Apollonius of Rhodes, I, 735; and the Scholia on the *Odyssey*, book 11, line 262). However, Zethus was entirely "unmusical" <note>original Greek: "ἄμουσος"</note> and bitterly rebuked his brother, whom he thought was occupied with trifles; see Dio Chrysostom <note>A Greek orator and philosopher of the Roman Empire.</note>, Oration 73,
<margin>10</margin> 10. In this place, we should not overlook that while Homer was well aware of the roles Zethus and Amphion played in building the walls of Thebes, he says nothing of Amphion’s musical fame. Now, if Amphion is said not to have *invented* the lyre but to have *received* it from some god, this should be understood as the connection of Amphion
<margin>15</margin> and his ancient Thracian music with *certain sacred rites of the gods*. The words that follow in Plutarch, "his father clearly having taught him" <note>original Greek: "τοῦ πατρὸς δηλονότι διδάξαντος αὐτὸν"</note>, are suspicious to me. For the story went that Amphion’s lyre was a gift from either Mercury <note>The Roman name for Hermes.</note> or—according to Armenidas in the first book
<margin>20</margin> of the *Thebaica* and Pherecydes—a gift of the Muses. Ancient writers are silent about Jupiter <note>Zeus</note> and silent about Apollo in this regard. I interpret this to mean that the beginnings of music lie hidden in the most ancient times, before the sacred rites of Apollo reached the Thracians. To be sure, Dioscorides wished to claim the lyre was a gift from Apollo, but
<margin>25</margin> this agrees poorly with the other myths which record that Amphion was killed by Apollo to avenge the insults his wife Niobe had offered to Latona <note>Leto, the mother of Apollo and Artemis.</note>, even though others relate this differently (see Heyne’s notes on Apollodorus, p. 595). Furthermore, the legend of Amphion is significant because it allows us to infer a Thracian origin for
<margin>30</margin> Theban music.

->"From the record in Sicyon"<- <note>original Greek: "ἐκ τῆς ἀναγραφῆς τῆς ἐν Σικυῶνι"</note>
*Anagraphai* <note>A term for public or sacred records.</note> are either Sacred Fasti <note>Calendars or chronological lists of religious events.</note>—pontifical books (what we call a "temple chronicle") in which were written whatever events concerning festivals, priests, and the sacred rites of the gods were deemed worthy of note in ancient times—or city annals (a "city chronicle"),
<margin>35</margin> from which the logographers <note>Early Greek chroniclers and historians who preceded Thucydides.</note>, historians, and later travel-writers irrigated their little gardens. Cf. Dionysius of Halicarnassus, *Judgment on Thucydides*, chapter 5: "all the local memories preserved among the tribes and cities, whether stored in temples or in secular places as writings." Notable among these are the Argive records of the priestesses of
<margin>40</margin> Juno <note>Hera</note> and the Spartan records of the *Carneia* festival. Concerning the Sicyonian Pythian records cited here and in chapter 8, cf. Krause, *Pythian Games*, p. 76, and O. Müller, *The Dorians*, vol. I, p. 130. Mostly, however, these records were composed in a later age and falsely claimed antiquity, so that the information taken from them deserves a doubtful trust. On this point, there is a notable passage
<margin>45</margin> in Josephus, *Against Apion*, I, 4: "For the fact that from the beginning no care was taken among the Greeks to make public records of the things done at each time—this more than anything else provided both the error and the license

<column-break/>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER 3.

to lie to those who later wished to write something about ancient history. For not only was the matter of record-keeping neglected among the other Greeks, but not even among the Athenians—whom they say are sons of the soil <note>original Greek: "αὐτόχθονας," meaning 'born from the earth,' referring to the Athenian claim to be original inhabitants.</note> and diligent in education—is anything of this kind found to have existed; rather, they say the oldest
<margin>5</margin> of their public writings are the laws concerning homicide written for them by Draco, a man who lived only a little before the tyranny of Pisistratus. For what need is there to speak of the Arcadians, who boast of their antiquity? For these men were only barely educated in writing even after these times." Cf. *ibid.* chapter 2. Generally, the overseers
<margin>10</margin> of sacred rites were industrious and harmfully diligent in inventing and forging antiquities. The fabrications of the Delphians are well known. On the contrary, Pausanias <note>A 2nd-century CE traveler and geographer.</note> (I, 38, 7) says: "The ancient Eleusinians, since they lacked genealogists, gave themselves over to inventing other things, especially regarding the lineages of heroes." Even oracles were written in the records. Plutarch, *Against Colotes*, 17, p.
<margin>15</margin> 1116 F: "The Spartans, having the oracle concerning Lycurgus in their most ancient records." Finally, one must note the difference between a *record* (anagraphe) and an *inscription* (epigraphe) or "epigram," which is an inscription on a votive offering kept in a temple. These too were sometimes cited by historians.

->"Linus from Euboea"<- <note>original Greek: "Λίνον τὸν ἐξ Εὐβοίας"</note>
<margin>20</margin> What I just said about Amphion applies equally to his supposed contemporaries: Linus, Anthes, and Pierus. These were ancient bards <note>original Latin: "vates"</note> whom it is credible to believe lived in the singing, shady valleys of Helicon near Thespiae (see Bernhardy, as above, p. 204). I have found nothing about *Anthes* among Greek writers, at least
<margin>25</margin> nothing regarding his musical fame; cf. Plutarch, *Greek Questions*, 19. Stephanus of Byzantium mentioned a certain later bard named Anthas from the writings of Philostephanus. Regarding *Linus*, there were two versions of the myth: according to the older one in Pausanias (IX, 29, 3), with which Hesiod agrees (cited in Eustathius, p. 1163), he was said to be the son of Urania and
<margin>30</margin> Amphimarus, and he died in a musical contest, defeated by Apollo. According to the later Theban version in Pausanias and Apollodorus (II, 4, 9), he was the son of Calliope and Ismenius, and even the son of Apollo and thus the older brother of Orpheus; when he arrived in Thebes, he taught Hercules the art of playing the lyre and singing, and was killed by his pupil
<margin>35</margin> (who resented the master’s severity) with a plectrum or a lyre. The end of this story reeks of the jokes of comic playwrights, playing on the ambiguity between the "plectrum" <note>The tool used to strike the strings.</note> and the verb "to strike" <note>original Greek: "πλήσσω"</note>. Indeed, it is well known that comic poets often treated this very legend of Linus. Now, if we hold to the earlier accounts, Linus appears to have been an ancient bard who opposed
<margin>40</margin> the spread of *new Apollonian rites* through Thrace; therefore, Heraclides rightly said he was a contemporary of Amphion, whom we just saw was also alienated from the rites of Apollo. The case is no different for *Pierus*, who Pausanias says brought the cult of the nine Muses from Thrace to Thespiae; however, Apollodorus (I, 3, 3)
<margin>45</margin> relates from other writers that he entered a contest with the Muses and was defeated and blinded by them. For if we trace the origin of this myth to the age when the Muses were worshipped as companions and associates of Apollo, Pierus will also find a place among the sacred...

<summary>This page analyzes the legendary origins of Greek music through figures like Amphion and Linus, while critiquing the reliability of ancient "temple chronicles" (anagraphai) as historical sources. It highlights the tension between ancient Thracian musical traditions and the later, dominant rites associated with Apollo.</summary>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER 3.

opponents of Apollo. This opinion is not even contradicted by the fact that in *Ovid’s Metamorphoses V, 300* and *Antoninus Liberalis 9* (or, if you prefer, *Nicander in the Heteroeumena*), a contest took place between the Muses and the daughters of Pierus; for we may establish that the musical art was passed down through a kind of inheritance in the <margin>5</margin> family of Pierus, though I do not wish to press this point too hard. Moreover, those who decided there was no difference between the Muses and the daughters of Pierus completely distorted the matter; Rinck spoke of these in *The Religion of the Hellenes, Volume I, page 292*.

**Philammon the Delphian** <note>original Greek: "Φιλάμμωνα τὸν Δελφόν"</note>] Between Philammon—whom Heraclides <note>Heraclides Ponticus, a philosopher and student of Plato who wrote on music.</note> named after Amphion, and not without cause—and the <margin>10</margin> other leaders of Thracian music just praised, there is this difference: in all the legends *his memory is linked to the cult of Delphic Apollo*. In this passage, I am only surprised that Heraclides made no mention of *Orpheus* and his associates, such as *Hymenaeus* (about whom *Vater* collected more in *Euripides' Rhesus, page 308 and following*), *Periclymenus*, <margin>15</margin> *Perimedes*, and others (of whom *Lobeck* spoke in *Aglaophamus, page 328 and following*), nor of *Olen*. For Orpheus is both the leader and the foremost champion of the musical arts sacred to Apollo (see *Ulrici, History of Lyric Poetry, page 47*); and *Olen*, if it is permitted to illuminate the clouds of myth, is the one under whose leadership the Thraco-Pe- <margin>20</margin> lasgian tribes in the most ancient times embraced the Greek Apolline rites, such as those celebrated at Delphi and Delos (see *Klausen in the Halle Encyclopedia, under the relevant entry*). Philammon himself—whose name should be referred to that age when some fame of the oracle of Ammon <note>An ancient Egyptian and Libyan oracle associated by the Greeks with Zeus or Apollo.</note> had already reached Greece, as *Bernhardy* advised in *History of <margin>25</margin> Greek Literature, Volume I, page 297* (but see *E. Hoffmann, Homer and the Homerid Legend of Chios, pages 51, 52*)—is said to be the son of Apollo and the beautiful nymph Philonis, who lived around Mount Parnassus (see *Conon, Narrations, chapter 7; Scholia on Odyssey, book 19, line 432*). When he was defending Delphi from the robberies and harassment of the Phlegyans <note>A mythical tribe of warriors known for their insolence toward the gods.</note> with <margin>30</margin> the help of the Argives, he was killed (see *Pausanias, IX, 36, 2*). After Chrysothemis the Cretan, Philammon was the most ancient bard who won victory by singing in the Pythian contest <note>The Pythian Games at Delphi, which originally focused on musical and poetic competitions.</note> (*Pausanias, X, 7, 2*); for this reason, he was held by some to be the first inventor of the *nomoi* <note>term: Nomoi (singular: nomos). Traditional musical "laws" or compositions for the cithara (lyre) and voice.</note> for the cithara, about which Plutarch himself reported below in Chapter V. He <margin>35</margin> is also said to have been the first to lead choruses of young women in the *Scholia on the Odyssey* at the place cited. Later writers erroneously attributed the Lernaean mystery-rite to him, about which *Pausanias spoke in II, 37, 2*. Without doubt, so many inventions were heaped upon the famous name of Philammon that many writers felt they had to distin- <margin>40</margin> guish between several men of the same name. Therefore, he is rightly called "the Ancient" or "the Delphian" here and below by Plutarch; in this place, the consistent spelling in the manuscripts should not have been changed by certain editors (already criticized by Burette) into "the brother" <note>original Greek: "ἀδελφόν" (adelphon).</note>.

**Of Leto and Artemis** <note>original Greek: "Λητοῦς τε καὶ Ἀρτέμιδος"</note>] "Amyot, in his version, omits <margin>45</margin> Leto, either through forgetfulness or because this name was missing in the Greek copy used by the translator, just as it was apparently missing in that of *Gyraldi (History of the Poets, Vol. II, chapter 65 D, edited by LB)*, who

<column-break/>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER 3.

makes the same omission, citing this passage of Plutarch." *Burette*, Volume XIV, page 314.

**Thamyris, a Thracian by birth** <note>original Greek: "Θάμυριν δὲ τὸ γένος Θρᾷκα"</note>] *Thamyris* or *Thamyras* (*Suda* <note>A 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note> under the entry; the author of the *Contest of Homer and Hesiod* knows the form *Tamyras*, page <margin>5</margin> 242, 2 or 314, 3 in Goettling’s edition. For the meaning of the name see E. Hoffmann, *Homer*, page 53 and following), whom the ancients almost always mention alongside Orpheus (see *Boissonade, Anecdota, Volume IV, page 446*), is by common consent handed down as the son of Philammon and a Thracian of the Hedonian or Odrysian people, because his mother Argiope or <margin>10</margin> Arsinoe (whom the *Suda* names) had given birth to him among the Odrysians (see *Pausanias, IV, 33, 4*). Pausanias also asserts that this man belongs to the cult of Apollo, since he lists him among the victors of the Pythian contest after his father Philammon. Nor is it very contradictory if Plutarch reports that he com- <margin>15</margin> posed a *Titanomachy* <note>A lost epic poem describing the war between the Olympian gods and the Titans.</note>, by which he joins the ranks of those bards, led by Hesiod, who were mostly priests. Thus the *Suda* lists him among the authors of heroic song who preceded Homer in age, and reports that a *Theology* in three books was composed by him. On the other hand, the popular legend—such as many poets <margin>20</margin> after Homer (*Iliad, Book 2, line 595 and following*) and finally Sophocles told without much variation (compare *Heyne on Apollodorus, 1, 3, 3; Leutsch on Zenobius, IV, 27*)—went off in far different directions, as it separated Thamyris from the sacred rites of Apollo and placed him first among those bards who lived in the courts of princes, whom <margin>25</margin> learned men today call by the *Greek* name *Aoedoi* <note>term: Aoedoi (sing. aoidos). Professional oral poets or minstrels who sang for aristocratic audiences.</note>. He is said to have encountered the Muses at Dorion while returning from a journey to Eurytus, the king of Oechalia; having boasted that he would easily surpass them in the skill of singing in a contest, he was deprived by them of both his eyesight and his art of playing the cithara. According to this legend, a statue at Helicon represented Thamyris <margin>30</margin> as blind and with a broken lyre (see *Pausanias, IX, 30, 2*), as did a painting by Polygnotus <note>A famous Greek painter of the 5th century BCE.</note> in the meeting hall at Delphi (*Pausanias, X, 30, 4*). Therefore, the significance of Thamyris seems to have been not at all slight in establishing or changing the cult of the Muses, although we can no longer investigate what the individual details told about him <margin>35</margin> really mean; it is certain, however, that these things hardly fit a bard of Apollo. This being so, I agree with Preller, who in *Greek Mythology, Volume II, page 342*, interpreted the whole legend such that in the age of Thamyris the art of singing had taken on higher spirits and bards had become independent, <margin>40</margin> so that they practiced that art either without the Muses or against the Muses' will.

The blindness of the eyes—which we find not only in Thamyris but also in Demodocus, in the blind bard of Chios (see *Homeric Hymn to Apollo, line 172*), and finally in Homer himself—its meaning is well known. However, that trend, whose first beginnings are seen in Tha- <margin>45</margin> myris, had already passed into custom by the age of Demodocus and Phemius, so that the bards practiced music free and released from the service of specific sacred rites: moreover, that Demodocus and Phemius truly lived at some time as either kinsmen or friends of Homer, is why...
