# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER 3.

Since he himself made mention of them in his poems, it is well known that no one among the ancients doubted him. For the rest, regarding Thamyris, one should consult Boissonade in *Euripides' Rhesus 913* and Heyler in *Julian's Epistle 41*. I do not know where Tzetzes <note>Joannes Tzetzes, a 12th-century Byzantine poet and grammarian.</note> drew his information in the Scholia <note>ancient marginal commentaries</note> found in Matranga’s *Roman Anecdotes page 599*: <note>original Greek: "ὁ Θάμυρις δὲ παλαιὸς ἦν..."</note> “Thamyris was an ancient man, a contemporary of Cadmus and the grandfather of Orpheus; Orpheus was the son of Menippe, the daughter of Thamyris, although allegorically he is called the son of Calliope.” Finally, Pliny in his *Natural History VII, 57* says: “Thamyris first played the cithara <note>a professional version of the lyre</note> without the voice, Amphion sang with it, or as others say, Linus; Terpander composed citharoedic songs <note>songs performed to the cithara</note>.” Pliny attributes the invention of the *psilocitharistic* art <note>purely instrumental cithara playing without singing</note> to Thamyris. <margin>10</margin> This is incorrect. For this was actually invented by **Aristonicus of Argos**, who lived in Corcyra and was a contemporary of Archilochus. It was further refined by **Lysander of Sicyon**, who employed longer strings to increase the sound of the instrument and varied the modes of instrumental cithara playing; see *Athenaeus XIV page 637 F*. Stratonicus was the first to introduce *polychordia* <note>the use of many strings</note> into instrumental cithara playing; see *Phanias in Athenaeus VIII page 352 C*. <margin>15</margin>

**'The Sack of Troy'** <note>original Greek: "Ἰλίου πόρθησιν"</note>] The word *porthesis* <note>meaning 'sack' or 'destruction'</note>, which is explained by Hesychius and other lexicographers as *overthrow* or *seizure*, is of a more recent form compared to the older and poetic form *porsis*. It appears in Hermogenes, the scholia to Sophocles' *Philoctetes 1376*, Diodorus XVI, 31, Aristides Volume I page 252, and Plutarch’s *Life of Titus chapter 8*—passages which the Paris edition of Stephanus's *Thesaurus* provides— <margin>20</margin> as well as Himerius's *Oration II, 19*, and Joannes Lydus's *On the Magistracies III, 61*.

**'They set melodies to these'**] Clement of Alexandria, in his *Stromata I page 308*, shortened these words of Plutarch. Meibomius, in his work on *Aristoxenus page 76*, was the first to express this very difficult passage by translating it as follows: “Neither was the language of the aforementioned musicians used in poems loose and lacking in meter; rather, it was of the kind used by Stesichorus and the ancient songwriters, who applied songs to the verses they had made. For Heraclides says that Terpander, a poet of the *nomes* <note>traditional musical compositions or 'laws' of performance</note> which are sung to the cithara, sang in contests by wrapping melodies around each of the *nomes*, both his own poems and those of Homer.” <margin>30</margin> He was followed by Burette, who translated it thus: “Terpander noted the music upon the verses of each of his *Nomes*, just as upon the verses of Homer.” <note>Burette was an 18th-century French scholar who specialized in ancient music.</note> Moreover, he believes that Terpander added musical notes to the poems for the reason that he might either recite them himself in the public games or hand them over to others to be recited; compare *Forkel’s History of Music Volume I page 292*. Not having set out from this, and also from the *Parian Marble epoch 35*: “from which time Terpander the son of Derdenes the Lesbian wished to innovate the *nomes* of the ancients and the flute-players, and transformed the earlier music,” <note>original Greek: "ἀφ' οὗ Τέρπανδρος..."</note> Boeckh, in his *Meters of Pindar page 244*, says: “and indeed it is handed down by chronologists that Terpander of Lesbos was the first to describe a certain and constant melody for both the citharoedic and auloedic *nomes*, and made them public property once they were so designated.” However, I have explained in the *German Ephemeris* cited above, pages 204 and following, why this interpretation did not please me. <margin>45</margin> For first, a precise interpretation of the words stands in the way; for 'to set a melody to a poem' is not the same as 'to describe a song with the help of musical notes,' and indeed without musical notes

---

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER 3.</header>

this cannot be done at all, which is termed *parasēmainesthai* <note>to mark or notate</note>, nor is it the same as composing the tune of a song, which is *melopoiein*. Furthermore, none of the ancients recorded that Terpander invented musical notes. Since Boeckh, unless I am mistaken, gathered this only from this passage, <margin>5</margin> it ought not to have been asserted as a certainty by other learned men such as Hoeck in his *Creta Volume III page 370*, especially since Bernhardy argues against it in his *History of Greek Literature Volume I page 298*. For not even those who thought Pythagoras invented the notes saw the truth. To be sure, in *Aristides Quintilianus page 28*, in a highly garbled passage, the name of Pythagoras is placed before the exposition of letters in the <margin>10</margin> fifteen modes according to the three genera; but I would attribute this either to an interpolator or to the negligence of the writer. For since the letters in the diagram <note>a visual representation of a musical scale</note> of the ancients represented musical sounds in the order that is the most recent Attic style, the diagram cannot have been invented by Pythagoras. <margin>15</margin> Added to this is the fact that the person who was the author of the diagram set out the letters according to the enharmonic genus, whereas it is likely that Pythagoras would have done so according to the diatonic genus, about which Fortlagius spoke in his *System of Greek Music page 96 note 1*. Moreover, I call a 'diagram' the exposition of sounds described through letters or notes, proceeding from the low to the <margin>20</margin> high or vice versa—what we call *the representation of a scale through notes*—on the authority of Bacchius Senior, page 15: “A diagram is a flat figure in which every musical genus is performed; we use a diagram so that things difficult for the ear to grasp may appear before the eyes of those learning.” Add the *Scholia to Aristides page 278*: “'in the <margin>25</margin> diagrams' means in the written descriptions of musical figures.” Furthermore, Phanias of Eresus, in *Athenaeus VIII page 352 C*, says that Stratonicus invented this diagram—that most famous and witty musician who was a contemporary of Alexander the Great and was the first to open a music school, in which he also taught boys harmonics. Thus, <margin>30</margin> the invention of notes falls into the age of Aristotle, at which time we know that the signs for accents and punctuation were also either invented or received into common use. Certainly Aristoxenus <note>a Greek philosopher and the most important ancient writer on music theory</note> knew the use of the diagram and knew what it meant to 'notate melodies' (*parasēmainesthai ta melē*), yet he judged this exposition to be foreign to the study of music; see *Harmonic Elements I page 2, II page 39* and elsewhere.
For the rest, the use of notes—if indeed one may dwell on this matter a bit longer—was *educational* <note>Latin: didascalicus</note> among the ancients, not *common*. That is to say, <margin>35</margin> teachers used the diagram so that they might pass on the elements of singing to boys. On the contrary, one will be wandering as far as the sky is from the earth if they believe that copies of Sophocles or Euripides existed in Athens marked with musical notes, of which <margin>40</margin> thing not even the slightest notice has reached our memory. Indeed, what should be decided about the Pindaric fragment of Athanasius Kircher <note>a 17th-century Jesuit scholar who claimed to have found a fragment of Pindar with ancient notation</note> need not be said: for I fear that regarding anyone who decides it is genuine, the old proverb holds: <margin>45</margin>
-> “Among the unmusical, <-
-> even a lark sings more tunefully than a swan.” <-
Nor do I think that the Hesiodic codex adorned with musical notes, which that rogue recently tried to sell...

<summary>This page analyzes the origins of Greek musical notation and the "diagramma" (scale representation), arguing that these were educational tools from the time of Aristotle rather than inventions of legendary figures like Terpander or Pythagoras. It clarifies technical terms for instrumental versus vocal performance and dismisses alleged archaeological finds of ancient notation as forgeries.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Chapter 3 of the treatise on ancient music. The discussion moves from legendary figures like Thamyris to the practical transmission of music in antiquity, focusing on the lack of musical notation and the development of "Nomoi" (musical laws or strains). It follows the previous page's exploration of the origins of instrumental performance.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER 3.

Simonides falsely claimed to have brought [these arts] from Greece. However, an ancient poet or musician, who had composed some melody for a poem, was obliged to teach others the correct rule of singing by his own living voice. He taught this without any help from musical notes, a practice that is well known to remain the custom throughout the East even now. The result was that all genuine melodies fell into oblivion shortly after the deaths of their authors. Thus, by the age of the Antonines <note>The Roman emperors from 138 to 192 AD, a period of high culture but far removed from classical Greece.</note>, there was no one who could sing any song of the ancient tragedies in the way that the *chorodidaskaloi* <note>term: chorodidaskaloi; literally "choir-teachers" or directors who trained the ancient Greek dramatic choruses</note> themselves had instructed.

Lest this seem a reckless claim on my part, I shall produce a notable passage from Dio Chrysostom, *Oratio XIX, 4*: <margin>10</margin> <note>original Greek: "τὸ μέντοι τῶν κιθαρῳδῶν..."</note> "Indeed, the performance of the cithara-singers and, by Zeus, the actors seems to me to differ not a little in terms of pleasure. For the voice is greater and clearly more careful, and the diction is not improvised—like that of the orators who mostly try to speak offhand—but is by poets who have composed with care and at leisure. <margin>15</margin> And most of their works are ancient and by much wiser men than those of today; all those of comedy; but of tragedy, the strong parts, as it seems, remain—I mean the iambics <note>the spoken or recited verses of Greek drama</note>—and they perform parts of these in the theaters; but the softer parts have flowed away, specifically the melodies. <margin>20</margin> Just as I think of old men: whatever parts of the body are solid remain through time, like the bones and the muscles, but the rest withers away." To this we may add the grammarian in Bekker’s *Anecdota page 752, 1*: "One must read lyric poetry with its melody, even if we have not inherited nor do we remember those melodies." Why else would <margin>25</margin> Apollonius of Alexandria, when arranging the poems of Pindar, occasionally hesitate in doubt regarding the melody of the songs? This could not have happened if the melodies had been recorded in notes.

<margin>30</margin> But to return the discourse to the passage in Plutarch from which it strayed: I believe Boeckh's interpretation should be rejected primarily because it poorly fits the connection of the ideas. For Heraclides says one must be careful not to think that the most ancient <margin>35</margin> bards—from Linus down to Phemius of Ithaca—wrote in "loose speech" <note>prose</note> without meter. On the contrary, their diction was like that of Terpander, Stesichorus, and other melic <note>lyric or musical</note> poets, which is to say, restricted to heroic verses. From this it is clear that any mention of musical notes is foreign to this context. Therefore, I think it simplest to translate these words as follows: *Terpander created melic songs from his own and Homer's heroic poems, which he then performed in public songs.* <margin>40</margin> Regarding this phrasing, cf. Plutarch, *On Listening to the Poets page 19, 41*: "Aeschylus clothed the whole tragedy in the myth, titling it *Psychostasia*," which means Aeschylus made the entire tragedy from this story, giving it the name *Psychostasia* <note>The "Weighing of Souls," a lost play by Aeschylus.</note>. Homer's poems, <margin>45</margin> however, became "melic" simply by being sung; strictly speaking, they were not sung to the cithara, but were recited by rhapsodes "upon a staff" <note>term: rhapsodes; professional reciters of epic poetry who traditionally held a staff (rhabdos) while performing</note>. If anyone objects to this interpretation—arguing that even if Terpander, Stesichorus, and others treated heroic themes musically like

---

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER 3.

melic songs, this does not refute the error of those who thought the diction of earlier bards was prose—perhaps these words should be understood this way: we might say Terpander and the others surrounded heroic verses with melic elements, such as the *basis*, *anacrusis*, and the *logaoedic* clause. <note>These are technical terms for ancient Greek rhythms: "basis" is a rhythmic step, "anacrusis" is a lead-in note, and "logaoedic" refers to a rhythm that mixes song-like and speech-like qualities.</note> This certainly applies to Stesichorus, if we may judge his metrical art from the surviving fragments.

**"He says that this man revealed the names"** <note>original Greek: "ἀποφῆναι δὲ τοῦτον λέγει ὀνόματα"</note>: See the notes at the beginning of the next chapter.
<margin>10</margin> **Clonas**: Except for Plutarch here and below, none of the ancients reported on Clonas. Polymnestus will be discussed in his own place. The words **"the first to establish the auloedic nomoi"** <note>original Greek: "τὸν πρῶτον συστησάμενον τοὺς αὐλῳδικοὺς νόμους"; songs performed to the flute</note> were correctly interpreted by Ulrici in *History of Greek Poetry Vol II, page 165*: "He elevated what was previously more a matter of custom and rule into an artistic <margin>15</margin> system and principle; he put together the individual parts and the entire structure of the *Nomoi*." Thus, according to this passage, it must be established that Clonas was the first to bring the art of flute-playing from Asia Minor to the Greeks in Europe; see Hoeck's *Crete III, page 376*.

<margin>20</margin> Furthermore, as to what a *Nomus* <note>term: Nomos; a "law" or "strain," a traditional melodic pattern or musical composition governed by strict rules</note> is, Suidas explained it best among the ancients under that entry: "A *nomos* is a citharoedic mode of melody, having a fixed harmony and a defined rhythm." After "citharoedic," the words "or auloedic" <note>flute-accompanied</note> have perhaps fallen out. The Scholiast on Aristophanes' *Knights v. 9* is less precise: "Hymns to the gods are called *nomoi*." Plutarch <margin>25</margin> himself comes closest to Suidas below in Chapter VI: "For they were called *nomoi* (laws) because it was not permitted to deviate from the established form of the pitch in each case." Bernhardy in *History of Greek Literature II, 1, page 554* showed that the stories told about the origin of the *nomos* in Aristotle's *Problems XIX, 28* are quite trivial: <margin>30</margin> "Why are the songs they sing called 'laws' (*nomoi*)? Is it because before men knew how to write, they sang the laws [legal codes] so as not to forget them, as is still the custom among the Agathyrsians? And so they called the first of the later songs by the same name as the first [laws]." No less false are the things Photius transmitted from Proclus on page 523: "The *nomos* is written for Apollo and takes its name from him; for Apollo was called <margin>35</margin> *Nomimus* (or *Nomius*)." If this were true, what should be concluded about the *auloedic nomoi*? These certainly could not have taken their name from the god Apollo, who despised the use of flutes <note>A reference to the myth of Apollo and Marsyas, where Apollo flayed the satyr Marsyas for challenging him with a flute.</note>, especially since it can by no means be proven that the name was later transferred from citharoedic strains to auloedic ones. Thus, Bernhardy correctly said <margin>40</margin> of the *nomoi*: "They are, as the name implies, an expression of what possessed normal value in moral, unwritten tradition and consciousness." Regarding the poetic form of the *nomoi*, two things must be held besides their extreme simplicity. First, it is certain that antistrophic composition <note>a structure of alternating stanzas used in choral lyrics</note> was foreign to them. <margin>45</margin> See Aristotle's *Problems XIX, 15*: "Why were the *nomoi* not made in antistrophes, while other choral songs were?" cf. Boeckh *On Pindaric Meter page 201*. In general, *nomoi* were sung only by individuals (they belonged to the "competitors" [*agonistae*], not the "dancers" [*choreutae*]), and never, except by Timotheus...

It also defines the "Nomos" as a structured musical strain and explores the roles of Clonas and Terpander in establishing these musical "laws."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on ancient musical theory, moving from the general history of musical performance to the technical classification of "Nomoi" (laws or traditional melodies). It bridges the discussion of Chapter 3’s conclusion on the origins of styles and begins the detailed breakdown of Chapter 4’s specific musical terms and inventors, such as Terpander.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS III—IV.

...only at their age, by the chorus. From this, it is evident why the ancients established three types or tropes of *melopoeia* <note>term: melopoeia; the art or practice of composing melody</note>: the dithyrambic, the nomic, and the tragic (Aristides Quintilianus, p. 30). Furthermore, until the age of Phrynis, the *nomoi* were restricted to only one meter: either the heroic (as were almost all the cithara-singing ones), or the elegiac, or the trochaic, or any other single kind. But they never allowed a variety of meters [within a single piece]. Similarly, they exhibited only one harmony—the Lydian, it seems, above the others—which I gather from Proclus’s words, interpreted thus: 
> <note>original Greek: οὐ μὴν ἀλλὰ καὶ ταῖς ἁρμονίαις οἰκείαις ἑκάτερος... Λυδίῳ.</note> 
> "Moreover, each [the nomus and the dithyramb] uses its own proper harmonies; for the one [the dithyramb] is adapted to the Phrygian and Hypophrygian modes, while the *nomus* is adapted to the Lydian system of the cithara-singers."

In later times, however, when the composition of *nomoi* began to fall into disuse among poets, those called *nomoi* were nothing other than sacred chants and melodies similar to those we call chorales. Various poems were sung according to their standard, or they were performed by solo flutes or strings without any vocal accompaniment—just as it seems the Pythic *nomos* and the Cradias were from the earliest times (see Hoeck, *Cretica* III, p. 382, note). But that the melodies of the *nomoi* were not always sacred and lofty is proven by the flute-nomus which the Greeks called the **hippothoron** <note>term: hippothoron; literally "stallion-leaping," a tune played during the mating of horses</note>, regarding which one should consult Plutarch (*Marriage Precepts* c. 1; *Table Talk* VII, p. 704 F), Clement of Alexandria (*The Instructor* II, p. 164 A), Aelian (*History of Animals* XII, 44), and the interpreters of Hesychius under this word. The disputes among musicologists such as Forkel (*History of Music* I, p. 212), following Burette and Marpurg (*Critical Introduction* p. 19), regarding the *nomoi* are of no value. Athanasius Kircher, in his usual manner, mixed truth with falsehood in his *Musurgia* I, p. 535. Furthermore, a passage comes to mind from a fragment following Censorinus, chapter IX (recently treated by L. Ulrichs in *Rheinisches Museum* N. XI, p. 160, who ordered the words *an non* to be deleted): "immediately modulated songs—yet you should not think the songs are older than the rhythms, but rather rustic and unrefined; and after these, the legitimate poetic art prevailed, with music becoming more free and more modulated"—where perhaps we should read: *nor however should you think the Nomoi existed before...*

**and the prosodia** The *prosodia* were a certain species of Hymns, songs accompanied by the flute (see Proclus c. 9, followed by the author of the *Etymologicum Magnum* under the words "hymnos" and "prosodiai," and Orion p. 155). Didymus explained the origin of the name in his book **on the lyric poets**, from which the details reported by Proclus flow (see M. Schmidt on Didymus, p. 391): 
> <note>original Greek: ἐπειδὰν προσίασι τοῖς βωμοῖς ἢ ναοῖς... ἑστώτων.</note> 
> "whenever they approach the altars or temples, [the song] was sung to the flute during the approach (*prosienai*); whereas the hymn, strictly speaking, was sung to the cithara while they stood still." 
Cf. Bernhardy II, 1 p. 560, 564.

## CHAPTER IV.

**Apothetos, Elegos** Commonly **Elegoi**. The name of the Elegies, which Franke (in *Callinus* p. 121) deleted in this passage on the grounds that it incorrectly refers to only more recent times. In Pausanias, *Elegoi* are even attributed to Ardalo. In general, the name is very ancient and of Asiatic 

---

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER IV.</header>

origin, about which Bernhardy has already remarked (II, 1 p. 399) and Boetticher has recently spoken more accurately in the *Marburg Annals* (1853, XI p. 88, comparing *Aricia* p. 34 n. 21). Boetticher derives the related words from the Armenian language and explains **elegeia** as a song performed to the instrument called the *elegn*. However, it must be conceded to Franke that the word **Elegoi** cannot be used in the plural here. Properly, the *Elegus* seems to have been nothing other than a flute-nomus (played either with or without singing) of a mournful character (<gloss>the *elegos* is a lament</gloss>, Orion p. 58). The words adapted to this melody seem to have been composed in the elegiac distich, so that later, distichs in general—even without the accompaniment of flutes—were called **elegeia**. Regarding the *Comarchium*, I would hardly agree with Burette that it took its name from "revelry" (*comessatio*) (Suidas defines *komos* as a "drunken flute-tune that provokes luxury while the wine lingers").

**Tenedius** I have written this with the approval of Burette, based on Amyot’s translation in which *Tenedius* is read. The manuscripts read **Leios**, which is nonsensical. That the island of Tenedos had some connection with flutes is to be gathered from the proverb *a Tenedian flute-player* found in Stephanus of Byzantium.

**Polymnasteia** I would prefer **Polymnasteia**.

**he was the first to name the citharodic [laws]** The manuscripts read "previously" (*proteron*), which I believe was repeated from the preceding lines through the negligence of scribes. It should be written "first" (*protos*). Thus, above in Chapter 3: "he says that this man [Terpander] first assigned names to the citharodic laws," i.e., Terpander first gave each citharodic law its own name. Cf. Pollux IV, 65: 
> "The laws of Terpander are named after nations from which they came, such as the Aeolian and Boeotian; or from rhythms, such as the Orthios and Trochaic; or from styles, such as the Sharp (*Oxys*) and the Four-song (*Tetraoidos*); or from himself and his beloved, such as the Terpandreios and the Capion." 
Suidas has similar entries under "nomos."

**Boeotian** By what right this *nomos*, which in the time of Aristophanes was circulated under the Boeotian name (see Scholia on Aristophanes, *Acharnians* v. 14), was attributed to Terpander is doubtful, provided you attend to the arguments of Lobeck (*Aglaophamus* p. 306). This *nomos* was originally calm and quiet, but had more rapid rhythms toward the end of the verse. Cf. the somewhat more obscure traditions in Zenobius II, 65 with Leutsch’s note. Suidas (under the word **Moschos**) attributed the invention of the Boeotian and Phrygian *nomoi*—of which no other mention is made—to Terpander, although Michael Apostolius (XI, 74) presents the text slightly differently: "Terpander found the so-called Boeotian [law], just as the Phrygian found the Phrygian [law]."

**The Sharp (Oxys)** I do not follow how this *nomos* took its name from a "trope" (i.e., from a tone or harmony) according to the words of Pollux just cited. Burette interprets it as follows:
> "The Sharp Air (**Oxys**) was played on the high and most acute sounds of the cithara, that is to say, in the Lydian mode, or in some other of the higher modes; which made it suitable for exciting the most vivid passions."

Cf. Aristotle, *Problems* XIX, 37: "It is more of an effort to sing high notes (*oxea*) than low ones, and few are able to sing the upper range; the *Orthioi* and *Oxeis* laws are difficult to sing because of their intense pitch." However, there were generally only a few sounds available on the cithara. Nor is it probable that the name...

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER IV.

...taken from the rhythm. For "sharp" rhythms are considered base, if you listen to Dio Chrysostom (Orat. II, 56), who lists these as signs of base and corrupt music: 
> "the shrill songs of flutes, lawless rhythms, and melodies broken by unmusical turns and the complexities of many-toned instruments." 
<note>original Greek: αὐλημάτων τε ὀξεῖς καὶ παρανόμους ῥυθμοὺς καὶ κατεαγότα μέλη ἀμούσοις καμπαῖς καὶ πολυφώνων ὀργάνων ποικιλίαις</note> 
On the other hand,
<margin>5</margin> the **Trochaeus** <note>term: Trochaeus; a "tripping" or "running" melody, named after the trochaic foot (long-short)</note> was named after the rhythm, according to the testimony of Pollux and Suidas. Therefore, we must reject the opinion of Wernsdorf (in his notes on Himerius, p. 810): "While musicians could play either more gently and carelessly, or more intensely, with more speed and greater art, they called the former the **Trochaeus**—the melody that runs along lightly and commonly—while the latter they called
<margin>10</margin> the **Orthius**—the one sounding more upright or erect—which they used whenever they wished to display a specimen of their art." Furthermore, one might wonder why Plutarch, in this passage, omitted the **Orthius** <note>term: Orthius; meaning "upright" or "high-pitched," a stirring, vigorous style of melody</note> *nomos* <note>term: nomos; a traditional melodic pattern or "law" of composition</note>, about which Hesychius teaches: "the *Orthius nomos*; they say it was so named from its rhythm, just as the **Trochaeus** was." 
<margin>15</margin> And Suidas says: "the *Orthius* and *Trochaeus nomoi*. Terpander named these two *nomoi* after their rhythms; they were high-strung and intense." (See the words of Aristotle just praised), though nothing certain about their rhythm can be gathered from these words. But Plutarch could not name the *Orthius nomos* in this place because it was 
<margin>20</margin> for the flute <note>term: tibialis; relating to the tibia or aulos, a reed instrument similar to an oboe</note> and not adapted for singing with the lyre. See the Scholiast on Aristophanes's *Acharnians*, line 16: "the *Orthius nomos*—an intense flute-melody characterized by a high pitch." Cf. Blomfield, Glossary to Aeschylus's *Persians*, line 395. And it was not even invented by Terpander, as will be discussed below. Moreover, since 
<margin>25</margin> the words of Suidas just quoted are immediately followed by these words of Homer from *Iliad* Book 11, line 10:
> "There the goddess stood and cried out loudly and terribly,
> high and shrill"
<note>original Greek: ἔνθα στᾶσ’ ἤυσε θεὰ μέγα τε δεινόν τε ὄρθι’</note>

I personally believe that Suidas’s account is incomplete, and that the *nomos* was called **Orthius** not because of its rhythm, but because of these 
<margin>30</margin> very verses of Homer: for it was composed in honor of Minerva <note>The Roman name for Athena</note>, as is established elsewhere; see Suidas under the entries "Alexander" and "Orthiasmata." It also bore her name according to Dio Chrysostom (Orat. I, 1): "the *Orthius* melody, called the *nomos* of Athena." For if it took its name from the rhythm, I truly do not understand what 
<margin>35</margin> could have been the difference between it and the *Trochaeus nomos*: for the *orthius* rhythm was the same as the *trochaeus*, except that it was performed with a slower movement, which the Greeks call *agoge* and our people call by the Italian word 
**tempo**. A slow movement was solemn and characteristic of almost all *nomoi*. It is well known that the warlike **Orthius** melody was used by Timotheus to incite Alexander 
<margin>40</margin> to battle, a story which our own Handel used brilliantly to celebrate the force and power of music in his composition <note>Handel's "Alexander's Feast," 1736</note>, which he adapted from Dryden’s "Ode for St. Cecilia's Day." This *nomos* was sung particularly well by **Chacris** or **Chares**, a flute-player of Thebes, about whom one should consult Leutsch in 
<margin>45</margin> *Paroemiographi* Vol. I, p. 462. Furthermore, **Nicopheles**, I believe the same Theban, is reported by Pollux (IV, 77) to have used a unique kind of flute called the "Athena" when singing the *nomos* of Minerva.
**Tetraoedius** <note>term: Tetraoedius; literally "four-song" or "four-part" melody</note>] This appears to Boeckh to have been structured similarly

---

to the *trimeles* <note>term: trimeles; a "three-part" melody</note>, a flute-song *nomos* of Sacadas, whose first stanza was Dorian, the second Phrygian, and the third Lydian, as he judges in *On the Meters of Pindar*, Vol. III, p. 251. This is roughly the same as the opinion of Burette." Plehn, *Lesbiaca*, p. 158. This opinion is supported by the 
<margin>5</margin> words of Pollux: "and from the modes [are] the *Oxys* and the *Tetraoedius*."
**Prooemia** <note>term: Prooemia; preludes or musical introductions</note>] *Prooemia* are certain minor musical introductions which were sung at sacred festivals. From collections of these, F. A. Wolf believed that the mass of Homeric Hymns originated (in *Hesiod's Theogony*, p. 60, cf. *Prolegomena*, p. CVI ff.).
<margin>10</margin> The first verse from a *prooemium* of Terpander for the *Orthius nomos*:
> "Let my mind sing again of the far-darting King"
<note>original Greek: ἀμφί μοι αὖτις ἀνάκτ’ ἑκαταβόλον ᾀδέτω ἡ φρήν</note>

has been preserved by the Scholiast on Aristophanes's *Clouds*, line 591, and Suidas under the entry "Amphianaktizein" <note>To sing a prelude to the King (Apollo)</note>. Cf. Bergk, *Poetae Lyrici*, p. 537. Now, *prooemia* seem to have been proper to the art of singing with the cithara <note>term: citharoedica; the art of singing while playing the cithara lyre</note>; the art of singing with the flute <note>term: auloedice</note> had the **pronomia** and 
<margin>15</margin> **proaulia** or **proaulias** <note>flute-preludes</note>, see Wernsdorf on Himerius, p. 460.
**Timotheus**] A distinction must be made between two musicians of the same name, both highly famous. The one discussed here is **Timotheus of Miletus** (or Melos), son of Thersander, a singer to the cithara and a poet of dithyrambs <note>term: dithyramb; a wild, choral hymn, usually to Dionysus</note>, who authors say died around the 106th Olympiad, 1st year (356 BC). 
<margin>20</margin> (Alexander Aetolus in Macrobius V, 22; Stephanus of Byzantium under "Miletos"; and Suidas). Cf. Bernhardy Vol. II, part I, p. 673. Following Phrynis, Cinesias, and his rival Polyidus (see Athenaeus VIII, p. 352 B), he is counted among the most outstanding innovators of Greek music, so much so that he is said to have increased the number of strings on the lyre. 
<margin>25</margin> Although his innovations won the applause of almost all Greece, they were initially disapproved of in both Athens and Sparta. This gave rise to the legend that the Spartans even took away Timotheus's lyre and cut off the "superfluous" strings (see Dio Chrysostom, Orat. XXXIII, 57; Artemon in Athenaeus XIV, p. 636 E; on which cf. Müller, *Dorians*, Vol. II, p. 317 ff.). 
<margin>30</margin> Besides the dithyrambs themselves, he composed eighteen books of *nomoi* for the cithara, according to Stephanus of Byzantium, which closely approached the loose rhythms of the dithyrambs. This can be gathered not only from the words of Clement of Alexandria (*Stromata* I, p. 365): "Timotheus of Miletus was the first to sing *nomoi* with a chorus and cithara" <note>The author notes: "l. πρῶτος" meaning "read 'first' [singular] instead of 'first' [plural]" in the Greek text</note> 
<margin>35</margin> (for since the age of Chrysothemis, *nomoi* were not intended for use by choruses), but also from Hephaestion, p. 66: "Released works are those written at random and without a fixed meter; such as are the cithara-singing *nomoi* of Timotheus." Therefore, Timotheus’s *nomoi* were released from the firm bond of meters, 
<margin>40</margin> composed without a certain or constant rhythm-making, so that the music seems to have prevailed over the poetic—or at least the metrical—composition, a path which had been blazed by Phrynis, who will be discussed below.
The other **Timotheus** is the flute-player from Thebes (or Boeotia). He was present at the wedding of Alexander the Great, who held him in the highest regard; he 
<margin>45</margin> was very influential in composing for or stirring up the spirit of the king (see Suidas under "Alexander"; Dio Chrysostom, Orat. I, 1; Himerius, *Eclogue* XVII, 4; and what was set forth above regarding the *Orthius nomos*). Without doubt, Stephanus of Byzantium, through an error, attributed a thousand words to the greater Timotheus that belonged to the lesser...

<summary>This page analyzes the origins and naming of ancient Greek musical modes (nomoi), specifically the "Trochaeus" and "Orthius" styles, while distinguishing between two famous musicians named Timotheus. It highlights the transition of the nomos from a strictly metered form to a more rhythmically free composition influenced by the dithyramb.</summary>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER IV.

...he adapted for the flute the introductions <note>original: prooemiorum</note> originally intended for the lyre, though he had nothing at all to do with flutes. However, this error is easily forgiven, since Timotheus of Thebes <note>A celebrated flute-player, often confused with Timotheus of Miletus</note> seems to have often sung certain pieces by the Milesian <note>original: Milesii; referring to Timotheus of Miletus, the famous innovator of the "New Music"</note> to the accompaniment of the flute, even though they were perhaps intended by the author for the lyre alone; concerning this, compare Lucian's *Harmonides*, chapter 1, page 852.

<margin>5</margin> **mixing in dithyrambic diction, he sang** <note>original Greek: διαμιγνύων διθυραμβικὴν λέξιν ᾔδεν</note> Some interpreters have mistakenly taken these words to mean that Timotheus preceded his *dithyrambs* <note>term: dithyramb; a passionate choral hymn, often dedicated to Dionysus</note> or his dithyrambic *nomoi* with introductions written in heroic verse. In fact, Plutarch wishes to prove from Timotheus's poems that the *nomoi* themselves—as is also <margin>10</margin> clear from other ancient testimonies—were not only restricted to hexameter verse in their introductions in ancient times. Kaltwasser also erred by translating: "he mixed the earlier *nomoi* in heroic verses into his dithyrambic poems." Rather, this is Plutarch's meaning: that Timotheus combined dithyrambic subject matter and diction with the traditional heroic form of the *nomoi*. This was <margin>15</margin> not so that his dithyrambs would proceed mostly in heroic meter (since they ought to have been composed in trochaic tetrameters; see Aristotle's *Poetics* 4 and *Rhetoric* III; compare Welcker's *Epilogue on the Aeschylean Trilogy*), but rather so that he might make the *nomoi* <margin>20</margin> very similar to dithyrambs; see the note above. As for what Genellius gathered from these words regarding the Athenian theater (that dithyrambs were dactylic <note>term: dactylic; a meter associated with epic poetry (long-short-short)</note> until the age of Timotheus), Geppert has refuted this, rightly seeing that these words prove that dactylic rhythm was not foreign or contrary to <margin>25</margin> dithyrambic composition, but was not its defining characteristic.

**he was at least older than Archilochus** <note>original Greek: πρεσβύτερον γοῦν αὐτὸν Ἀρχιλόχου</note> Phanias of Eresus (cited in Clement of Alexandria's *Stromata* I) disagrees with this opinion of Glaucus of Rhegium regarding the age of Terpander <note>An early 7th-century BCE poet/musician from Lesbos</note>, which Alexander approved in chapter five. Phanias <margin>30</margin> makes Terpander later in age than Archilochus <note>A famous Greek lyric poet known for his iambic verse</note>. But greater trust should be placed in Glaucus as the older writer. To be sure, Hellanicus (cited in Athenaeus XIV) says that Terpander was the first to win at the Carneian Games celebrated at Sparta. <margin>35</margin> But these points are trivial, since the same Hellanicus—an otherwise insufficiently accurate author—recorded Terpander as living during the legendary times of King Midas (according to Clement). Furthermore, O. Mueller erred when he misused Hellanicus's words in Athenaeus to assert that Terpander lived around the 26th Olympiad. For it was actually Sosibius the Laconian, the chronographer, who <margin>40</margin> traced the origin of the Carneian Games to that period, not Hellanicus, who it is agreed set a much more ancient origin for the games. Therefore, nothing prevents us from siding with Glaucus regarding Terpander's age, especially since Terpander—according to what the ancients recorded of him—comes much closer to that age when <margin>45</margin> heroic song flourished among the Greeks than does Archilochus. This did not escape the notice of Bernhardy, a subtle and most accurate judge of such matters (*History of Greek Literature* I, p. 301). Certainly, Ter-

---

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS IV—V.

pander is regarded as the first melic poet <note>term: melic poetry; lyric poetry intended to be sung to a musical instrument</note>, but legendary Greece indicates that his lyric poetry differed little from epic by the fact that it called Terpander a relative of Homer and Hesiod. Furthermore, if some maintain it was possible for Terpander the Aeolian to remain without any contact with Archilochus the Ionian, <margin>5</margin> even if he were older than him, they act with little consideration. For Boeckh rightly says (*Corpus Inscriptionum Graecarum* II): "you may gather that Terpander had some connection with flutes (that is, with the Asiatic art which Archilochus later cultivated above all others) from the fact that Glaucus of Rhegium said in Plutarch's *On Music*, chapter 4, that he [Terpander] <margin>10</margin> **was the second after the first creators of singing to the flute**." <note>original Greek: δεύτερον γενέσθαι μετὰ τοὺς πρώτους ποιήσαντας αὐλῳδίαν</note> Thus, a middle ground must be held between Ritschl's opinion on Terpander (in the *Halle Encyclopedia* under "Olympus") and the arguments Hoeck disputed in the opposite direction in *Creta* III.

**the first creators of singing to the flute** <note>original Greek: τοὺς πρώτους ποιήσαντας αὐλῳδίαν</note> <margin>15</margin> He means Olympus and his Phrygian musical disciples; see Bernhardy I, p. 296.

### CHAPTER V.

**Alexander** <note>original Greek: Ἀλέξανδρος</note> This is Alexander Cornelius, a <margin>20</margin> freedman of Cornelius Lentulus, surnamed Polyhistor, from Miletus (according to Suidas). He is distinct from Alexander of Cotyaeum, the son of Asclepiades and teacher of the Emperor Marcus Antoninus. He was a Pergamene grammarian and called a disciple of Crates, though this can hardly be reconciled with the chronology. He lived in Rome during the times of Sulla and afterward. Compare Karl Mueller's <margin>25</margin> *Fragments of the Greek Historians*, Vol. III, where the errors of Vossius, Jonston, Clinton, and others are refuted. The pseudo-Plutarch in *On Rivers* (X, 1) cited the third book of Alexander’s *Phrygian History*.

**instrumental pieces of Olympus** <note>original Greek: κρούματα Ὄλυμπον</note> The word *kroumata* <note>term: kroumata; literally "beatings" or "strikings," referring to instrumental music played on strings or percussion</note> should not be understood only as refers to stringed instruments <margin>30</margin> introduced to Greece, which Ritschl perversely contended was the only truth. Rather, *kroumata* refers either to cymbals and rattles, which Cassiodorus called *crumata* and Martial called *crusmata* (his "Baetic *crusmata*" were wooden rattles which are today called *cascabillos*); this is likely <margin>35</margin> because of the added mention of the Idaean Dactyls <note>Mythical beings associated with the discovery of iron and rhythmic music</note>; see Hoeck's *Creta* I. I do not understand "instrumental music" in Suidas any differently: "becoming a leader of instrumental music and of music through flutes." Or, they refer to the art of the flute, <margin>40</margin> for the sounds of flutes were sometimes called *kroumata*; see Plutarch's *Table Talk* II, 4: "For newer things fittingly enjoy the names belonging to older things. Just as they say the flute is 'tuned,' and they call flute-pieces *kroumata*, taking the names from the lyre." In Pollux VII, <margin>45</margin> *kroumata* in flute-playing are mentioned, just as in IV, 84 there are "trumpet *kroumata*," and a "maker of *kroumata*" is a flute-player in Athenaeus VIII. One can see the reason for this phrase from its etymology...

It also defines technical terms like "kroumata" (instrumental sounds) and identifies Alexander Polyhistor as a key historical source for these musical traditions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the historical origins of musical forms, transitioning from the discussion of the "nomoi" (traditional musical laws) in Chapter IV to the legendary figures associated with the invention of specific instruments and styles in Chapter V. # COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER V.

E. Hoffmann, in *Homeros* p. 52 n. 61, has rendered this as derived from etymology. In general, then, <term>kroumata</term> <note>original Greek: κρούματα; literally "strikes" or "beats"</note> are whatever sounds are produced by musical instruments of any kind, though most often they refer to the sounds of strings. However, regarding the migration of stringed instruments from Asia into Greece, the ancients handed down only a few <margin>5</margin> and contradictory accounts. The things Ritschl said about this passage seem too elaborate and false: that the younger Olympus was concerned with string music, but that it was Pratinas who finally distinguished between the "Greater Olympus" and the "Lesser Olympus," not Alexander [Polyhistor], who recognized only one Phrygian Olympus. Because of the <margin>10</margin> extreme antiquity of stringed instruments in Greece, Alexander could easily have been led into the error of stating that these, too, had been brought into Greece by that famous Olympus.

**The Idaean Dactyls** <note>original Greek: Ἰδαίους Δακτύλους; legendary metalworkers and magicians from Mount Ida</note>] Compare Solinus, *Polyhistor* ch. 11: "the study of music began there, when the Idaean Dactyls translated the melodies <margin>15</margin> they had discovered through the clashing and ringing of bronze into rhythmic order," and Isidore, *Etymologies* <note>original: Origg.</note> XI, 6: "the study of music began with the Idaean Dactyls." Diodorus comes closest to these, saying that the Curetes <note>mythical attendants of the infant Zeus</note> taught the Greeks music, poetry, and the art of dancing. Concerning the Idaean Dactyls themselves, if anyone desires more, let him consult the previously cited work <margin>20</margin> and Lobeck in *Aglaophamus* p. 1156 and following. They are also said to have been most skilled in music in the Townley Scholia on the *Iliad* X, 391.

**then Olympus** <note>original Greek: εἶτ’ Ὄλυμπον</note>] This family tree—Olympus son of Marsyas, who was son of Hyagnis—was well known to antiquity; see Muncker on Hyginus 165 and Brunck on Aristophanes' *Knights* line 9. One exception is Apollodorus <margin>25</margin> I, 4, 2, who made Olympus the father of Marsyas; compare Clavier on this passage and Burman on Ovid's *Metamorphoses* VI, 400, unless in this passage too "father" <note>original: πατέρα</note> should be read instead of "son" <note>original: παῖδα</note>. I owe this note to Ritschl in the *Halle Encyclopedia* in the place cited.

**the songs of Orpheus** <note>original Greek: Ὀρφέως δὲ τὰ μέλη</note>] What is meant by this—that Terpander <margin>30</margin> is said to have imitated the lyric poetry of Orpheus—becomes clear by considering what is narrated about Terpander's life. Namely, Terpander, the son of Derdenes (<term>Derdenes</term> <note>original Greek: Δερδένεος</note> in the Parian Marble, epoch 35; on the form of the name see Boeckh, *Corpus Inscriptionum Graecarum* II p. 316b), was reported to be a Lesbian from Antissa. If Tzetzes in *Chiliades* 1, 16, appealing to Diodorus, <margin>35</margin> calls him a Methymnaean, this should be attributed to the negligence of the Byzantine compiler. Why we should doubt that Terpander truly lived at some point, I see no reason; for as far as the name is concerned, which tastes somewhat of symbolism <note>the name Terpander suggests "delighting men"</note>, see Bernhardy, *History of Greek Literature* I p. 299; this is a trivial point. Now, if it is handed down that he obtained the lyre of Orpheus <margin>40</margin> and imitated his poems, I attribute this to Terpander's origin from a certain Lesbian clan which transmitted the musical art from father to son through a kind of inheritance. Indeed, the most ancient Lesbian colonies had come from Boeotia, and they brought the ancient music of the Thracians with them from there; see what I said above <margin>45</margin> in Chapter III concerning Amphion. Legend testifies to this by recounting that the head and lyre of Orpheus, after he was torn apart by women, were carried by the waves of the sea to Antissa (Phanocles, *Elegies* fragment 1). That this lyre later came into the hands of Terpander is a story which Nicomachus expanded with absurd comments

<column-break/>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER V.</header>

in *Manual of Harmonics* II p. 29. Therefore, we see Terpander joined by a close bond with the old Thracian discipline of music, so that we may also understand why Terpander was considered by some to be a Boeotian from Arne or a Cumean, for which Suidas is the authority under the entry <term>Terpander</term> <note>original Greek: Τέρπανδρος</note>. However, Bernhardy used this testimony of Alexander [Polyhistor] differently in *History of Greek Literature* II, 1 p. 371.

**Archilochus is handed down to have been** <note>original Greek: Ἀρχίλοχος παραδίδοται γενέσθαι</note>] Whatever in this chapter pertains to chronological calculations is unstable and uncertain. Here, then, <margin>10</margin> Archilochus is presented as later in age than Terpander and Clonas; see what we discussed concerning Terpander's age at the end of Chapter IV. Strabo (IV, 647) and Clement of Alexandria (*Stromata* p. 397) make him more recent than Callinus, but the age of all these figures is doubtful. Nor do other sources help us, such as Clement (in the same work, p. 347) <margin>15</margin> placing him among the contemporaries of Simonides of Amorgos, or Plutarch saying below that he lived before Thaletas, or finally Eusebius placing him around the 29th Olympiad. Concerning all these, see Liebel, *Fragments of Archilochus* p. 8, 11.

**Ardalus of Troezen** <note>original Greek: Ἄρδαλον Τροιζήνιον</note>] Even regarding the age of Ardalus, the ancients seem <margin>20</margin> to have hesitated, inasmuch as they thought two men of the same name should be guarded against confusion: the first was a mythical son of Vulcan, who was said to have brought the cult of the Muses to Troezen (concerning whom see Mueller, *Dorians* I p. 344 and following); the second was a more recent figure, a contemporary of Thaletas, about whom Plutarch argued in *The Banquet of the Seven Sages* p. <margin>25</margin> 150 A: "Ardalus was a Troezenian, a flute-singer and priest of the Ardalid Muses, whom the ancient Ardalus the Troezenian had established." <note>original Greek: ἦν δὲ Τροιζήνιος ὁ Ἄρδαλος, αὐλῳδὸς καὶ ἱερεὺς τῶν Ἀγδαλέων Μουσῶν, ἃς ὁ παλαιὸς Ἄρδαλος ἱδρύσατο ὁ Τροιζήνιος</note> Pausanias (II, 31, 3) agrees, recording that Ardalus was considered by some to be the inventor of the flute. See Wyttenbach, *Animadversiones* II, 224 (934). In this passage, Plutarch is speaking of the earlier Ardalus.

<margin>30</margin> **Polymnestus the poet** <note>original Greek: Πολύμνηστον ποιητήν</note>] Regarding Polymnestus as well, no more certain facts can be known than what Plutarch provides. The Lexicographers testify to his Colophonian origin. His age falls between Thaletas and Alcman, the latter of whom made mention of Polymnestus in his poems. For the Spartans, he composed a heroic poem in <margin>35</margin> honor of Thaletas (Pausanias I, 14, 4). Bernhardy, in *History of Greek Literature* I p. 325, shows that it was only through error that some grammarians attributed "lascivious and playful" poetry to him because of certain light-hearted jokes by comic poets; see also his entry in Suidas II, 2. 347. Nevertheless, because of these very words of the comic poets, I <margin>40</margin> maintain that the Athenians had found out very little regarding his true and genuine poems. Indeed, the things read in this passage concerning "Polymnestus" and "Polymnesta" as if they were names of coins <note>original: numorum</note> are corrupt. If I see clearly, these words allow for no other interpretation than that of Amyot's translation: "and that there was also <margin>45</margin> a Polymnestus the poet, son of Meles of Colophon, who with a woman named Polymnesta made the laws of the flutes" <note>original French: et qu’ il y eut aussi un Polymnestus poëte, fils de Meles Colophonien, qui avec une femme nommée Polymneste fit les loix des flustes</note>—which is certainly ridiculous. But the things Burette reasons in Vol. XIV p. 419 are false: "Plutarch, above, speaks of those airs which he names..."

<summary>This page analyzes the mythical and historical figures credited with the origins of Greek music, focusing on the lineage of Olympus, the Idaean Dactyls, and the connections between Terpander and Thracian Orphism. It also attempts to resolve chronological confusion surrounding the poets Archilochus, Ardalus, and Polymnestus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Chapter V of Plutarch's "On Music," focusing on the specific "nomoi" (traditional musical laws or compositions) and the historical figures of the second "establishment" (katastasis) of Spartan music. It transitions into Chapter VI, which discusses the perceived decline or "corruption" of music beginning with Phrynis.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER V.</header>

Polymnastian compositions (called *ta Polymnastia* <note>original Greek: τὰ Πολυμνάστια καλούμενα</note>) where one must understand "melodies" <note>original: μέλη</note> or "songs" <note>original: ᾄσματα</note> to be implied. This naming convention suggests that Polymnestus had not composed just a single air, but that there were several of his creation. Indeed, all the authors who cite the works of this musician call them *Polymnastia*. <margin>5</margin> It appears, then, from this passage in Plutarch, that these "nomoi" <note>musical laws or standard melodies</note> of Polymnestus were two in number; one of which was called the *Polymnestos* and the other the *Polymneste*.? For who, I ask, could have a musical law named "Polymneste"? For it is tiresome to describe the fabrications of Burette <note>a reference to the 18th-century French scholar Pierre-Jean Burette</note> regarding this matter. <margin>10</margin> I indeed believe it should be written: "that he [Polymnestus] created both the *Polymnestos* and the *Polymnistion* law."

**That the "Apothetos" law and the "Schoenion"]** Both of these musical laws were also attributed to Terpander <note>the semi-legendary father of Greek music</note> by mistake, a view which Pollux <note>an 2nd-century Greek grammarian</note> (IV, 65) disputes thus: "those who also attribute the *Apothetos* and the *Schoenion* to him are mistaken; for these are for the flute (*auletic*)." <margin>15</margin> This is correct; for the consensus among the learned men of antiquity was that Terpander composed only "citharoedic" laws (for the lyre and voice), not "auletic" ones (for the flute), whatever more recent writers may say to the contrary. It is certainly possible that they were later performed with the accompaniment <margin>20</margin> of flutes as well—consider "cithara-playing flutes" <note>original Latin: *aulos kitharisterios*</note>—though even this is not proven by any ancient testimony; see Plehn’s *Lesbiaca*, p. 161. This was also the reason why no mention was made of the *Orthian* law by Plutarch above, which the lexicographers should not have attributed to Terpander, a point discussed in the notes to that passage. No other evidence for these <margin>25</margin> laws has come to light, except that Hesychius and Pollux call the *Schoenion* an auletic law; Casaubon <note>Isaac Casaubon, the famous Renaissance scholar</note> (commenting on Athenaeus XIV, p. 894) thought it was so named because it was "broken" or "effeminate" in its dance style. I recall Thiersch somewhere attributing the name of the *Schoenion* to dithyrambs <note>choral hymns to Dionysus</note>, though his words are not at hand. <margin>30</margin> This is hardly correct. What if one were to think of the Boeotian city of Schoenus? Regarding the *Apothetos*, Burette (Vol. XIV, p. 353) has this to say: "to stick to the literal Greek term <note>original: *apotheton* means 'set aside' or 'hidden'</note>, it could be an air of distinction, which was not lavished on every occasion, and which was kept in reserve for great festivals or ceremonies of splendor." *Which may be true.* <margin>35</margin>

**And Pindar [mentions] Polymnestus]** See Strabo XIV, p. 643: "Pindar also speaks of a certain Polymnestus among those famous in music... 'You have known the voice, common to all, of Polymnestus, the Colophonian man.'" Pindar, fragment 163 (Bergk).

**And Alcman — mentioned [him]]** O. Müller (*The Dorians* II, p. 315) <margin>40</margin> hesitated at the mention of Polymnestus made by Alcman, and preferred to write "Alcaeus." He argued that Alcman lived around the 27th Olympiad <note>c. 672 BCE</note> and therefore could not have mentioned Polymnestus, whom Plutarch made a contemporary of Sacadas around the 48th Olympiad <note>c. 588 BCE</note>. This learned man spoke in vain. <margin>45</margin> Rather, it should be established that a new piece of evidence arises from this passage for the opinion of those chronographers who placed Alcman at the 47th Olympiad, which is a well-known matter. Thus, below in Chapter XII, the "Alcmanic innovation" is mentioned only after those things which

---

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS V—VI.</header>

Polymnestus, Thaletas, and Sacadas had innovated in music. In general, if anyone should contend that the "second establishment" <note>original Greek: *katastasis*; a period of musical reform in Sparta</note> of music at Sparta preceded the age of Alcman, I would not object.

**They say Philammon — established [this]]** Suidas <note>a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> refers to this opinion under the entry *Terpander*: <margin>5</margin> "he was the first to write lyric laws, even if some wish to say Philammon wrote them." It is helpful to add the words of Plehn (*Lesbiaca*, p. 158): "but these stories, especially those told of Chrysothemis, I say are no better than fables: if we consider the certain and investigated accounts of the ancients, we must conclude <margin>10</margin> that Terpander was the first to apply a fixed and constant melody to the citharoedic laws he himself composed." Chrysothemis the Cretan, however, is said to have won a victory in the Pythian games with a hymn or law recited to Apollo, according to Pausanias (X, 7, 2) and Photius (from Proclus, p. 523). In general, Plehn is correct. Nevertheless, <margin>15</margin> one may gather from these fables that Terpander did not only compose new laws, but also diligently collected the earlier beginnings of this art and converted them to his own use. Burette noted in this place: "I must not dispense with observing here that in the Petau manuscript <note>a manuscript formerly belonging to Denis Pétau</note> one reads *hyper Terpandrou*, which could <margin>20</margin> mean *before Terpander*, and then one would have to translate: Philammon collected several laws for the cithara, which had been composed before the time of Terpander."

### CHAPTER VI.

**Until the age of Phrynis]** Whatever the ancients <margin>25</margin> handed down about *Phrynis* comes down to what is read in Suidas (under his name) and the Scholia on Aristophanes' *Clouds* (969), and in abbreviated form in Eudocia (p. 420). He was from Mitylene, the son of Camon—that is, Scamon (Pollux IV, 65)—a most famous musician who <margin>30</margin> flourished at Athens during the time of the Persian Wars. First he practiced "auloedic" art (singing to the flute), then he turned to the striking of the cithara under the teacher Aristoclides, who himself claimed to be a follower of Terpander, and he was the first to make the citharoedic art popular among the Athenians. Phrynis carried off a victory <margin>35</margin> in the Panathenaic contest, see the *Parian Marble*, during the archonship of Callias (Olympiad 80, 4) or rather Callimachus (Olympiad 83, 3). See Meier on the *Panathenaea*, p. 285, and O. Müller’s *History of Greek Literature*, II p. 286. Phrynis, however, as a leader of the dithyrambic style, was considered the first to have corrupted music, and <margin>40</margin> for that reason he incurred the heaviest criticism from the Comic poets. But if you seek more accurate details of his new attempts, nothing is handed down except that story sung about in relation to all the most excellent musicians: that he added two new strings to the seven ancient strings of the lyre, which Ecprepes the ephorus <note>a high-ranking Spartan official</note> (Mich. Apostolius XI, 38 has *Emerepes*; <margin>45</margin> from which the true form of the name *Emprepes* should be restored everywhere) had cut off, or at least had wanted to cut off, when Phrynis came to Sparta (Plutarch, *Sayings of the Spartans*, p. 220 C). Furthermore, his melodies are criticized for their "twisted bends" <note>original Latin: *tortuosi flexus*</note> that were difficult to sing, see...

<summary>This page analyzes the historical timeline and authenticity of various musical "nomoi" attributed to Terpander and Polymnestus, debating the chronological placement of the poet Alcman. It also introduces Phrynis of Mitylene, a pivotal figure blamed by ancient critics for "corrupting" traditional music through technical innovations like adding strings to the lyre.</summary>
