--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Plutarch's "On Music," specifically focusing on Chapter VI. The previous page ended with a discussion on the "Apothetos" and "Schoenion" laws, attributed to Terpander but corrected as flute melodies. This page moves into the career of Phrynis and the technical evolution of the cithara strings.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER VI.</header>

Pollux in the place cited, Suidas under the word *bomologeúsaito* <note>original: βωμολογεύσαιτο; meaning "to act like a buffoon"</note>, Hesychius under *Lesbios oidos* <note>original: Λέσβιος ᾠδός; "Lesbian singer"</note>, and Aristophanes in *The Clouds* (969, 972). However, the influence of Phrynis began to fade before the new glory of Timotheus; see Plutarch's *On Praising Oneself Without Envy* (Chapter 1). Compare also Plehn’s *Lesbiaca* (p. 197), Meineke’s *Fragments of the Comic Poets* (Vol. II, p. 329), <margin>5</margin> and Bernhardy in Suidas (Vol. II, part 2, p. 1554). Phanias of Eresus, in Athenaeus (Book XIV, p. 638 C), recorded the "nomos" <note>a "nomos" was a traditional musical law or standard melody</note> of Phrynis. Therefore, there is no doubt that for this reason he should be included among the lyric poets, a point already considered by Schweighäuser in the Index to Athenaeus (Vol. IX, p. 172), although this cannot be <margin>10</margin> inferred with certainty from Pollux (IV, 65). Moreover, he changed the form of the "nomoi" by being the first to "join the hexameter with the loose style" <note>original: τὸ ἑξάμετρον τῷ λελυμένῳ συνῆψε</note> according to Proclus in Photius—that is, he introduced freer rhythms into the heroic verses of the "nomoi." Since no one had done this before him, the elder Timotheus later did so in a much greater way.

<margin>15</margin>->the cithara-songs as they are now]<-
These final words refer to the age of the person whose account Plutarch summarized, whether you believe it was Heraclides or Aristoxenus.

->for they were called "nomoi" (laws)]<-
See what I explained above regarding the third chapter.

->to deviate as they wished]<-
<margin>20</margin>I have written this based on a conjecture. Grammatical consistency required the Imperfect tense instead of the Present. These very words were previously read incorrectly, placed after "for that which concerns the gods," likely due to a scribe's error. For this is Plutarch’s meaning: that the ancient musicians were permitted nothing by their own whim, not <margin>25</margin> even the preludes—as indicated by the word *aphosiosámenoi* <note>original: ἀφοσιωσάμενοι; meaning "having performed the sacred rite"</note>. On the contrary, they duly paid their respects to the gods, as many as had solemn festivals.

->they transitioned immediately]<-
Plutarch says it can be gathered from Terpander’s preludes that there was a ready transition from the sacred ritual of the gods to the Homeric poetry of others. Bernhardy (History of Greek Literature, Vol. II, part 1, p. 555) teaches that this applies only to the recitation of "nomoi" <margin>30</margin> restricted to heroic verse, citing the testimony of Santenius in *Terentianus* (p. 144) and Nitzsch’s *History of Homer* (Vol. I, p. 40).

->the shape of the cithara]<-
The ancients teach nothing regarding the form of the Asiatic cithara attributed to Cepion. In Plutarch’s speech, some parts <margin>35</margin> seem to me to be missing. Burette (Vol. XIV, p. 428) approved of the incorrect word order: *‘then they manufactured the first model of the cithara made in the manner, following the idea or the design of Cepion.’* Those who take this to mean a seven-stringed cithara, however, are mistaken. For although more recent writers usually confirm as a certainty <margin>40</margin> that the seven-stringed cithara or lyre was invented by Terpander, this is highly uncertain. First, it is inherently absurd to claim that the Greeks—who were otherwise most devoted to music—were content for several centuries with the empty tinkling of an instrument with only three or four strings, from which not even those sounds could be produced that are contained within the <margin>45</margin> span of an octave. Since the Greeks were unfamiliar with that piece of wood which on our sensitive instruments is called the *fingerboard* <note>original German: das Griffbrett</note>, by whose aid the sounds of a string are multiplied, every instrument produced no more notes than it had strings.

---

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER VI.</header>

Forkel saw this most correctly in his *History of Music* (Vol. I, p. 322). There is also the very weighty testimony of the most learned grammarian Euphorion in Athenaeus (Book XIV, p. 635 F): "many-stringed instruments have changed in name only, but their use is ancient." Likewise, <margin>5</margin> Aristotle's *Problems* (XIX, 32): "Why is it called 'through all' (*dia pason*) <note>The Greek term for an octave</note>, and not 'through eight' according to its number, just as we say 'through four' and 'through five'? Is it because the strings were seven in ancient times, and then Terpander, removing the third, added the *nete* <note>the highest string</note>, and because of this it was called 'through all' and not 'through eight'? For it was through seven." Certainly, the legends <margin>10</margin> attribute the invention of the seven-stringed lyre to the first discoveries of Thracian music, assigning it to Mercury (Hermes), Amphion, and Orpheus. Great authority in solving this question is found in the *Homeric Hymn to Mercury*, which in express words attributes seven strings to the lyre invented by Mercury (lines 47–51):

<margin>15</margin>> "He cut stalks of reed to measure and fixed them,
> Piercing through the back of the stony-skinned tortoise.
> Around it, he stretched the hide of an ox with his skill,
> And added the arms, and joined a bridge to both,
> And then stretched seven harmonious strings of sheep-gut."

<margin>20</margin>Regarding this passage, Ilgenius noted: "the ancient writers do not agree on the number of strings; some say there were three at first, others four. Lucian (*Dialogues of the Gods*, VII, 223) and Horace (*Odes*, I, 15) agree with our author. See Macrobius (*Saturnalia*, I, 19) and Spanheim on Callimachus’ *Hymn to Delos* (253). From the seven strings, it is called *heptaphthongos* <note>seven-voiced</note> in Euripides (*Ion*, 881), *heptatonos* <note>seven-toned</note> in the *Alcestis* (line 446), the *heptaglossos phorminx* <note>seven-tongued lyre</note> in Pindar (*Pythian Odes*, II, 157), and *heptaktypos* <note>seven-striking</note> in Pindar (at the same place, line 129)." Whatever we decide about the age of this hymn, it proves that the seven-stringed lyre was invented already before Terpander. For if the hymn precedes Terpander in age, the matter is obvious; if <margin>30</margin> you contend for a later origin, although it cannot be much later, the matter is no less clear. For how could it occur to the author of the hymn to refer the invention of an instrument which he himself undoubtedly used to Mercury—that is, to the most ancient times—if it had been invented shortly before by Terpander, <margin>35</margin> that most famous musician? Furthermore, in the thirtieth chapter of this dialogue, it is read that "up until Terpander"—not "beginning from Terpander"—the lyre was equipped with seven strings. Although these words are perhaps not from Plutarch but from some learned interpolator, which will be discussed in its proper place, they are nevertheless noteworthy in themselves <margin>40</margin> and not to be rejected lightly. Therefore, if Terpander increased the number of strings, he could not have done this without adding new ones to the previous seven. But perhaps Terpander did not even increase them. For I care little for the story told in Plutarch's *Spartan Customs* (chapter 17): "If anyone deviated from the ancient music, they did not permit it; indeed, even though Terpander was of the old style and the best <margin>45</margin> of the cithara-singers of his time, and a praiser of heroic deeds, the Ephors nonetheless fined him and nailed up his cithara because he had stretched one extra string..." <note>The text cuts off here as the story of Terpander's fine for musical innovation continues</note>

<summary>This page debates the historical origins of the seven-stringed lyre, arguing against the tradition that Terpander invented it. By citing the Homeric Hymns and various classical authors, the text suggests that seven-stringed instruments existed long before Terpander's innovations.</summary>

<keywords>Terpander, Plutarch, heptachord, lyra, music history, Greek music, Phrynis, nomos, Mercury, Homeric Hymn to Hermes, cithara strings, fingerboard</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," specifically examining the historical evidence regarding Terpander of Lesbos and the evolution of the lyre from four strings to seven. It follows a discussion on the musical "laws" (nomoi) and transitions into the technical debate over string counts.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER VI.</header>

...[preferred the] more [diverse], for the sake of variety in the voice; for they only approved the simpler types of songs <note>original: τέραν, τοῦ ποικίλου τῆς φωνῆς χάριν· μόνα γὰρ τὰ ἁπλούστερα τῶν μελῶν ἐδοκίμαζον</note>. In this passage, it must first be noted that nothing is said regarding the number of strings. However, they act recklessly who cite *Athenaeus* (Book XIV, p. 628 B) here, who depicts the Spartans boasting that they had thrice saved music from destruction when it was corrupted, for *Athenaeus* makes no mention of Terpander. But the things *Plutarch* records are doubtful and hardly credible, a fact which did not escape even *Forkel* (*History of Music*, Vol. I, p. 291), a man otherwise of meager judgment. For how do these things agree: the sullen severity of the Ephors <note>the Ephors were high-ranking Spartan overseers known for their strictness</note>, and the great memory of Terpander as the first founder of music among the Spartans, which was always held in the highest honor? Regarding this, one should consult Leutsch on *Zenobius* (V, 9), "after the Lesbian singer" <note>original: μετὰ Λέσβιον ᾠδόν; a proverb referring to Terpander's primacy</note> and Schneidewin’s *Heraclides on Politics* (p. 51 and following). Furthermore, if you consider that the Spartan Ephors twice besides this cut the strings of the lyre—in the cases of Phrynis and Timotheus—and that the same Ephors punished a certain musician <note>a "psaltes" or harp-player</note> who used his fingers to strike the cithara instead of a plectrum <note>a pick or tool for plucking strings</note>, you will surely understand that these are mere trifles, perhaps the jests of comic poets. To be sure, they fit well enough with the severe customs of the Spartans, who, as praisers of time past, hated and condemned every innovation in music, no matter how charming. Therefore, this tale must be dismissed to the same place as many other things: the stories told of Simonides visiting Scopas, of the dolphin that saved Arion, of the cranes of Ibycus, of the lascivious loves of Sappho, and countless others of that sort. Indeed, how little the ancients agreed regarding old musical instruments is revealed even by the contradictory and clashing reports *Athenaeus* gave concerning the *pektis* and the *magadis* <note>ancient stringed instruments similar to harps or lutes</note>, which were settled neither by *Boeckh* in *On Pindaric Meters* (p. 251) nor by *Plehn* in *Lesbiaca* (p. 154). Whatever the case may be, Anacreon’s *magadis* was a perceptible instrument equipped with twenty strings. Nevertheless, *Pindar*—himself no ignoble musician—did not hesitate to attribute its invention to Terpander, whom I believe for this very reason did not consider him the inventor of the seven-stringed lyre.

But someone might say: has it escaped you that *Mueller*, *Hoeck*, and others have attributed the invention of the seven-stringed lyre to Terpander, relying on certain testimonies of the ancients? Let us hear the witnesses. First, they appeal to Suidas. We have the source from which Suidas drew his information in *Strabo* (Book XIII, p. 618): "And they say Terpander was a practitioner of the same music and from the same island" (he means the art of singing to the cithara and the island of Lesbos), "the first to use a seven-stringed lyre instead of a four-stringed one, just as is said in the verses attributed to him: <note>original: καὶ Τέρπανδρόν δὲ τῆς αὐτῆς μουσικῆς τεχνίτην γεγονέναι φασὶ καὶ τῆς αὐτῆς νήσου... τὸν πρῶτον ἀντὶ τῆς τετραγόρδου λύρας ἑπταχόρδῳ χρησάμενον</note>

> But we, turning away from the four-voiced song,
> will sing new hymns to you on a seven-toned phorminx." <note>original: σοὶ δ’ ἡμεῖς τετράγηρυν ἀποστρέψαντες ἀοιδὴν / ἑπτατόνῳ φόρμιγγι νέους κελαδήσομεν ὕμνους; the phorminx was an early form of the lyre</note>

I do not say that Strabo’s authority in musical matters is slight, but I urge that these verses are only *attributed* to Terpander. They appear again in *Euclid* as if they were Terpander's (*Harmonic Elements*, p. 19) with a variation in the text—"having loathed" <note>original: ἀποστέρξαντες</note>—from which Euclid proves that in ancient times the word "tonos" <note>tone</note> was used instead of "phthongos" <note>vocal sound or note</note>. Another [witness]

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER VI.</header>

is *Clement of Alexandria* in the *Stromata* (Book VI, p. 814), adding these words: "A poet of no small fame writes—teaching also that the ancient lyre was seven-toned." <note>original: ποιητής τις οὐκ ἄσημος γράφει — καὶ τὴν παλαιὰν λύραν ἑπτάφθογγον εἶναι διδάσκων</note> Now, I consider it certain that the entire opinion regarding the invention of the seven-stringed lyre arose solely from these verses, attributed to Terpander whether rightly or falsely. But I ask you, how is "four-voiced song" <note>original: τετράγηρυς ἀοιδή</note> the striking of a four-stringed cithara? What "four-voiced" <note>original: τετράγηρυς</note> actually means, I do not know, and perhaps not even Strabo knew. Comparing it to the Homeric "honey-voiced" <note>original: μελίγηρυς</note>, it certainly follows that any mention of a *string* is foreign to this word; this, of course, did not prevent a false interpretation from dragging in those dreams about the seven-stringed lyre invented by Terpander. Perhaps the word should be associated with the "four-part" melody <note>original: Nomo tetraodio; a musical form</note>. Thus, Terpander says at the beginning of some melody or other that he will produce a new song on a seven-stringed lyre—a new melody—having rejected the ancient four-part form. If these views please anyone less, I shall not dwell on it. I think it is manifest that these testimonies are worth nothing for weakening the previous exposition. Furthermore, lest I seem reckless to anyone for using the words "lyre" and "cithara" interchangeably, let him consult the appendix <note>original: epimetrum</note>.

**Regarding Cepion**] <note>original: κατὰ Κηπίωνα</note> Concerning Cepion, the disciple and also friend of Terpander (see *Pollux* IV, 65), nothing is certain. Whether the cithara-singing melody <note>nomos</note> was composed by him (*Clement of Alexandria, Exhortation*, p. 2 D: "And my Eunomus sings not the melody of Terpander, nor that of Capion") or was adorned by Terpander with his friend's name, *Hesychius* also knew of it in addition to *Pollux* in the place cited: "Capion: the name of a cithara-singing melody." <note>original: Καπίων κιθαρῳδικοῦ νόμου ὄνομα</note>

**And it was called Asian**] <note>original: ἐκλήθη δ’ Ἀσιάς</note> The explanation of the name brought forward by *Plutarch* is undoubtedly correct, as is almost everything preserved in this golden little book, yet it was not common to all antiquity. For the *Scholia on Apollonius of Rhodes* (II, 777, p. 429, 16) offer another: "and the cithara is called Asian, since it was first found in Lydia"; more accurately the *Etymologicum Magnum* (p. 153, 31) and *Zonaras* (p. 318): "The three-stringed cithara is called Asiatic because it was first found in the city of Asia in Lydia, situated on Mount Tmolus in Lydia. Or from Asias the Lydian." <note>original: εἴρηται δὲ Ἀσιάτις ἡ τρίχορδος κιθάρα...</note> Regarding the various spellings of the name, see *Naeke’s Fragments of Choerilus* (p. 114). The Etymologist falsely attributed the three-stringed cithara to Terpander's disciple. On the other hand, Strabo cited this name to show that the music of the cithara also reached the Greeks from Asia (Book X, p. 471), an opinion that is only correct if we establish some kinship between the ancient Thracians and Phrygians; cf. *Hoeck’s Crete*, Vol I, p. 225 and following. The Asiatic cithara seems to have been especially favored in satyr plays. *Euripides, Cyclops*, lines 443 and following: "Say that we would not more gladly hear the sound of the Asian cithara than the Cyclops destroyed." <note>original: λέγ’ ὡς Ἀσιάδος οὐκ ἂν ἥδιον ψόφον κιθάρας κλύοιμεν, ἢ Κύκλωπ’ ὀλωλότα</note> Agatho mentioned the "striking of the Asian [lyre]" in *Aristophanes’ Women at the Thesmophoria*, line 120.

**The continuity of the... succession**] <note>original: τὸ συνεχὲς τῆς — διαδοχῆς</note> Amyot correctly translates: "after whose death there failed in Lesbos the continuation of the succession of cithara players." By what right *Ulricius* (*History of Lyric Poetry*, p. 84) says that Plutarch does not contend that Terpander’s discipline entirely ceased with Periclitus, but that its glory vanished in the games...
<sig>6</sig>

<summary>This page analyzes the legendary origins of the seven-stringed lyre, arguing that the attribution to Terpander may be based on a poetic misunderstanding of "four-voiced" vs. "four-stringed." It also discusses the "Asian" style of cithara music and its geographical origins in Lydia.</summary>
<keywords>Terpander, cithara, lyre, heptachord, Ephors, Sparta, music history, Strabo, Plutarch, Lydia, Asian cithara, musical succession, Lesbos</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the history of Greek music, transitioning from the school of Terpander of Lesbos to the origins of flute-playing (auletics) and the legendary figure of Olympus.</meta>

<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS VI—VII.</header>
<page-num>82</page-num>

I do not know if it was prepared for the Carneia <note>The Carneia was a major national festival of the Spartans, held in honor of Apollo Carneios</note>. Certainly, even later, many musicians boasted of their origin from the school of Terpander. It cannot be proven that *Euacnetides* was more recent than Periclitus. There was certainly *Aristoclides*, the teacher of Phrynis, who was a follower of the Lesbian school of Terpander <margin>5</margin> (see Eustathius's commentary on the *Iliad*, book I, 129, p. 741, 16; and the Scholia on Aristophanes' *Clouds*, 965). Plehn, in *Lesbiaca* p. 197, suggests this as well. That Arion belonged to the school of Terpander is merely a conjecture of Plehn’s in the same work, p. 165. There were also other distinguished Lesbian musicians. Athenaeus (Book XII, p. 538) mentions *Cratinus*, the solo-lyre player <term>psilocitharista</term> <note>original: "psilocitharistam"; a musician who plays the cithara without accompanying a singer</note> from Methymna. From Mitylene was *Agenor*, <margin>10</margin> who was an authority on the new musical discipline before Aristoxenus. In general, the Mitylenaeans claimed for themselves the primary roles in the art of singing to the cithara (see Menander, *On Rhetorical Displays*, p. 360, 21 in Spengel’s edition).

"That Hipponax lived"] According to the most weightly testimonies of the ancients, Hipponax belongs to the 60th Olympiad, the age of Darius Hystaspis <note>reigned 522–486 BCE</note> (Pliny, *Natural History* XXXVI, 5; Proclus, *Chrestomathy* 7). Compare Bernhardy, <margin>15</margin> *History of Greek Literature*, Vol. II, part 1, p. 472.

### CHAPTER VII.

"Only for the flute-players"] It was necessary to write this <margin>20</margin> <note>original: ἐπὶ μόνους τοὺς αὐλητικούς</note> instead of the common "aulodic" <note>original: αὐλῳδικούς; referring to those who sing to the flute</note>, as is apparent from what follows. Franke already noted Plutarch's error (on Callinus, p. 130) and attributed it to the ignorance of a writer whom he had wrongly persuaded himself was unskilled in music. Furthermore, instead of "only" <note>original: μόνους</note>, I would prefer to restore the reading of earlier editions, such as the Wechelian, which is "laws" <note>original: νόμους; referring to the musical 'nomoi'</note>. <margin>25</margin>

"The aforementioned Olympus"] See the explanation in Chapter V regarding the ancient Olympus. In this place, the later Olympus ought to be mentioned. This error should not be attributed to Plutarch's negligence here, as he was following Alexander or someone else who established no distinction between the two Olympi, which was only done by those <margin>30</margin> whose opinion he mentions below.

"The so-called Many-headed"] This melody-type <term>nomos</term> <note>original: Πολυκέφαλον; "Polycephalus"</note> seems to have been very ancient, since Pindar (Pythian Ode XII, 18–32) records that it was invented by Minerva <note>Athena</note>. The scholia on that passage (v. 15) say: "When Perseus beheaded Medusa, her two sisters wailed for their sibling, and <margin>35</margin> from their heads and the snakes around their heads a certain hissing was emitted. Having heard this hissing from the snakes, Athena, in order to imitate the lamentation and the sound produced by the snakes, invented the art of flute-playing, and for this reason named it the 'Many-headed' nomos. Since a single hissing was emitted from the many heads of the snakes—for the snakes naturally had many heads—she composed and named the imitative flute art the 'Many-headed' nomos." <margin>40</margin> The same things are said at verse 39, where these words are added: "But some say he called it 'Many-headed' because there were fifty men from whom the chorus was composed, who performed the melody while the flute-player led. Others understand 'heads' to mean 'preludes.' Therefore, it was a song consisting of many preludes, which they say Olympus first discovered." This is false <margin>45</margin>

<column-break/>

<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER VII.</header>
<page-num>83</page-num>

because the Polycephalus nomos was not sung by a chorus, but played on solo flutes <note>original: "assis tibiis"</note>. This nomos was threnodic <note>mournful or funereal</note>, imitating the sad hissing of the snakes on the heads of the sisters mourning the death of the Gorgon. (Compare Boeckh, *Explanations of Pindar*, p. 345; Ulrici, *History of Lyrical Poetry*, p. 174). Thiersch erred in his *Introduction to Pindar* (p. 61) when <margin>5</margin> he claimed that the Polycephalus nomos did not differ from the Pythic nomos.

"This Olympus"] This is the same Olympus whom Suidas (under the same entry) calls the second and third. If he says he lived in the times of Midas, son of Gordias, this is notable for the reason that <margin>10</margin> Terpander was also assigned to the same age by Hellanicus. In general, I believe the more recent Olympus was a contemporary of Terpander, since almost everyone recognizes that his first disciples knew Phrygian flute-playing <term>auletica</term>. Ritschl’s discussion on the two Olympi in the *Halle Encyclopedia* is most sagacious; there the learned man teaches <margin>15</margin> that the name of the "lesser Olympus" belongs to a certain later age of Phrygian flute-playing—one in which that old contention between Greek cithara-singing and Asiatic flute-playing (signified to us by the Athenian fables about the contest between Apollo and Marsyas) began to disappear, and the flute was practiced even <margin>20</margin> in the sacred festivals of the Greeks. The same writer teaches that Plutarch’s following words, "one of those from the first Olympus," should be understood not as a true biological relationship, but as a musical discipline handed down as if by inheritance.

Now, if the things which Plutarch and the Peripatetic writers <margin>25</margin> on music have recorded here and there are gathered into one, it must be established as follows: in the most ancient times there were two schools or musical disciplines from which perfect Greek music later emerged. One was the *Thracian* school of Orpheus and Amphion, dedicated to *singing with the cithara* <term>citharoedica</term>; the other was the *Phrygian* school of Olympus, dedicated to *flute-playing* <term>auletica</term>. The *Lesbian school* of Terpander joined <margin>30</margin> and increased the inventions of both; from this school, the entire music of the Dorians in the Peloponnese depended. Aside from the art of flute-playing itself, the enharmonic genus as well as the Phrygian and Lydian modes <term>tonus</term> were the property of the Phrygian school. If you look for Phrygian musicians, the symbolic names of *Hyagnis*, *Marsyas*, and *Olympus* <margin>35</margin> are brought forward, to which Strabo (Book X, p. 470) added a fourth, *Silenus*, although in other writers Silenus sometimes designates a Corybant <note>a frantic worshiper of Cybele</note> and sometimes Marsyas himself. Added to these is *Babys*, the brother of Marsyas, who was such a poor musician that Apollo did not even deem him worthy of punishment (see Athenaeus XIV, p. 624 B; Zenobius IV, 81). Compare Leutsch on this passage and Welcker, *Fragments of Alcman*, p. 73. Furthermore, *Agnes*, a Phrygian musician, <margin>40</margin> is mentioned by Eusebius in the *Preparation for the Gospel* (whose words Sylburgius inserted into Clement, *Stromata* I, p. 132, 45) as the inventor of the diatonic genus. Finally, the list ends with *Codalus*, named by Hipponax, of whom a certain composition for singing to the flute was said to exist, mentioned in an epigram of Hedylus in Athenaeus IV <margin>45</margin> p. 176 C; compare Bergk in the *New Rhenish Museum*, I, p. 359. Athenaeus (XIV p. 624 B) provides other names of Phrygian musicians.

<summary>This page analyzes the origins of the "Many-headed" flute melody (Polycephalus nomos) and distinguishes between the legendary Thracian and Phrygian schools of music that merged into the Greek tradition. It highlights how the art of the flute (auletics) and the lyre (citharoedics) were eventually synthesized by the school of Terpander.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Plutarch's "On Music." The previous page discussed the transition from the school of Terpander to the origins of flute-playing (auletics), specifically mentioning the legendary figure Olympus. We now move into specific melodies (nomes) and their legendary inventors.</meta>

<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER VII.</header>
<page-num>84</page-num>

*[of Marsyas (who) made]* <note>original: "τοῦ Μαρσύου (τοῦ) πεποιηκότος"</note> The word *tou* [the] should be inserted so that it refers to the older Olympus. — WYTTENBACH.

*[the harmonic nomes]* <note>original: "τοὺς νόμους τοὺς ἁρμονικούς"; "nomes" were traditional musical compositions or "laws" of melody</note> It appears from the preceding context that the discussion is only about the melodies for the flute <term>auletic nomes</term>. See Ritschel, p. 335. *Harmonikos* [harmonic] is the same as *enharmonios* [enharmonic], as Burette has already said concerning the word *harmonia* [harmony], referring to the *enharmonic genus* <note>The enharmonic was one of the three "genera" of Greek music, characterized by very small intervals</note>, as in Aristoxenus, *Elements of Harmony*, Book I, p. 2: "The earlier harmonicists, therefore, only wished to be 'harmonic'; for they touched only upon harmony itself, and of the other genres they never had any concept." Compare Meibomius on this passage, p. 76.

*[which the Greeks now use]* <note>original: "οἷς νῦν χρῶνται οἱ Ἕλληνες"</note> This refers to the age of Glaucus or Heraclides, or whichever other ancient author Plutarch followed. Certainly, in the age of Aristotle, some fragments of poems attributed to Olympus still survived, along with ancient melodies that were entirely orgiastic in nature <note>original: "prorsus orgiasticis"; refers to the wild, ecstatic music associated with the cults of Dionysus or Cybele</note>, see Aristotle, *Politics* VIII, 5. Perhaps from Plato’s words in the *Minos*, p. 318 B: "Of these (Marsyas and Olympus) the flute-melodies are the most divine, and they alone move and reveal those who stand in need of the gods; and even now they alone remain as things divine," we should conclude that these were the only remnants of ancient music. Whether they were genuine, I strongly doubt.

*[others say of Crates]* <note>original: "ἄλλοι δὲ Κράτητος"</note> Diogenes Laertius, who in Book IV, 23, enumerated ten men named Crates, omitted an ancient musician. Nor is he mentioned by any other writer, as far as I know. The name itself is Greek, not Phrygian, and is perhaps a corruption. Therefore, I believe that the *Cradia* nome, which Plutarch mentions in the following chapter, took its name from a certain musician named Cradias—just as the *Cepion* and *Polymnestus* nomes were named after their inventors—and that his name lies hidden here as well.

*[the Harmatian nome]* <note>original: "Ἁρμάτιον νόμον"; the "Chariot-song"</note> It took its name, it seems, because it was sung from a wagon [plaustrum] in a solemn procession. See Euripides, *Orestes*, line 1385, with the Scholia; Hesychius under the entry; and Ulrici’s *History of Lyric Poetry*, p. 160. I was not able to consult Klein's note on the fragments of Stesichorus, p. 117.

*[the son of Hyagnis, the first inventor]* <note>original: "Ὑάγνιδος υἱὸν τοῦ πρώτου εὑρόντος"</note> Just as in this passage, the *Parian Marble* (epoch 10) also says that Hyagnis discovered the flutes and the Phrygian mode at Celaenae. Regarding the Phrygian mode, Aristoxenus agrees in Athenaeus, Book XIV, p. 624 B. Apuleius, in *Florida*, p. 113, knows Hyagnis as the most ancient musician, the father and teacher of Marsyas. He is shown as a disciple of Mariandynus in the Scholia on Aeschylus's *Persians*, line 933, and by Eustathius in his commentary on Dionysius the Periegete, line 791. On the other hand, Marsyas—whether the son of Hyagnis, or of Olympus (whom I discussed in the notes to Chapter V), or of Oeagrus in Hyginus 165—is praised as the inventor of the flute by Suidas and in the Athenian fables, which I believe are specifically in Apollodorus I, 4, 2. Compare Plato, *Republic* III, p. 399 E. According to Metrodorus in Athenaeus, Book IV, p. 184 A, he invented both the pan-pipes [fistula] and the flute [tibia]; according to Euphorion, the pan-pipes alone. Diodorus (III, 58), however, placed Marsyas's greatest merit in music in the fact that he transferred the individual sounds of the pan-pipes, which were previously scattered, into a single flute-reed. From these little stories, it must be taken as certain that the flute came from Phrygia

---

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS VII—VIII.</header>

to the Greeks; the rest are empty tales, for the less certain the traditions of the inventors' origins were, the more joyfully a crop of fictions sprouted among the "lying little Greeks." <note>original: "Graeculos mendaces"; a common Roman idiom disparaging Greek reliability</note> Furthermore, I believe the Latin name for the flute, *tibia*, should also be traced back to the Phrygian origin of the instrument. For *Tibia* is an old name for Phrygia. See Stephanus of Byzantium: "Tibion, a mountain of Phrygia, from a certain Tibius." Suidas: "Tibia: all of Phrygia is so called," and *Greek Proverbs* I, p. 431. That Roman writers knew of the foreign origin of the flute is indicated by its common epithet. It was only in the age of the Emperors that the word was also transferred to the leg bones [cruris ossa], by analogy with the Greek name *aulos*. <note>The Greek word "aulos" can refer to a pipe or a hollow tube, and similarly, "tibia" came to mean both the musical pipe and the shinbone</note>

*[having used the Harmatian nome — they say it is]* <note>original: "χρησάμενος τῷ ἁρματίῳ νόμῳ — φασὶν εἶναι"</note> I do not understand this whole passage. For it is established from elsewhere that Stesichorus devoted himself solely to the art of the lyre-singer <term>citharoedica</term>, which his fragment XXXIX does not contradict. How then could he employ the *Harmatian* nome, which belongs to the flute [auletic]? Furthermore, what is the *type based on the dactyl* <note>original: "τὸ κατὰ δάκτυλον εἶδος"</note> which is said to be taken from the *Orthian* nome? Is it the dactylic meter or something else? The words of the scholiasts on Aristophanes' *Clouds*, 651, do not resolve the matter: "It is a type of rhythm and beat, the dactylic, which the flute-players use before the nome." I do not understand Burette's interpretation. Whatever this may be, we may nevertheless seek from these words a new testimony that Terpander had nothing to do with the *Orthian* nome, which I discussed above in chapter four.

*[that this nome was found by the Mysians]* <note>original: "ὑπὸ Μυσῶν εὑρῆσθαι τοῦτον τὸν νόμον"</note> It completely escapes me who would attribute any part of the development of the art of flute-playing to the Mysians, unless you wish to refer to the fact that some said Olympus was a Mysian—though this would not be absurd in itself, as the Mysians were very close neighbors to the Phrygians. I am surprised that there were some who would divine [ariolarentur] about Libyan inventors of flutes. Certainly Athenaeus (Book XIV, p. 618 C), following Duris in the second book of his *History of Agathocles*, says a certain *Seirites*, a Libyan of the Nomads, was the inventor of the flute art, "who also was the first to play the Mother's rites on the flute," for which the authority rests with that author.

### CHAPTER VIII.

*[the ancient nome called Cradia]* <note>original: "ἀρχαῖος νόμος καλούμενος Κραδίας"</note> Concerning the *Cradia*, Hesychius says: "Cradia nome: a certain melody they play on the flute for the 'scapegoats' being sent out, as they are struck with fig-branches [kradeas] and stalks." These things refer to the solemn custom of the *Thargelia* <note>An Athenian festival where "pharmakoi" or scapegoats were ritually purified/expelled</note>. However, Franke correctly showed (in *Callinus*, p. 129) that this interpretation contains mere trifles. For my part, I think the name should be derived from some unknown inventor, as seen above. The same Franke very correctly saw that this nome was for the flute [auletic], not for singing to the flute [auloedic].

*[that Mimnermus played the flute]* <note>original: "Μίμνερμον αὐλῆσαι"</note> These words are either to be interpreted such that Mimnermus is said to have used the ancient *Cradia* nome in composing elegies (compare Ulrici, *History of Lyric Poetry*, p. 176), so that *to play the flute* [aulēsai] would be the same as *to sing to the flute* [pros aulon āisai]—which is the true [opinion] of Plutarch...

<summary>This page analyzes the origins of specific Greek musical "nomes" (traditional melodies), discussing their legendary Phrygian and Mysian inventors like Hyagnis and Marsyas. It also explores the etymological connection between the Phrygian region and the Latin term for the flute, "tibia."</summary>
<keywords>Olympus, Marsyas, Hyagnis, Phrygia, auletic nomes, Harmatian nome, Cradia nome, tibia, aulos, Stesichorus, Mimnermus, Thargelia, Greek music history</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," moving into Chapter VIII. The previous discussion concluded with the evolution of the flute-playing tradition (auletica) and its early legendary practitioners. This page focuses on the poet Mimnermus, the technical distinction between types of elegies, the history of musical "modes" or "tones" (Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian), and the historical role of the poet as a chorus-master.</meta>

<header>### COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER VIII.</header>

...this seems to have been the opinion. Or, it should be translated simply that the ancient Mimnermus played the flute-song <term>nomos</term> on the pipe, which seems more consistent with the facts. That Mimnermus was a flute-player did not escape the notice of Strabo (Book XIII, p. 442). Bernhardy, in his *History of Greek Literature* (Vol. I, p. 337; Vol. II, Part 1, p. 402), has shown that these points only prove that elegiac
<margin>5</margin> poets sometimes began their careers in the arts of flute-playing <term>auletica</term> or singing to the flute <term>auloedica</term>, but were not always and everywhere associated with them.

*[For in the beginning, elegies]* <note>original: "ἐν ἀρχῇ γὰρ ἐλεγεῖα"</note> Bernhardy (in the passage cited above) noted that instead of "elegiac verses" <note>original: "ἐλεγεῖα" (elegeia)</note>, Plutarch should have said "elegies set to music" <note>original: "ἐλέγους" (elegous)</note>. For "elegies set to music" are properly called *elegoi*, whereas *elegeia* refers to the verses themselves. Although Plutarch could easily have made an error by
<margin>10</margin> confusing these terms, he seems in reality to have determined that Mimnermus and the ancient authors of elegiac poetry strictly followed the art of flute-singing and lyrical poetry, perhaps because he had misunderstood the words of Hipponax regarding Mimnermus, which were just
<margin>15</margin> mentioned. See Ulrich (p. 180) and Frank on Callinus (p. 129). Indeed, if he appealed to the records of the Panathenaic musical contests to defend this opinion, the evidence is weak, since the same Plutarch stated elsewhere that these records were only established later by Pericles; see Frank, *Callinus*, p. 127 sq. Nor is the testimony of Pausanias (X, 7, 3) valid:
<margin>20</margin> *“For flute-singing was the most somber of flute melodies, and elegies [and dirges] were sung to the accompaniment of flutes,”* because this arose from a confusion between the terms *elegoi* in the inscription of Echembrotus and the word *elegeia*. It appears that the same error led to the later description of Sacadas as a "poet of elegies set to music."
<margin>25</margin> *[and a good flute-player]* <note>original: "καὶ αὐλητὴς ἀγαθός"</note> I have written "flute-player and poet," as this was clumsily repeated by a scribe's error from what preceded. This emendation is supported both by the mention of the Pythian contest and by the passage cited from Pausanias: *“The victors were announced: Cephallen, who shone
<margin>30</margin> in singing to the cithara; Echembrotus the Arcadian, a flute-singer; and Sacadas of Argos on the pipes. This same Sacadas also won the next two Pythian festivals.”* This same Sacadas is called a "flute-player" in Pausanias IX, 30, 2, words which are worth describing further because they shed light on what follows: *“He who made the statue of Sacadas of Argos,
<margin>35</margin> not understanding Pindar's introductory poem to him, made the statue of the flute-player no larger than the length of his flutes.”*

*[there being, at least, three tones]* <note>original: "τόνων γοῦν τριῶν ὄντων"</note> Regarding the three most ancient tones—the Dorian, Phrygian, and Lydian—Ptolemy (*Harmonics* II, 10) and Porphyry (p. 350) agree with Plutarch. These sounds differed from one another by the interval of a single tone in
<margin>40</margin> pitch, and their distinction lay both in the sharpness of the tension of the principal note and in the order in which the remaining notes followed the principal one. Even the melodies—that is, the songs—were divided into three types. Scholion on Aristides (p. 203): *“For there were three melodies: the Lydian, the Phrygian, and the Dorian.”* A fourth tone,
<margin>45</margin> the Ionian, was added later. Thus, in Dio Chrysostom (Or. XXXIII, 42), four harmonies are listed: *“the Ionian, the Dorian, the Phrygian, and another, the Lydian.”* Less accurately, Themistius (p. 336 A) asserts there are three kinds of songs: Dorian, Phrygian, and Iasian melodies.

<column-break/>

On the other hand, if Aristotle (*Politics* IV, 3), following the authority of others, mentions only two tones, the Dorian and Phrygian, this is just as false as if Heraclides Ponticus (in Athenaeus XIV p. 624 D, followed by Pollux IV, 65) lists the oldest tones as Aeolian,
<margin>5</margin> Ionian, and Dorian. For the ideas Heraclides had convinced himself of regarding the character and nature of the tones were entirely false and contradictory, a judgment that applies to others as well in the age of Aristoxenus, by his own testimony (*Harmonics* II, p. 37). Compare Athanasius Kircher, *Musurgia* I, p. 151. Nor is there more agreement on this matter
<margin>10</margin> among more recent authors. While it is not the place here to examine their opinions more closely, I would dare to affirm for certain that what the Greeks called tones, modes <term>tropoi</term>, or harmonies <term>harmoniai</term> correspond to what are called "species of the octave" <term>diapason species</term> in modern sacred music; it is well known that each of these affects the ears in its own
<margin>15</margin> unique way. Many ancient writers are also involved in describing the ethical <note>referring to 'ethos' or the emotional character of music</note> distinction of the tones. Later, however—I believe due to the innovations of the dithyrambic poets—just as with the genera of sounds, the species of the octave began to be reduced to a smaller number. Thus, just as it had perhaps long been the custom for practicing musicians, even those
<margin>20</margin> who studied musical disciplines theoretically wanted one species of the octave to be the foundation of all music, such as the "immutable system" exhibits. Its various transpositions (to use our modern musical term), separated from each other by the tension of a semi-tone, would bear the names of the tones. Similarly, our own musicians,
<margin>25</margin> who strictly speaking use only two tones—which we call the major and minor scales <note>original: "scalam duram et mollem"</note>—nevertheless, through a certain misuse, also give the names of tones to various transpositions of a single diagram, such as C Major, G Major, and the rest. For those who wish to understand this matter more accurately, as it is a subject of
<margin>30</margin> deeper debate, they should consult Ptolemy (II, 9), Euclid (p. 15), Gaudentius (p. 17), Aristides Quintilianus (I, p. 17), and among more recent writers: Marpurg, Burney, Forkel, Boeckh (*On the Meters of Pindar*, p. 214 sqq.), Bellermann in the book titled *The Scales of the Greeks* (p. 4 sqq.), Fortlage (*On the Musical System of the Greeks*, p. 16 sqq.), Trinkler (*On Harmony*, p. 21 sqq.), and Thiersch (*Introduction to Pindar*, p. 39 sqq.). Nor should
<margin>35</margin> we omit the points which Uhdolph debated with more subtlety than truth, if I see correctly, in a school pamphlet published in Glogau in 1841 (p. 11, 18 sq.).

*[to teach the chorus to sing]* <note>original: "διδάξαι ᾄδειν τὸν χόρον"</note> This custom persisted, where the poets or musical masters themselves taught the chorus the song by singing it first,
<margin>40</margin> even in the age of the Attic writers. An inscription regarding Phrynichus (in Plutarch, *Life of Themistocles*, ch. 5; Boeckh, *Corpus Inscriptionum Graecarum* I, p. 342) testifies to this. Regarding Euripides, compare the charming story told in Plutarch’s *On Listening to Lectures* (ch. 15): *“The poet Euripides, while he was singing a certain song he had composed to the members of the chorus, was laughed at by one of them;
<margin>45</margin> Euripides replied: 'Unless you were senseless, you would not have laughed at me singing in the Mixolydian mode.'”* In this fact, I would suggest there is a strong argument to undermine the opinion of those who wish to claim that the common use of musical notation was widespread among the Greeks.

<summary>This page discusses the historical and technical development of Greek musical modes and the role of poets like Mimnermus and Euripides as both composers and performers. It clarifies the terminology of ancient elegies and debates whether the Greeks relied on written notation or oral instruction to teach choruses.</summary>

<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER VIII.</header>

*[to be called three-membered]* <note>original: "καλεῖσθαι δὲ τριμελῆ"</note> I have written it this way, as in the fourth chapter, instead of the word "tripartite" <note>original: "τριμερῆ" (trimerē)</note> which was written twice. In Hesychius under the entry for *Polymnestus*, we read "melodious" <note>original: "εὐμελὴς" (eumelēs)</note> where the manuscripts have "well-proportioned" <note>original: "εὐμερὴς" (eumerēs)</note>; there, the editor remarks that "melodies" <note>original: "μέλη" (melē)</note> and "parts" <note>original: "μέρη" (merē)</note> are known to be frequently confused. For the *parts* of the <term>nomoi</term> (traditional musical compositions)—if indeed we establish that the older modes already consisted of parts—according to what has been handed down regarding the parts of the Pythian mode, had very little to do with the changing of tones. In this passage, we must not overlook the opinion of Boeckh in his *Metrics of Pindar* (p. 242), which has been approved by many as far as I know. He says: "it is certain that the same modes were distinguished by various rhythms; and just as the melody varied in tension, intervals, and melodic figures, so the rhythm was greatly varied by the pace, whether fast or slow, and the metric feet and figures formed from it. To this was added the diversity of musical instruments and the varied choice of dialect, different connections of thoughts, and different formations of words, both grammatical and logical: finally, there were different movements of the dance, different figures, and different gestures." I find this hard to believe; for besides the fact that these ideas lack adequate authority, imagine if those ancient modes used by Sacadas were of such a kind—how could he have joined three of them into the unity of a single mode, given the simplicity of ancient Greek music at that time? However, Burette rightly saw that Terpander’s "four-part song" and Sacadas's "three-membered" mode <note>original: "τριμελῆ" (trimele)</note> were similarly structured; Boeckh followed him in this (p. 251). Compare also Bernhardy’s *History of Greek Literature* (Vol II, 1, p. 532).

*[on account of the modulation]* <note>original: "διὰ τὴν μεταβολήν"</note> Regarding the nature and types of musical modulation <note>original: "metabole"</note>, the opinions of the ancients diverged into different camps. This will seem hardly surprising to anyone considering the specific meaning of this word, which is just as varied and multifaceted as the German word *transition* <note>original: "uebergang"</note>. See Trinkler’s *Harmonics* (p. 31) and Bojesen in the program of the Sorana Academy of Copenhagen published in 1846, specifically the section titled: *An inquiry into what the Greek harmonicists taught regarding modulation and composition* (p. 35 ff.). In this passage, we should understand "modulation according to tone" <note>original: "κατὰ τόνον" (kata tonon)</note>, which Euclid (p. 20) teaches occurs "whenever it goes from Dorian to Phrygian, or even from Phrygian to Lydian or Hypermixolydian, or generally, whenever a change occurs from one of the thirteen tones to any of the remaining ones." Compare Phrynichus’s *Sophistic Preparation* under the entry "tuning" <note>original: "ἁρμογή" (harmogē)</note> in Bekker’s *Anecdota* (p. 15): "Modulation is a transition from a similar tone to a dissimilar one, as if one in a single poem or piece of music makes the melody (which was perhaps Dorian) into Hypodorian, mixing the Hypodorian with the Dorian." This modulation according to tone was used by Stesichorus and Pindar, and especially by the <term>dithyrambic</term> poets (authors of wild, choral hymns to Dionysus); see Dionysius of Halicarnassus (C. V. 24) and Aristotle’s *Politics* (VIII, 7).

*[Clonas is recorded as the inventor]* Compare the beginning of Chapter IV. Modulation of tone could not have been used except in a more advanced state of the art of flute-playing <term>auletica</term>, of which Clonas was said to be the author and propagator throughout Greece.

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER IX.</header>

### CHAPTER IX.

*[So then, the first establishment]* <note>original: "ἡ μὲν οὖν πρώτη κατάστασις"</note> If the various accounts that Plutarch and others have handed down concerning the first state of music among the Spartans are collected into one, we see that by his time, the art of singing to the lyre <term>citharoedica</term> had been established, the lyre-songs <term>nomoi</term> had been collected, the Dorian harmony had been refined, and finally, certain innovations had been made to the form of the lyre itself. Regarding the Dorian harmony, Bernhardy (*History of Greek Literature* Vol I, p. 321) correctly showed that Mueller’s opinion—which suggests it existed before the time of Terpander—is improbable. The art of playing the flute or singing to it was unknown to the Asians, or at least little used. In contrast, it is a characteristic of the *second age of music* that the art of the flute <term>tibina</term> prevailed and was received into the cult of Apollo, even to the neglect of lyre-singing. Thus, while in the first age the famous Spartan musicians seem to have continued and refined the ancient music of Thrace, the second age was dominated by the Asian style of music received from the island of Crete. From Lacedaemon (Sparta), this style of music spread to other cities, but it took deeper root only among the Arcadians and Boeotians, who perhaps already approved of it through the Bacchic rites. Furthermore, the "enharmonic" genus is characteristic of this second age, which, having intentionally neglected the "chromatic" style, was used almost exclusively. The ancient form of the modes was preserved, but a new <term>hyporchema</term> (a lively choral dance-song) was added, which contained the first seeds of the <term>dithyramb</term>. And since the Dorians, especially the Spartans, seem to have stood firm in this degree of musical perfection as if it were absolute—so long as the Spartan discipline flourished—it is no wonder that they looked down upon whatever the dithyrambic poets at Athens and the flute-players at Thebes later innovated in musical matters. In general, the form of the dithyramb as the Athenian genius devised it was displeasing to the Dorians in the Peloponnese, who were content with the simpler dance-song.

*[and of the second, Thaletas]* It is believable—indeed, it seems so because of the chain of following testimony—that Plutarch here listed the musicians in chronological order; see Hoeck’s *Creta* (Vol III, p. 378) and Bernhardy (Vol I, p. 324). I will soon show that any doubts raised about this because of Xenocritus are vain.

*[became leaders]* As for the individual men: Polymnestus has been discussed above; Thaletas will be discussed below. Of Xenodamus of Cythera, nothing has been committed to memory except that Athenaeus (Book I, p. 15 D) says he was the leader of the "style of the choral dance-song" <note>original: "τοῦ ὑπορχηματικοῦ τρόπου"</note>. Regarding Xenocritus of Locri, Boeckh collected several points (*Explanations of Pindar*, p. 197). First, Heraclides says he was born blind. The author of the Scholia on Pindar (Olympian XI, 17) reports that he refined "Locrian harmony," moving away from the words of Callimachus, who in a fragment preserved in the same place had praised Xenocritus’s "Italian harmony." On the other hand, Heraclides in Athenaeus (Book XIV)...

It also begins a biographical survey of early Greek musicians like Xenodamus and the blind Xenocritus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Plutarch's "On Music," specifically examining the history of musical modes and the figures associated with the "second establishment" of music in Sparta. The previous page discussed the structure of the "nomoi" (musical laws or traditional compositions) and the various rhythmic elements that defined them. This page transitions into a detailed study of Chapter IX, focusing on the Locrian harmony and the historical musicians Sacadas of Argos and Thaletas of Crete.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER IX.

p. 625: *It is necessary for the harmony to have a form of character or emotion; just as the Locrian does. For some of those who lived in the time of Simonides and Pindar once used it, and later it was again looked down upon.* <note>original Greek: "δεῖ δὲ τὴν ἁρμονίαν εἶδος ἔχειν ἤθους ἢ πάθους· καθάπερ ἡ Λοκριστί. ταύτῃ γὰρ ἔνιοι τῶν γενομένων κατὰ Σιμωνίδην καὶ Πίνδαρον ἐχρήσαντό ποτε καὶ πάλιν κατεφρονήθη."</note> That this Locrian harmony was the same as the Hypodorian is a mere conjecture of Boeckh’s in his *Metrics of Pindar* (p. 212). But the traditions regarding Locrian songs <note>original: "Λοκρικὰ ᾄσματα"</note> found among ancient writers, when combined with the words of Heraclides, lead me instead to believe that the Locrian harmony was not so much a specific tone as a particular style of composition. Therefore, you should be careful not to believe that Xenocritus, who is said to be the inventor of the Locrian mode, was either a contemporary of Sacadas or lived before him simply because Sacadas only knew the three tones named above—as Ulrici did (p. 223)—since it can also be gathered from this that the Locrian harmony was not named in the same way as the Dorian, Lydian, and Phrygian. *Finally, concerning Sacadas of Argos*, besides Plutarch, Pausanias also reported on him in several places. One of these I cited above among the notes for Chapter VIII. According to Book II, 22, 9, Sacadas was the first to introduce flute music <note>The "flute" here refers to the "aulos," a double-reed instrument more like an oboe.</note> at the Delphic festivals in the sacred rites of Apollo, and he appeased the god's hatred, with which he had previously persecuted this music. The songs <note>original: "μέλη" (melē)</note> of Sacadas were still being sung as late as the third Messenian War (IV, 27, 4). His statue, made after the time of Pindar, was placed on Mount Helicon (IX, 30, 2). Another monument was kept at Argos (II, 22, 9). The only surviving trace of Sacadas having any connection with stringed instruments is that Hesychius mentions the *Sacadium*, a sensitive instrument that bore his name. However, we do not know whether all these men who are said to have established the state of music, with the exception of Thaletas, were actually present in Sparta, nor what the specific roles of each were in organizing the Gymnopaedia. And so that no one wonders at Sacadas being an Argive flute-player, it must be kept in mind that the art of Olympus seems to have been brought to Argos through Hierax, about whom Pollux says (IV, 79): *Hierax died young, but he was a servant, student, and beloved of Olympus.* In the festival of the Anthesphoria celebrated among the Argives in honor of Juno <note>The Greek goddess Hera.</note>, the *Hieracian song* <note>original: "μέλος Ἱεράκιον"</note> was played on the flutes. Plutarch mentions his *running-song* <note>original: "ἐνδρομήν"</note> below in Chapter XXVI, played at the festival of the Sthenia.

*concerning the Gymnopaedia*] <note>original Greek: "περὶ τὰς γυμνοπαιδίας"</note> The festival of the Gymnopaedia, in which choruses of naked boys celebrated the memory of those who had fallen bravely in battle, was a very ancient tradition among the Spartans, established, it seems, in honor of Apollo Dromaeus; see Pausanias III, 11, 7, Suidas under this entry, and the material collected here by Bernhardy. Creuzer showed they were older than Thaletas in his *Commentary on Herodotus* (I, p. 230). However, Thaletas was the first to combine them with <term>hyporchemes</term> <note>A type of choral song accompanied by expressive, often mimetic, dancing.</note> and the art of the hyporchemic dance, a notion that reached others besides Plutarch, such as Athenaeus (XIV p. 631, XV p. 678), where the poems or songs of Thaletas at the Gymnopaedia are mentioned; cf. Suidas under *Thaletas*. Hoeck has illustrated their Cretan restoration, following Ephorus in Strabo (X p. 738)

<column-break/>

91
# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER IX.

in his *Creta* (III, 381, 384). On various occasions in later times, it seems that some innovations were made in the solemnities of the Gymnopaedia, such as after the battle at Thyrea (58th Olympiad), according to Suidas, and Mueller in *The Dorians* (II, 315), or after the battle at Thermopylae, according to the *Etymologicum Magnum*.

*demonstrations in Arcadia*] <note>original Greek: "ἀποδείξεις τὰς ἐν Ἀρκαδίᾳ"</note> The word *demonstrations* <note>original: "ἀποδείξεις" (apodeixeis)</note> is to be understood generally as referring to scenic games or some representation of a chorus, not to one specific festival. Regarding the zeal of the Arcadians for musical matters, see Polybius (IV, 20, 21), whom Athenaeus follows (XIV p. 626); cf. Bernhardy, *History of Greek Literature* (I p. 305).

*the Endymatia*] <note>original Greek: "τὰ ἐνδυμάτια"</note> Learned men think the *Endymatia* was a festival in which the statue of Juno was adorned with new garments. But what if you were to think of a certain parade in which masked actors changed their clothes, as you might say in German: "a masked, disguised procession" <note>original German: "ein maskirter, verkleideter aufzug"</note>? The ancients, as far as I know, handed down nothing on this matter.

In other Dorian cities as well, the sacred rites of the gods were celebrated with a specific kind of dance accompanied by the sound of flutes. Certainly, Athenaeus is a witness regarding the Syracusans (XIV p. 629 E): *among the Syracusans there is a certain unique dance of Artemis Chitonia involving the flute.*

*and Xenocritus*] <note>original Greek: "καὶ Ξενόκριτον"</note> As in the scholia of Pindar, the manuscripts in Plutarch also fluctuate between *Xenocritus* in this passage and *Xenocrates* in the following chapter. From Aristoxenus, Diogenes Laertius (IV, 2, 13) mentioned a poet Xenocrates, from which we gain little. Since the same doubt recurs regarding other men of the same name, I maintain that the sculptor Xenocrates in Diogenes is not different from the Xenocritus mentioned by Pausanias (IX, 14, 4).

*those around Sacadas [composed] elegy*] <note>original Greek: "οἱ δὲ περὶ Σακάδαν ἐλεγεῖον"</note> The songs of Sacadas that Pausanias reported on (IV, 27, 4) could have been elegies, but not his *Sack of Troy*, which listed the names of the Achaeans who entered the wooden horse with Ulysses, as mentioned in Athenaeus (XIII, 610 C), although the name of Sacadas is uncertain in that passage.

*the paean, because it differs from the hyporchemes*] <note>original Greek: "ὁ δὲ παιὰν ὅτι διαφορὰν ἔχει πρὸς τὰ ὑπορχήματα"</note> Boeckh explained the difference between the <term>Paean</term> <note>A hymn usually addressed to Apollo, often for healing or victory.</note> and the <term>Hyporcheme</term> according to the ancients in his *Metrics of Pindar* (p. 202), and more accurately Bernhardy in *History of Greek Literature* (II, 1 p. 356 ff.). Thus, the poetic subject matter of both seems to have been nearly the same. But in the hyporcheme, dancing combined with mimetic action prevailed. Furthermore, there seem to have been unique songs for the hyporcheme—that is, to be recited by individuals without the harmony of a chorus—so that it occupied a middle ground between the art of dance <note>original: "orchestrice"</note> and poetry, and could be counted among both in its own right, according to Plutarch in *Table Talk* (IX, 15 p. 748). Besides flutes, they also accompanied the hyporcheme with the lyre and the cithara, if Lucian is to be believed in *On the Dance* (c. 16). In passing, it should be noted that there were many writers who used the word *paean* not for a specific poetic genre, but for almost any kind of song, as witnessed by the Scholion on Aristophanes (p. 215 C): *not only is the hymn sung to Apollo for the removal of a plague called a paean...*

It provides a detailed comparison between different choral genres like the Paean and the Hyporcheme, noting their performance contexts and instrumentation.</summary>
