<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on the history of Greek music, moving from the discussion of the Locrian mode into Chapter X, which focuses on the legendary figure Thaletas of Crete and the evolution of rhythmic forms like the Paean and the Hyporchema.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS IX—X.

...it is said, but also simply every hymn to the gods and to men; some also say the *paean* <note>original Greek: "παιῶνα" — a song of praise or healing, often dedicated to Apollo</note> is understood as a war-song.
<margin>5</margin> *for he has written*] See Bergk’s *Poetae Lyrici Graeci*, the fragments of Pindaric Paeans pages 221—225, and of the Hyporchemata <note>original: "Hyporchematum" — high-energy dance-songs</note> pages 237—243.

### CHAPTER X.

*but if in the Orthian mode*] <note>original: "εἰ δὲ τῷ Ὀρθίῳ νόμῳ"</note> I have written this based on a conjecture, having also removed the particle *de* after *echomen* in place of the common reading: "in the Orthian mode he used musical composition, just as the theorists of harmony say. But we cannot say accurately; for the ancients have said nothing about this," which is absurd. For in every single musical composition it is so obvious that musical melody <note>original: "melopoeiam"</note> must be employed that it requires neither testimony nor doubt. These words seem to have flowed from Aristoxenus, <margin>10</margin> who often cited the writers on harmony.

*concerning Thaletas of Crete*] Thales or *Thaletas* (for the forms *Thales* (not *Thalês*), *Thaletos*, *Thaleti*, *Thaleta* are just as valid and <margin>15</margin> ancient as *Thaletas, Thaleti* in Athenaeus Book XV page 678 C, *Thaleta, Thaletan*; see the passages collected by Hoeck, *Creta* volume III <margin>20</margin> page 339) was, according to the weightiest testimonies of Plutarch and Polymnestus in Pausanias Book I, 14, 3, a man of Gortyn; according to Suidas, he was from Elyrus or Knossos. The latter seems false, for Gortyn and Elyrus were famous for the rites of Apollo, with which the person of Thaletas is closely connected, but Knossos was not. I believe the lexicographer named this <margin>25</margin> city by confusing Thaletas and Epimenides. Regarding the age of Thaletas, there is no reason to depart from the testimony of Glaucus, according to whom he flourished between the years 647 and 586 BC, being active in Sparta around the year 620, in the 40th Olympiad. See Hoeck, work cited, page 341; Mueller, *Dorians* II, 322; Litzinger, *On Thaletas* page 8. The order in which Thaletas is named after Terpander in Aelian's *Varia Historia* XII, <margin>30</margin> 50 also agrees with Glaucus’s testimony. Truly, certain mythical fictions and followers of Euhemerus make him a contemporary of Lycurgus, claiming he met him on the island of Crete and brought him to Sparta, such as Ephorus in Strabo Book X page 482 (followed by <margin>35</margin> Sextus Empiricus, Plutarch in the *Life of Lycurgus* chapter 4), Aristotle in the *Politics* II, 10, as well as Suidas and Diogenes Laertius I, 38, who even contend he surpassed Homer in age. This is just as absurd as if someone were to record King Numa as being among the disciples of Pythagoras <note>A common chronological error in antiquity, as Numa lived long before Pythagoras</note>. On the other hand, there seem to have been those who <margin>40</margin> assigned Thaletas to a much more recent age, since they record Onomacritus of Locri among his friends; Aristotle criticizes these people in *Politics* II, 12. But from this very discrepancy of dates, two things can be gathered: first, that Thaletas was certainly very ancient; second, and much more importantly, it did not <margin>45</margin> seem inconsistent to the Greeks to refer a lyric poet to such a remote age without him holding the office of a priest. Regarding the deeds performed by Thaletas, the reports are no more certain than those regarding his age, as

---

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER X.

is proven by the words of Philodemus in *On Music* column XVIII. For you will find all sorts of things heaped upon his name. Thus Ephorus in Strabo Book X page 736 is the authority for the claim that almost all Cretan songs were attributed to him. And just as <margin>5</margin> fame held that Terpander set the laws of the Spartans to musical modes, similar things seem to have been told about Thaletas, since in Strabo Book X, 733 he is called a "man of lawmaking" <note>original Greek: "νομοθετικὸς ἀνήρ"</note>; on this, see Hoeck, work cited, page 365. Since things are so, it is extremely difficult and slippery if one wishes to follow the conflicting fictions of the ancients and pursue the praises of Thaletas in detail; Bernhardy said this most correctly against Ulrici and O. Mueller in his *History of Greek Literature* volume II, part I, page 529.

*it is disputed whether he was a composer of paeans*] <note>original: "εἰ παιάνων γεγένηται ποιητὴς ἀμφισβητεῖται"</note> There could have been a double cause for this doubt. For either it proceeded from an accurate knowledge of the distinction that exists between a paean and a <margin>15</margin> *hyporchema* <note>a song accompanied by mimetic dance</note>. For imagine—as is likely—that in the time of Glaucus or Aristoxenus, only the words of Thaletas's songs survived and the musical modes had perished; you must concede that from the words alone, according to what I explained a little earlier, it could not be judged whether the words belonged to a paean or a hyporchema. For even if the Cretic rhythm <note>A rhythm of five beats (long-short-long)</note> seemed more characteristic of the hyporchema, what if it was not <margin>20</margin> used in a uniform way everywhere? Perhaps, however, there was simply doubt as to whether the songs that bore Thaletas's name in that age were rightly attributed to him. For the rest, besides Plutarch, Ephorus also knew Thaletas as an author of paeans in Strabo X page 736, as did Porphyry in the *Life of Pythagoras* page 37. <margin>25</margin>

*the melodies of Archilochus*] <note>original: "τὰ Ἀρχιλόχου μέλη"</note> But if Thaletas is said to have imitated the tunes or songs of Archilochus, this should perhaps be understood as a greater variety of rhythms employed by him, which he certainly could not have taken from epic poetry; see Hoeck, work cited, page 356. <margin>30</margin> But what is this: *to extend the melodies to a greater length*? <note>original: "μέλη ἐπὶ τὸ μακρότερον ἐκτεῖναι"</note> Could it mean *meters*? Indeed, since there is talk of the new Paeonic and Cretic rhythms, this is not inconsistent.

*and the Maion*] <note>original: "καὶ Μαίωνα"</note> The books have *and the Magon*. But this rhythm does not exist, and if it did, no proper reasoning for the name could be <margin>35</margin> given. Therefore Burette conjectured *and the Maphonea*, the Maronean rhythm, as if named from Maronea, the wine-rich city of Thrace. Boeckh followed this in *Metrics of Pindar* page 156, thinking that by "Maronean rhythm," the "Greater Ionic" rhythm was meant, so named because the most famous verse of this kind was the Sotadean, and Sotades <margin>40</margin> was a Maronean. But that reasoning is hindered by the fact that, in general, the rhythms of the Greeks followed one another slowly over the course of time; we should not attribute such an artificial rhythm to Thaletas. Rather, after the Cretic rhythm, because of the extreme affinity between the two, we expect the Paeonic. Santen first proposed *Paiona* <note>the Paeon rhythm</note> <margin>45</margin> in *Terentianus Maurus* page 99, followed later by Mueller in *Dorians* I page 352 and Ritschl in the *New Rhenish Museum* volume I page 284, whose argument I wrote above.

*for from the flute-playing of Olympus*] These words do not mean that...

<summary>This page provides a scholarly commentary on the Cretan musician Thaletas, debating his historical dates, his birthplace, and his contributions to Greek rhythm, specifically the Cretic and Paeonic meters. The author critiques ancient sources like Ephorus and Aristotle for chronological errors and explores the technical distinctions between different types of choral songs like the Paean and the Hyporchema.</summary>

<keywords>Thaletas of Crete, Terpander, Archilochus, Cretic rhythm, Paeonic rhythm, Paean, Hyporchema, Ancient Greek music theory, Glaucus, Ephorus, Spartan music history</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Plutarch's "On Music," specifically concluding the discussion on Thaletas and the Cretan style before moving into Chapter XI, which deals with the complex origins of the enharmonic scale and the legendary musician Olympus.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS X—XI.</header>

...so that we might believe Olympus invented the Cretan and Paeonian rhythm, but rather it indicates that a certain closer necessity intervened between Cretan music and Phrygian flute-playing <note>original Latin: "auleticam" — the art of playing the *aulos*, a double-reed instrument similar to an oboe</note>, which was otherwise the case in the music of Terpander: and in Archilochus, who generally flourished more in poetic praise than in music, certainly less than in the case of Thaletas. Thus rightly says Hoeck in the place cited, page 361. But why that learned man contended that these words should be understood as referring to the "Greater Olympus," I for my part do not understand.

*having affairs*] <note>original Greek: "πράγματα ἐχουσῶν" — literally "having matters" or "involving difficulties"</note> The words which are commonly read thus: "for they say he became a poet of heroic themes involving difficulties," lack good sense. Therefore, Burette (Volume X, page 308) proposed "prideful snortings" <note>original Greek: "φρυάγματα" (phryagmata)</note> instead of "matters" <note>original: "πράγματα" (pragmata)</note>, so that the meaning would be: he is said to have treated heroic arguments in songs in a boastful and even swollen manner. Yet the proper meaning of the word *phryagma* seems to oppose this conjecture, as it is used for the neighing of a high-spirited horse in Aeschylus (*Seven Against Thebes* 230, 460), Sophocles (*Electra* 717), and Xenophon (*On Horsemanship* 11, 12); or for children boasting because of the nobility of their parents in Plutarch's *On the Education of Children* chapter 2 (cf. Wyttenbach on this passage, page 70). Truly, Proclus in chapter 16 says: "he delighted in the *iobacchus* <note>a festive song for Dionysus</note> in the feasts and sacrifices of Dionysus, immersed in much *phryagma* [pride/excitement]." What Geppert discussed regarding this passage of Plutarch in *The Greek Theater* (page 30, note 5) is vain and obscure. Since Plutarch wishes to prove to his readers that even the songs of Xenocritus ought to be considered among the *Hyporchemata* <note>dance-songs</note> rather than the *Paeans*, nothing was more fitting for this purpose than to show that a certain mimetic action was inherent in them; perhaps we should read "dramas" <note>original: "δράματα" (dramata)</note> instead of "matters." Indeed, it seems quite probable that those songs in which heroic arguments were treated in a certain dramatic and lyric form could easily have been considered dithyrambs; cf. Ulrici page 472, compared with Welcker in *Jahn's Annals* 1829, I page 166. I did not refrain from receiving this emendation into the context of the words because it seemed too uncertain, but because Amyot omitted the words "having matters" in his translation. Therefore, I have considered it an interpolation, and a corrupt one at that. Furthermore, I suspect a corruption underlies the same word in Dio Chrysostom (Oration IV, 52) in the words of the boy Alexander: "For what is left for me after I have conquered Babylon and Susa and Ecbatana and the *pragmaton* [affairs] in India?" Read: *Brachmanon* [the Brahmans].

### CHAPTER XI.

*to have been the inventor of the enharmonic genus*] <note>original: "τοῦ ἐναρμονίου γένους εὑρετὴς γεγενῆσθαι" — The enharmonic was the most complex of the three ancient Greek musical scales, using quarter-tones</note> I hardly think there exists in this whole booklet a more difficult or more intricate passage, which the interpreters have failed to satisfy. See Burney’s *History of Music* Volume I, page 34, with whom Bellermann agrees. See also Burette Volume XIX page 274 ff., Forkel’s *History of Music* I page 335, and Trinkler page 17 ff., who illustrated the matter best. Add what I myself wrote in the *Pedagogical Review* July 1856, page 33 ff. Either the passage is foully corrupt, or Plutarch poorly understood the words of Aristoxenus. The sum of the discussion turns on this: that Aristoxenus thinks Olympus

<column-break/>

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XI.</header>

was considered the inventor of the enharmonic genus because of the *spondaic trope*. The specific and peculiar nature of this trope <note>a "trope" here refers to a particular melodic style or mode</note> was seen in the omission of certain sounds, but omitted differently than in the chromatic scale. For in the chromatic, the "movable notes" <note>original: "φθόγγοι κινούμενοι" (phthongoi kinoumenoi) — the inner notes of a four-note scale that could be tuned differently</note> remained, even if they were tuned differently than in the diatonic scale. On the contrary, in the spondaic trope, if I am not mistaken, one of the movable sounds of the diatonic was utterly removed from the middle. By this, it happened that there was a certain agreement between the spondaic trope and the enharmonic division of the tetrachord <note>a four-note scale segment</note>, namely the "uncomposite interval of a ditone" <note>a large gap of two whole tones, characteristic of the enharmonic style</note>. Therefore, it was not absurd to attribute the invention of this genus to Olympus.

*the system consisting of analogy*] <note>original: "τὸ ἐκ τῆς ἀναλογίας συνεστηκὸς σύστημα"</note> What kind of system this was, I do not grasp. For if he composed the whole system according to the analogy of a single tetrachord in which the *lichanos* <note>the "forefinger" note, the third note from the top</note> was omitted, he undoubtedly omitted the *paranete* <note>the note next to the highest</note> in the following tetrachord. But in Chapter XIX we read that the *tritae* and *netae*, not the *paranetae*, were omitted in the spondaic trope. If these things are correct, I for my part miss the "analogy" of the system.

*for they place the spondasmus first among these*] <note>original: "τιθέασι γὰρ τούτων πρῶτον τὸν σπονδειασμόν"</note> I have written this from conjecture because of the following words which deal with the *more intense spondasmus*. The books read "spondeion." But a *Spondeus*, if you look at the musical notion of the word, is nothing other than a certain mode of chant used in libations, the words of which were conceived in a spondaic meter (Scholiast on Hephaestion, page 82, 4). The *Spondaula* <note>a flute player for sacrifices</note> accompanied you on long flutes. Cf. Marius Victorinus I, page 2487: "the *spondeus* — named from the drawing out of its song, which is produced through long flutes in temples by those supplicating. Whence also the *spondaulae* who were accustomed to blow such flutes, each of whom retains as much in the *arsis* [upbeat] as in the *thesis* [downbeat]." Therefore, the spondaic song had something elevated, slow, and severe, similar to our own music which is called a *choral*, in which the individual parts of each meter fill out almost the same amount of time. Cf. Santen on Terentianus Maurus, page 63. But what has this to do with the enharmonic division of the tetrachord? Aristides Quintilianus (page 28) teaches what a *Spondiasmus* is. Namely, besides the six more common divisions of the tetrachord (enharmonic, intense or tonic chromatic, soft chromatic, hemiolic chromatic, intense diatonic, soft diatonic), of which cf. Aristoxenus *Harmonics* II page 50, Euclid page 3, 9 ff., there were three "affections of the intervals" <note>original: "πάθη τῶν διαστημάτων" (pathe ton diastematon)</note> so called "because of the rarity of their use," employed by the ancients for the "differences of the harmonies": namely *eclysis* (a lowering by three uncomposite quarter-tones), *spondiasmus* (a raising by the same), and *ecbole* (a raising by five quarter-tones). Bacchius the Elder (page 9) is the authority that *eclysis* and *ecbole* have a place only in the enharmonic genus, and cannot be produced by the common movable sounds of a single tetrachord. Whether what applies to the *spondiasmus* also applies to the *more intense spondasmus*, I do not know; I mention this against Trinkler (page 19) who was unmindful of this difficulty.

*to be likened to the diatonic*] Burette arranged the following with a comma interposed at page 288: "and if [not] someone in that of the tonic—"

<summary>This page provides a technical philological commentary on the origins of the enharmonic scale in Greek music, specifically examining the role of the legendary Phrygian musician Olympus and the "spondaic trope." The author debates various manuscript emendations and explains ancient musical terms like spondasmus, eclysis, and ecbole in the context of tetrachord divisions.</summary>

<keywords>Plutarch, Aristoxenus, Olympus, music theory, enharmonic genus, spondaic trope, tetrachord, spondasmus, eclysis, ecbole, ancient Greek music, philology, musical intervals</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Plutarch's "On Music," specifically moving from the analysis of musical scales (Chapter XI) into the history of musical innovation and the shift toward the "New Music" of the late classical period (Chapter XII). It follows the discussion of Olympus and the origins of the enharmonic scale.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XI—XII.</header>

...peculiar, and clearly also false and discordant, for anyone who posits such things; false because of the underlying facts; and discordant because it would result in two successive "ditones" <note>original Greek: "δίτονα" — an interval of two major tones, roughly a major third</note> being placed together, one uncompounded and the other compounded. Although these explanations are probable, I have refused to accept them because of the rather harsh syntax and because they do not resolve all the difficulties.

<margin>5</margin> *for the enharmonic "dense" in the middle strings*] These words are to be understood as referring to the simple division of the "tetrachord" <note>a scale of four notes</note> through the enharmonic genus, in which two enharmonic "dieses" <note>original: "dieses" — microtonal intervals smaller than a semitone</note> followed the "mese" <note>the middle string or central note of the Greek scale</note>. For the rest, Burette rightly notes (XIX, p. 301): 'but what our author adds is not exact enough, namely, that today they employ the <margin>10</margin> enharmonic "dense" <note>original Greek: "πυκνόν" (pyknon) — a close grouping of three notes in the lower part of a tetrachord</note> on the *meses* or fourth strings. For it is certain that they used it in the same way on the *hypate* <note>the lowest string</note> of the first or lowest tetrachord—that is, between the *hypate* and the *parhypate*, and between the latter and the *lichanos*. One should therefore read in the text: *for the enharmonic "dense" on the hypates and meses*: <margin>15</margin> and perhaps Plutarch had written it thus.' The same applies to Plutarch’s following words.

### CHAPTER XII.

<margin>20</margin> *the innovation of Terpander*] In other places in Plutarch, the word "innovation" <note>original Greek: "καινοτομία" (kainotomia)</note> is used to mean "novelty," but here it signifies a "reforming innovation," as in many passages of Plato. The phrase "rhythm newly innovated" appears in Dioscorides (Anthologia Palatina VII, 707). We do not know exactly what Terpander's rhythmic innovation was. But without doubt, it was Terpander <margin>25</margin> who introduced the first lyric rhythms, so that we should agree with Ulrici (p. 349), who believes that the beginnings of the formation of "strophic" songs <note>songs organized into repeating stanzas</note>—which Alcman and Alcaeus later knew in a perfected state, such that they cannot be considered its inventors—originated with Terpander, especially since the ancients record no other inventor: <margin>30</margin> unless the first traces of strophic formation already existed in Homer's *Catalogue of Ships* and in Hesiod's *Theogony*.

<margin>35</margin> *which he also used*] Thus Duebner follows Wyttenbach's conjecture. The books read "and which." But the "and" is redundant and perhaps should be placed before "Polymnestus." To Wyttenbach, the speech did not seem intact. What if the name of Polymnestus is due to an interpolator? Amyot translates: 'for the first innovation of Terpander introduced a beautiful method into music, which he himself used while adhering to the beautiful form: Thaletas and Sacadas did the same.'

<margin>40</margin> *and the Stesichorean*] Burette, following Valgulius, already saw that "and" must be inserted. Then I wrote "these very things" <note>original Latin: "καὶ αὐταὶ" (kai autai)</note> instead of "these women" <note>original: "καὶ αὗται" (kai autai with different accentuation)</note>, as was written a little before: "and he himself, however, holding to the beautiful form." The "innovation" of Alcman and Stesichorus was seen in the fact that <margin>45</margin> they applied strophes even to songs destined for choral use, which the Aeolian lyric poets used only in solo songs. The stories told about Alcman in Himerius (Oration IV, 3) are less relevant here: "Alcman, the one who mixed the Dorian lyre with Lydian songs," regarding which Wernsdorf says (p. 477):

<column-break/>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XII.</header>

'the sophist says he tempered the Dorian lyre with Lydian modes, that is, he was the first to divert the severity of lyric songs toward light and amorous subjects.'

*Crexus, Timotheus, and Philoxenus*] Through these men, <margin>5</margin> Plutarch transitions to the most recent age of Greek music, in which, having been carried to the highest degree of perfection, it began to fall apart again. Into this age fall—and in like manner draw our attention—the *Attic Dithyrambic poets* and the *Theban Flute-players*. Both allowed music, now loosened from the tighter bonds of those arts to which it had previously been <margin>10</margin> restricted, to wander more freely, naturally despising the customs of antiquity. For the Attic "dithyramb" <note>a wild, choral hymn to Dionysus</note> generally agrees with that genre of musical composition which we call by the Italian word *opera*, in which everyone knows music prevails over poetry—a fact which many, like the admirers of antiquity in Athens, <margin>15</margin> bear with difficulty. If we seek the specific innovations of the Dithyrambic poets, besides Chapter XXX of this booklet, there is a classic passage in Dionysius of Halicarnassus (Comp. V, c. 19): 'the dithyramb-makers even changed the modes, creating Dorian, Phrygian, and Lydian melodies in the same song. And they altered the melodies, <margin>20</margin> sometimes making them enharmonic, sometimes chromatic, and sometimes diatonic. And they continued to exercise great license with rhythms, specifically those in the time of Philoxenus, Timotheus, and Telestes. For among the ancients, the dithyramb was a strictly ordered thing.' Indeed, by frequently admitting changes of tones, the edge of judgment regarding the graceful <margin>25</margin> properties of each tone was dulled; from which it happened that a single "unchangeable system" <note>term: "systema immutabile" — the standard fixed scale of Greek music</note> was established, and various transpositions of it were employed, as discussed above in Chapter VIII. Furthermore, in the same age, the enharmonic and chromatic styles seem to have fallen more and more into disuse in favor of the single "diatonic" <note>the standard scale of whole and half steps used in modern music</note>, so that soon they had no place <margin>30</margin> except in that "promiscuous" or "common" genus mentioned by Euclid. The first foundations of this transformation of Greek music were laid by *Lasus of Hermione*, a contemporary of Pindar. Other innovators followed this first one: Phrynis, Melanippides, and Cinesias. Timotheus and Philoxenus surpassed them all in audacity. <margin>35</margin> I have spoken of Timotheus in the previous note. Regarding Philoxenus, it is helpful to consult Bernhardy (II, 1 p. 669 ff.). Nothing is certain regarding *Crexus*; besides Plutarch, Philodemus mentions him in his treatise *On Music*.

*the mode now named*] I have added the last word <note>original Greek: "τρόπον" (tropon) — mode or way</note> by conjecture, following the lead of Scheibel (on Melanippides II, p. 18). Certainly, <margin>40</margin> "rhythm" cannot be understood here. Burette increased this error by another, taking the words "humane" and "thematic" as referring to the Ionic rhythm, which he persuaded himself was called "humane" <note>original: "φιλάνθρωπον" (philanthropon)</note> because it was calm and pleasant, foreign to the severity of ancient meters: and "thematic" <note>original: "θεματικόν" (thematikon)</note> because music for which prizes (*themata*) were offered in <margin>45</margin> public games was of the Ionic genus. In this, only the point that "humane" signifies calm and lovely is correct; one example of this meaning may be produced from Plutarch as well as other writers...

<sig>7</sig>

<summary>This page analyzes the historical shift from the "severe" traditional styles of Terpander and Stesichorus to the complex, multi-modal "New Music" of the Dithyrambic poets like Timotheus and Philoxenus. It describes the decline of the microtonal enharmonic genus in favor of the diatonic scale, likening the later style to modern opera.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," transitioning from the technical discussion of musical theory in Chapter XII to the historical and ritualistic aspects of music in Chapters XIII and XIV. It maintains the previous page's focus on the tension between traditional musical laws and the innovations of the "New Music."</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XII—XIV.</header>

...to place this, and that it pertains to musical matters, from Themistius, *Oration* XXXIII, p. 364, c: "Aristoxenus—unless it were possible at the same time to remain faithful to the laws of the art and to sing things pleasing to the many, he chose the art instead of popularity <note>original Greek: "φιλανθρωπίας" (philanthropia) — here meaning a desire to please the public</note>." Regarding the word *thematic* <note>original Latin: "thematicus"</note>, the opinion of Ulrici in *History of Lyric Poetry* II, p. 597, is not unpleasing; he wished it to be <margin>5</margin> understood as singing through intervals in which "dry" flutes <note>original Latin: "assae tibiae" — flutes playing without vocal accompaniment</note> were interposed, proceeding in such a way that whenever a new part of the melody began, the rhythm and harmony would change; in German: "on the pausing and restarting of the song after the instrumental interlude, so that with each new entry a <margin>10</margin> new rhythm and certainly also a new harmony of the key began." If this is correct, both terms signify nothing other than the common practice of <term>dithyrambic</term> <note>Dithyrambs were wild, passionate choral hymns, often criticized by traditionalists for their complexity</note> composers in musical composition, which utterly overthrew the ancient and simple practice.
<margin>15</margin>

### Chapter XIII.

**the manual part of music** <note>original Greek: "χειρουργικῷ μέρει τῆς μουσικῆς" (cheirourgiko merei tes mousikes)</note>] Generally speaking, the "manual part" corresponds to that part of music which we call "practical," but according to the discipline of the ancients, it corresponds <margin>20</margin> with the second part of practical music, the "expressive" <note>original Greek: "ἐξαγγελτικῷ" (exangeltiko)</note>, which also included the "instrumental" <note>original Greek: "ὀργανικόν" (organikon)</note>, that is, the doctrine of the art of singing to the flute and singing to the lyre. Compare Rossbach’s *Rhythm*, p. 5, on the division of musical discipline.

<margin>25</margin>
### Chapter XIV.

**Alcaeus in one of his hymns**] Alcaeus in the hymn written to Apollo, the knowledge of which Himerius (*Oration* XIV, 10) preserved, collected with Pausanias (X, 8, 10). See Wernsdorf’s *Himerius*, p. 623, and Bergk’s *Lyric Poets*, p. 569.
**from the dances and the sacrifices**] Nevertheless, the sacred rites of Apollo <margin>30</margin> generally disdained the <term>tibia</term> <note>The "tibia" or "aulos" was a double-reed pipe, often translated as a flute</note>, so much so that they were employed in the contests only during the first Pythiad and lay neglected by the second; see Pausanias X, 7, 5. On the island of Delos, following the example of Crete, the flute seems to have been received into the worship of this god even in the most ancient times. Lucian, in *On the Dance* ch. 16, speaks of choirs of boys who danced <term>Hyporchemata</term> <note>A lively type of choral song and dance performed in honor of Apollo</note> at the Delian rites to the <margin>35</margin> combined sound of flutes and the lyre. However, it should be noted that the author of the *Homeric Hymn to Apollo* was still ignorant of the use of the flute in the Delian rites.
**the consecration of his statue in Delos**] Plutarch is mistaken, along with Anticles and Istros, regarding the age of the statue, which does not go back beyond the 55th <margin>40</margin> Olympiad <note>Approximately 560 BCE</note>. For it had been fashioned by Tectaeus and Angelion, who, according to Sylburg's conjecture, were disciples of Dipoenus and Scyllis; see Pausanias II, 32, 5 and IX, 35, 3; compare Hoeck’s *Creta* III, p. 385. Regarding the meaning of the statue, as well as the <term>Graces</term> <note>The Charites, goddesses of charm and beauty</note> appearing in place of the Muses as musical goddesses, see K. Schwenck in *New Rhenish Museum* I, p. 638, against Creuzer (*On Gemology* p. 112; *On the Karlsruhe Vases* <margin>45</margin> p. 61). For the Graces, by their primary and true nature, are elsewhere also considered goddesses of music and song, as in Pindar’s *Olympian Odes*.

---

<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XIV.</header>

XIV and Theognis, line 15. The festivals of the Graces celebrated at Orchomenos from ancient times were joined with musical contests; see O. Mueller’s *Orchomenos* (edited by Schneidewin) p. 172, collected with the inscriptions in Boeckh’s *Public Economy of the Athenians* II, p. 359 ff., and the *Corpus of Greek Inscriptions* number <margin>5</margin> 1583, 1584.
**Anticles and Istros**] The *Epiphanies* <note>original Greek: "Ἐπιφανείας" — accounts of the miraculous appearances of gods</note> or *On the Apparitions of the Gods* by Istros was also mentioned by Harpocration under the word *Pharmakos* and Photius under the word *trittyan*; compare Voss’s *Greek Historians* p. 145, Westermann, and C. Mueller’s *Fragments of Greek Historians* I, 422, 33. Phylarchus <margin>10</margin> had written an *Epiphany of Jove* in the same work, volume I, p. XXIX. Since other authors are silent about Anticles, Valesius (in Harpocration p. 134, 111) and Burette seem to have correctly determined that he is the same person as Anticlides the historian, who is known to have written similar books, and whose mythological opinion Plutarch attacks in *On Isis and Osiris* ch. 37. For "Anticles" is "Anticlides," <margin>15</margin> just as "Megacles" is "Megaclides" (Bernhardy, *History of Greek Literature* II, 1 p. 260), and similarly "Philo" for "Philonides," "Execestus" for "Execestides," and "Protagoras" for "Protagorides." In general, many proper names ending in *-kles* have a derived form in *-kleides*, of which Papias collected examples in the preface to his <margin>20</margin> *Greek Onomasticon* p. 20, even if it cannot be demonstrated in every case that the same man is hidden under both forms. Examples of this double formation of proper names are not lacking in writers after the age of Alexander the Great; see Lobeck’s *Pathology* p. 204. Plutarch depends on his sources in this matter and is not consistent with himself. The story which <margin>25</margin> happened to Thymbraeus in the temple of Apollo was brought forward from Anticlides by the Scholiast <note>An ancient commentator on a text</note> on the *Iliad* VII, 44; a similar one is in the Scholiast on the *Odyssey* XVI, 471, though the book is not named.
**of those under Hercules [the Meropes]**] Regarding the Meropes, the ancient inhabitants of the island of Cos against whom Hercules waged war, Plutarch himself narrated certain things in *Greek Questions* 58. *Meropis* was the ancient <margin>30</margin> name of the island according to the "eponym" <note>The person for whom a place is named</note> born of the earth, as seen in Stephanus of Byzantium under the words *Kos* and *Merops*, Suidas under *Kos*, Pausanias VI, 14, 12, Diodorus XIII, 42, and Strabo XV p. 686, 701. The Scholiast on the *Iliad* I, 250, knew of Merops the son of Hyas, "who after the flood was the first to gather men together into a community," from which they took the name *meropes* <note>A poetic term for "articulate-speaking men"</note>. Moreover, since neither <margin>35</margin> Amyot nor Valgulius (about whom Burette spoke) seem to have read the word "Meropes" in their copies, I have included it in brackets as an interpolation.
**to the boy bringing it down**] Aelian narrates in *Historical Miscellany* III, 1, that every nine years the Delphians sent several youths of noble birth to Tempe, <margin>40</margin> with a "Chief Envoy" <note>original Greek: "ἀρχιθεώρῳ" (architheoro)</note> leading the way. When they arrived in the valley, they offered a splendid sacrifice to the God, and after this was finished, they returned to Delphi crowned with the laurel of the god. The Tempe laurel was the prize for victors in the Pythian games; see the Scholiast on Pindar’s *Pythian Odes* p. 298, where the origin of the custom is traced back to Apollo himself. It is <margin>45</margin> probable that Plutarch had this procession in mind.
**the sacred things from the Hyperboreans**] The stories about the Hyperboreans <note>A mythical people living "beyond the North Wind"</note>, of whom Hesiod made the first mention, are quite recent. Along with Niebuhr, I believe the Hyperboreans are Pelasgians in Italy, and—

<vocab>Aristoxenus, music, dithyrambica, Alcaeus, Apollo, Delos, Plutarchus, Anticles, Istros, Meropes, Hyperborei, Gratiae, Orchomenos</vocab>

It also includes a philological discussion on the naming conventions of ancient Greek historians like Anticlides.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," moving from the ritual origins of music in Chapter XIV to a critique of theatrical corruption and the ethical impact of various musical "modes" in Chapter XV. It bridges the gap between ancient mythical foundations and the later decay of musical standards in the Roman and Byzantine eras.</meta>

->page 100<-
->COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XIV—XV.<-

...and I accept the fable regarding the migration of the Hyperborean sacred rites to the island of Delos <note>original Latin: "migratione sacrorum Hyperboreorum in insulam Delum"</note> (see Herodotus IV, 33; Pausanias I, 31) as referring to the most ancient connection between Pelasgian and Greek religions. Compare what I said above regarding Olene in Chapter III.

<margin>5</margin>
### CHAPTER XV.

**they bring into the theaters** <note>original Greek: "εἰς τὰ θέατρα εἰσάγουσι" (eis ta theatra eisagousi)</note>] To this point belong the words from Chapter XXVII: "Indeed, in our own times, the form of corruption has increased so much that there is neither memory nor any understanding of the educational style of music; rather, all who touch music have turned toward theatrical music." Compare *Table Talk* <note>original Latin: "Quaestt. Conviv." referring to Plutarch's *Quaestiones Convivales*</note> IX, page 1332. In these complaints about the license of the theater, Plutarch has imitated <margin>10</margin> Aristoxenus, whose words in Athenaeus XIV, page 632 B, are as follows: "And so we too, since the theaters have become barbarized <note>original Greek: "ἐκβεβαρβάρωται" (ekbebarbarōtai)</note> and this common music has proceeded into great corruption, being few by ourselves, we remember what music once was." Even Athenaeus deplores the depraved condition of music <margin>15</margin> in his own age, along with the unbridled luxury of clothing and footwear (I, page 18 E; XIV, page 631 F). Now, only the applause of the theaters is sought as the highest end of the art, and that depravity cannot be removed unless there is a return to the simplicity of ancient music (ibid, page 633 C). Even the Alexandrians, whose studies regarding music <margin>20</margin> Athenaeus praised (IV, page 174 B, page 176 E), delighted in depraved and languid music, as witnessed by Dio Chrysostom in *Oration* XXXII. The people of Tarsus also cultivated the soft and delicate music of the barbarians more than the good and noble music of the Greeks, as the same author states in *Oration* XXXIII, 42: "Just as a certain <margin>25</margin> Ionian harmony <note>a "mode" or scale</note> prevailed, and another Dorian and Phrygian and Lydian; so now the music of the Aradians prevails, and the Phoenician rhythms please you, and you have loved this rhythm exceptionally, just as <margin>30</margin> others love the <term>spondaic</term> <note>a slow, solemn rhythm used in libations</note>." The lyre and the flute <note>original Latin: "tibia"</note> were held in contempt as "ancient and still harsh instruments of a certain rustic music." Regarding the latest age of Athenian music, there is a notable passage in Philostratus’ *Life of Apollonius of Tyana* IV, 21: "Apollonius is said to have rebuked the Athenians concerning the <margin>35</margin> festival of Dionysus, which is held for them in the season of the month Anthesterion. For he thought they flocked to the theater to listen to monodies and melodies, and the <term>parabases</term> <note>a part of ancient comedy where the chorus addresses the audience directly</note> and rhythms used in comedy and tragedy. But when he heard that, at the signaling of a flute, they perform twisting dances <note>original Greek: "λυγισμοὺς" (lygismous)</note> and, in the midst of the epic poetry and theology of Orpheus, they act some parts as the Hours, others as Nymphs, and <margin>40</margin> others as Bacchantes, he set himself to rebuke this," etc. In this passage, the interpreters are mistaken in translating: "with the flute accompanying, they lead winding dances," for "winding/twisting" <note>original Greek: "λυγισμοὺς"</note> pertains to the "signaling of the flute," so that it should be translated: "when he heard that they dance while the flute plays winding <margin>45</margin> melodies."
Finally, when theatrical music had so far prevailed that the other types of music lay entirely uncultivated, it happened that—

---

->page 101<-
->COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XV.<-

—among the Byzantines, all secular music was held in contempt as the dishonorable and unseemly delight of the lowest common people. This was the severe judgment of the Church. Thus, Emperor Leo forbade music from being practiced by anyone on Sundays. So great was the change <margin>5</margin> of the times in Greece. The sole patrons of entirely neglected music were jesters <note>original Latin: "ioculatores"</note>, or by the Greek name <term>thymelici</term> <note>stage-performers, specifically those associated with the altar of Dionysus</note>, a vile and contemptible class of men. Thus, the writers of Byzantine history cry out that morals have perished whenever the emperors have admitted music into the court and even into royal banquets (see Reiske on Constantine's <margin>10</margin> *Book of Ceremonies*, page 363). But long before this, secular music had been an offense to both Christians and pagans, about which Julian the Apostate testifies at the beginning of the *Misopogon*: "The mode of education now prevailing among free men removes the music found in songs. For it seems shameful now to practice music, which long ago used to seem like <margin>15</margin> possessing wealth unjustly." Truly, Plutarch's words are not to be taken as if the study of severe and genuine music had been entirely buried in oblivion, of which certainly some traces remained until the age of Theodosius. To this point, among other things, belongs the 56th letter of the same Julian, written to Dioscorus, the leader of a music school in Alexandria, in <margin>20</margin> which the emperor promises that he will bestow rewards upon the study of "sacred music," that is, the singing of hymns and <term>nomoi</term> <note>traditional melodic patterns or "laws"</note> in pagan sacrifices. However, these traces are very rare.
**Plato in the third book of the Republic** <note>original Greek: "Πλάτων ἐν τῷ τρίτῳ τῆς Πολιτείας"</note>] He refers to the passage of Plato in *Republic* III, page 398 sq., where these things are read: "Which then are the wailing <margin>25</margin> harmonies? — The Mixolydian and the Syntonolydian and others of that sort. Are these not then to be removed, I said? For they are useless even for women who must be decent, let alone for men." And a little further on: "Which then are the soft and convivial harmonies? The Ionian, he said, and the Lydian, <margin>30</margin> those which are called 'slack.' Will you have any use for these, my friend, for warlike men? Not at all, he said; but it seems you have the Dorian and the Phrygian remaining." Compare *Laches*, page 188 D: "And such a man seems to me to be truly a musician, having harmonized a most beautiful harmony, not a lyre nor instruments of play, but having harmonized his own life itself, his life in accord with his words and his deeds, simply in the Dorian <margin>35</margin> mode and not the Ionian—I think neither the Phrygian nor the Lydian—but that which is the only truly Greek harmony."
**to play a funeral song on the flute in the Lydian mode** <note>original Greek: "ἐπικήδειον αὐλῆσαι λυδιστί"</note>] Clement of Alexandria seems to have drawn from this passage in *Stromata* I, page 132, 42, where Olympus is said to have discovered the Lydian harmony.
<margin>40</margin>
**Melanippides — by Anthippus** <note>original Greek: "Μελανιππίδην — ὑπ᾿ ᾿Ανθίππου"</note>] Unless the name of Melanippides were corrupt, it could be sufficiently proved from this one passage that whatever has been handed down to us concerning the ancient music of the Greeks is highly uncertain. But it seems to me that the name of Anthippus already lies hidden here under "Melanippides," which certainly should have been restored in the following section <margin>45</margin> following Bergk in the lyric poets from Valgulius’s version: "In the Paeans indeed Pindar sings that in the nuptials of Niobe, Anthippus first taught the Lydian harmony." Compare Pollux IV, 78: "The Syntonolydian (this is the Lydian harmony itself), which Anthippus discovered."

<summary>This page explores the perceived decline of Greek music from a noble educational tool to a corrupt theatrical spectacle, citing various ancient authorities like Plutarch, Plato, and Julian the Apostate. It contrasts the "slack" and "wailing" musical modes rejected by philosophers with the "Dorian" mode, which was seen as the only truly Greek and virtuous harmony.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," concluding the discussion on Chapter XV regarding the inventors of the Lydian mode and moving into Chapters XVI and XVII, which analyze the emotional character of the Mixolydian mode and the influential musical theories of Damon of Athens.</meta>

->page 102<-
->COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XV—XVI.<-

For the arguments put forward by Ulricius (page 592)—that perhaps Melanippides first composed the "Death of Python" for solo pipes <note>original Latin: "assis tibiis"</note>, while Olympus composed it for singing with the flute <note>original: "aulocdia"</note>—are not very convincing. Furthermore, the words should not be interpreted as if Anthippus was the first to use the Lydian mode <note>term: "harmony" or "mode" refers to the specific tuning and emotional character of a scale</note> at the wedding of Niobe. Rather, the phrase *at the wedding of Niobe* <note>original Greek: "ἐπὶ τοῖς Νιόβης γάμοις"</note> refers to the *hymns of praise* <note>original Greek: "ἐν παιᾶσιν" (paeans)</note>, and should be translated: Pindar (fragment 37. 38. Bergk. *Lyric Poets*, page 224) in a certain hymn, in which he happened to mention the wedding of Niobe, also inserted a mention of Anthippus as the inventor of the Lydian mode.

<margin>10</margin> **others say Torrhebus** <note>original Greek: "ἄλλοι δὲ Τόρρηβον"</note>] Regarding Torrhebus, a musician of the mythical age, there is testimony from Nicolaus of Damascus found in Stephen of Byzantium (under the entry "Torrhebus"). Based on G. Hermann’s correction (*Opuscula* II, page 290), it should be read as follows: *wandering near a certain lake, which was named Torrhebia after him, he heard the voices of the Nymphs, whom the Lydians also call Muses; he was taught music by them and he himself taught the Lydians. And because of this, the songs* <margin>15</margin> *were called Torrhebian.* I have decided to adopt the spelling of the name with a double "r" from Stephen.

**Dionysius Iambus** <note>original Greek: "Διονύσιος ὁ Ἴαμβος"</note>] Dionysius, surnamed "Iambus," was a poet (see Clement of Alexandria, *Stromata* V, page 673) and a grammarian. He is cited *in his work on dialects* <note>original Greek: "ἐν τῷ περὶ διαλέκτων"</note> by Athenaeus (VII, 7). Regarding his era, <margin>20</margin> Suidas (under "Aristophanes") testifies that he was the teacher of Aristophanes of Byzantium around the 160th Olympiad <note>roughly 140 BCE</note>. See Jonsius, *Historical Script* III, 8, page 251; Nauck, *Aristophanes of Byzantium*, page 2. Burette remarks: "his talent for iambic verse and his slanderous temperament no doubt earned him the nickname 'Iambe'." <margin>25</margin>

***

### CHAPTER XVI.

**the Mixolydian is passionate** <note>original Greek: "ἡ μιξολύδιος δὲ παθητική τίς ἐστι"</note>] This is not different from Aristotle (*Politics* VIII, 7), who says that the Mixolydian mode inspires a sense of sadness and longing for grief. <margin>30</margin>

**in the historical memoirs of harmony** <note>original Greek: "ἐν δὲ τοῖς ἱστορικοῖς τῆς ἁρμονικῆς ὑπομνήμασι"</note>] I have added the last word based on Wyttenbach’s conjecture.

**Pythoclides the flute-player** <note>original Greek: "Πυθοκλείδην τὸν αὐλητήν"</note>] Regarding Pythoclides of Ceos, who is praised as an excellent musician by Plato (*Protagoras*, page 316 E), the Scholiast <note>an ancient commentator</note> on Plato’s <margin>35</margin> *Alcibiades* (page 118 C) says: *he was a musician, a teacher of solemn music, and a Pythagorean; his student was Agathocles, whose student was Lamprocles, whose student was Damon.* He lived in Athens even before the Persian War; see Schneidewin, *Eustathius' Prooemium to Pindar*, page 20. According to Aristotle, he had also taught Pericles music (Plutarch, *Pericles* 4). But he cannot <margin>40</margin> be regarded as the inventor of the Mixolydian mode. For Plato certainly would not have praised the inventor of a "plaintive" mode that was considered unsuitable even for women. Furthermore, the invention of the Mixolydian is attributed to Terpander in Chapter XXVIII.

**Lamprocles the Athenian** <note>original Greek: "Λαμπροκλέα τὸν Ἀθηναῖον"</note>] Bellermann has illustrated the words of Plutarch or Lysis regarding <margin>45</margin> the "disjunct" <note>original Latin: "diazeuctico," a technical term for a specific interval between musical scales</note> sound of the Mixolydian in his work on diagrams (page 12). *Lamprocles*, the son of Midon, according to the Scholiast on Plato just mentioned, was a student of Agathocles and a teacher of Damon.

->page 103<-
->COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XVI—XVII.<-

He is undoubtedly the one praised as a dithyrambic poet <note>a composer of wild, choral hymns to Dionysus</note> by Athenaeus (XI, page 491 C). The Scholiast on Aristophanes' *Clouds* (line 966) mentions his hymn to Pallas Athena. See Bernhardy, *History of Greek Literature* II, 1, page 561. I would argue that this Lamprocles is scarcely different from Lamprus, the Athenian <margin>5</margin> musician and contemporary of Socrates, who was the master of Sophocles in musical matters (Bergk, *Commentary on Attic Comedy fragments*, page 376), whom Plato seems to have held in low esteem according to the *Menexenus* (page 236 A). Compare also Athenaeus XI, page 506 F.

**which is opposite to the Mixolydian** <note>original Greek: "ἤπερ ἐναντία τῇ Μιξολυδιστί"</note>] I have written *which* <note>original Greek: "ἤπερ"</note> instead of *if* <note>original Greek: "εἴπερ"</note> based on <margin>10</margin> Wyttenbach’s conjecture. Burette incorrectly attempted to read it as: "if indeed it was an opposite to the Mixolydian and similar to the Ionian." Regarding this matter, see Bellermann, who thinks the "relaxed" Lydian is the same as the Hyperlydian; his words on page 11 are as follows: "only this (hyperlydian) is similar to the Ionian, as a secondary scale <note>original German: "plagiale tonart"</note> to <margin>15</margin> that authentic one, just as one can call the Locrian similar to the Aeolian. The name of the 'relaxed' Lydian and the 'tightened' Lydian (he means the Syntonolydian) is explained if one compares them with each other and with the Lydian mode, which spreads its Lydian four-note scales <note>original German: "tetrachorde"</note> over the whole <margin>20</margin> octave, while the 'tightened' Lydian has stretched them upward as a Lydian seven-note scale <note>original German: "heptachord"</note> and the 'relaxed' has let them down into the depths." I have no intention of refuting these points, nor do I approve of them in every part: for as I reconsider the matter, the Syntonolydian does not seem different from the Lydian itself, just as <margin>25</margin> a "tightened" diatonic scale is not different from a simple diatonic, nor a "tightened" chromatic scale from a regular chromatic.

**they say it was discovered by Damon the Athenian** <note>original Greek: "ὑπὸ Δάμωνος εὑρῆσθαί φασι τοῦ Ἀθηναίου"</note>] Damon, a student of Agathocles or Lamprocles or both, was a famous musician and a good lyre-player, and generally a learned man, <margin>30</margin> being an intimate associate of the Sophists, especially Prodicus (see Plato, *Laches*, page 180 D, 197 D). Since he also concerned himself with public affairs, he was forced into exile by ostracism as a "busybody" and a "friend of tyrants" (see Plutarch, *Pericles* chapter 4, *Aristides* 1, *Nicias* 6, the first of which affirms that he taught Pericles music). How much Plato valued his judgment <margin>35</margin> on musical matters is shown in the *Republic* (III, page 400 B). Damon did not approve of musical innovations, as his opinion was: *the modes of music are never changed without affecting the most important laws of the state*, a view which Plato embraced in the *Republic* (IV, page 424 C). <margin>40</margin>

***

### CHAPTER XVII.

**Draco the Athenian** <note>original Greek: "Δράκοντος τοῦ Ἀθηναίου"</note>] I do not understand why Wyttenbach wrote in his annotation: "there is no doubt the author wrote, or certainly should have written, *Damon*, and thus I have translated it." <margin>45</margin> Indeed, Burette says: "No mention is made in any other place of these two musicians who taught this art to Plato. Neither Diogenes Laertius, nor Olympiodorus, nor any other says a word of this."

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," transitioning from the discussion of musical modes in Chapter XVI to the pedagogical influences of Plato and the technical evolution of stringed instruments and harmonic theory in Chapters XVII through XIX.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XVII—XVIII.

"from those who have written the life of this philosopher." But these claims are false. For Olympiodorus, in his *Life of Plato* (chapter 2), says in explicit words: "and he had as a music teacher Draco, the pupil of Damon" <note>original Greek: *μουσικῆς δὲ διδάσκαλον ἔσχε Δράκοντα τὸν Δάμωνος μαθητήν*</note>.

<margin>5</margin> **Metallus of Agrigentum**] I have written "Metallus" instead of "Metellus," using a Greek and Sicilian name instead of a Roman one, which certainly was not in use in Sicily at that time. It could also have been "Tellias" or "Gellias," which would be an Agrigentine name. For I know of a certain Gellias or Tellias of Agrigentum, famous for his wealth and generosity, who was killed in a fire in a certain temple during the siege of his homeland by the Carthaginians, from Diodorus Siculus (XIII, 83. 90), <margin>10</margin> Athenaeus (I p. 4 A), and Suidas (under the entry "Athenaeus").

**Maiden-songs** <note>original Greek: "Παρθένεια" (Partheneia); songs composed for choruses of young women</note>] After this word, the word "others" <note>original Greek: "ἄλλα"</note> was commonly read, which was first suspected by Burette, and after him by Wyttenbach and Welcker in their fragments of Alcman (page 10). Burette says this: "one must erase *others* from this passage, where it signifies nothing and is only a <margin>15</margin> repetition by the copyist, caused by the following word (*Alcmâni*)." Whether Dübner gave "together" <note>original Greek: "ἅμα"</note> based on Wyttenbach’s conjecture or from the handwriting of the manuscripts, I do not know; I do know that neither word can be tolerated in this place.

<margin>20</margin> **tragic laments** <note>original Greek: "τραγικοί οἶκτοι"</note>] I think the discussion concerns lyric tragedy, specifically the Dorian style.

### CHAPTER XVIII.

**the poems of Olympus and Terpander**] Unless I am mistaken, <margin>25</margin> poems are also attributed to Olympus by Suidas, who calls him "a flute-player and a poet of melodies" <note>original Greek: "αὐλήτην καὶ ποιητὴν μελῶν"</note>; see the notes on Chapter VII. It must be held as a general rule that in ancient times the province of poetry was very rarely separated from music; this separation only became customary around the time of the Persian Wars <note>original Latin: "Medici belli," referring to the conflict between Greece and Persia</note>. <margin>30</margin>

**for being few-stringed** <note>original Greek: "ὀλιγόχορδα γὰρ ὄντα"</note>] The books say "three-stringed" <note>original Greek: "τρίχορδα"</note>. But it is foolish to establish that Terpander and his disciples used a three-stringed instrument in composing musical modes. Certainly, Burette (Volume XIV, page 337) says: "it is not a question—there—of instruments. It is only a matter <margin>35</margin> of songs or airs (*poems*) which revolved only around three strings, although the cithara on which they were played could actually have seven"—nevertheless, he himself (Volume XIX, page 415) established that "three-stringed" is the same as "few-stringed," "so that it is a certain number put in place of an uncertain one, to ex-<margin>40</margin>press the small number of strings or sounds used by a musician in the composition of an Air." But these views are distorted; rather, it must be established that "three-stringed" is a corruption of "few-stringed," a reading which is proved by the preceding word "few-stringedness" <note>original Greek: "ὀλιγοχορδίας"</note> and followed by "many-stringed" <note>original Greek: "πολυχόρδων"</note>. It is hardly necessary to say that this word refers uniquely to the modes of Terpander. They <margin>45</margin> agreed with the modes of Olympus in their extreme simplicity.

**but that theirs lag behind his**] I have written the last words based on Wyttenbach’s conjecture. The books say "these" <note>original Greek: "τούτους"</note>.

<column-break/>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XVIII—XIX.

**applied to the versatile** <note>original Greek: "πολυτρόπῳ καταγινομένους"</note>] Here, that "versatile" <note>term: "polytropon" (multi-turning/versatile)</note> type of musical composition is named in which a change <note>term: "metabole"</note> in tone or mode <note>term: "tropos"</note> was often admitted; see the notes on Chapter VIII. Certainly, the figurative meaning does not delay anyone who, following Thucydides, <margin>5</margin> considers this word to be nothing other than *multiplex* (manifold).

### CHAPTER XIX.

**to have used the parhypate consonantly** <note>original Greek: "πρὸς τὴν παρυπάτην κεχρῆσθαι συμφώνως"</note>] All intervals whose sounds, when struck together, blend into a beautiful union were called *symphonies* or *symphonic* intervals by the Greeks; today these are called "consonant" intervals. They were also called *paraphona* and *paraphonia*. <margin>10</margin> In their number were included the ditone <note>term: "ditonon"; roughly a major third</note>, the fifth <note>original: "dia pente"</note>, the fourth <note>original: "dia tessaron"</note>, and finally the octave <note>original: "dia pason"</note> or *antiphony*. The opposites of symphonies are "diaphonic" intervals, whose sounds do not blend when struck together: beware <margin>15</margin> of confusing these with "dissonant" intervals, whose sounds the ears do not even allow to be struck together. For there were intervals which the Greeks reckoned among the diaphonic, even though they seem consonant to us. <margin>20</margin> Concerning others, it was doubtful whether they were symphonic or diaphonic, which Theo the Musician (chapter 5) seems to indicate with these words: "even the symphonic notes are diaphonic [in a sense]. Such is the interval of a tone or a diesis <note>term: "diesis"; a microtonal interval smaller than a semitone</note>. For the tone and the diesis are the beginning of symphony, but not yet symphony." Even the sacred music of the Middle Ages knew no other <margin>25</margin> consonances than the octave, the fifth, and the fourth—to use modern terms; see Gerbert, *Sacred Music*, Volume II, page 113 ff; Marpurg, *Critical Introduction*, page 225. Furthermore, the musical theory of a more recent age fluctuated for a long time regarding the division of intervals. For Franco of Cologne knew five consonances: one perfect (the oc-<margin>30</margin>tave), two middle (the fifth and fourth), two imperfect (the major and minor third), and four dissonances: two imperfect (the minor and major sixth) and two perfect (the second and seventh). But our people today consider the octave, fifth, and fourth as perfect consonances; both the major and minor third <margin>35</margin> and both sixths as imperfect consonances; the major second and minor seventh as imperfect dissonances; and the remaining intervals as perfect dissonances. Thus, what pleases the ears of one century displeases another. On the symphonies and diaphonies of the ancients, compare Forkel, *History of Music*, I, page 395; Boeckh, <margin>40</margin> *On Pindaric Metre*, page 254 ff; Drieberg, *Musical Lexicon*, page 17; and finally Stallbaum, *Music from Plato* (Leipzig, 1846), page 23, from which it is helpful to transcribe this: "for what our people are accustomed to call *harmony*—that is, the harmony of several voices and sounds reaching the ears at one and the same time—the Greeks called instead *sym-<margin>45</margin>phony*; by *harmony* they understood a fit and elegant succession of various sounds according to the types of modes made by the laws of art, such as is seen in a melody." Thus Suidas and Hesychius interpret *harmony* <note>original Greek: "ἁρμονίαν"</note> as an "orderly succession" <note>original Greek: "εὔτακτος ἀκολουθία"</note>; compare Plato.

<summary>This page analyzes the educational lineage of Plato, textual disputes regarding the string-count of early Greek instruments, and the evolving technical definitions of "harmony" and "consonance" (symphonia) from antiquity through the Middle Ages.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," transitioning from the discussion of musical modes in Chapter XVI to the technical evolution of harmonic theory and the introduction of the chromatic scale in Chapters XIX and XX.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XIX.

References to Plato’s *Laws* (Book II, p. 665 A), *Philebus* (p. 17 D), and Trinkler’s *Harmony* (p. 11). Plutarch, in chapters XV and XVIII, used the word "harmony" <note>original Greek: *ἁρμονία* (harmonia)</note> to mean "tone" <note>original Greek: *τόνος* (tonos), referring to a musical key</note> or "mode" <note>original Greek: *τρόπος* (tropos)</note>. Concerning this, compare Theo the Mathematician’s *On Music* (chapter 4): "Harmony is the composition of systems, such as the Lydian, Phrygian, and Dorian" <note>original Greek: *ἁρμονία ἐστὶ συστημάτων σύνταξις, οἷον Λύδιος, Φρύγιος, Δώριος*</note>. Furthermore, the word "harmony" <margin>5</margin> signifies the enharmonic genus <note>One of the three ancient Greek musical scales, known for using very small intervals called quarter-tones</note>. Finally, among the Pythagoreans, it signifies the octave or the "through-all-strings" system <note>original Greek: *διὰ πασῶν* (dia pason)</note>, according to Aristides Quintilianus (Book I, p. 17) and Boeckh’s *Philolaus* (p. 65).

On this occasion, I may add a few words on the question of whether the ancients employed the harmonic concord of the moderns <note>This refers to "polyphony" or "harmony" in the modern sense—multiple different notes played at once to create a chord</note> in their musical modes, <margin>10</margin> about which different scholars have reached different conclusions. I have not examined the discussion by Muenchov in the *Annals of the University of the Rhine* (I, 4, p. 356 ff.), but Athanasius Kircher in his *Musurgia* (Book I, p. 552) seems to have correctly determined that the Greeks possessed only "natural polyody" <note>singing the same melody in different octaves</note> and not the "artful" kind <note>modern counterpoint or chords</note>; John Wallis agrees with this in his commentary on Ptolemy (p. 317). Choral singing among the ancients proceeded by "antiphony," which is to say, by <margin>15</margin> octaves. This relates to Aristotle’s *Problems* (XIX, 18): <margin>15</margin> "Why is the concord of the octave the only one that is sung?" <note>original Greek: *διὰ τὶ ἡ διὰ πασῶν συμφωνία ᾄδεται μόνη;*</note>. Even the concert of the human voice with musical instruments was done through antiphony, if I correctly understand Plutarch in *On Having Many Friends* (chapter 8): "For the harmony regarding the psaltery and the lyre maintains concord through antiphonies, a similarity being produced somehow between the high and <margin>20</margin> low notes" <note>original Greek: *ἡ μὲν γὰρ περὶ ψαλμοὺς καὶ φόρμιγγας ἁρμονία δι’ ἀντιφώνων ἔχει τὸ σύμφωνον, ὀξυτήσι καὶ βαρύτησι ἀμωσγέπως ὁμοιότητος ἐγγινομένης*</note>. Certainly, it could not fail to happen that individual singers, according to the nature and strength of their voices, might take up some consonant sounds alongside the antiphonies, so as to produce some appearance of harmonic concord between two voices—provided that you do not assume this was done <margin>25</margin> according to a fixed rule or laws. See Stallbaum (in the place cited, pp. 27, 28). Thus, the matter corresponds closely to what can be observed daily in our own folk songs sung by the common people. But one must stop there; it would be rash to imagine some perfect system of harmonic concord, even though a few passages seem to indicate this—for example, Plutarch in *Precepts of Marriage* <margin>30</margin> (chapter 11): "If two sounds are taken in concord, the melody belongs to the lower one" <note>original Greek: *ἂν φθόγγοι δύο σύμφωνοι [reading: συμφώνως] ληφθῶσι, τοῦ βαρυτέρου γίνεται τὸ μέλος*</note>. But the opinion of those who have persuaded themselves that two tones were used together in a symphonic concert is utterly false; I do not even understand how this could ever have happened according to the laws of music. For the <margin>35</margin> passages of writers which Thiersch cited in his *Introduction to Pindar* (p. 58) prove nothing. Of these, Horace (*Epistles* IX, 5) is to be understood as referring to musical instruments where one took over from the other. As for Pindar (*Olympian* I, 17, 100), if the poem was truly fitted to the Aeolian mode, the "Dorian lyre" in verse 17 is nothing but a poetic <margin>40</margin> formula. Finally, those things told about the "tripod" of Pythagoras of Zacynthus <note>A triple-faced lyre designed to play different modes quickly</note> are of such a kind that it cannot be doubted that one tone followed another in its playing. Compare Trinkler against Boeckh in the notes to chapter XXXIII.

**and sounding in concord with the middle note** <note>original Greek: *καὶ συμφώνουν πρὸς τε μέσην*</note>] <margin>45</margin> This addition, based on the conjecture of Meziriacus and approved by Burette and Wyttenbach, was received into the text by Dübner, whom I follow.

**with the highest note of the conjuncts** <note>original Greek: *τῇ συνημμένων νήτῃ*</note>] I have written this instead of the "highest note of the conjunct" [singular], because

<page-break/>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XIX—XX.

even if it could be tolerated by implying the word "tetrachord" <note>A scale of four notes</note>, it is nonetheless a rarer expression. Wyttenbach also correctly changed "of the disjunct" [singular] to "of the disjuncts" [plural] <note>original Greek: *διεξευγμένων* for *διεζευγμένου*</note> in chapter XXII on the authority of the books. I truly do not understand this whole passage. For first, the "conjunct" tetrachord <margin>5</margin> is more recent than Olympus <note>A legendary early Greek musician</note>. Second, the "highest note of the conjuncts" does not differ from the "next-to-highest note of the disjuncts," which Plutarch attributes to Olympus. How then do these things agree with one another?

**and even cause shame for the one using it** <note>original Greek: *κἂν αἰσχυνθῆναί τῷ χρησαμένῳ*</note>] Wyttenbach asks: "did the word 'since' <note>original Greek: *ἐπεὶ*</note> fall out between these two words? Or does the indirect construction <margin>10</margin> hold, as it does immediately after in this part of the discussion?" Certainly the indirect construction holds; however, because there is nothing for the dative case to depend upon, I have placed a sign of a gap <note>lacuna</note> after "cause shame."

---

## CHAPTER XX.

<margin>15</margin> **for in the chromatic genus and in the enharmonic**] The common reading "and in the rhythm" is manifestly corrupt. I have given "and in the enharmonic" (namely, genus) based on the version by Vulgulius. By "cithara," Plutarch means the music played on the cithara <note>a professional version of the lyre</note>. Moreover, these words show that this chapter was <margin>20</margin> copied from Aristoxenus <note>A famous Greek music theorist and pupil of Aristotle</note>. For only he—who hated and despised all new attempts in music—could have said that the chromatic genus is not used in tragedies; for a more recent writer to say this would have been utterly ridiculous. Thus Thiersch was mistaken in his *Introduction to Pindar* (p. 37): "tragedy abstained from <margin>25</margin> the chromatic scale even until Plutarch's time." For the chromatic genus was first used in tragedy by Agathon, as Plutarch himself testifies in *Table Talk* (III, 1). This is *Agathon, son of Tisamenus*, the Athenian tragic poet, defamed by comic writers for his "softness" <note>Agathon is a central figure in Plato's *Symposium*</note>, who moved with Euripides to the court of Archelaus, King of Macedonia (Scholiast on Lucian, *The Professor of Rhetoric* § 11; Cramer’s *Anecdota* IV, 269). He also practiced the "soft and broken" style of flute-playing which flourished among the Thebans at that time; see Zenobius (I, 2): "The Agathonian piping: soft and neither sharp nor loose, but well-tempered and most sweet. It is named after Agathon the piper, who was <margin>35</margin> mocked for his softness." Cf. Leutsch on this passage. Some present Olympus as the inventor of the chromatic genus, others Epigonus of Sicyon. Athenaeus (XIV p. 363 A) records that Lysander of Sicyon, a musician of the most ancient age, used it. Therefore, it was not invented in the age of the Attic poets, and Boethius erred in attributing its invention to Timotheus <margin>40</margin>. Without doubt, the dithyrambic poets used it frequently, and one might conclude that Agathon received it from them, as he was not opposed to the innovations of the dithyrambists, if I see things correctly. For even if individual parts of that song which Aristophanes preserved for Agathon in *Thesmophoriazusae* cannot be <margin>45</margin> regarded as dithyrambic preludes, one must nevertheless be careful not to disturb them with too many conjectures based on the stricter rules of meter, as Marxenius very rightly warned in *On the relationship between Plato's Symposium and Aristophanes' Thesmophoriazusae* (Rendsburg program, 1853, p. 8).

<vocab>Plutarch, Aristoxenus, harmony, tetrachord, enharmonic, chromatic, Agathon, citharoedic, dithyramb, polyody</vocab>

It also identifies the tragic poet Agathon as the innovator who introduced the chromatic scale into tragedy, likely influenced by the stylistic freedom of dithyrambic poetry.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," specifically examining the historical figures and technical terminology mentioned in Chapters XX and XXI. The previous page discussed the definitions of "harmony" and the debate over ancient Greek polyphony.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XX—XXI. | COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XXI.

<margin>5</margin> **For the same man [refers to] Pancrates** <note>original Greek: ὁ γὰρ αὐτὸς καὶ Παγκράτην</note>] Nothing is certain regarding Pancrates the musician. He certainly flourished before Aristoxenus, since I have just mentioned that Plutarch excerpted him in this chapter as in the following one. This opinion is further confirmed by the fact that Dorio and Antigenides are cited as contemporaries. Burette <note>Jean-Baptiste de Burette (1665–1747), a French scholar of ancient music</note> believes that the "Pancratian" verse, which Servius mentions, was invented by this Pancrates, the musician and poet. There were also other poets named Pancrates, such as Pancrates the Arcadian, who composed a poem about fish titled *Sea Works* <note>original Greek: θαλάσσια ἔργα</note>, and another Alexandrian poet from the time of the Emperor <margin>10</margin> Hadrian, whom Bergk discussed in the *Rhenish Museum*.

## CHAPTER XXI.

**Of Andreas of Corinth and Thrasyllus of Phlius** <note>original Greek: Ἀνδρέα τοῦ Κορινθίου καὶ Θρασύλλου τοῦ Φλιασίου</note>] We know nothing of these <margin>15</margin> men, whom Aristoxenus certainly included in the list of ancient musicians. Regarding musical studies in Arcadia, among the Corinthians, the Phliasians, and other Dorian cities, see Bernhardy’s *History of Greek Literature* and Mueller’s *Dorians*. Nor is anything certain regarding Tyrtacus of Mantinea, which is truly surprising. Indeed, <margin>20</margin> the diligence of grammarians usually records the namesakes of great poets. Burette thought of that ancient Spartan elegiac poet [Tyrtaeus], whose homeland is debated, as authors call him sometimes an Athenian and sometimes a Milesian. Perhaps there is some corruption hidden in the word "Mantinean."
<margin>25</margin> **and of the interpretation** <note>original Greek: καὶ ἐρμηνείας</note>] "Interpretation" <note>original Greek: ἐρμηνεία (*hermeneia*)</note> is what we call "the practical execution" or "the playing of a musical piece." Burette, who translates it as "execution or tablature," added these remarks: "it is nothing else here than the manner of executing a melody, a piece of music noted either by singing it or playing it on some <margin>30</margin> instrument."
**so that the flute-makers — upon the flutes** <note>original Greek: ὥστε τοὺς αὐλοποιούς — ἐπὶ τοὺς αὐλούς</note>] This passage seems corrupt. For what does it mean to "place pipes [syringes] upon the flutes [auloi]"? Does it mean a harmony of flutes and panpipes? In that case, one should read "flutists" <note>original Greek: αὐλητάς (*auletas*)</note> instead of "flute-makers" <note>original Greek: αὐλοποιούς (*aulopoious*)</note>. For the remarks made by Burette <margin>35</margin> are false: "the *syrinx* is a reed, a wind instrument analogous to what we call the *reed* of an oboe or an organ pipe. The *aulos* is here a recorder. If one removes the mouthpiece from a flute and adapts a reed in its place, one makes an oboe, whose sound, less sweet than that of the flute, <margin>40</margin> resembles that of the reed-pipe. Plutarch therefore means that Telephanes had such an aversion to reed-pipes that he never allowed the flute-makers (that is, of all kinds of wind instruments) to apply them to the flutes they manufactured for his use, thereby turning them <margin>45</margin> into oboes"—for this definition of the word *syrinx*, as if it were the tongue of a flute, is entirely unheard of. Nevertheless, Papius followed Burette in his Lexicon; Papius, if I am not mistaken, received most of what he recorded regarding musical matters

<column-break/>

from Bellermann. Furthermore, it is probable that in addition to flutes, panpipes <note>original: *fistulae* / *syringes*</note> were also employed in the Pythian contest. Generally, panpipes were never absent whenever a harmony of all available instruments was produced (in German: *bei einem vollstaendig besetzten orchester* — "in a fully staffed orchestra"), <margin>5</margin> just as we learn from Lucian’s *On the Dance* (chapter 68) that in the performance of a pantomime, panpipes were used alongside flutes, lyres, and cymbals. Regarding the phrasing, the construction "to place something upon something" <note>original Greek: ἐπιτιθέναι τι ἐπί τι</note> is also used in other passages of Plutarch, such as in the *Sayings of Kings and Commanders*: <margin>10</margin> "If I thought," he said, "that this tunic was aware of my secrets, I would strip it off and place it upon the fire." Or in the *Sayings of the Spartans*: "Having taken the liver from the seer, he placed it upon the hand held beneath it."
As for Telephanes, this was the name of many flutists. The one <margin>15</margin> mentioned here perhaps corresponds to the one mentioned by Demosthenes in *Against Meidias*. I know of Telephanes of Samos from Pausanias; Cleopatra, the sister of Philip, erected a monument to him on the road leading from Megara to Corinth. I believe this is the same Telephanes whom Stratonicus knew intimately as a young man (see Athenaeus). <margin>20</margin>
**but also of the Pythian contest** <note>original Greek: ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῦ Πυθικοῦ ἀγῶνος</note>] Concerning the Pythian contest, the most important passage is from Strabo (Book IX): "The ancient contest at Delphi consisted of *citharoedes* [singers with lyres] singing a paean to the god; the Delphians established it. But after the Crisaean War, the <margin>25</margin> Amphictyons <note>An ancient religious association of Greek tribes</note>, under Eurylochus, organized equestrian and gymnastic games for a crown and called them the Pythian Games. To the *citharoedes*, they added flutists and lyre-players [without singing], who were to perform a certain melody called the *Pythic Nomos*. It has five parts: the opening flourish, the trial, the inciting battle, the iambics and dactyls [rhythms], and the pipes <note>original Greek: ἄγκρουσις ἄμπειρα κατακελευσμὸς ἴαμβοι καὶ δάκτυλοι σύριγγες</note>. <margin>30</margin> It was composed by Timosthenes, the admiral of Ptolemy II, who also wrote the work *On Harbors* in ten books. He intended to celebrate the battle of Apollo against the dragon through this melody: the 'opening flourish' representing the prelude; the 'trial' representing the first attempt at the contest; the 'inciting battle' representing the contest itself; the 'iambic and dactyl' representing <margin>35</margin> the song of victory after the win with such rhythms (one of which is suited to hymns and the other, the iambic, to insults); and the 'pipes' representing the expiration of the beast, mimicking the final hissing sounds as it perished." To this passage should be added the words of Pausanias, <margin>40</margin> who also records the individual victors of the parts of the Pythian contest. Therefore, from the most ancient times, a hymn was sung in honor of Apollo with the accompaniment of the lyre. Whether the *citharoedic* contests were only instituted from the time of Terpander (as Hoeck suggested) cannot be clearly proven due to the lack of evidence. The expansion of the musical <margin>45</margin> contest by adding the art of the flute—the names of the flutists can be found in Pausanias—and the new institution of the contest by the Amphictyons after the Crisaean War (around the 48th Olympiad) coincides with the second age of Spartan music, in which, as I said above, the art of the flute was used in the Apolline worship...
