<meta>This page continues the detailed commentary on Chapter XXI of Plutarch’s "On Music," focusing on the history of the Pythian Games, the specific musical compositions known as "nomoi," and the famous flute players of the classical period like Antigenides.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XXI.

...that the cult was established. Furthermore, Pausanias records that *singing with the flute* <note>original Greek: αὐλῳδίαν; "auloidia" refers to a vocal performance accompanied by a flute player</note> as a newly invented performance was again neglected in the second Pythiad: "for the singing with the flute," he says, "consisted of the most somber flute melodies and elegies (and laments) sung to the accompaniment of flutes," <note>original Greek: ἡ γὰρ αὐλῳδία, μέλη τε ἦν αὐλῶν τὰ σκυθρωπότατα καὶ ἐλεγεῖα (καὶ θρῆνοι) προσᾳδόμενα τοῖς αὐλοῖς</note> and he proved this by a certain inscription on a tripod dedicated to the Theban Hercules by Echembrotus. See Hoeck, *Crete*, volume III, page 386. Regarding this passage, we only wonder why the flute players were unwilling to use a sweeter musical composition, since we know that flutes were also suited to more joyful modes. Finally, in this matter, Pausanias is more accurate than Strabo, because he records that *solo lyre players* <note>original Greek: ψιλοκιθαριστάς; "psilocitharistas" were musicians who played the lyre without singing</note> were only admitted to the competition in the eighth Pythiad, that is, the 55th Olympiad, year 3 [560 BCE]. Regarding the competition in general, Scaliger in *Poetics* I, 23 has already explained it well, and in this age, especially Kraus in *The Pythian, Nemean, and Isthmian Games*, Leipzig, 1841, pages 12, 17, 25.

The "hinge" of the competition, as it were, turned on the *Pythian Strain* <note>original Greek: νόμῳ Πυθικῷ; the "nomos" was a traditional, highly structured musical composition</note>, that ancient invention of Olympus which Sacadas spread throughout Greece, also called the *Pythian flute-song* <note>original Greek: Πυθικὸν αὔλημα</note> and the *Pythian melody* <note>original Greek: Πύθιον μέλος</note>. That "nomos" was a major musical composition which represented for the listeners the victory of Apollo over the Pythian serpent. In this place, it is doubtful to what extent a melodic song was added to the musical modes in the most ancient times. Certainly later, in Strabo's time and in common life, it was performed by flute players (*auletes*) with the flutes alone, not by singers (*aulodes*), such as at the wedding of Alexander the Great (Athenaeus XII, page 539 F); therefore, it is called the "Pythian flute-strain" by Pollux. Theopompus, quoted in Athenaeus XIII, page 605 B, relates that the flute player Broemiada, when she had received some gifts from the Delphic treasury from Phayllus, the tyrant of the Phocians, wanted to sing the *Pythian themes* <note>original Greek: τὰ Πύθια</note>, but the people stopped her. The *Pythian flute-song* itself seems to have undergone various changes over time, so that the Scholiast on Pindar (Introduction to the Pythian Odes, page 297), Pollux (IV, 84), and Strabo (in the place cited) do not entirely agree on the individual parts of the strain. On this matter, Boeckh <note>August Boeckh (1785–1867), a foundational figure in modern philology and the study of Greek rhythm</note> in *On the Meters of Pindar*, page 182, note 16, where he himself described the strain, says most correctly: "all clearly speak of the same song, but one lists some parts and another lists others, undoubtedly because the individual parts consisted of several smaller pieces, of which one author names this one, another none." See also Mueller, *Dorians*, volume I, page 349, and Thiersch, *Introduction to Pindar*, page 60, who however mixed truths with falsehoods.

I consider the Pythian strain noteworthy primarily because it is the most ancient specimen of the artistic music of the Greeks. Indeed, it cannot be doubted that its individual parts differed from each other in both rhythm and melody, and their names signified the individual periods, as it were, of the musical composition, about which Boeckh rightly spoke against Forkel. The parts of a single strain have already been mentioned above in Sacadas’s "triple-modulated" strain, and if any trust is to be placed in Pollux (IV, 66), already in the time of Terpander the lyre-song strain consisted of seven parts: *the beginning, the after-beginning, the turning, the after-turning, the navel [center], the seal, and the epilogue* <note>original Greek: ἔπαρχα, μέταρχα, κατάτροπα, μετακατάτροπα, ὀμφαλός, σφραγίς, ἐπίλογος</note>, concerning which you may consult Boeckh in the place cited and Plehn, *Lesbian Matters*, page 163.

---

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XXI.

But I believe that Pollux rather carelessly applied the rules that were valid for strains in his own time to antiquity, so I would not want to misuse his words to illustrate Terpander’s verse *upon the seven-toned lyre* <note>original Greek: ἑπτατόνῳ φόρμιγγι</note>. However, it seems not improbable to me that the individual parts of the Pythian strain were also played on individual instruments in the competitions. For besides the fact that there was a "Pythian flute" which Pollux (IV, 81) recorded was used both in the Pythian strain and in the Paeans <note>hymns of praise</note>, "dactylic flutes" are also mentioned which seemed suited to the harmony of *dance-songs* <note>original Greek: Hyporchematum</note> (see Pollux, ibid., and Athenaeus IV, page 176 F), and these could have had a place in the Pythian strain. Finally, I gather that panpipes (*fistulas*) were used both from Strabo’s and Plutarch’s words written above. However, I do not wish to press these points. Moreover, he who learned to play the Pythian strain on the flutes is called a *Pythaules*, whether he practiced his art at Delphi or at some other festival of Apollo, as inscriptions testify in the *Corpus Inscriptionum Graecarum*, numbers 1719 and 1720. Therefore, all flute players are called by this more honorable name, as in Seneca’s *Epistles* (IX, 5): "that theater (he means the theater at Naples) is packed, and with great zeal it is judged who is a good *pythaules*." At Delphi, the *Pythaulae* seem to have been dressed in a certain unique and splendid garment. See what Proclus narrated about Chrysothemis in Hephaestion, page 382. Also Chrysogonus, a victor in the Delphic competition, when the exiled Alcibiades was returning to his homeland, played a certain naval song (*a rowing song, the trireme-song*) <note>original Greek: εἰρεσίαν, τὸ τριηρικόν</note> on the flute for the strokes of the rowers, *dressed in the Pythian robe* <note>original Greek: ἐνδεδυκὼς τὴν Πυθικὴν στολήν</note>, as Athenaeus related in volume XII, page 535 D.

**of the Dorians** <note>original Greek: τῶν μὲν Δωριενείων</note>] Dorio the flute player was present at the table of King Philip after the battle of Chaeronea (Athenaeus X, page 435 B).

**of the Antigenideans** <note>original Greek: τῶν δὲ Ἀντιγενιδείων</note>] Aristoxenus had written about Antigenides in his book *On Flute Players* (Tatian, *Against the Greeks*, page 86). Suidas says: "Antigenides, son of Satyrus, a Theban, musician, singer to the flute of Philoxenus. He was the first to use Milesian shoes and he donned a saffron-colored cloak in *The Reveller* <note>original Greek: ἐν τῷ Κωμαστῇ</note> (that is, in Philoxenus's play so inscribed). He wrote melodies." Harpocration called him the son of Dionysius and a "most famous flute player." A story in Plutarch’s *Sayings of Kings and Commanders* (page 193 F) shows that he flourished with great musical praise in the time of Epaminondas. Gellius (XV, 17), citing Pamphile, records that by the order of Pericles he taught music to the boy Alcibiades, in which passage Antigenides seems to have been confused with Pronomus. He inflamed Alexander with warlike passion when he once played the *Harmatian* strain (see Plutarch, *On the Fortune of Alexander*, 40, page 335 A). Plutarch (Life of Demetrius, 1) preserved *his opinion that the youth listen to good flute players more pleasantly if they also experience the bad ones*; another story about him is in Athenaeus XIV, page 631 F. Among musicians, he prepared a name for himself especially because he was the first to play *inflections* <note>original Greek: πλάσματα; "plasmata" likely refers to highly stylized or modulated notes</note> on the flute, that is, artistic bends of the modes, which brought about a change in the structure and shaping of flutes (cf. Theophrastus, *History of Plants*, IV, 11, 4).

**cobbled-together pieces** <note>original Greek: καττύματα</note>] This is what the melodies of the lyre-players are called in contempt, as if one were to say in German: "roughly cobbled together" <note>original German: grob zusammengeschusterte</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the historical evolution of the Pythian musical competitions, specifically the technical structure of the Pythian Nomos and the biographies of famous flute players like Antigenides. It highlights the transition from vocal accompaniment to purely instrumental flute performances and the prestigious status of the Pythaules.</summary>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XXI.

...melodies. Burette says: "our music provides us with something similar in certain musical 'centos' <note>A 'cento' is a composition made up of passages from various sources</note>, made of several airs of different movements and tones, placed end to end, which form an uninterrupted sequence of song." He says this
<margin>5</margin>is what we call: "a musical *quodlibet*, a *potpourri*." Properly, *kattymata* <note>original Greek: καττύματα; literally "things stitched together"</note> is a type of footwear according to Pollux (VII, 86), or rather the leather from which shoes were made, as the Scholiast on Aristophanes (*Acharnians* 300) says: "kattymata are certain thin and hard skins which are placed under sandals and other footwear."

<margin>10</margin>**The poems of Polyidus**] Concerning Polyidus—a dithyrambic poet <note>A dithyramb was a passionate choral hymn sung in honor of Dionysus</note> and musician, and a rival of Timotheus around the 95th Olympiad [c. 400 BCE]—see Jahn on the *Fragments after Censorinus* (IX, p. 86) and Bernhardy’s *History of Greek Literature* (II, I, p. 676). We may dismiss Burette’s weak conjecture of "polyede" <note>original: πολυειδῆ; meaning "manifold forms"</note>.

**Instrumental**] This reading from the Paris manuscript—*kroumatikas* <note>original Greek: κρουματικάς; referring to the striking of strings or instruments</note>—
<margin>15</margin>already seemed preferable to Wyttenbach over the common reading, and it was approved by Papius in his Lexicon, who, following Burette, interprets it as "instrumental dialects": "the expression used in the playing of instruments." But I hardly think "dialects" <note>original: διαλέκτους</note> used in a metaphorical sense is sufficiently supported by common usage, nor does Chapter 46 prove anything. Perhaps it was written
<margin>20</margin>"and those things concerning both the strikes and the dialects," which is how I have rendered it in the version, instead of what was there before: "but even the expressions of the pulsations <note>meaning the striking of the strings</note> were then more varied." Furthermore, the manuscripts very often fluctuate between *krousmata* and *kroumata* <note>both terms refer to instrumental music or the act of striking an instrument</note>, as seen in Dio Chrysostom (Orations 32.4, 24, 26, etc.).

<margin>25</margin>**For those who are now fond of myths**] Usually read as *philomatheis* <note>original Greek: φιλομαθεῖς; "fond of learning"</note>. But this signifies those eager for doctrine, and thus is not at all the opposite of the other term, *philorrhythmoi* <note>original Greek: φιλόῤῥυθμοι; "fond of rhythm"</note>. Wyttenbach’s interpretation in the Plutarchan Lexicon—"show-offs of learning"—is certainly suited to the context of the words, but it is an invention. Valgulius does not seem to have read *philomatheis* in his manuscript,
<margin>30</margin>for he translates: "at this time indeed they embraced melodies, but in that time rhythms, studiously and eagerly." I do not doubt that it was *philomythoi* <note>original Greek: φιλόμυθοι; "fond of myths"</note>, a word Plutarch used in *On Listening to Poets* (11, p. 30 D): "the myth-lover should not miss what is newly or exceptionally told," which was once
<margin>35</margin>corrupted into *philomatheis* and subsequently led to a more serious error. It is certainly not improbable that Aristoxenus opposed the "myth-loving" of his own age (in which dithyrambic and tragic poets innovated greatly in myths and turned even the most obscure ones to their own use) to the "rhythm-loving" of the ancients. Aristotle’s *Problems* (XIX, 31) is relevant here:
<margin>40</margin>"Why were those around Phrynichus more 'song-makers'? Or was it because the melodies in tragedies then were many times greater than the verses?"

**Of the broken melodies**] Whenever artistic flexions of the voice were employed, the melodies became "broken" <note>original: κεκλασμένα; "kékla-smena" implies a fractured, overly ornate, or effeminate style</note> or "many-curved" <note>original: πολυκαμπῆ</note> or, to use the Comic poets' word, "hard-to-twist" <note>original: δυσολοκάμπτα</note> (Pollux IV, 68). The author of this
<margin>45</margin>broken style of singing was Phrynis, according to the Scholiast on Aristophanes (*Clouds* 969): "the comic poets often mention him for the innovations he made by breaking the song."

<column-break/>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XXII—XXIII.

### CHAPTER XXII.

**In the soul-generation in the Timaeus**] The matters discussed in this chapter regarding the theory of numbers according to the passage in the *Timaeus* were set forth more accurately by Plutarch, although in almost the same words, in his
<margin>5</margin>book *On the Procreation of the Soul in the Timaeus* (Chapters 15, 29, etc.). Regarding this subject, see Boeckh in Daub and Creuzer’s *Studies* (Vol. III, I, pp. 1–96). Plato’s words in the editions of the *Timaeus* (p. 36 A) read as follows: "After these things, the double and triple intervals were being filled up, still cutting off portions from there and placing them in the space between these,
<margin>10</margin>so that in each interval there were two means."

**But it is found neither by ratio nor by number**] It is established from the schools of the Mathematicians that the harmonic mean is found neither by subtraction (like the arithmetic mean) nor by division (like the geometric mean), but by
<margin>15</margin>using both methods of calculation. For if there are three lines AD, AC, and AB, of which AD is the largest, AC the middle, and AB the smallest, their ratio is harmonic if AD : AB = (AD – AC) : (AC – AB). Moreover, the ratio is called "harmonic" because the harmony of strings is based upon it. For if there are three strings of the same tension and
<margin>20</margin>thickness, they produce the three principal sounds—the Octave, the Fifth, and the Fourth—if the ratio of their lengths is 3, 4, 6. For strings of 3 and 6 produce the Octave, 4 and 6 the Fifth, and 3 and 4 the Fourth. Moreover, the numbers 3, 4, and 6 are so related that 3 : 6 = (4 – 3) : (6 – 4). It is well known that Plato toiled long and hard in solving
<margin>25</margin>the problem of finding two means between two lines; since it could not be solved geometrically, he himself attempted it mechanically. See van Swinden’s *Elements of Geometry* (pp. 130, 496, etc.) from Jacob's version.

**It is observed in double ratio**] The numeral adjective *diplasion* <note>original Greek: διπλασίων</note>
<margin>30</margin>instead of *diplasios* <note>original Greek: διπλάσιος</note> is not rare among later writers; see Lobeck, *Phrynichus* 411. To the examples collected by Struve (*Opuscula* II, p. 235), add, if it is worth the effort, Dio Chrysostom (Or. 41.12) and Josephus (*Antiquities* VIII.3.8). From Plutarch, *pollaplasion* <note>original Greek: πολλαπλασίων; "manifold"</note> belongs here (*Sayings of Kings and Commanders*, p. 191 C); on the other hand, *eikosaplasion* <note>twenty-fold</note> (*On the Face in the Moon* 35, p. 925 C) has already been removed from the Parisian edition.

### CHAPTER XXIII.

**Aristotle, the student of Plato, says these things**] Understand "student" <note>original: μαθητής</note> to be implied.
<margin>40</margin>Schaefer illustrated this rarer locution with examples in Bos’s *Ellipses* (p. 261). Later writers sometimes indicate that someone is a disciple of someone else by using "the one from [someone]" <note>original: ὁ ἀπό τινος</note>, about which Schaefer said the same in *Meletemata* (p. 27). The following things, which are missing from our editions of the Stagirite <note>Aristotle</note>, were claimed by Patricius (*Peripatetic Discussions*, p. 76) for the lost book of Aristotle
<margin>45</margin>*On Music*, which was mentioned by Diogenes Laertius (V, 26).

**And in the multitudes according to Aristotle**] After "multitudes" <note>original: πλήθεσι</note> in the editions of Reiske and Wyttenbach, there follows "and" <note>original: καί</note>, which I have deleted (having been bracketed by Dübner), as it likely arose from the following word "according to" <note>original: κατά</note>.

<summary>This page provides a philological and mathematical commentary on Plutarch's text, identifying historical figures like the musician Polyidus and explaining the complex "harmonic mean" used in Plato's Timaeus to describe the soul and musical intervals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music." The previous page discussed the history of musical competitions and technical terms for musical "centos" or medleys. This page shifts to the mathematical and philosophical foundations of music (even and odd numbers), the use of specific melodies in Spartan and Cretan warfare, and the moral role of music in ancient education.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XXIV—XXVI.

### CHAPTER XXIV.

*from the even and the odd* <note>original: ἔκ τε τῆς ἀρτίας καὶ περισσῆς</note>] The common reading is: *from the infinite and the limiting* <note>original: ἔκ τε τῆς ἀπείρου καὶ περαινούσης</note>. For Burette rightly opines in Vol. 23, 73: ‘I believe one should read *from the even and the odd* <note>original: ἔκ τε τῆς ἀρτίου καὶ περισσοῦ</note> (though I preferred what I wrote above) and *from the even-odd nature* <note>original: ἐκ τῆς ἀρτιοπερίσσου φύσεως</note>: unless the two words *infinite* and *limiting*, the infinite and the finite, were also taken in the sense of the *pair* [even] and the *impair* [odd], and of this, until now, I have no example.’ For the error of the scribe seems to have arisen from poorly understood abbreviations. *Even-odd numbers* <note>original: Ἀρτιοπέρισσοι</note>, which differ from *even-times-even* numbers, are those numbers which have the number two as a factor only once <note>original German: "die zwei nur einmal zum einfachen factor haben"</note>, such as 2, 6, 10, 14: *even-odd numbers are those measured by a pair and any odd number*, Theo of Smyrna, *Arithmetic* ch. 9 with the note by Bullialdus p. 233. Add Nicomachus, *Arithmetic* I, 9. Philoponus, *On the Creation of the World* p. 3 C.

### CHAPTER XXV.

*perception through harmony* <note>original: τὴν αἴσθησιν διὰ τὴν ἁρμονίαν</note>] The common word order is: *but indeed also the perceptions that arise in bodies through harmony, some being heavenly and divine, etc.*, which I have decided must be changed.

### CHAPTER XXVI.

*primarily* <note>original: προηγουμένως</note>] See the notes in chapter II.
*The Castorean melody* <note>original: Καστόρειον μέλος</note>] Thucydides (V, 70; cf. Gellius I, 11), Lucian (*On the Dance* ch. 10), Polyaenus (I, 10), Dio Chrysostom (Oration XXXII, 60), Valerius Maximus (II, 6) and others record that the Spartans <note>original: Lacedaemonios</note> went to war with flutes <note>term: tibiis; double-piped woodwind instruments, often called 'auloi'</note> playing. The people of Croton also led a troop of flute players into battle, Athenaeus XII p. 520 D. Although this military use of flutes was very ancient, according to what was told of the Lydians by Herodotus (I, 17) and Athenaeus (XII p. 517 A), it seems to have been adopted among the Spartans only in a more recent age. At least Alcman (fragment 14) mentioned the use of the cithara <note>A professional version of the lyre</note> in war. But it could not be helped that stringed instruments were found to be poorly suited for playing before a large number of soldiers. Pausanias (III, 17, 5) contends that flutes, lyres, and citharas were used simultaneously. However, the flute tunes usually adapted to an anapaestic rhythm <note>A "marching" beat: short-short-long</note>, which were played whenever the Spartans went out to battle, are called by the common name *embateria* [marching songs], or *epibateria*, or *enoplia* [armed songs], Athenaeus XIV p. 630 F. There were various types of these. Regarding the "Adonian Lesbian" tune, which is numbered among the marching songs by Hesychius, it is not clear. Boeckh has proved against Valckenaer that Hesychius's *Sarsiteian dance* should not be included among the marching songs. But the *Castorean melody* was a specific type of marching tune, which accompanied the "Marching Paean" <note>A hymn of prayer or victory</note> whenever the soldiers proceeded to battle in dense ranks, Plutarch, *Life of Lycurgus* 22; Pollux IV, 78; Scholiast on

<column-break/>

<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XXVI—XXVII.</header>

Pindar, *Pythian Odes* II, 127. Moreover, the name seems to indicate that the "Castorium" was originally a melody specific to the cavalry <note>Castor was the legendary patron of horsemen</note>. See Santen on Terentianus Maurus p. 57, Mueller’s *Dorians* II p. 335 ff., Boeckh’s *Metrical Systems of Pindar* p. 276.
<margin>5</margin> *to use this style — the Cretans*] Athenaeus agrees (XII p. 517 A). Furthermore, we learn that the flute was also used among the Cretans from Strabo (X p. 679) and Polybius (IV, 20, 6) whom Athenaeus copied (XIV p. 627 D). Gellius (I, 11) names the cithara. In general, the use of these instruments in battle seems to have come to Sparta from the Cretans.
<margin>10</margin> *To Zeus Sthenios*] Hesychius: *Sthenia; a certain contest in Argos was so named*. Regarding Zeus as a god of war, of strength and virtue, see Preller, *Greek Mythology* I p. 90. Musical contests in honor of Zeus were also established at Ithome, Pausanias IV, 33, 2.
<margin>15</margin> *it is customary for flutes to be played for the pentathletes*] Pausanias recorded the same regarding his own time (V, 17, 10). In an earlier age, flutes played for wrestlers, which, besides Pausanias in the place cited, Pollux (IV, 56) also reported from Aristotle's *On the Tyrrhenians*.
*that which was composed by Hierax*] Regarding Hierax, see the notes in chapter IX.
<margin>20</margin>

### CHAPTER XXVII.

*the theatrical muse*] Regarding the degenerate music of the theaters, see the notes in chapter XV.
<margin>25</margin> *and the education of the young*] Bernhardy teaches what roles music played in the education of boys in *Greek Literary History* I p. 55, 77. The principal passage is Strabo I p. 15 f: *And the cities of the Greeks educate their children first and foremost through poetry, certainly not for the sake of mere entertainment, but for discipline;*
<margin>30</margin> *since even the musicians, when teaching how to pluck the strings and play the flute, claim this virtue; for they say they are educators and correctors of character,* to which words are added those which Polybius (IV, 20; Athenaeus XIV p. 626) told regarding the Arcadians. The Cretans also, besides the art of reading, taught
<margin>35</margin> boys the first elements of music, Strabo X p. 483, 679. This was also the reason why the ancient singers and lyre-players wished to excel in integrity of character. Plutarch, *On Superstition* ch. 3: *Those who thought to preserve traditional music commanded the singers to the cithara to sing with a "just mouth"* <note>Meaning singing with moral correctness or proper pronunciation</note>.
*that the theater took its name from the god* <note>original: ὅτι τὸ θέατρον ἀπὸ τοῦ θεοῦ τὴν προσηγορίαν ἔλαβε</note>] I
<margin>40</margin> do not remember reading this in other writers. However, the *Etymologicum Gudianum* (p. 200, 19) comes very close under the word *epitheiasas*: *from the name "theos" [god], the verb "theao" or "theo," the aorist "etheasa," the participle "theasas," with the addition of the letter "i" it becomes "theiasas"; or from "theiazo" [to be inspired by a god]* <note>The author is discussing a folk etymology linking 'theater' with 'god'</note>. The Athenian theater, being separate from the temple of Bacchus in the Lenaeum (Photius under *Lenaion*,
<margin>45</margin> Hesychius *contest at the Lenaeum*, Bekker's *Anecdota* p. 278, *Etymologicum Magnum* p. 361, 39) does not seem to have been built before the time of the Persian Wars. Regarding that theater which, on the authority of Suidas under the words *Pratinas* and *ikria*, is said to have been in Aegina in the age of Draco, Geppert rightly discusses it in *The Ancient Greek Theater* p. 31.

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," specifically focusing on Chapters 27 and 28. The discussion transitions from the historical antiquity of theaters to the specific technical inventions of early Greek musicians like Terpander and Archilochus, particularly regarding musical modes, rhythms, and the origins of specific song types like the drinking song (skolion).</meta>

COMMENTARY ON CHAPS. XXVII—XXVIII.

n. 1 he doubted. Nor does it seem that greater antiquity should be granted to the theater of Syracuse.
*corruption* <note>original: διαφθοράς</note>] Thus the Vatican manuscript, instead of *differences* <note>original: διαφοράς</note>. Burette had already 
<margin>5</margin>conjectured this in Volume XXIII, page 87.

### CAP. XXVIII.

*the Dorian nete* <note>original: τὴν Δώριον νήτην; the 'nete' was the highest-pitched string on the lyre</note>] Even if we grant that this was invented by Terpander, as Aristotle also testifies in *Problems* XIX, 32, the name itself is more recent. For the names of the 
<margin>10</margin>*Hypate* [lowest string], *Mese* [middle], *Paramese* [next to middle], and *Nete* [highest] were invented by Pythagoras, according to Iamblichus' *Life of Pythagoras*, 26. Furthermore, this passage confirms the opinion of Bernhardy against Mueller (*Dorians* II, p. 312), who denied that the Dorian mode was older than Terpander. For Mueller, *whom Ulrich follows in his History of Lyric Poetry, p. 276*, thought the name itself was enough to prove its extreme antiquity.
<margin>15</margin>*the Mixolydian mode* <note>original: τὸν Μιξολύδιον τόνον</note>] see the notes on chapter XVI.
*along with the orthios and the marked trochee* <note>original: πρὸς τε τῷ ὀρθίῳ καὶ τὸν σημαντὸν τροχαῖον</note>] Commonly written as *along with the marked orthios trochee*. These words appeared corrupt to interpreters, as almost all scribes thought some words were repeated without cause by error. Thus, Burette emended it to *the one according to 
<margin>20</margin>the rhythms, the orthios and the marked trochee*. Dübner wrote: *the one according to the orthios and the marked trochee*. But since one can gather nothing certain about the corruption from the variation in manuscripts, I thought the passage should be healed with an easier remedy. 
<margin>25</margin>In this writing, do not believe there is anything superfluous. For if I see correctly, the "upright melodies" <note>original: nomi orthii</note> composed in honor of Minerva [Athena] did not properly have their name from the rhythm, but from the Homeric verse in *Iliad* XI, 10, which I explained in the notes to chapter IV. There were also "upright melodies" proceeding in dactylic meter, as you may gather from chapter seven, 
<margin>30</margin>and that kind of melody originated with Olympus. Others, on the contrary, had trochaic rhythms; when this system later prevailed, that rhythm was named "orthios" [upright] from the melody, rather than the melody from the rhythm. Therefore, the meaning of the words is this: Terpander is said to have discovered both the style of the "upright melody," which 
<margin>35</margin>consisted of "upright trochees," and to have formed the "marked trochees" in addition to the "upright" ones. Rossbach also understood this passage this way in *Rhythm*, page 100: <note>original German: "Terpander soll, so sagt Plutarch, die weise der ὄρθιος μελῳδία nach orthischen Rhythmen und nach analogie des orthius auch den trochaeus semantus erfunden haben."</note> "Terpander, so Plutarch says, is supposed to have invented the style of the *orthios melody* according to 'upright' rhythms and, by analogy of the 'upright,' also the *marked trochee*." 
<margin>40</margin>He explained what "upright" and "marked" trochees are on page 99: <note>original German: "beide rhythmen sind nichts als gedehnte molossen in langsamster ἀγωγή. traegt ein gedehnter molossus auf der ersten laenge den ictus, so ist er ein trochaeus semantus, hat er ihn auf der zweiten, so ist er ein orthius."</note> "both rhythms are nothing but extended molossi [a foot of three long syllables] in the slowest *tempo*. If an extended molossus carries the beat on the first long syllable, it is a *marked trochee*; if it has it on the second, it is an *upright* [orthius]." Whatever the case, the "upright melody" did not 
<margin>45</margin>originate with Terpander, as it was performed on the flute (Scholiast on Aristophanes' *Acharnians* 16), but from the art of Olympus it was finally received into common use by the authors of the second musical establishment, as you may gather from chapter IX: *those 

<column-break/>

COMMENTARY ON CHAP. XXVIII.

around Polymnestus of the so-called orthian melodies*. Furthermore, a significant argument for this is added by the fact that in the earlier chapter IV, in which Plutarch followed the best authors, he did not list this melody among Terpander’s inventions, and here too 
<margin>5</margin>he pronounced the matter more cautiously.
*and furthermore — Terpander was the inventor of drinking songs* <note>original: ἔτι δὲ — καὶ τῶν σκολιῶν μελῶν Τέρπανδρος εὑρετής</note>] I wrote *moreover* [eti] instead of *if* [ei] following Wyttenbach's conjecture. But if Pindar attributes the invention of drinking songs <note>original: skolia</note> to Terpander, it is to be inferred that a certain fixed musical norm 
<margin>10</margin>was applied by him to songs of this kind. See Ilgen's *Inquiry into Scolic Poetry*, page LXXVII; Plehn's *Lesbiaca*, page 164; Bernhardy's *History of Greek Literature* I, page 63.
*but indeed also Archilochus*] That Plutarch’s opinion is otherwise correct—that whatever metrical invention Archilochus made was also a musical one—Ulrich has already concluded in *History of Lyric Poetry*, page 276, both from the whole passage and from the words 
<margin>15</margin>*and the striking of the strings regarding these things*. Liebel has collected the passages of those who, besides Plutarch, have discussed the metrical inventions of Archilochus in *Archilochus*, page 23.
*the tension into non-homogeneous rhythms*] I would refer this only to the invention of "asyntartons" [disconnected meters], and not to "epodes," about 
<margin>20</margin>which Liebel thought on page 32.
*the parakataloge* <note>original: τὴν παρακαταλογήν; a style of musical recitation or "speaking to music"</note>] What *parakataloge* is can be understood neither from Plutarch's words nor from Aristotle's *Problems* XIX, 10: "Why is the *parakataloge* in songs tragic? Is it because of its inequality? For the unequal is pathetic and found in the greatness of fortune or grief. But the 
<margin>25</margin>equal is less mournful." Liebel's opinion (page 33) that *parakataloge* does not differ from a rhythmic shift <note>original: metabole</note> is not very pleasing. We gain nothing from Burette's definition (Vol. XIV, p. 384): "a disorder in the arrangement of sounds and in the rhythm, from which resulted an unequal modulation, rough, irregular, and by 
<margin>30</margin>that very fact all the more suited to the tragic and pathetic, as Aristotle says." Regarding Thiersch's opinion (*Introduction to Pindar*, p. 51), appealing to Schneider's *Lexicon*, that *parakataloge* is the ending of a melody in minor harmonies or "paraphones," opposed to a melody ending on the principal sound or the "antiphone," I 
<margin>35</margin>doubt it for this reason: that then *parakataloge* would be a purely musical invention and not also a metrical one. Since "to speak in catalog" <note>original: καταλογάδην εἰρημένον</note> is that which is pronounced in prose [pedestrian speech], perhaps *parakataloge* is a certain prose-like recitation alongside and within the singing. Then it would certainly be clear why Aristotle 
<margin>40</margin>called its use irregular.
*and the Cretic rhythm* <note>original: καὶ τὸ κρητικόν</note>] Thus Burette, Liebel in *Archilochus* p. 36, Hoeck in *Crete* III p. 358, and Bernhardy (as above) p. 314. The books say *the prokritikon*. To be sure, Glaucus taught in chapter X that the Cretic rhythm was only used by Thaletas around the 40th Olympiad. But this must be 
<margin>45</margin>tolerated in Plutarch: that he sometimes brings forward the contrary and conflicting opinions of others on the same subject in the same booklet, especially if in one place he followed worse authors than in another. On the other hand, Ritschel in the *Rhenish Museum* N. I p. 285 [reads] *and the pro-Cretic...*

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," specifically focusing on the metrical and rhythmic innovations attributed to the poet Archilochus and other early Greek musicians. The text evaluates the theories of modern scholars like Ritschl and Bernhardy against the ancient testimony regarding poetic structures like the tetrameter and the prosodiac meter.</meta>

<page-num>118</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CAP. XXVIII.</header>

...ordered [the word] *chotic* to be read, so that it might be a trochaic tetrameter <note>a line of four metrical units</note>, which the ancient experts on meter derived from a six-foot line preceded by a cretic <note>a foot consisting of long-short-long syllables</note>, as seen in Terentianus Maurus (v. 2280 ff.), Marius Victorinus (p. 2572), and Rufinus (p. 2708-9). Concerning the analogy of why the <term>prochretic</term> <note>original: προχρητικόν</note> should not only be that which precedes a cretic, 
<margin>5</margin> but also that to which a cretic is prefixed, Ritschl appealed to the <term>probrachys</term> <note>original: πρόβραχυς</note>, by which name this meter is designated in Diomedes (p. 478) - - - -. Therefore, he suggests that in the preceding sections, the phrase *the tetrameters* <note>original: τὰ τετράμετρα</note> either came from an interpolator or from a scribe’s error, who intended to write *the trimeters* <note>original: τὰ τρίμετρα</note>, that most noble invention of Archilochus. 
<margin>10</margin> Since these things were already mentioned before, it matters little in this place, where Plutarch sought three accounts from different sources (1. *But truly also Archilochus — the striking* <note>original: ἀλλὰ μὴν καὶ Ἀρχίλοχον — κροῦσιν</note>, 2. *and to him first — the increase* <note>original: πρώτῳ δὲ αὐτῷ — αὔξησις</note>, 3. *and by some, etc.* <note>original: ὑπ' ἐνίων δὲ κτλ.</note>), in which there are both unique and common elements. This whole argument, to which I do not wish to deny 
<margin>15</margin> the praise of sharp wit and subtlety, seems to me more ingenious than true. Therefore, I thought we should rest with Bernhardy on Burette's emendation.

<term>the prosodiac</term> <note>original: τὸ προσοδιακόν; a processional meter</note>] Regarding the passages of the metricians concerning this meter, which took its name from processional songs (*prosodia*), Ritschl collected them in the place cited (p. 287) following Liebel (Archil. p. 35). From these, it appears the verse was bipartite, 
<margin>20</margin> beginning from a dactylic order <note>long-short-short</note> shortened into two syllables with an iambic lead-in, and ending in three feet, either full or shortened, like this:
-> ˘ | ́¯ ˘ ˘ | ́¯ ˘ ˘ | ́¯ | ˘ ˘ ¯ | ¯ | ˘ <-
Even now, verses corresponding to this meter survive among the fragments of Archilochus.
<margin>25</margin> <term>and the increase of the heroic [meter]</term> <note>original: καὶ ἡ τοῦ ἡρῴου αὔξησις</note>] Commonly read as *and the increase of the first* <note>original: καὶ ἡ τοῦ πρώτου αὔξησις</note>, which Liebelt and Burette wrongly understood as referring to the cretic. Ritschl also approved Salmasius’s emendation to *heroic*. 
<margin>30</margin> Nothing is more frequent than the confusion between *pr* <note>πρ</note> and *er* <note>ηρ</note>. Thus, the names of Prodicus and Herodicus are often confused, as Ast noted regarding Plato’s *Republic* (p. 465). Ritschl likewise considered the use of a double *to* <note>τό</note> for *ton* <note>τόν</note> in the following parts. Indeed, this "increased" or extra-syllable hexameter should be understood as the type of verse shown in Horace’s *Odes* IV, 7, compared with Terentianus Maurus (p. 2422).

<term>and by some, also the elegiac</term> <note>original: ὑπ' ἐνίων δὲ καὶ τὸ ἐλεγεῖον</note>] Furthermore, Orion of Thebes (p. 58) names Archilo-
<margin>35</margin> chus as the inventor of the elegiac, citing Didymus’s *On Poets* (Schmidt, Did. fragm. p. 387); see also Marius Victorinus (p. 2555, 2588) and Isidore’s *Etymologies* (I, 38). Compare Liebel (Archil. p. 28) and Francke (Callinus p. 33).

<term>the stretching of the iambic toward the epibatus paeon</term> <note>original: ἡ τοῦ ἰαμβείου πρὸς τὸν ἐπιβατὸν παιῶνα ἔντασις</note>] This is what was called above, in general, *the stretching into non-homogeneous rhythms*. Regarding the Paeon epibatus, see Aristides Quintilianus (I, p. 38) and 
<margin>40</margin> Rossbach’s *Rhythmics* (p. 105, 106), where we read: ‘the Paeon epibatus is a foot of 5 two-beat lengths—. Archilochus first applied it, doubtless for the cult songs for Dionysus and Demeter, which had a corresponding character. 
<margin>45</margin> Then Olympus used it for the enharmonic Phrygian mode, probably in similar compositions, such as the *metroa* <note>hymns to the Mother of the Gods</note> sung to Cybele (Plut. *On Music* 33),

<column-break/>

<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CAP. XXVIII—XXIX.</header>

yet the Paeon epibatus did not remain restricted to the cult; we also find it in comedy.’

<term>the tragic poets</term> <note>original: τοὺς τραγικοὺς ποιητάς</note>] Unless Suidas (under the entry *Arion*) had mixed up different things, as Geppert warned (Theatr. Gr. p. 14 ff.), this would apply to the poets of lyrical tragedy. For he reports that Arion, 
<margin>5</margin> in his dithyrambs, introduced Satyrs reciting verses: *it is said — that he also introduced Satyrs to speak in meter* <note>original: λέγεται — καὶ Σατύρους εἰσενεγκεῖν ἔμμετρα λέγειν</note>. Thus, this was the custom for a long time before the poets of Attic tragedy.

<term>the striking under the song</term> <note>original: τὴν κροῦσιν τὴν ὑπὸ τὴν ᾠδήν</note>] I understand this to refer to solo flutes or strings interspersed with the singing at individual intervals, whereas among the 
<margin>10</margin> ancients, while the singing was suspended, the striking of instruments was also practiced. Cf. Aristotle, *Problems* XIX, 39 (p. 921 a, 25). <term>Proschordos</term> <note>original: Πρόσχορδον</note> means "in unison" or "fitting"; therefore, *to strike in unison* <note>original: πρόσχορδα κρούειν</note> is to play instruments such that every sound of the instrument matches the sound of the voice or melody. Ulrici erred 
<margin>15</margin> in his *History of Lyric Poetry* (p. 597) by deciding that the false reading of some books, *prochorga* <note>πρόχοργα</note>, should be defended. Plato’s words in the *Laws* (VII, p. 812 D) illustrate the phrase somewhat:
> One must use the notes of the lyre for the sake of the clarity of the strings, both the lyre-player and the student, rendering the notes in unison with the vocal notes.
<margin>20</margin>

### CAP. XXIX.

<margin>25</margin> <term>To Polymnestus indeed</term> <note>original: Πολυμνήστῳ δή</note>] Concerning Polymnestus, see the notes on chapter VIII; regarding the *eklysis* and *ekbole* <note>musical terms for lowering or raising pitch</note>, as well as the enharmonic genus invented by Olympus, see chapter XI; and for the Prosodiac, see chapter XXVIII.

<term>and the choreic</term> <note>original: καὶ τὸν χορεῖον</note>] Understand this to mean the spondee, specifically the double spondee <note>original: σπονδεῖος διπλοῦς</note>, as is always the case whenever the 
<margin>30</margin> discussion concerns the spondaic meter of hymns and nomes <note>traditional musical compositions</note>.

<term>that these things are so</term> <note>original: ὅτι ταῦτα οὕτως ἔχει</note>] Amyot translates: ‘now it is certain, that none of the ancient songs have them.’ Therefore, he seems to have read *not yet* <note>original: οὔπως</note> instead of *thus* <note>original: οὕτως</note>, which Burette defends: ‘indeed, Plutarch proposes to show that the ancients made ad-
<margin>35</margin> ditions to their music—and after having made the count, he adds: a proof that these modes, these rhythms, etc., were introduced anew into the ancient music, is that one manifestly sees there is no trace of these novelties in the airs or nomes older than the authors 
<margin>40</margin> of these innovations.’ But, I ask, who were those older nomes? Rather, Plutarch says that in the ancient nomes of Olympus all these things exist: therefore their invention is not owed to a more recent age.

<term>Lasus of Hermione</term> <note>original: Λᾶσος ὁ Ἑρμιονούς</note>] Lasus, son of Charminus, lived at Athens under 
<margin>45</margin> Hipparchus around the 63rd and 66th Olympiads and later. Along with Apollodorus and Agathocles, he was Pindar’s teacher in music. Concerning the wit of the man who accused Onomacritus of interpolation, he composed an *asigmic song* <note>original: ᾠδὴν ἄσιγμον; a song without the letter 'S'</note> and published what are called *Asigmata*...

<summary>This page analyzes the origins of Greek poetic meters and musical techniques, specifically debating whether certain rhythms and the practice of instrumental accompaniment (krousis) were invented by Archilochus or existed in earlier "nomes." It also discusses Lasus of Hermione's technical experiments, such as his famous songs written without the letter sigma.</summary>
<keywords>Archilochus, Polymnestus, Lasus of Hermione, poetic meter, tetrameter, prosodiac meter, Paeon epibatus, musical accompaniment, krousis, asigmic poetry, Plutarch De Musica</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," specifically examining the innovations of Lasus of Hermione and the technical definition of "Agoge." It follows the previous page's discussion of Archilochus and early metrical structures.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XXIX.

<page-num>120</page-num>

...clever and witty sayings, the ancients have passed down many things, some of whom even ranked him among the number of the Seven Sages. Compare Schneidewin’s commentary on Lasus of Hermione, *Introduction to the Gottingen Lectures, 1842*. He refined the **dithyramb** <note>a wild, choral hymn dedicated to Dionysus</note> previously <margin>5</margin> established by Arion, and introduced it into the public festivals at Athens. Therefore, the invention of the later or "Attic" dithyramb is attributed to him, according to the *Scholia on Pindar, Olympian XIII, 25* and *Tzetzes’ Prologue to Lycophron, p. 252*. By some, the invention of the dithyramb in general is incorrectly attributed to him, as in the *Scholia on Aristophanes' Birds, 1403*. But as to what exactly Lasus <margin>10</margin> changed in the genre of dithyrambs, we remain ignorant. Not without good cause, however, does Ulrici in his *History of Lyrical Poetry, p. 140*, contend that he abolished the antistrophic form <note>the balanced, repeating structure of choral song</note> of the dithyrambs, despite the objections of O. Mueller in his *History of Greek Literature, Vol. II, p. 283 ff.*, and Schneidewin in the work cited above, p. 15. Furthermore, Lasus was the first who composed a book on musical theory in prose; from this source, that strange <margin>15</margin> opinion of Lasus in Aristoxenus’ *Harmonics I, p. 3* seems to have flowed: "that the sound has breadth" <note>original: πλάτος τὸν φθόγγον ἔχειν</note>. Finally, the author Theo in *Music, ch. 12, p. 91 (Bull edition)*, states that Lasus, along with Hippasus of Metapontum, the Pythagorean philosopher, conducted certain observations pertaining to harmony using earthenware vessels filled with water, in order to show "that the speeds and slownesses of the movements, through which the harmonies exist, correspond to one another" <note>original: συνέπεσθαι τῶν κινήσεων τὰ τάχη καὶ τὰς βραδυτῆτας, δι’ ὧν αἱ συμφωνίαι</note>. <margin>20</margin> Except by Plutarch, however, Lasus has been categorized as a "corruptor" of music by no one else, as far as I know.

**[He transformed the rhythms] into the dithyrambic agoge** <note>original: εἰς τὴν διθυραμβικὴν ἀγωγὴν μεταστήσας τοὺς ῥυθμούς</note>] <term>Agoge</term> <note>literally "the lead" or "conduct"</note> is a technical term of both the Rhythmic and Harmonic arts. And indeed, the <margin>25</margin> *rhythmical* <term>Agoge</term>, of which mention is made here, is determined by the slower or faster rate at which the rhythmic durations, which fill the metrical foot, are delivered. It is, therefore, what musicians among our own people signify by the name of musical time (they use the Italian word *tempo*), as well as those types of time which are indicated by the superscribed words *crescendo* <note>increasing in volume</note>, <margin>30</margin> *decrescendo* <note>decreasing in volume</note>, *calando* <note>dropping in speed and volume</note>, and similar terms. Compare Aristides Quintilianus, p. 42: "Rhythmical <term>agoge</term> is the speed or slowness of the time-lengths; for example, when the ratios remain the same which the downbeats make in relation to the upbeats, we may deliver the magnitudes of each time (that is, of the upbeat and downbeat) differently" <note>original: ἀγωγὴ δέ ἐστι ῥυθμικὴ χρόνων τάχος ἢ βραδυτής· οἷον ὅταν τῶν λόγων σωζομένων, οὓς αἱ θέσεις ποιοῦνται πρὸς τὰς ἀρσεις, διαφόρως ἑκάστου χρόνου (i. e. ἄρσεως καὶ θέσεως) τὰ μεγέθη προφερώμεθα</note>; <margin>35</margin> add Aristoxenus’ *Harmonics, p. 34*, and Plato’s *Republic, III, p. 400 C*. The recent editors of Aristoxenus' *Rhythmics*, Feussner (p. 34) and Bartels (p. 44), have correctly explained this matter against Meibom, Boeckh, and Hermann. Compare Rossbach’s *Rhythm, p. 173*. It is clear that the <term>agoge</term> itself varied according to the different "ethical character" (as the Greeks say) of the musical compositions. <margin>40</margin> Nor were different types of rhythms more or less suited to different types of <term>agoge</term>. Thus, Plutarch’s words mean this: that because of the dithyrambic <term>agoge</term>, Lasus innovated or changed certain things in rhythmic composition. Conversely, the *Harmonic* <term>Agoge</term> is called one of the four types of melody-making <note>melopoeia</note>, <margin>45</margin> placed in the joining of those sounds through some song which followed one another in the system. See Euclid’s *Harmonics, p. 22*: "Agoge is therefore the path of the melody through successive notes" <note>original: ἀγωγὴ μὲν οὖν ἐστὶν ἡ διὰ τῶν ἑξῆς φθόγγων ὁδὸς τοῦ μέλους</note>.

<column-break/>

<page-num>121</page-num>
# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XXIX.

...and Aristides Quintilianus I, 19, compared with Drieberg’s *Musical Discipline, p. 57*. Athenaeus took the word no differently in *Book XIV, p. 624 D*: "The <term>agoge</term> of the melody, then, which the Dorians used to make, they called harmony" <note>original: τὴν οὖν ἀγωγὴν τῆς μελῳδίας, ἣν οἱ Δωριεῖς ἐποιοῦντο, ἐκάλουν ἁρμονίαν</note>. There were also different types of harmonic <term>agoge</term>, according to Aristides Quintilianus, <margin>5</margin> Book I, p. 29.

**He brought the pre-existing music into a state of change** <note>original: εἰς μετάθεσιν τὴν προϋπάρχουσαν ἤγαγε μουσικήν</note>] Burette says: "It appears from the version of Valgulio and from that of Amyot, that they read this passage differently in their Greek copies. Here is the first: *he brought music to inflections and mutations which had not existed before.* Here is the second: *he introduced a great mutation in the Music, which was not there previously.* <margin>10</margin> It is visible that they both read in the Greek, 'brought music into a change *not* previously existing' (using <note>original: μὴ</note> instead of <note>original: τὴν</note>). But the following context justifies the other reading."

<margin>15</margin> **For this man—scattered [the melody] into more notes** <note>original: οὗτος γὰρ — διέρριψεν εἰς πλείονας φθόγγους</note>] These words are placed lower in the editions, in Chapter XXX, after the phrase "but neither Philoxenus, nor Timotheus." But this is wrong. For then they would have to be referred to Timotheus, whom they do not fit any more than they do Philoxenus. But they cannot even belong to Melanippides, <margin>20</margin> to whom, since a ten-stringed instrument is later attributed, he certainly was not the first to change the seven-fold number of strings. Furthermore, many are named even before Melanippides who increased the number of strings. For already the eight-stringed instrument of Pythagoras came into use through two perfect tetrachords <note>units of four notes</note>, compare <margin>25</margin> Boeckh’s *Philolaus, p. 71*. The eighth string is said to have been discovered by Simonides, a contemporary of Lasus, according to Pliny’s *Natural History VII, 57*. Phrynis introduced a nine-fold number of strings. Even Ion of Chios mentioned a ten-stringed lyre in Euclid’s *Harmonics, p. 19*. Even if this does not mean that we should attribute an <margin>30</margin> eleventh string to Melanippides (for an eleven-stringed instrument seems never to have been in use), nevertheless it appears, I think, that Melanippides is not the one whom Plutarch could have reported to have increased the number of strings beyond all others. On the other hand, if these words are referred to Lasus, everything proceeds well in both Chapter XXX and XXIX. For Lasus, <margin>35</margin> in order to equal the "polyphony" <note>many-voicedness</note> of the flutes, was said to have employed several new sounds on the lyre, to which "scattered notes" <note>original: φθόγγοι διερριμμένοι</note> uniquely belong. Nor should it be omitted that the ancients knew Lasus as a lyre-player, and perhaps even a flute-player, see *Scholia on Pindar, p. 4*. There remain the words **down to Terpander of Antissa** <note>original: ἕως εἰς Τέρπανδρον τὸν Ἀντισσαῖον</note> and these <margin>40</margin> are troublesome. For if it was only Lasus or some of the more recent musicians who increased the ancient seven-fold number of strings, it is foolish to say that "up until Terpander" the lyre was equipped with seven strings <note>since Terpander lived much earlier</note>. Therefore, these words are either corrupt or show the hand of an interpolator <note>someone who added to the text later</note>, which seems more likely to me. Moreover, <margin>45</margin> Wyttenbach also understood that the order of words was disturbed in this place, and he judged that the words "similarly also Melanippides — more notes" <note>original: ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ Μελανιππίδης — πλείονας φθόγγους</note> should be moved from here further down between... <margin>50</margin>

<summary>This page discusses the musical innovations of Lasus of Hermione, particularly his role in evolving the dithyramb and musical theory, while providing a detailed technical definition of "agoge" in both rhythm and harmony. It also addresses textual disputes regarding when and by whom the number of lyre strings was increased beyond the traditional seven.</summary>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XXIX—XXX.

<page-num>122</page-num>

...was corrupted <note>original: φθάρη</note> and "as also Pherecrates," as well as Boeckh in *On Pindaric Meters, p. 205 n. 8*.

### CHAPTER XXX.

*Melanippides the lyric poet* <note>original: Μελανιππίδης ὁ μελοποιός</note>] Melanippides of Melos was a **dithyrambic** <note>a choral song honoring Dionysus</note> poet descended from a family in which sons seem to have received musical skill from their fathers as a kind of inheritance. For both his grandfather Melanippides and his father Crito <margin>5</margin> held a reputation among the lyric poets. He was younger than Diagoras of Melos (see Suidas, under the entry) but older than Pherecrates and Democritus of Chios the musician (see Aristotle, <margin>10</margin> *Rhetoric* III, 9), both of whom mentioned Melanippides in their books. Therefore, he lived in Athens between the 75th and 85th Olympiads <note>roughly 480–444 BC</note> and later, from whence he went to King Perdiccas in the 2nd year of the 91st Olympiad <note>415 BC</note>. He was still alive during the reign of Archelaus. Scheibel, in *Melanippides of Melos, Dithyrambic Poet, Part I* (Guben school program, 1848), <margin>15</margin> ingeniously reconstructed the man’s age by untangling the confused order of facts about Melanippides found in Suidas. It is inferred by probable conjecture, rather than certain ancient testimony, that Melanippides’ family practiced the art of singing to the **cithara** <note>a professional version of the lyre</note>. However, if <margin>20</margin> Plutarch, following the mockery of the Comic poets, placed Melanippides first among those who corrupted genuine music with a passion for innovation after Lasus, one must weigh this against the judgment of Aristodemus in Xenophon’s *Memorabilia* (I, 4, 3). Aristodemus valued Melanippides among the dithyrambic poets as highly as Homer, Sophocles, Polycletus, and Zeuxis <margin>25</margin> in each of their respective genres. The surviving fragments of Melanippides’ work strikingly confirm this judgment. It cannot be gathered from these words of Plutarch, nor is it established elsewhere, that Melanippides increased the number of strings. But the words of Suidas—*Melanippides, who innovated most greatly in the <margin>30</margin> composition of dithyrambs*—gain clarity from a passage in Aristotle’s *Rhetoric* (III, 9, p. 1409 b), where Melanippides is said to have introduced **anabolai** <note>lyrical interludes or preludes</note> in place of **antistrophes** <note>the responding stanzas of a choral song</note>.

Scheibel best explained what an *Anabole* is in *Melanippides, Part II* (1853). Properly and from the beginning, it was any kind of introduction to an epic or lyric song, the same as a **Prooemium** <note>a prelude</note>. The word <margin>35</margin> took on a stricter meaning around the 85th Olympiad in dithyrambic poetry. For then, the introductions of the dithyrambs were called by this name, characterized by a wandering and diffuse plot that had little or nothing to do with the actual story of the dithyramb (cf. Scholia on <margin>40</margin> Aristophanes' *Peace*, 831). The style was bombastic and luxuriant, especially regarding unusual word formations and meanings, while the rhythmic composition was loosened and dissolved—specifically, by removing the strophic division that had been characteristic of the older dithyramb. See Aristotle, *Problems* XIX, 15: *The dithyrambs, since they became imitative, no <margin>45</margin> longer have antistrophes, whereas they previously did.* To this was added a mixed harmony of instruments with frequent changes of tone, according to the words of Dionysius mentioned above.

So far, everything seems clear. However, if the *Anabolae* were

<column-break/>

<page-num>123</page-num>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XXX.

introductions to the dithyrambs, how did Melanippides put them in the place of *antistrophes*, out of which the entire dithyrambs were composed from beginning to end? Furthermore, in the fragments of Pindar’s dithyrambs, no traces of *antistrophes* are found, <margin>5</margin> nor is it at all likely that Pindar’s dithyrambs were ever restricted to an antistrophic form. (Compare Boeckh on the Pindaric fragments, Vol. II, part 2, p. 575; Hermann on Aristotle’s *Poetics* I, 2, p. 89). Moreover, Pindar himself already speaks of an old and obsolete kind of dithyramb in Strabo, p. 469, frag. 5 (Boeckh, p. 581 sq.). <margin>10</margin> Given these facts, Geppert (*Greek Theater*, p. 3) seems to have understood the matter more correctly; he believes Melanippides adopted *anabolae* to replace the *antistrophes* whose absence was felt as a loss in dithyrambs that were already being composed without them. It seems to me that there were several *anabolai* <margin>15</margin> in a single dithyramb, sung not only by the chorus but also by individuals, which served as preludes to each individual part of the dithyramb—not unlike how in tragedies all the episodes are separated by strophic songs. If these things are true, two conclusions follow. First, Ulrici (*History of Lyric Poetry*, p. 140) was not without good cause when he stated <margin>20</margin> that the antistrophic form of dithyrambs was already abolished by Lasus, Pindar’s teacher (as discussed regarding Chapter XXIX). Second, Geppert rightly accused O. Mueller of error for contending in *History of the Dorians* (II, p. 371) that the dithyramb was antistrophic as long as it remained a species of Dorian lyric art; <margin>25</margin> Ulrici also thought this (p. 66 n. 5) based on Dionysius and Plutarch’s words.

*for of old, until Melanippides* <note>original: τὸ γὰρ παλαιὸν, ἕως εἰς Μελανιππίδην</note>] These last three words seem no less interpolated than the earlier phrase "until Terpander of Antissa." Be that as it may, Plutarch by no means says <margin>30</margin> that Melanippides himself was the author of this change <note>the separation of instrumental music from song</note>. Bernhardy (*History of Greek Literature*, I, p. 323) states most correctly: "This passage leaves it doubtful who first caused such a separation; the flute victory of Sakadas at the Pythian games had not decided it; rather, the indignant tone with which Pratinas in Athenaeus XIV <margin>35</margin> p. 617 B—*Pratinas of Phlius, when hired flute-players and dancers occupied the orchestras, was indignant that some people complained the flute-players did not play along with the choruses as was ancestral custom, but rather the choruses sang along with the flute-players, etc.*—condemns the flute to its earlier servitude, suggests a later period."

<margin>40</margin>*and they say Music* <note>original: καὶ τὴν Μουσικὴν λέγουσιν</note>] The manuscripts read *poetry* <note>original: ποίησιν</note>. But Wyttenbach noted: "Valgulius and Burette translate it as *Music*. Certainly the author either wrote this or should have. Likewise, Brunck published it this way in *Fragments of Aristophanes*, Vol. III, p. 170."

*I will speak, not unwillingly* <note>original: λέξω μὲν οὐκ ἄκουσα</note>] This fragment of Pherecrates <margin>45</margin> was terribly corrupted in the manuscripts and was first emended by conjecture by Brunck. He was followed by Heinrich, Jacobs, Hanov, and Burges, who [offered] strange...

<summary>This page discusses the 5th-century BC musical innovator Melanippides of Melos, specifically his introduction of the "anabole" (a lyrical prelude) into the dithyramb. The commentary examines scholarly debates regarding when the traditional strophic structure was abandoned and how instrumental music began to dominate vocal performance.</summary>

<keywords>Melanippides, dithyramb, anabole, antistrophe, musical innovation, ancient Greek music theory, Aristotle, Pindar, Pherecrates, philology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," specifically analyzing Chapter 30 and transitioning to Chapter 31. It focuses on reconstructing fragments from the comic poet Pherecrates and other sources regarding musical innovators.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTER XXX.

<page-num>124</page-num>

...with perversity he did not emend this passage but defiled it; finally, the illustrious Meineke in *Fragments of Comic Poets, Vol. II, p. 326*, whom Dübner followed for the most part. I have adopted Meineke’s text in its entirety as he presented it on page 334, in which not only are individual points excellently <margin>5</margin> corrected, but the verses which were previously 14–18 have been correctly restored to their place after the eighth verse. Again, the word order was disturbed after verse 25, where the books read: "And Aristophanes the comic poet <note>original: καὶ Ἀριστοφάνης ὁ κωμικὸς</note> mentions Philoxenus, and says that he introduced melodies into the cyclic choruses <note>dithyrambic choruses performed in a circle around an altar</note>. And Music says these things: 'disharmonious' <note>original: ἐξαρμονίους</note> etc." <margin>10</margin> Brunck was the first to understand that the following verses belong to the same Pherecrates, and are taken from the same play as the previous ones; others approved, and finally Meineke, at the suggestion of Bergk, placed these verses after verse 23. This having been done, no other emendation is needed except that the words "And Music says these things" <note>original: ἡ δὲ Μουσικὴ ταῦτα λέγει</note> be cast out <margin>15</margin> as a scribe’s addition that attempted to restore the disturbed order to some extent. There remains a corruption in the fifth verse, where instead of "twelve strings" <note>original: χοδραῖς δώδεκα</note> it seems we should read "ten strings" <note>original: χορδαῖσιν δέκα</note>. Furthermore, in the tenth verse, what the books show—"on five strings" <note>original: ἐν πέντε χορδαῖς</note> (the Vatican manuscript has "in five-strings" <note>original: ἐν πενταχόρδαις</note>)—is false; <margin>20</margin> in place of this, Burette, Marpurg, and finally Meineke wrote "on seven strings" <note>original: ἐν ἑπτὰ χορδαῖς</note>. But it should be read as "on nine strings" <note>original: ἐν ἐννέα χορδαῖς</note>, regarding which Ulrici (p. 605) very correctly advised. See what we noted above concerning Phrynis. Recently, Bothius also treated this passage in the Parisian collection of *Comic Fragments*, from whom I have only <margin>25</margin> adopted the punctuation he proposed for verse 22. For what he conjectured for verse 5:
->And he made it [the music] looser with twelve strings<-
->(where before there were eleven); but nevertheless this man was<-
->sufficient —<-
and for verse 13: "but nevertheless this man was not intolerably cruel," are far too bold <margin>30</margin> and arbitrary. It is helpful to transcribe the following from the commentaries of Meineke and Hanov:
Verse 12: "We learn from this passage that Phrynis later returned to a saner method of art, from which it is understood that these things were written after Aristophanes' *Clouds* (v. 970)." *Meineke.*
<margin>35</margin> Verse 13: "He called Cinesias 'Attic,' not to distinguish this Cinesias from

---

*) Forkel erred by the whole heaven <note>idiom: "was completely mistaken"</note> because in *History of Music, Vol. I, p. 301*, he translated this verse as follows: "after him it came into Phrynis's head to abuse me with his coloraturas and runs, to bend and turn me at his pleasure, and to produce 12 different tones from his 5 strings," and then adds: "from this passage one can <margin>40</margin> obviously conclude there was an instrument with a neck that served as a fretboard for the multiplication of tones, so that the tones of the 5 strings could be increased to 12." Forgetful of these words, he later argued on p. 322: "Kircher (Mus. I p. 536) already knew that the ancients knew absolutely no instrument <margin>45</margin> which had a fretboard of any kind." Furthermore, it must be noted that the comic poet's words about twelve harmonies, uttered with a certain poetic license, should not be pressed further.

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XXX—XXXI.

another Cinesias, whom they claim was a Theban—which was Hanov’s opinion—but so that he might oppose Cinesias to the other poets, Melanippides, Phrynis, and Timotheus, who were foreigners by birth. I have shown in *Critical History, p. 229* that there was only one Cinesias famous for the lyric art." *Meineke, p. 328.*
<margin>5</margin> Verse 15: "I believe that 'disharmonious' <note>original: ἐξαρμονίους</note> refers to the movements of the cyclic chorus, which did not harmonize either with the measure of the flutes or the lyre, nor with the song. Thus it seems to have happened in the dithyrambs of Cinesias that the chorus made its turns <margin>10</margin> before they were signaled by the flutes; wherefore, when according to musical logic the chorus should have been positioned on the right side, it was seen on the left, and vice versa. And this explanation is confirmed both by the Scholium on *The Frogs* 153, which says of Cinesias: 'he used much movement in the choruses,' <margin>15</margin> and by the humorous comparison which Pherecrates uses: 'just as on shields, his right side appears as his left.' For 'shields' <note>original: ἀσπίδες</note> refers to the ranks of soldiers, for which meaning see Bergler on Aristophanes' *Lysistrata* 282 and Sturz’s *Xenophon Lexicon Vol. I, p. 444*. In the ranks of soldiers, no one's right side can appear as the left when you look at the front of the ranks, unless someone turns his back; <margin>20</margin> thus the poet simultaneously mocks the cowardice of Cinesias, which is likewise noted quite clearly by Lysias in *Defense regarding the Bribery, p. 708 Reiske*: 'and those who, though Cinesias is in such a [weak] state, have served on more campaigns than he, are the ones who are indignant about the city’s affairs.' It is clear, therefore, that Music, though not changed, was nevertheless in a way corrupted by Cinesias and <margin>25</margin> weakened, with the harmony of the dance and the flutes being removed." *Hanov.*
Verse 23: "To sing 'ant-tunes' <note>original: ᾄδειν μνημηίας; a pun on *myrmekiai*, meaning complex or 'warty' paths</note> means nothing else than to hum something which, on account of the incredible variety of sounds, resembles the likeness of ants running back and forth." *Meineke.*
<margin>30</margin> **And Aristophanes the comic poet**] Some lost play of Aristophanes is being considered. Meineke explained the passage in *Critical History, p. 89 ff.* The conjectures Bothius made regarding an Aristophanic play titled *The Musicians* <note>original: Μουσικοί</note> are entirely baseless.

<margin>35</margin> ### CHAPTER XXXI.

**But that regarding the modes of training** <note>original: Ὅτι δὲ περὶ τὰς ἀγωγάς</note>] *Agoge* <note>literally "a leading" or "guidance"</note>, regarding whose rhythmic and harmonic meaning we spoke in the note to Chapter 29, in this passage means nothing other than education <note>original: παιδεία</note>. It is used thus quite often in <margin>40</margin> Plutarch, both in the book *On the Education of Children* and elsewhere, as in the following chapter. In general, Sextus Empiricus (p. 34, 4 Bekker) interprets *agoge* as: "a choice of life or of some matter involving one or many people."
**Dionysius of Thebes**] The memory of this man returns, who <margin>45</margin> taught Epaminondas the art of singing to the cithara <note>original: citharoedicam artem</note>, as found in Cornelius Nepos' *Epaminondas, ch. 2*: "for he was taught both to play the cithara and to sing to the sound of the strings by Dionysius, who was of no less glory in musical matters than Damon and Lamprus, whose names are widespread." Perhaps he is the same...

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Plutarch's treatise "On Music." The previous page discussed the comic poet Pherecrates and the perceived "corruption" of ancient music by innovators like Philoxenus. This page moves into Chapters 31 through 33, focusing on technical aspects of Greek music theory, such as musical modes (scales), composition (melopoeia), and the structure of traditional hymns known as "nomes."</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XXXI—XXXIII.

<page-num>126</page-num>

...is Dionysius, whose son Antigenidas is mentioned in the writings of Harpocration <note>Valerius Harpocration, a 2nd-century grammarian of Alexandria</note>.
<margin>5</margin> *so as to despise beautiful things* <note>original: ὡς καταφρονῆσαι τῶν καλῶν</note>] It seems we should read "having despised" <note>original: καταφρονήσαντα</note> and, in the following parts, "the works of Philoxenus and Timotheus" <note>original: τὰ Φιλοξένου τε καὶ Τιμοθέου; Philoxenus and Timotheus were famous 5th-century BCE musicians known for their complex, innovative styles</note>.

### CHAPTER XXXII.

<margin>10</margin> *let him imitate the ancient style* <note>original: τὸν ἀρχαῖον ἀπομιμείσθω τρόπον</note>] With these words, Plutarch gathers, as it were, the sum of the entire dialogue, following Aristoxenus <note>Aristoxenus of Tarentum, a pupil of Aristotle and the most important ancient Greek music theorist</note> as the author of this opinion.
*for there being three genera* <note>original: τριῶν γὰρ ὄντων γενῶν; the three genera of Greek scales were the Enharmonic, Chromatic, and Diatonic</note>] The manuscripts mistakenly read "parts" <note>original: μερῶν</note>, which can only be tolerated if we assume that more text has been lost in this passage. Burette <note>Jean-Bernard Burette (1665–1747), a French scholar who pioneered the study of ancient music</note> realized this same point.
<margin>15</margin> *both the Mantineans and the Pelleneans*] Compare the notes in chapters 9 and 27, and the passage of Polybius cited there <note>Polybius notes that the Arcadians—including those from Mantinea—maintained strict musical education to civilize their character</note>.

### CHAPTER XXXIII.

*is capable of knowing systematic mutations* <note>original: μεταβολῶν συστηματικῶν ἐστι γνωστική</note>] "In enumerating the parts of the Harmonic art, the art of melody-making <term>melopoeia|The creative process of composing a melody, as opposed to the theoretical study of scales</term> is included by some writers <margin>20</margin> and omitted by others. For neither Plutarch in *On Music* chapter 33, nor Nicomachus, nor Ptolemy, nor Porphyry—who explained Ptolemy—bring forward anything regarding this part. The cause of this seems to be that they consider Harmonics to be a theoretical science, not a practical one; this can certainly be affirmed regarding Porphyry (I, 1 ed. <margin>25</margin> Wall.). However, Aristides Quintilianus (p. 8) teaches that melopoeia belongs to the practical genus. See the *Dissertation on the Harmonic Science of the Greeks*, p. 17." So says Bojesen <note>Peder Hjort Bojesen, a 19th-century Danish scholar</note> in *On Greek Mutation and Melopoeia*, p. 40. But to me, a fault seems to lie hidden in the preceding word "systematic" <note>original: συστηματικῶν</note>, which I believe arose from a scribe's error for the phrase "and of systems" <note>original: συστημάτων καί</note> written above it. If we accept this, other words, such as "and of melopoeia" <note>original: καὶ μελοποιίας</note>, may have fallen out. For since there were four types of mutations—generic, systematic, by mode, and by character <note>original: γενική, συστηματική, κατὰ τρόπον, κατὰ ἦθος</note>—all of which were treated in harmonic science, surely no one understands why Plutarch would have mentioned only one, and a relatively minor one at that. <margin>35</margin>
*so as to speak, musically* <note>original: ὡς οἷον εἰπεῖν εὐμούσως</note>] The manuscripts show: "similar" <note>original: ὅμοιον</note> or "as if to speak among the Muses" <note>original: ὡς οἷον εἰπεῖν ἐν μούσοις</note> or "among musicians" <note>original: μουσικοῖς</note>. Burette conjectured "as if to speak among musicians" <note>original: οἷον εἰπεῖν ἐν μουσικοῖς</note>. Wyttenbach <note>Daniel Wyttenbach (1746–1820), a Swiss-Dutch classical scholar</note> suggested "and, so as to speak, musically" <note>original: καὶ οἷον εἰπεῖν εὐμούσως</note>.
*or the Hypophrygian and Phrygian modes at the middle* <note>original: ἢ τὸν Ὑποφρύγιόν τε καὶ Φρύγιον ἐπὶ τὸ μέσον</note>] These last words had to be written this way of necessity, in place of which the manuscripts have "the middle string" <note>original: <margin>40</margin> τὴν μέσην</note>. Furthermore, Boeckh <note>August Boeckh (1785–1867), a monumental figure in 19th-century German philology</note>, in his work *On Pindaric Meters*, judged this whole passage to mean that the Hypophrygian and Phrygian modes, as well as the Dorian and Mixolydian—which were separated from each other by the interval of a fourth <note>original: διὰ τεσσάρων</note>—were used by the Greeks in an equal "paraphonic" harmony, which we call harmonic concord. "Here," <margin>45</margin> he says, "if Plutarch had wanted to say whether the poet had rightly used either the Hypophrygian or the Phrygian in the middle of the song, and either the Mixolydian or the Dorian at the end, he would certainly have said, 'either the Hypo-

---

<page-num>127</page-num>

*phrygian or the Phrygian, and then either the Mixolydian or the Dorian* <note>original: φρύγιον ἢ Φρύγιον, et deinde ἢ τὸν Μιξολύδιον ἢ Δώριον</note>. But if the meaning were whether the poet, in the middle of the song, had used first the Hypophrygian, then the Phrygian after the Hypophrygian, and at the end first the Mixolydian, then the Dorian: the writer would have said, *either the Mixolydian and the Dorian* <margin>5</margin> <note>original: ἢ τὸν Μιξολύδιόν τε καὶ τὸν Δώριον</note> and *either the Hypophrygian and the Phrygian* <note>original: ἢ τὸν Ὑποφρύγιόν τε καὶ τὸν Φρύγιον</note>, because each one is distinct. Thus, where one reads *the doctor and the physical trainer* <note>original: ὁ ἰατρός τε καὶ ὁ παιδοτρίβης</note>, these are different men; but where it is *the doctor and trainer* <note>original: ὁ ἰατρός τε καὶ παιδοτρίβης</note>, he is one and the same person. Thus, therefore, *the Hypophrygian and Phrygian* <note>original: ὁ Ὑποφρύγιός τε καὶ Φρύγιος</note> and *the Mixolydian and Dorian* <margin>10</margin> seem to signify that the Hypophrygian and Phrygian, and the Mixolydian and Dorian, were sung and mixed together, being separated by the interval of a fourth." — Certainly, two substantives joined by "and" <note>original: τε καὶ</note> without a repeated article indicate a certain unity among accurate writers, a group in which Plutarch, however, is not numbered. But <margin>15</margin> Trinkler, in *Harmonics* p. 41, rightly advised that this unity should not be sought in "paraphonic" use (simultaneous harmony), but in the ethical type or character of each mode. Thus, Plutarch in chapter 16 related that tragic poets joined the Mixolydian with the Dorian, "since the one provides magnificence and dignity, while the other provides pathos; and through <margin>20</margin> these elements tragedy is mixed" <note>original: ἐπεὶ ἡ μὲν τὸ μεγαλοπρεπὲς καὶ ἀξιωματικὸν ἀποδίδωσιν, ἡ δὲ τὸ παθητικόν, μέμικται δὲ διὰ τούτων ἡ τραγῳδία</note>. So also the Phrygian and Hypophrygian agree in an excited style, like some Bacchic frenzy. See Aristotle's *Politics* VIII, 7 p. 1342, 46 and *Problems* XIX, 30.
*the harmonic study* <note>original: ἡ ἁρμονικὴ πραγματεία</note>] Thus Wyttenbach, following Burette, corrected the common reading "in the harmonic study" <note>original: τῇ ἁρμονικῇ πραγματείᾳ</note>. So at the end of the <margin>25</margin> chapter: "and there is a question about those Paeonic rhythm-compositions, whether the rhythmic study is capable of distinguishing them, as some say, or whether it does not extend that far."
*for the so-called Harmony — the character of the trial differs greatly* <note>original: ἡ γὰρ καλουμένη ἁρμονία — πολὺ διέστηκε τὸ ἦθος τῆς ἀναπείρας</note>] The individual parts of which the Nome of Olympus <note>Olympus was a legendary Phrygian flutist</note> in honor of Minerva <note>Athena</note> <margin>30</margin> consisted are enumerated: *beginning, trial, harmony* <note>original: ἀρχή, ἀνάπειρα, ἁρμονία</note>. This is illustrated by a passage from Strabo concerning the Pythian Nome cited in the notes to chapter 21, the parts of which were named *prelude, trial* <note>original: ἄγκρουσις, ἄμπειρα</note>, etc. If the name "Harmony" is correct here, this part of the nome was so called because it uniquely used the enharmonic genus. Burette says: <margin>35</margin> "I am persuaded that the harmony must be taken here for the body of the piece or the song, thus named either from the enharmonic genus in which it was composed, or from the assembly of all its parts, which made only one whole, 'well-fitted' <note>original: εὐάρμοστον</note> (properly compacted and joined together)."
<margin>40</margin> *the understanding of the paeon* <note>original: τὴν τοῦ παίωνος ξύνεσιν; the paeon is a rhythmic foot</note>] Thus the Paris manuscripts read, with the approval of Wyttenbach in his *Plutarchean Lexicon*. The reading "composition" <note>original: ξύνθεσιν</note> was approved by the same author in his notes and adopted by Dübner, having arisen from Xylander's version <note>Wilhelm Xylander (1532–1576) was the first to translate Plutarch's complete works into Latin</note>. The rest read "benefit" <note>original: ξύνοισιν</note> without sense. "Understanding" <note>original: ξύνεσις</note> of a thing looks only at "the what" <note>original: τὸ τί</note>, not at "the relation" <note>original: τὸ πρὸς τί</note>. Thus below in chapter 36: "I speak of that <margin>45</margin> which contributes to the understanding of the harmonious, and further that of rhythm."

<summary>This page provides a scholarly analysis of Chapters 31-33 of Plutarch's work, focusing on the technical definitions of musical modes and the structure of ancient hymns (nomes). It debates whether certain modes were played simultaneously or sequentially based on Greek grammar and the emotional "character" (ethos) of the music.</summary>
