<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," specifically examining Chapters 34 through 37. The previous page discussed the ancient musical "nomes" and the opinions of the theorist Aristoxenus. This section focuses on the criteria for judging music—the balance between reason and perception—and the historical reputation of the Argives as master musicians.</meta>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XXXIV—XXXV.

### CHAPTER XXXIV.

<margin>5</margin> *Since there are three genera* <note>original: τριῶν δ' ὄντων γενῶν</note>] The words that follow here at the beginning of the chapter strangely disturb the order of the explanation. Therefore Burette <note>Jean-Bernard Burette, an 18th-century French scholar</note>, with Wyttenbach’s <note>Daniel Wyttenbach, a Swiss-German philologist</note> approval, judged that they should be moved lower down into Chapter 37, between the words "having attempted" and "Pythagoras." However, it is better to place them at the start of Chapter 38, which I certainly would have done, had these words not seemed to have originated from some half-learned interpolator who plundered the works of Aristoxenus. For the entire sentiment belongs to Aristoxenus (see his *Harmonics* I, 2; II, 35). Therefore, I have <margin>10</margin> enclosed these in brackets.

*To speak generally, then, they must run together* <note>original: καθόλου μὲν οὖν εἰπεῖν, ὁμοδρομεῖν δεῖ</note>] These words of Plutarch agree with those which Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century polymath</note> explained more accurately in the first chapter of the first book of his *Harmonics*, concerning the criteria in harmony. This, moreover, was the point upon which the entire <margin>15</margin> dispute between the most excellent schools of ancient musicians turned, as if on a hinge. Compare Ptolemy’s *Harmonics* I, 2, page 8: "Regarding this proposition (the goal of using both criteria for harmony), some seem not to have cared at all, applying themselves only to manual practice and the bare and irrational habit of perception; others <margin>20</margin> approached the goal more theoretically. These would be mostly the Pythagoreans and the Aristoxenians. Both would be in error. For the Pythagoreans, not even following the strike of the ear in things where it was necessary for everyone to do so, fitted inappropriate ratios to the differences in sounds, which were in many ways at odds with the phenomena. Thus, they even created a discredit for such a <margin>25</margin> criterion among those of different opinions. But the Aristoxenians, giving the greatest weight to things grasped through perception, used reason as a mere byproduct of the journey, as it were, contrary both to reason itself and to the phenomena. Contrary to reason, because they do not fit numbers—that is, the images of ratios—to the differences in sounds, but <margin>30</margin> rather to their intervals. Contrary to the phenomena, because they compare these intervals to divisions that are inappropriate to sensory perceptions."

### CHAPTER XXXV.

<margin>35</margin> *For it is always necessary* <note>original: Ἀεὶ γὰρ ἀναγκαῖον</note>] Ulrici <note>Hermann Ulrici, 19th-century German philosopher and historian</note> explained in his *History of Lyric Poetry* (p. 31) how much value these words have for understanding the true and proper nature of Greek music.

*The so-called* <note>original: τὸ λεγόμενον</note>] Amyot <note>Jacques Amyot, 16th-century French translator of Plutarch</note> translates this as: "that which is called manners [ethos]." Thus, he read "the so-called character" <note>original: τὸ λεγόμενον ἦθος</note>. Incorrectly.
<margin>40</margin> *And to see that which is missing* <note>original: καὶ συνορᾶν τὸ δ' ἁμαρτανόμενον</note>] Here we must refer to that wonderful saying of Plutarch in *On the Right Way to Hear* (ch. 7): "For just as many errors of those singing to the flute escape the listeners, so a flowery and grand style shines against the listener, hiding the meaning." He is speaking about unskilled listeners, not about those who <margin>45</margin> have musical training or are professional musicians themselves. Choeroboscus <note>a Byzantine grammarian</note> speaks slightly differently in Bekker’s *Anecdota* (p. 1186), where he brings forward the opinion of musicians concerning the "unpronounced iota": "that although it is pro-

<column-break/>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XXXV—XXXVII.</header>

nounced, it is not heard clearly because of the size of the long vowels—just as when a flute and an organ <note>likely referring to a water organ or a large stringed instrument</note> are sounding, the flute is not heard clearly because the organ has a louder sound and covers the sound of the flute, which is weaker."

<margin>5</margin> *According to the use of* <note>original: κατὰ τὴν χρῆσιν τῶν</note>] The common word order, "of the uncomposed parts according to use," cannot be sustained. For uncomposed parts exist "by nature" <note>original: φύσει</note> and are thus treated in the discipline of harmonics, but "by use" <note>original: χρήσει</note> they are always joined together.

<margin>10</margin> ### CHAPTER XXXVI.

*For it is not possible to become a complete musician* <note>original: οὐ γὰρ οἷόν τε τέλεον γενέσθαι μουσικόν</note>] Wyttenbach has given examples in his *Lexicon* of this form, which is properly Ionic but also used by Attic writers, taken from Plutarch’s other writings. However, the other form, *teleios* <note>meaning "complete" or "perfect"</note>, is more frequent in his work.

<margin>15</margin> *One must try to understand* <note>original: πειρατέον καταμαθεῖν</note>] Plutarch’s argument in this place is somewhat obscure, about which Burette already warned (Vol. XXVI, p. 59): *"The main difficulty consists in knowing why the musician's inability to judge sovereignly in matters of music comes, in* <margin>20</margin> *the first place, from the fact that the things submitted to his judgment are some perfect (téleia), others imperfect (atelē)."*

*Or the interpretation of each of them* <note>original: ἢ ἡ ἑκάστου αὐτῶν ἑρμηνεία</note>] I have written *autōn* <note>them</note> instead of *autou* <note>it</note>. What is meant by "interpretation" <note>term: hermeneia — here referring to the technical performance or delivery of music</note> has been stated above.

*Secondly — and itself* <note>original: δεύτερον — καὶ αὐτή</note>] Burette thinks these words should be moved from here to lower down <margin>25</margin> (at the end of the chapter) between the words "poems" and "since therefore." I do not see to what good. *Wyttenbach.* In the following parts, however, I have written *aulōdou* <note>a singer to the flute</note> instead of *aulētou* <note>a flute player</note> and "of the aulodic interpretation" instead of "of the auletic interpretation," because it is clearer than light that the discussion here is about flute music accompanying a song, not about solo flute playing. I believe this was also the <margin>30</margin> reason why Burette thought about transposing the words in this passage.

### CHAPTER XXXVII.

<margin>35</margin> *For they say the Argives —* <note>original: Ἀργείους μὲν γὰρ — φασί</note>] The musical praise of the Argives <note>citizens of Argos</note> was greatest both among the Dorians and among the rest of the Greeks. According to Herodotus (III, 131), in the time of Polycrates (around 520 BCE), the Argives were considered the first among the Greeks in music. We have already spoken of Sacadas and Hierax in Chapter 9. Lasus was also an Argive, as was the poetess Tele- <margin>40</margin> silla. In a more recent age, the glory of the Argive flute players endured (see Schneidewin in *Fragments of Simonides*, p. 198). Aristo, an Argive flute player, is commemorated in an epigram of Simonides or Bacchylides in Gaisford’s *Minor Greek Poets* (Vol. I, p. 383; see Nevius, *Bacchylides* fr. 61). Furthermore, a certain specific type of flute <margin>45</margin> was called "Argive" (Pausanias IV, 27, 4). See also Mueller’s *Dorians* (Vol. II, p. 332).

*Having attempted* <note>original: ἐπιχειρήσαντα</note>] This word seems to have been repeated twice by a scribe's error, so I have enclosed it in brackets in the first instance. Then, I wrote...

<sig>9</sig>

<vocab>Plutarch, Aristoxenus, Ptolemy, Burette, Wyttenbach, Harmonics, Greek music, Argives, flute players, interpretation, complete, Ionic, Attic.</vocab>

<summary>This page critiques the text of Plutarch's "On Music," discussing the philosophical divide between mathematical and sensory approaches to harmony and highlighting the historical musical excellence of the city of Argos.</summary>
<keywords>Aristoxenus, Ptolemy, music theory, aesthetics, Argive music, flute performance, philology, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Plutarch’s "On Music," focusing on the technical transition between musical modes and the historical development of musical scales. The previous page discussed the criteria for harmony and the relationship between reason and sensory perception in music theory.</meta>

...was written as *to the many* <note>original: ταῖς πλείουσι instead of τοῖς πλείοσι</note>. Moreover, I interpret the word *to play in a manner near the Mixolydian* <note>original: παραμιξολυδιάζειν</note> alongside Burette <note>Jean-Bernard Burette, an 18th-century French scholar</note>: to transition from the Mixolydian mode into another neighboring and related one, which could be none other than the Hyperionian mode.

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

*nor the chance perception — to be present in the many* <note>original: μηδὲ τὴν τυχοῦσαν ἀντίληψιν — τοῖς πολλοῖς ὑπάρχειν</note>] The words read above in Chapter 11—*it is easy to see, if one hears someone playing the flute in the ancient style, etc.*—refer to the age of Aristoxenus <note>a Greek philosopher and music theorist of the 4th century BCE</note>, not to that of Plutarch. But Plutarch, deceived by a certain prejudiced opinion, concludes that the ancient musical genres fell into disuse solely due to ignorance. In fact, the cause of this matter was that ears accustomed to softer sounds now loathed the intervals characteristic of the chromatic and enharmonic genres <note>The "enharmonic" genus used microtones or quarter-tones, which were difficult to sing and hear</note>, and instead favored the diatonic genus as if it were more natural. Ptolemy discussed this matter with much greater judgment than Plutarch in his *Harmonics* (Book 1, Chapter 16). On the whole, Plutarch seems to have delivered a somewhat unfair judgment regarding the musicians of his own age. Indeed, the things he reported about them in the following chapter are utterly incredible.

*the enharmonic diesis* <note>original: τὴν ἐναρμόνιον δίεσιν</note>] A *diesis* <term>diesis: a small musical interval, often a quarter-tone in ancient Greek theory</term> is generally an aliquot part of a tone—a fourth, a third, or a half, depending on the genre. Compare Boeckh's *Philolaos*, page 74. Usually, however, as in Aristoxenus, it signifies a fourth part of a tone, which those who wish to speak more accurately call the "enharmonic diesis." See Euclid's *Harmonics*, page 10. Proclus <note>a Neoplatonist philosopher</note> says in his commentary on Plato’s *Timaeus*: *the diesis is like a fourth of a tone, though not truly a fourth, just as the 'leimma' is not exactly a semitone—and some of the ancients called the semitone a diesis.* The Pythagoreans did this, according to Theo the Mathematician.

*of the intense diatonic* <note>original: τοῦ συντόνου διατόνου</note>] I have removed the word *and* <note>original: καὶ</note> which follows *intense* in the editions, following Burette. The "intense diatonic" is nothing other than the diatonic genus itself; see the notes on Chapter 11. Therefore, Burette rightly omitted the words *intense and* when translating, for which Wyttenbach <note>Daniel Wyttenbach, a scholar of Plutarch</note> should not have criticized him. I would judge that another *and* is redundant in Dio Chrysostom (Orations 2, 35): *for this world is altogether better than temples, or emeralds and sardius and onyx.* Read *sardius-onyx*, that is, *sardonyx*.

### CHAPTER XXXIX.

*the forefinger strings and the next-to-the-lowest strings* <note>original: τὰς τε λιχανοὺς καὶ τὰς παρανήτας</note>] Should Plutarch not have said *and the strings beside the lowest* <note>original: καὶ τὰς παρυπάτας</note>?

### CHAPTER XL.

*learn, says Homer* <note>original: μάθε, φησὶν Ὅμηρος</note>] These are Homer’s words from the *Iliad* (I, 186–189), where I wrote *upon* <note>original: ἐπὶ</note> in the second verse instead of *about* <note>original: περί</note>, which cannot be tolerated.

---

<page-num>131</page-num>

# COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS XL—XLI.

Otherwise, Plutarch’s interpretation of this passage generally agrees with that which Porphyry’s scholia <note>ancient marginal commentaries</note> provide on the same text, as well as Sextus Empiricus in *Against the Musicians*. Athenaeus (Book 14) recorded similar details about certain other Homeric verses, a part of which we read flowed from Aristoxenus. One may also compare Plutarch’s *On Hearing the Poets*, where the verse from the *Odyssey*—*But come now, change the theme, and sing of the construction of the horse*—is ingeniously illustrated. Furthermore, the words of Alexander in Dio Chrysostom and the stories told about the same Alexander in Plutarch’s *On the Fortune of Alexander*, his *Life of Alexander*, Aelian’s *Various History*, and Stobaeus are relevant here. Compare Geier’s *Alexander and Aristotle*, page 120.

*found an exercise for one who is idle* <note>original: ἀργοῦντι γυμνάσιοv ἐξεῦρεν</note>] Aristotle expresses the same sentiment in his *Politics* (Book 8). Among the ancients, the power of music in training the minds of children was especially highly valued for this reason: human nature is so constituted that it requires not only "right business" but also "beautiful leisure." *For now, most people participate in music for the sake of pleasure, but those from the beginning placed it in education because nature itself seeks—as has often been said—not only to be busy correctly but also to be able to use leisure well.*

*Chiron — teacher of music* <note>original: Χείρων — μουσικῆς διδάσκαλος</note>] Chiron <note>the centaur known as a wise tutor</note> is recognized as a musician by the commentators on the *Iliad*, Aratus, and Germanicus, as well as by Dio Chrysostom. Linus taught music to Hercules, about whom I spoke in the notes on Chapter 3. The fable was clumsily transferred to Alexander the son of Philip in Aelian’s *Various History*. Compare Geier in the work cited above. The word *educator* <note>original: παιδευτής</note> is frequent in Plutarch; see the Plutarch Lexicon.

### CHAPTER XLI.

*and through music* <note>original: διὰ μουσικῆς δὲ</note>] The books read *te* <note>a different Greek conjunction</note>.

*to no one in either deed or word* <note>original: μηθενὶ μήτ᾿ ἔργῳ μήτε λόγῳ</note>] Porson <note>Richard Porson, a famous English classical scholar</note> stated that the forms *outheis* and *metheis* <note>both meaning "no one"</note> belonged to later Attic Greek, but Lobeck has shown that they are found more frequently among later writers from the age of Aristotle onward. In using these forms, the scribes fluctuate, and the authors themselves fluctuate in a marvelous way. A clear example of the former is found in the excellent Gisianus manuscript of Dionysius of Halicarnassus, in which these forms are almost always written with a "d" <note>the letter delta</note>, while they are produced with a "th" <note>the letter theta</note> in other books. I prove the latter from Dio Chrysostom and Julian. From Dio, although I have recorded *outhen* 48 times in my notes, 21 examples are taken from the thirty-first oration alone. In Plutarch, they are not very frequent in number, though more frequent in this very dialogue (Chapter 5 and Chapter 33). To the instances collected by Wyttenbach in his Lexicon, add *methen* in *On the Procreation of the Soul* and *That a Pleasant Life is Not Possible*. In some places, Wyttenbach neglected the authority of the manuscripts by following the common spelling.

9* <note>This "9*" likely refers to a printer's signature or a specific volume section.</note>

The author critiques previous scholars' editions and explores the evolution of specific Greek grammatical forms found in the text.</summary>

->COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS 42–43.<-

### CHAPTER XLII.

**One might take Terpander as an example** <note>original: Τέρπανδρον δ' ἄν τις παραλάβοι</note>] On Terpander <note>a 7th-century BCE poet and musician credited with founding Spartan music</note>, see the notes in Chapter 3. "Because of the secret and miracle-like power of his songs, by which he calmed the minds of the Spartans, Christodorus attributes a *mystic song* <note>original: μύστιδα μολπὴν</note> to Terpander in his *Ecphrasis* <note>a poetic description of statues</note>, verse 111." These are the words of Lobeck in *Aglaophamus*, page 306, note *n*. In the same work, on page 314, I believe by a typesetter's error—who omitted several lines, though Lobeck himself noted nothing about this in his corrections—it happened that what was narrated about Thales was transferred to Abaris. I mention this for the following reason: so that no one might misuse the words of this great man without examining the evidence.

**Since you have already... set forth...** <note>original: ἐπεὶ φθάσας — προαπέρφηνας</note>] Soterichus <note>a speaker in Plutarch's dialogue</note> is referring to a passage in the second chapter. Note the active form of the verb *to show further* <note>original: προσαποφαίνω</note>, as only the middle forms were known to the lexicographers.

**the purification and tunefulness of the soul** <note>original: τὸ τῆς ψυχῆς καθάρσιον καὶ ἐμμελές</note>] Dio Chrysostom <note>a Greek orator of the 1st century CE</note> put forward views very similar to Plutarch’s, though more elegant, in Oration 32, 57: "Music seems to have been discovered for the sake of healing the passions of man, and especially to turn back souls that are in a harsh and wild state. Because of this, some of the philosophers tuned themselves to the lyre in the morning to stop the agitation caused by dreams. And we sacrifice to the gods with song, so that we may be orderly and composed. There is also another way of using the flute and song in mournings, which I think heals the hardness and unyielding nature of grief, making the sorrow more feminine through a song that slips in with lamentations—just as doctors, by bathing and softening inflamed wounds, make them painless." To this context belongs, first, the story of the youth who was cured of insanity by a spondaic melody <note>a slow, solemn rhythm</note>, which Sextus Empiricus attributed to Pythagoras and Iamblichus also mentions. Next, there is the story of the Pythagorean Clinias in Athenaeus and Aelian; the story of Achilles restraining his anger; and finally, those things which Josephus, following the Holy Scriptures, recorded about David calming Saul by striking the *cinyra* <note>an ancient stringed instrument, often translated as a harp or lyre</note>. Regarding the medical use of music, one may look to what Athenaeus cited from Theophrastus, and what Boethius told of Ismenias the flute-player, who supposedly cured sciatica with the sound of the flute.

**general** <note>original: ἐγκυκλίους</note>] I have written this based on Wyttenbach’s conjecture instead of the common reading *encircling* <note>original: ἐπικυκλίους or ἐπικυκλικείους</note>, which Dübner received into the text following Salmasius and Hemsterhuis. *Encyclical education* <note>the "liberal arts" or "well-rounded education"</note> is praised in Soterichus in Chapter 13.

### CHAPTER XLIII.

**kept his own** <note>original: τὴν αὑτοῦ ἐφύλαξεν</note>] Commonly but incorrectly written as *he himself kept his own* <note>original: τὴν αὐτὸς αὑτοῦ ἐφύλαξεν</note>.

**during a drinking party, as the noble Homer says** <note>original: παρὰ πότον, ὡς ὁ καλὸς Ὅμηρος</note>] Thus Wyttenbach and Dübner, following the correction of Bryan and others, whom I follow. The manuscripts read *the noble Homer having been stretched out* <note>original: παρατετονὼς ὁ καλὸς Ὅμηρος</note>.

<page-num>133</page-num>
->COMMENTARY ON CHAPTERS 43–44.<-

**at the dinners and gatherings of the ancients** <note>original: εἰς τὰ δεῖπνα καὶ τὰς συνουσίας τῶν ἀρχαίων</note>] From the speech of Dio mentioned above, Chapter 58 is particularly relevant here: "The power of music seemed no less appropriate for social gatherings, introducing a kind of spontaneous harmony and order to the souls, and consoling the instability of pleasure found in wine with a kindred power; so that, being mixed with the wine itself, the pleasure becomes tuneful and moderate." Both authors perhaps had the same passage of Aristoxenus in mind.

**by order and symmetry** <note>original: τάξει τε καὶ συμμετρίᾳ</note>] One may compare a passage from Plutarch’s *On Superstition*, Chapter 5: "Plato says that music, the creator of tunefulness and rhythm, was given to humans by the gods not for the sake of luxury or the tickling of the ears, but because the turbulent and wandering parts of the soul’s revolutions and harmonies in the body—which often break out into excess and error through a lack of the Muses and Graces—are brought back into order and guided appropriately by music." Wyttenbach says these ideas were not taken from a single passage of Plato, but were collected from several books where they are scattered. *Revolutions of the soul* comes from the *Timaeus* (p. 352). *Unrollings of the universe* from p. 175 and p. 182. Many things, however, come from the same *Timaeus* (p. 533), which Philo also used. Cf. Theo the Mathematician on Plato, p. 12.

### CHAPTER XLIV.

**Those around Pythagoras** <note>original: Οἱ περὶ Πυθαγόραν</note>] Regarding the musical harmony of the spheres according to the Pythagoreans, the matter is well known. A notable passage on this subject exists in Lydus, *On the Months*. Moreover, Pythagoras borrowed this doctrine from the Egyptians, if any faith is to be had in what Seyffarth relates. Plutarch himself explained Plato’s views in his book *On the Procreation of the Soul according to the Timaeus*. Ptolemy in his *Harmonics* (I, 13) teaches that Archytas of Tarentum was particularly skilled in music: "Archytas of Tarentum, who of all the Pythagoreans most diligently studied music"; in that chapter, he reviews Archytas’s division of genres and tetrachords. Cf. Boethius, *On Music*. Another opinion of Archytas on musical matters was preserved by Theo the Musician. Athenaeus is the authority that Archytas wrote a book *On Flutes*. But he seems to have confused Archytas of Tarentum and Archytas of Mitylene (a musician mentioned by Diogenes Laertius), whom Menage judges Athenaeus himself called *the Harmonic*. Without doubt, Nicomachus cited the *Harmonic Book* of this same Archytas. For Diogenes records no writings of Archytas of Tarentum. Cf. Themistius: "I leave aside Pittacus and Bias and Cleobulus, and I leave aside Archytas of Tarentum, who engaged in more deeds than writings."

### CHAPTER XLII.

**One might take Terpander as an example** <note>original: Τέρπανδρον δ' ἄν τις παραλάβοι</note>] On Terpander <note>a 7th-century BCE poet and musician credited with founding the first musical school in Sparta</note>, see the notes in Chapter 3. "Because of the secret and miracle-like power of his songs, by which he calmed the minds of the Spartans, Christodorus <note>a Greek poet of the 5th century CE</note> attributes a *mystic song* <note>original: μύστιδα μολπὴν</note> to Terpander in his *Ecphrasis* <note>a poetic description of the statues in the gymnasium of Zeuxippus</note>, verse 111." These are the words of Christian August Lobeck <note>a prominent 19th-century German classical scholar</note> in *Aglaophamus*, page 306, note *n*. In the same work, on page 314, I believe by a typesetter's error—who omitted several lines, though Lobeck himself noted nothing about this in his corrections—it happened that what was narrated about Thales <note>the philosopher Thales of Miletus, often credited with healing through music</note> was transferred to Abaris <note>a legendary Scythian priest of Apollo</note>. I mention this for the following reason: so that no one might misuse the words of this great man without examining the evidence.

**Since you have already... set forth...** <note>original: ἐπεὶ φθάσας — προαπέρφηνας</note>] Soterichus <note>the character in Plutarch's dialogue representing the Platonic and Pythagorean view of music</note> is referring to a passage in the second chapter. Note the active form of the verb *to show further* <note>original: προσαποφαίνω</note>, as only the middle forms were known to the lexicographers.

**the purification and tunefulness of the soul** <note>original: τὸ τῆς ψυχῆς καθάρσιον καὶ ἐμμελές</note>] Dio Chrysostom <note>a famous Greek orator and philosopher of the 1st century CE</note> put forward views very similar to Plutarch’s, though more elegant, in Oration 32, 57: "Music seems to have been disco... <note>The text likely intended to quote Dio's view that music was "discovered by the gods for the delight and soothing of men."</note>

<summary>This page provides a scholarly commentary on the historical and mystical reputation of Terpander of Sparta, including a correction of a bibliographical error regarding Thales and Abaris. It transitions into a discussion on the soul-purifying properties of music as described by Soterichus and Dio Chrysostom.</summary>

### §. 1.
#### *On the classification of instruments.*

Among the ancients, just as among the moderns, there were three types of musical organs or instruments:
<margin>5</margin> the percussion class <note>original: "genus percussionale"; German: "schlaginstrumente"</note>, the tensile class <note>original: "genus tensibile"; German: "saiteninstrumente"</note>, and the inflatable class <note>original: "genus inflatile"; German: "blasinstrumente"</note>. Thus says Cassiodorus <note>a Roman statesman and writer of the 6th century CE</note> in *On Music*, page 573. Athenaeus <note>a Greek rhetorician and grammarian, c. 200 CE</note> does not state it differently in Book XIV, page 636 E, except that he divides the instruments in a different order into those "blown into" <note>original: ἐμφυσώμενα</note>, those "divided by strings" <note>original: χορδαῖς διειλημμένα</note>, and those "producing only noise"
<margin>10</margin> <note>original: ψόφον μόνον παρασκευαστικά</note>, or with changed names, into "wind" <note>original: ἐμπνευστά</note>, "stringed" <note>original: ἔγχορδα</note> or "tensile" <note>original: ἐντατά</note>, and "resonant" <note>original: ἔνηχα</note>. In addition to the wind and tensile types, the anonymous author edited by Bellermann <note>refers to Friedrich Bellermann’s 1841 edition of anonymous Greek musical treatises</note> on page 27 mentions "bare" <note>original: ψιλά</note> instruments, by which he also includes the human voice. Furthermore, the percussion class—from which we must take our
<margin>15</margin> beginning because it is the most imperfect—includes Rattles <note>Crepitacula</note>, Cymbals <note>Cymbala</note>, Sistra, and Drums <note>Tympana</note>. To the wind class belong Pipes <note>Fistulae</note>, Flutes <note>Tibiae; specifically the aulos</note>, Trumpets <note>Tubae</note>, and Horns <note>Cornua</note>, as well as the Water Organ <note>Hydraulica</note>, although in the age of Athenaeus there was doubt whether these belonged to the tensile or wind instruments. Finally, to the tensile class belong, above all others, Lyres and Citharas
<margin>20</margin> and various types of Psalteries.

### §. 2.
#### *On Rattles, Sistra, and Vinegar-cups.*

Among the rattles, the *Krembala* <note>original: Κρέμβαλα</note> corresponded quite closely to those which we call castanets <note>German: "castagnetten"</note>.
<margin>25</margin> They were made of bronze plated with gold and were struck together with the fingers; see Athenaeus, Book XIV, page 636 C. From this are derived the words "to play the castanets" <note>original: κρεμβαλίζειν, κρεμβαλιάζειν</note> and "castanet-playing" <note>original: κρεμβαλιαστός</note>, which were already known to the author of the *Homeric Hymn to Apollo*, verse 162. Very closely related was the *Platage* <note>original: Πλαταγή</note> or Rattle <note>Crotalum</note>. This invention of Vulcan
<margin>30</margin> <note>Hephaestus</note> had been received by Hercules from Minerva <note>Athena</note> to drive away the Stymphalian birds <note>man-eating birds of Greek myth</note>, according to the Scholiast on Apollonius Rhodius, page 441, 14. Later, the *platage* was a toy for infants; Aristotle in *Politics* VIII, 6, 1, names Archytas as its inventor—certainly not the philosopher, as Suidas <note>the 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> wrongly asserts, nor the musician from Mitylene who was distinct from the philosopher, but rather a different Archytas...

<summary>This page outlines the tripartite classification of ancient musical instruments—percussion, stringed, and wind—citing authorities like Cassiodorus and Athenaeus. It begins a detailed survey of percussion instruments, starting with castanets (krembala) and rattles (platage), including their mythological origins.</summary>

<keywords>organology, percussion instruments, stringed instruments, wind instruments, Cassiodorus, Athenaeus, castanets, Archytas, mythology, musical classification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of ancient percussion instruments, moving from the general classification on the previous page to specific descriptions of rattles, clappers, and rhythmic devices used in both domestic life and religious rituals.</meta>

# ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS

...just as the architect whom we know from Vitruvius <note>a famous Roman architect and engineer of the 1st century BCE</note>. See Diogenes Laertius II, 98 and Michael Apostolius XIV, 37: "For Archytas, being a carpenter, invented the rattle <note>original: πλαταγή / platage</note>, which they gave to children so that they would not shake <note>and break</note> any of the household vessels." Compare the discussions of the rattle in Lobeck, *Aglaophamus* page 701,
<margin>5</margin> and Lampius, *On the Cymbals of the Ancients* I, 9.

Strictly speaking, however, the *Crotalum* <note>a clapper or castanet</note> was a piece of wood or a reed split at
the end. A Scholion <note>ancient commentary</note> on Aristophanes' *Clouds* line 260 says: "The *crotalum* is specifically a split reed prepared for this purpose, so that it makes a sound if someone shakes it with their hands, just as if making a clapping noise." See Scaliger
<margin>10</margin> on Virgil’s *Copa* page 254, and on Eusebius page 156, whom Hildebrand praises in the *Excursus on Apuleius' Metamorphoses* page 721. The sound of the *crotalum*
greatly resembled the cry of the stork, which is why some people
called that bird the "rattle-player" <note>original: *crotalistria*</note>, as noted by Laberius in Petronius, chapter
55. A rattle-player is present at Phidis's banquet in Propertius V, 8, 39.
<margin>15</margin> An image of the instrument is shown by Pignorius in *On Slaves* page 87. Nor
did the *Psithyra* or the *Askaros* differ much from it; Pollux says this
of them in IV, 60: "The *psithyra* was a Libyan invention, used mostly by the
Troglodytes <note>an ancient term for 'cave-dwellers' in North Africa</note>, and its shape was square. Some think the *psithyra* is the same
as the so-called *askarfos*. It was a small frame about a cubit long <note>roughly 18 inches</note>,
<margin>20</margin> having drawn-through bobbins which, when turned, made a sound
similar to a rattle." To this same category belongs the *Rhombus* <note>a bullroarer or spinning toy</note>, which Apollonius of Rhodes (I, 1139) says the Phrygians used with the drum in the rites of Rhea <note>the mother-goddess of the gods</note>,
where the Scholia on page 372 state: "The *rhombus* is a small wheel which they spin by beating it with straps, and in this way they produce a noise. Some call it a *rhymbus*,"
<margin>25</margin> as do Eupolis in the *Baptists* and Didymus.
Without a doubt, the *rhombus* corresponds to that hollow boxwood toy filled with pitch which boys today spin in the streets with whips <note>German: *ein brummkreisel* (a humming top) or *eine nonne*</note>.

The *Sistrum*, derived from the verb *seio* <note>meaning "to shake"</note>, was a rattle
<margin>30</margin> originating from Egypt. For this reason, Virgil says of Cleopatra in *Aeneid* VIII, 696: "she calls her troops," he says, "with her native *sistrum*." See Plutarch’s *On Isis and Osiris* chapter 63, Apuleius' *Metamorphoses* XI page 292, and Hildebrand in the previously cited work page 644.
It was brought to Rome along with the rites of Isis. Images of the vessels of the *sistrum*
have been preserved, which generally agree in that they
<margin>35</margin> show a bronze oval-shaped ring equipped with a handle, through
the circle of which several movable shaking-rods were pierced; when
shaken by hand, these emitted a sound that was perhaps harmonic.

The *Acetabula* <note>literally 'vinegar cups'</note>, called *oxybapha* or *oxybaphoi* in Greek (as well as *opsobapha* in the form Suidas provides), were clay or bronze vessels,
<margin>40</margin> or even silver according to Cassiodorus; according to Suidas, they were

<column-break/>

# APPENDIX ON THE MUSIC OF THE ANCIENTS. §. 2—3.

intentionally made from various materials which, when struck with a small stick,
produced a harmonic sound similar to the instrument known in German as
a *glockenspiel* <note>a percussion instrument with tuned metal plates or bells</note>. Suidas, under the entry "Diocles," names Diocles of Athens, a poet of Old Comedy, as their inventor,
<margin>5</margin> though perhaps he is confused with Diocles of Elea, who, according to the entry "Alcidamas," wrote on musical matters (see Meineke, *History of Comedy* page 253). For more on this instrument, consult Bellermann in *Anonymous on Music* page 28. Lasus <note>a Greek lyric poet</note> and Hippasus
of Metapontum <note>a Pythagorean philosopher</note> seem to have used this or a similar instrument (along with a third man, Eubulides, mentioned in Boethius’s *On Music* II, 18)
<margin>10</margin> in their acoustic studies, which Theo reported in *On Music* chapter 12, and Zenobius more accurately in II, 91.

### §. 3.
### On Cymbals, Drums, and the Symphonia.

<margin>15</margin> Cymbals and Drums gained far greater fame and use than all
the instruments we have discussed so far. Seneca says of them in *Natural Questions* II, 29: "likewise," he says, "drums and cymbals sound because the former strike the air which resists them
<margin>20</margin> from the inner part, while the latter only ring when the bronze itself is hollow." These instruments were sacred to Cybele <note>the Phrygian Earth Mother</note>; see the *Homeric Hymn* XVII, 3:

> She who delights in the sound of rattles and drums, and the booming of flutes.

<margin>25</margin> These were unknown to Homer himself; see Millin, *Unpublished Monuments* Vol. I, 164.
Cymbals had the shape of the leaves of a cotyledon <note>a succulent plant with cup-shaped leaves</note>, for
which reason they appeal to Dioscorides and Scribonius Largus (*Compositions* 55).
They were very similar to our cymbals <note>German: *becken*</note>, but more hollowed out,
so much so that according to Clement of Alexandria (*Protrepticus* page 11, 3) one could
<margin>30</margin> drink from them. They were made of bronze ("cymbals were broad and large bronze objects," says Josephus in *Antiquities* VII, 12, 3) and therefore are often called "the bronzes," undoubtedly copper at first, and later gilded. They were struck together with both hands, and the sound thus produced was called *psophos* by the Greeks and *tinnitus* <note>ringing</note> by the Latins; see Catullus 65, 261; Virgil, *Georgics* IV, 64; Ovid, *Fasti* III,
<margin>35</margin> 342; and Arnobius III, 32. They were invented and first
used in the rites of Rhea by the Phrygians; Himerius says in *Eclogues* 13, 3: "For the Phrygians were the first to be credited with breathing into the flute and mixing cymbals into their ceremonies." From Phrygia they were brought to the island of Crete. Nor were they unknown to the Egyptians; see Himerius, *Oration* XIV, 9. Their use
<margin>40</margin> in the rites of Liber <note>Bacchus/Dionysus</note> was noted by John Lydus in *On the Months* IV, 38: in pantomimes...

<summary>This page catalogs various ancient percussion instruments including the crotalum (clapper), sistrum (Egyptian rattle), and acetabula (tuned jars). It details their construction, mythological origins, and their roles in domestic life and religious ceremonies like those of Cybele and Isis.</summary>

<page-num>140</page-num>

...threats in Lucian's *On the Dance*, chapter 68. The Muse of Cymbals was Erato, according to Arsenius <note>a 15th-century Greek scholar and bishop</note> XXXII, 83, in which passage for the name "Hermes" <term>Hermes</term> perhaps one should read "Eratosthenes to Hermes" <term>Eratosthenes to Hermes</term>. You may consult, in addition to Lampius, Hildebrand, Book I, page 652 and Hoeck’s *Creta*, Volume I, page 220, regarding cymbals.

<margin>5</margin> The cymbal in the sacred rites of Rhea <note>the mother of the gods in Greek mythology</note> was always accompanied by the *Tympanum* or *Typanum*, our tambourine or hand-drum—a wooden or bronze circle surrounding a stretched skin, which was moved by one hand and struck by the other, unless one preferred to strike it with a quill or a plectrum <note>a small pick used to pluck or strike instruments</note>. See Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century encyclopedist</note>, *Etymologies* II, 21, and Perizonius on Aelian's *Various History* <margin>10</margin> IX, 3, page 538. Alexander Aetolus <note>a Greek poet of the 3rd century BCE</note> used the phrase "to strike the drums" <term>to strike the drums</term> in an epigram written on Alcman, and Statius in *Thebaid* X, 644, writes of "bursting the drums with the right hand." This instrument was also of Asiatic origin and even now is very common in the East, from where it reached Europeans through the Gypsies. Nor <margin>15</margin> is the "hide-stretched circle" <term>hide-stretched circle</term> of the Corybantes <note>armed priests of Rhea</note> in Euripides' *Bacchae*, line 124, anything other than a drum. Cf. Hildebrand, in the place cited, page 653, and Hoeck, page 219. Images of drums are shown by Spon in *Miscellaneous Ancient Erudition*, page 21. In the rites of Rhea, flutes usually accompanied the drums; see the passages collected by Perizonius on page 539. Often, therefore, the flute-players were the same as the drum-players, as with Oenan-<margin>20</margin>the in Plutarch’s *Amatorius*, chapter 9. The mother of the orator Aeschines was a drum-player, according to Lucian’s *Dream* 12; she was otherwise a low-born woman nicknamed Empusa <note>a shape-shifting specter in Greek folklore</note>, as noted in Bekker’s *Anecdota*, page 250, a passage Lobeck explained in *Aglaophamus*, page 121. In war, however, drums were used only by barbarians, as Sextus Empiricus says in *Against the Musicians*, page 361, <margin>25</margin> such as by the Parthians, according to Justin XLI, 2, and Plutarch’s *Life of Crassus*, chapter 23: "For the Parthians do not encourage themselves to battle with horns or trumpets, but by stretching hide-bound beaters over hollow bronze sounding-vessels, they strike them all at once from many sides, etc." <term>For the Parthians do not encourage themselves to battle with horns or trumpets, but by stretching hide-bound beaters over hollow bronze sounding-vessels, they strike them all at once from many sides, etc.</term> For indeed, these "beaters" <term>beaters</term> are *drums*, as seen in the *Etymologicum Magnum* under that entry.

<margin>30</margin> On the other hand, those which are in use today among more modern people as military drums, you might call by the Latin term *Symphonia*, about which Isidore says in the cited work:
> "The symphonia is the name for a hollow wood with skin stretched on both sides, which musicians strike from this side and that with small sticks."

<margin>35</margin> The same author said a little earlier regarding the drum:
> "The drum is skin or hide stretched over wood on one side; for it is half (that is, divided in two, as Capitolinus says in *Life of Antoninus Pius*, chapter 4: 'the gold that had been offered, he returned the whole to the Italians, and half <note>original: medium</note> to the provincials,' with Casaubon’s note) of a symphonia, in the likeness of a sieve."

# SUPPLEMENT ON THE MUSIC OF THE ANCIENTS §. 3—4.

<page-num>141</page-num>

### §. 4.
### On the Shepherd’s Pipe or Syrinx.

The Syrinx <note>Panpipes</note> was considered an invention of Pan, which testifies to its Phrygian <margin>5</margin> origin, since Pan is counted by everyone among the companions of the Phrygian goddess <note>Cybele</note>, see Pindar's *Pythian Odes* III, 139 and the Scholion on this passage, and Plutarch’s *Amatorius*, page 758. Pan played the pipe at the wedding of Bacchus and Ariadne in Himerius, *Oration* I, 5. There were those who related that Pan received the pipe from a small boy playing among the pastures, as in Julian, <margin>10</margin> *Epistle* 41. Others, however, maintained the instrument was made by Pan in honor of the nymph Syrinx, out of unequal reeds joined together with a binding of wax, as Ovid says in *Metamorphoses* I, 711; add Achilles Tatius VIII, 6 (where someone is said "to play on the syrinx" <term>to play on the syrinx</term>, and similarly Eumathius IV, 18: "the syrinx, as if playing like a flute" <term>the syrinx, as if playing like a flute</term>). The individual reeds, <margin>15</margin> ordered according to the sharpness of the sound based on their difference in length, were called "little flutes" <term>little flutes</term> in Greek; the whole instrument, because the reeds were made of cane, was called "reeds" <term>reeds</term>, according to Aelian in Porphyry's *Commentary on Ptolemy*, page 216. Because it presented the shape of an extended wing (Pollux IV, 69: "so that its shape resembles the wing of a bird" <term>so that its shape resembles the wing of a bird</term>, Ovid, *Metamorphoses* VIII, 191), it was <margin>20</margin> sometimes also called a "wing" <term>wing</term>, see Bellermann in *Anonymous on Music*, page 28. Furthermore, Hesychius has: "syringes made of reeds, called pandouria" <term>syringes made of reeds, called pandouria</term>. Even Cassiodorus listed the *panduria* among the wind instruments, and for the Byzantines, "pandourian" <term>pandourian</term> or "pandourin" <term>pandourin</term> generally signifies a flute, see Reiske in *On the Ceremonies of the Byzantine Court*, page 361. However, the principal <margin>25</margin> passage concerning the pipe and its blowing is Achilles Tatius VIII, 6:
<term>The syrinx consists of many pipes, and each of the pipes is a reed; all the reeds play as if they were a single pipe, joined together in a row one after another, united; the front and the back are equal. And as many of the reeds as are slightly shorter than the next, <margin>30</margin> the one after it is larger, and the first is larger than the second by as much as the second is larger than the one after it, and according to this proportion the rest of the choir of reeds follows, each having an equal difference from the one before, and the inner part is the middle through this excess. The reason for such an arrangement is the distribution of the harmony; for the sharpest pitch is at the top, and as much as <margin>35</margin> the first is low toward the bottom, the outer pipe at each end has received its portion; but the intervals of the rhythm between the ends are all the reeds in between, each carrying down the sharpness to the one nearby until it joins with the final depth. As much as the flute of Athena speaks, so much also does that of Pan play in the mouths. But there the <margin>40</margin> fingers govern the tunes, while here the mouth of the craftsman...</term>

<summary>This page discusses the historical origins and physical construction of ancient percussion instruments like the drum (tympanum) and the shepherd's pipe (syrinx). It highlights their association with the cult of Rhea, their use in barbarian warfare, and provides a detailed technical description of how Panpipes were assembled and tuned.</summary>

...mimics the fingers. In the case of the flute <note>Greek: *aulētēs* (flute-player)</note>, the player closes the other holes and opens only one through which the breath flows down; but here [with the panpipes], he leaves the other reeds free and places his lip only against the one he does not want to remain silent. He leaps from one to another, wherever the harmony of the melody calls him. Thus his mouth dances around the pipes. <note>Original Greek: "μεῖται τοὺς δακτύλους... χορεύει τὸ στόμα."</note> Since the words of this passage are generally plain and easy to understand, I have not been able to resolve certain minor errors that remained even after the recent efforts of Hirschig <note>Willem Adrian Hirschig, a 19th-century editor of Greek romances</note>. Moreover, other erotic writers <note>Novelists who wrote about pastoral love</note> have often made mention of the shepherd’s 
<margin>10</margin>pipe (*fistula*). Images of this instrument are found everywhere in ancient monuments.

### §. 5.
### *On the Pipes (Tibia).*

<margin>15</margin>There is little doubt that the *tibia* <term>tibia: the Roman name for the Greek aulos, a reed instrument often played in pairs</term> evolved from the shepherd’s pipe (*fistula*). For the sounds that were once produced by individual reeds of differing lengths were now transferred into a single reed-pipe. In this new instrument, the air—at times allowed to flow through individual holes and at times compressed by varying the breath—
<margin>20</margin>produced the same range of sounds. This instrument, which should be understood whenever the Greek word *aulos* or the Latin *tibia* is used generally, corresponds properly to the instrument we call by the Italian name *clarinetta* <note>the clarinet</note> or the French *flûte à bec* <note>the recorder</note>. For the pipe was 
<margin>24</margin>equipped with a "little tongue" <term>lingula: the vibrating reed or mouthpiece</term> (a mouthpiece), which the Greeks call a *glōssa* or *glōssis*. This could be removed and was kept in a "tongue-box" (*glōssokomeion*); see Pollux <note>Julius Pollux, a 2nd-century Greek grammarian</note>, Book X, 153. Midas of Agrigentum, a pipe-player, once unexpectedly broke his reed while performing at the Pythian or Panathenaic Games; to the amazement of the spectators, he continued the song by using the pipe itself as if it were a shepherd’s pipe <note>i.e., playing it like a flute without the reed</note>, and thus 
<margin>30</margin>carried away the victory; see the Scholiast <note>ancient commentator</note> on Pindar’s *Pythian Ode* XII, 1. Reeds worn out by frequent use were called "played-out" (*exēulēmenai*) or "worn-down" (*parexēulēmenai*); see the *Etymologicum Magnum* and Leutsch on Zenobius, V, 65. The pipe of the *tibia* itself (the tube) was made of reed, wood, or bone. The 
<margin>34</margin>"fawn-pipes" (*elymoi auloi*) of the Phrygians were made of boxwood, according to Pollux IV, 74. Libyan pipes 
<margin>35</margin>were made of lotus-wood, according to Athenaeus <note>a 3rd-century author on Greek culture</note> IV, p. 182 D, and Pollux in the place cited. Most notably, the shinbones of deer were used for the pipes; see Athenaeus and Pollux, as well as the Scholiast on Aristophanes' *Acharnians* 871, and Spanheim on Callimachus, p. 294. Even donkey bones were used, which were thought to produce a particularly sweet sound; see Plutarch's *Banquet of the Seven Sages*, chapter 5. 
<margin>39</margin>The pipes of the Phoenicians were made of ivory. Bronze appears to have been used initially only for the sake of 
<margin>40</margin>ornament, though regarding Pindar's *Pythian Ode* XII, 25—a verse which Thiersch in his *Introduction*, p. 55, refers to a bronze reed—

---

<page-num>143</page-num>

it is not entirely clear. For instance, regarding the "beast-pipe" (*thēreios aulos*) of the Thebans, Pollux IV, 75 says: "The Thebans made it from the limbs of a fawn, and it was bronze-forged in its outer appearance." Later, however, bronze was used to increase the volume of the sound, which relates to the words of Horace in *The Art of Poetry*, lines 202 and following:

<margin>5</margin>> The pipe, not as now bound with brass and a rival 
> to the trumpet, but thin and simple with few holes, 
> was useful for accompanying and assisting the choruses, 
> and it filled the rows of the theater, which were not yet 
> too crowded, with its breath.

<margin>10</margin>The bottom of the pipe had a main opening called the *bombyx* ("The *bombyx* is the largest and first hole of the pipe," according to the Scholiast on Aristotle, p. 832 a, 45; though sometimes *bombyx* refers to the whole pipe). There were also several side holes (*trypēmata*), regarding the number of which Pollux IV, 80 says: "For a long time the pipe had four 
<margin>15</margin>holes, but Diodorus of Thebes made it 'many-holed' by opening side-paths for the breath." Initially, the pipe could only produce a single tone. Later, Pronomus of Thebes, the teacher of Alcibiades in the art of piping, transferred several tones into one and the same pipe, according to Athenaeus XIV, p. 631 E, and Pausanias IX, 12. 
<margin>20</margin>To do this, he evidently added certain bone rings or sliders to the pipe, with which individual holes could be either closed or opened according to the needs of the tone. We occasionally find sliders of this kind on the pipes shown in ancient monuments; see Boettiger's *Shorter Works* I, p. 24, and Caylus' *Collection of Antiquities* III, 206–208. What the *holmoi* or 
<margin>25</margin>*hypholmia* <note>parts of the mouthpiece</note> mentioned in Pollux IV, 70 actually are, I do not understand; there were some who took them to be the reed or some sort of ornament on the pipe. The best reeds for piping were produced in Boeotia near Orchomenus and the Cephissus river; see Pindar, *Pythian Ode* XII, 27, where the commentator speaks with little 
<margin>30</margin>accuracy: "He speaks of the reeds which grow by the Cephissus, from which the piping-reeds are made; for the pipes for the flute grow in the Cephissus." <note>Original Greek: "τοὺς δόνακας λέγει... αὐλητικοὶ κάλαμοι φύονται."</note> How the pipes and especially the reeds were manufactured from those reeds was described by Theophrastus in *History of Plants* IV, 11, and after him by Pliny in *Natural History* XVI, 36.

Furthermore, a pipe is either single-tubed (*monocalamus*) or a "single-pipe" (*monaulus*), mentioned in Athenaeus IV, 
<margin>35</margin>p. 175 F, the inventor of which Pliny (*Natural History* VII, 56) names as Pan; or it is double. The double pipe either consists of two single pipes—see Gerhard's *Ancient Images* 85—or of two tubes joined through a single mouthpiece. This latter type Nonnus in *Dionysiaca* XX, 301 calls a "pair of pipes" (*synōphides aulōn*); the mouthpiece was not called a *glōssis* but a *zeugos* <note>a yoke or pair</note>, see 
<margin>40</margin>Salmasius on Solinus, p. 83. Whether the word *diopos* <note>perhaps meaning 'two-holed' or 'two-toned'</note> in Athenaeus...

<summary>This page describes the evolution and physical construction of the tibia (aulos), explaining how it transitioned from a simple reed to a complex instrument with multiple holes and mechanical sliders. It details the materials used, such as bone, ivory, and bronze, and highlights the technical innovations of Pronomus of Thebes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on wind instruments, specifically the classification and variety of the tibia (aulos or double-pipe) based on length, gendered associations, and ceremonial use. It transitions from the physical construction of the pipes to their specific cultural roles in weddings, banquets, and ensembles.</meta>

# ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS

<page-num>144</page-num>

Regarding the double reeds <note>Latin: *binos calamos*</note>, whether this refers to the number of holes is doubtful. Now indeed, the reeds were either of equal length, making "equal pipes" <note>Greek: *auloi isoi*</note>, such as the "banquet pipes" <note>Greek: *auloi paroinioi*</note> used in festivities according to Pollux (IV, 80); or the pipes were unequal, 
<margin>5</margin> such as the "wedding pipes" <note>Greek: *auloi gamēlioi*</note>, which Cicero called "nuptial pipes" and Apuleius called *zygia* <term>zygia: "yoked" pipes, typically a pair of unequal length used at weddings</term>. Regarding these, Pollux writes in the place cited: "and the wedding tune was played on two pipes, one being larger, signifying a harmony, and that the man ought to be greater" <note>original: *καὶ τὸ μὲν γαμήλιον αὔλημα δύο ἦσαν αὐλοί, μείζων ἅτερος, συμφωνίαν μὲν ὑποδηλοῦντες, μείζω δ’ εἶναι χρῆναι τὸν ἄνδρα*</note>. To this category 
<margin>10</margin> undoubtedly belong the "male and female pipes" of Herodotus (I, 17): "he campaigned to the sound of panpipes and harps and female and male pipes" <note>original: *ἐστρατεύετο δὲ ὑπὸ συρίγγων τε καὶ πηκτίδων καὶ αὐλοῦ γυναικηίου τε καὶ ἀνδρηίου*</note>. One should compare Boeckh’s *On Pindaric Meter* (p. 265), from which it is useful to describe these points: "Equal pipes are either 'right-handed,' which are high-pitched, or 'left-handed,' which are deep. In unequal pairs, one was right and the other left. Varro, cited in Servius’s commentary on the *Aeneid* (IX, 618), says: 'The 
<margin>15</margin> Phrygian right pipe has one hole, the left has two, of which one has a high sound and the other a deep sound'" — though personally, I do not grant much weight to Varro’s words.

The individual types and species of pipes that were commonly cited depended entirely on the length of the reed and differed either in the sharpness or 
<margin>20</margin> the depth of the sound. The longest were the "male pipes" or "manly pipes" <note>Greek: *andreioi auloi*</note>, which Pollux (IV, 81) says accompanied the choruses of men, or were used for the "flute-melody without a chorus" <note>Greek: *pros to achoron aulēma*</note>, as in the Pythian competition. Didymus, in Athenaeus (XIV p. 634 E), distinguished two species of these: "complete pipes" <note>Greek: *aulous teleious*</note> and "extra-complete pipes" <note>Greek: *hyperteleious*</note>. 
<margin>25</margin> The "children's pipes" <note>Greek: *auloi paidikoi*</note> held the second place, of which Athenaeus (IV, 176 F) says: "these pipes are smaller than the complete ones" <note>original: *εἰσὶ δ’ οἱ αὐλοὶ οὗτοι ἐλάσσονες τῶν τελείων*</note>; they were also called "half-holed" <note>Greek: *hēmiopoi*</note> (Athenaeus IV p. 182 C). They played these pipes at banquets, undoubtedly interspersed with the choruses of boys. Conversely, the "maiden pipes" <note>Greek: *auloi parthenioi*</note> accompanied the choruses of young women (Pollux IV, 81). Other types of pipes 
<margin>30</margin> took their names not from a difference in structure, but from various uses, such as the "Pythian" and "dactylic" pipes, the pipe of Minerva (of which I spoke above in the commentary), as well as the "tragic" and "lyric-song" pipes <note>Greek: *auloi tragikoi* and *lysiōdoi*</note>, and also the "cithara-accompanying pipes" <note>Greek: *kitharistērioi auloi*</note> which accompanied the ensemble of harps. For, especially in more recent 
<margin>35</margin> times, harps very often produced a harmony with pipes called a "synaulia" <term>synaulia: a concert of pipes and strings playing together</term> (Scholiast on Aristophanes' *Knights* line 9; Athenaeus XIV p. 617 F; Spanheim on Callimachus p. 471). Moreover, regarding the proper definition of *synaulia*, there was little agreement among the ancients; for Semus of Delos, in the passage of Athenaeus cited, interpreted it as "a reciprocal contest of harmony 
<margin>40</margin> between pipe and rhythm, without the words of a singer" <note>original: *per ἀγὼν συμφωνίας ἀμοιβαῖος αὐλοῦ καὶ ῥυθμοῦ, χωρὶς λόγου τοῦ προσμελῳδοῦντος*</note>, while Hesychius, appealing to a fragment of Sophocles (fr. 79), defined it as "the 

---

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>SUPPLEMENT ON THE MUSIC OF THE ANCIENTS §. 5.</header>

flute-playing performed by two people" <note>original: *ἡ ὑπὸ δυοῖν ἐπιτελουμένη αὔλησις*</note>. It must be held as certain, therefore, that the accompaniment of the voice was foreign to the *synaulia*; furthermore, the term was applied sometimes to the harmony of different instruments, such as the harp and pipe, and sometimes to the harmony of two instruments of the same kind, much like 
<margin>5</margin> the ensemble of instruments in our own music called a "duetto."

The pipes of the Arabs seem to have been the longest, so much so that "an Arabian piper" <note>Greek: *Arabios aulētēs*</note> became a proverb to denote a talkative and wordy person. Zenobius (II, 39) says: "they say the Arabs in their 
<margin>10</margin> night watches used a long pipe. This pipe was passed from one to another, and they played while keeping the fires burning until daybreak" <note>original: *φασὶ τοὺς Ἀραβίους ἐν ταῖς νυκτεριναῖς φυλακαῖς κεχρῆσθαι αὐλῷ ἐπιμήκει...*</note>. Leutsch collected passages from other writers on this point, such as Libanius (ep. 758). However, Diogenianus (I, 28) reported it differently: "measured from a certain Arabian piper, who played for 
<margin>15</margin> a drachma, but stopped for four" <note>note: implying he was paid more to stop playing than to start</note>. A tiny pipe, a span in length and with a very high-pitched sound ("shrill and mournful" <note>Greek: *oxy kai goeron phthengomenos*</note>), used by the Phoenicians and Carians in dirges, was mentioned by Athenaeus (IV p. 174 F) as the "gingrainon pipe" <note>Greek: *gingrainon aulon*</note>. — There were 
<margin>20</margin> also "transverse pipes" <note>Greek: *plagioi auloi*</note>, very similar to our modern flutes, regarding which Pollux (IV, 74) says: "Types of pipes include the transverse, made of lotus wood, an invention of the Libyans; the Libyans call it the *plagiaulos*" <note>note: the Greek word *plagiaulos* literally means "side-pipe"</note>. These pipes too, images of which are shown in the Capitoline Museum (IV, 57) and the Pio-Clementine (V, 113; see Jahn, *Archaeological Symbols* p. 191, 436), were considered an invention of Pan (cf. Bion, Idyll V, 7). Nor does the *photinx* 
<margin>25</margin> differ much, an Egyptian invention (see Juba in Athenaeus IV p. 175 E, 182 E). And not even the *magadis* pipe, a Lydian instrument that produced high and low sounds at the same time, thus spanning an octave interval, and usually accompanied the harp (see Athenaeus IV p. 182 D): "the so-called *magadis* pipe, also 
<margin>30</margin> called the *plagiomagadis* (for so Meineke conjectured instead of *palaiomagadis*), demonstrates a high and low sound at the same time" <note>original: *ἐν ταὐτῷ ὀξὺν καὶ βαρὺν φθόγγον ἐπιδείκνυται*</note>. Furthermore, a certain unique species of double Phrygian pipe comes into account, the *elymoi* pipes, which have a deep sound (Athenaeus, as cited, p. 176 F), the ends of whose reeds were curved like horns (Pollux 
<margin>35</margin> IV, 74): "the *elymos* is made of boxwood, a Phrygian invention; a horn is attached to each of the pipes, curving upward" <note>original: *κέρας δ’ ἑκατέρῳ τῶν αὐλῶν ἀνανεῦον πρόσεστιν*</note>. It even had a "bell" <note>Greek: *kōdōna*</note> of horn like a trumpet, as the same Athenaeus reports (IV p. 185 A): "In the second book of *The Phoenix*, Ion says: 'bringing the loud, deep pipe in a running rhythm,' speaking thus of the Phrygian one. For this one is deep. On account of which they even attach the horn to it, proportional to the 
<margin>40</margin> bell of trumpets." They were also called *synaulia*...

<summary>This section catalogs the various types of ancient pipes (tibiae/auloi), categorizing them by their length, pitch, and cultural associations—ranging from wedding and banquet music to the long pipes of the Arabs and the curved Phrygian elymoi.</summary>

# ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS

<page-num>146</page-num>

...the thickness <note>Greek: *reian tou pachous*</note>) and were in use especially on the island of Cyprus, and then also in the rest of Greece, as Athenaeus records (p. 177 A). Compare Gerhard’s *Ancient Images* (112 and 108, 2), where only one pipe is curved while the other is straight. We must also speak of the **bagpipe** <note>Latin: *tibia utriculari*; literally "a pipe with a skin bag"</note>, that is, a pipe which is inflated from a bag. This was undoubtedly a recent invention; indeed, I only know of it from Dio Chrysostom (Orat. LXXI, 9), where the conversation concerns Nero, if I am not mistaken: "they say also — that he was able to play the pipe both with his mouth and by placing a skin bag under his armpits, so that he might avoid the unseemliness of Athena." <note>This refers to the myth that Athena threw away the pipes because blowing them distorted her face.</note> Compare Suetonius’s *Life of Nero* (ch. 54):

> "He had vowed that he would come forward as a hydraulic organist, a choral piper, and a bagpiper."

The Greeks used to imitate the sound of the pipe with the word **Tēnella**, which was originally the beginning of a hymn composed by Archilochus in honor of Hercules; see the Scholia on Aristophanes' *Knights* (276) and Leutsch in *Proverbs* (I, p. 435). However, Eratosthenes states the meaning of the word somewhat differently in the Scholia on Pindar (Olympian IX, 1): "Eratosthenes says that when the piper or the lyre-player was not present, the leader of the song took it up and spoke it outside of the melody, and the chorus of revelers added the 'hail to the victor' etc." Compare Liebel’s *Archilochus* (p. 178) and Boeckh on Pindar (loc. cit.). To avoid an unseemly and dishonest appearance of the mouth, and to distribute the breath evenly while blowing, pipers used a **mouth-strap** <note>Greek: *phorbeia*</note>, which was a leather binding that constricted the lips and mouth, leaving a slit through which the reed of the pipes could be inserted, to use the words of Salmasius on Solinus (p. 583); compare Gerhard’s *Ancient Images* (86). Hesychius, under the entry *phorbeia*, calls it: "the piper’s mouthpiece, also called a lip-binder." The use of this apparatus is attested by the Scholia on Aristophanes’ *Wasps* (580): "so that the breath, being sent forth in a measured way, might make the sound of the pipe sweet." Furthermore, while playing, pipers would place a **foot-clapper** under their feet to indicate the rhythms by striking; this was called a **batalon** or **kroupezai**. The Scholia on Aeschines *Against Timarchus* (p. 126), following Meineke’s emendation in *History of Comedy* (p. 336), says: "and even now, pipers have a double footstool under their right foot, and when they play, they strike the footstool with their foot at the same time, giving back the same rhythm, which they call a *batalon*." Add Photius’s *Lexicon* (p. 180, 21): "kroupezai: wooden shoes — others call it a rattle, which pipers strike, the *batalon*." Casaubon has discussed the use of these foot-clappers in his notes on Suetonius’s *Caligula* (ch. 54).

Moreover, as far as I know, no one among the Greeks doubted that the pipe was a foreign instrument. They held its homeland to be either Phrygia <note>An ancient kingdom in west-central Anatolia (modern Turkey).</note> (Himerius, *Eclogue* 13, 3; see commentary p. 84, 48) or Libya (Athenaeus XIV, p. 618 C). For those myths which attribute the

<column-break/>

<page-num>147</page-num>

# APPENDIX ON THE MUSIC OF THE ANCIENTS § 5.

invention of the pipe to **Minerva** <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Athena.</note> (Pindar, *Pythian* XII; Bion, *Idyll* V, 7) seem to be of more recent origin. Certainly, the stories told about the pipes being cast away by Minerva, snatched up by **Marsyas**, and his ill-fated contest with Apollo, smack of Athenian wit, as Boettiger explained in *Minor Works* (I, p. 12). From Phrygia, the pipe was brought to the island of Crete along with the sacred rites of **Rhea**; for the **Curetes** <note>Mythical armed dancers who protected the infant Zeus.</note>, according to the testimony of the *Phoronis* in Strabo (X, p. 723), were Phrygians and pipers; from Crete it moved to the rest of Greece, but not before the time of the Trojan War. For Homer knew of the use of the pipe only among the Trojans (Iliad X, 11 ff.), a point which the ancients noted here and there (Scholia on Iliad XVIII, 495; Olympiodorus on Plato’s *Phaedo* p. 18, 2, and *Alcibiades* p. 66; Athenaeus I, p. 16 A): "Agamemnon, at any rate, hears the sound of pipes and panpipes... but he did not bring them into banquets. Except in the description of the making of the shield, where he mentions pipes during a wedding. He attributes pipes to the barbarians." Compare Hoeck’s *Crete* (I, p. 223) and Lobeck’s *Aglaophamus* (p. 298). Soon, however, the use of pipes spread through all of Greece, especially with the sacred rites of **Bacchus** <note>Dionysus</note> (see Athenaeus IV, p. 184 D), and thus it flourished in Corinth, Sicyon, Argos, and most of all among the **Thebans**, whose very public institutions favored the music of the pipes (see Plutarch’s *Life of Pelopidas*, ch. 19). Regarding the Spartans, I have spoken in the commentary (p. 89, 13). The study of pipes, brought from Thebes to Athens even before the time of the Persian Wars, at first received immense applause (see Aristotle, *Politics* VIII, 6; Athenaeus IV, p. 184 D). Thus **Theodorus**, the father of **Isocrates**, acquired such a great fortune through the manufacture of pipes that he was not only able to educate his children excellently but also to provide a musical chorus for his family's festivals (see Plutarch’s *Life of Isocrates*). But around the time of the **ochlocracy** <note>Rule by the mob; a term for radical democracy.</note>, the study of pipe-playing again fell into disuse, especially after it was rejected by **Alcibiades**, who was the model of respectability for all Athens (Plutarch’s *Life of Alcibiades*, ch. 2; Gellius, *Attic Nights* XV, 17):

> "When that matter became widely known, by the consensus of all Athenians at that time, the discipline of pipe-playing was abandoned."

Even **Plato** and **Aristotle**, those great philosophers, looked with little favor on the art of the pipe; and generally, the opinion prevailed that the handling of the cithara <note>A professional version of the lyre.</note> and lyre had a greater educational power and was far preferable to the effeminating song of the pipe—a memory of which even reached Cicero (*Pro Murena*, ch. 13):

> "as they say among Greek artists, those are pipe-players who could not become lyre-players."

Nevertheless, the Athenians always summoned Theban pipers at a very high price for their solemn festivals (cf. Boeckh, *Public Economy* I, p. 132; Nevius on Bacchylides).

# ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS

<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS</header>

In Demosthenes' speech *Against Meidias* (p. 72, p. 43 A), it is written: "This man once acted as a *choregos* <note>A wealthy citizen who financed the costumes and training for a festival chorus.</note> for tragic actors; I, however, for male flute-players; and no one, I presume, is unaware that this expenditure is much greater than that former cost." Indeed, even in the most recent age, the pipes <note>Latin: *tibiae*</note> were never exiled from festivals, especially those of the stage; see Philostratus, *Life of Apollonius* (IV, 21). Thebes, moreover, remained the primary seat and, as it were, the home of the art of the pipe until the times of the successors of Alexander the Great, and this was by the unanimous consent of all Greece. See the elegant account given by Dio Chrysostom in his seventh Oration (121). Certainly, the Thebans were conscious of this praise, not without some pride; compare Menander on *Rhetorical Encomia* (p. 360, 22 Spengel). Bartholinus once wrote a specialized book on pipes. After him, Spanheim in his commentary on Callimachus and Julius Sommerbrodt in the *Halle Encyclopedia* discussed the matter excellently, and I have adapted their labors for my own use.

### §. 6.
### *Of Trumpets and Horns.*

The Greeks considered the trumpet, or ***salpinx*** <note>Greek: *σάλπιγγα*</note>, to be an invention of the Tyrrhenians <note>The Etruscans, renowned in antiquity for their bronze-working.</note> (Athenaeus IV, p. 184 A: "Horns and trumpets are a discovery of the Tyrrhenians"), and for this reason, it bore the name "Tyrrhenian." It was made of bronze, as noted in Sophocles' *Electra* (v. 709); compare Eumathes (V, 3): "A dream recast his tongue into a Tyrrhenian trumpet." We gain light regarding its form from Achilles Tatius (IV, 4), who says of an elephant’s trunk: "it is like a trumpet in both appearance and size." Nor does the description of the Jewish **buccina** <note>A curved horn or trumpet.</note> (*bykanē*) in Josephus' *Antiquities* (III, 12, 6) differ much. To increase the sound, the trumpet ended in a **bell** <note>Greek: *kōdōna*</note>. The sound itself (the trumpet "speaks" or "shouts out," according to Osann in Apuleius; it "bellows" almost always in Nonnus' *Dionysiaca*) was high and vehement, and almost equaled the braying of a donkey, according to Plutarch (*Dinner of the Seven Wise Men*, ch. 5); and Josephus (*Antiquities* III, 7, 7) compared the "clatter of the bells" to the crash of thunder.

In general, however, the trumpet was regarded as a cheap and worthless instrument, hence the proverb "you compare a pipe to a trumpet" found in Diogenes (I, 15). It was used for sounding the **classicum** <note>The military signal for battle or assembly.</note> in battles and at sacred games. Certainly, the Argives and doubtless other Greeks as well used the trumpet in wars (Pausanias II, 21, 3; compare the Scholia on Sophocles’ *Ajax* 14 and Euripides’ *Phoenician Women* v. 1386). The Argives also...

---

<margin>40</margin>*) Seyffarth (in *Sacred Egyptian Theology*, p. 118) gathered that the Jews received the trumpet from the Egyptians, based on the Hebrew name **shophar**.

<column-break/>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>SUPPLEMENT ON THE MUSIC OF THE ANCIENTS §. 5 — 6.</header>

...also made Minerva <note>Athena</note>, as a goddess of war, the patroness of trumpets, whereas pipes had already been consecrated to her by others; see O. Müller, *The Dorians* (II p. 327 n). And tradition held that Hegelaus, the grandson of Hercules, founded a temple to Minerva Salpinx <note>Minerva of the Trumpet</note> at Argos; see Pausanias (II, 21, 3), Hesychius (under the word *salpinx*), and Lycophron (v. 915) with the Scholia. If Himerius (Oration XXII, 6) attributes trumpets to the Muses, the word *salpinx* is used metaphorically, as is often the case among the Sophists, just as more recent writers speak of the "trumpet of glory" or even the "buccina"; compare Wernsdorf on this passage. Regarding the use of trumpets in sacred games, the same Himerius (Oration XII, 1) says generally: "the trumpets lead the procession of the judges of the games." And in the description of the Pythian contest in Sophocles’ *Electra* (v. 709), the signal is sounded by a trumpet for the runners on the track:
> "And when the appointed judges had shaken the lots
<margin>15</margin>> and stationed them at their chariots,
> they darted forth at the sound of the bronze trumpet."
Heliodorus (IV, 3) records the same in his description of the Pythian contest. The Argives used trumpets in celebrating the mysteries of Bacchus <note>Dionysus</note>, according to Plutarch (*On Isis and Osiris*, ch. 35). And it is likely that the art of trumpeters and heralds <note>Hesychius notes that *ho salpinx*—the trumpeter—is the same as *ho kēryx*—the herald.</note> constituted a specific type of competition both at Olympia and in the other sacred games; see Boeckh, *Public Economy* (II p. 361).

The Romans acted no differently. Among the Romans, too, the signal for the start of the games in the circus and amphitheater was given by a trumpet, as Sidonius says (*Poems* XXII, 339) and as Wernsdorf notes regarding Himerius. Compare Livy (XXXIII, 32): "The Romans sat down for the spectacle, and the herald with the trumpeter, as is the custom, proceeded into the middle of the arena, from where the games are usually announced with a formal song." Trumpets and horns were employed in the army to sound the signals, as noted by Tacitus (*Annals* I, 28). However, there is little certainty regarding the distinction between **trumpets** (*tubae*), **buccinae** (which the Greeks call *bykanas*), **lituī** <note>A J-shaped cavalry trumpet.</note>, and **horns** (*cornua*). Yet the difference seems not to have been great, since John Lydus (*On Magistracies* I, 46) translates *tubicines* as "trumpeters of the infantry" and *buccinātores* as "trumpeters of the cavalry," and (*On Months* IV, 50) he interprets the *lituus* (from the word *lites*, "strife") as a "priestly trumpet." A trumpeter is called by the Greeks a ***salpistēs*** or ***salpinktēs***; sometimes even an *aulētēs* (flute-player), as by Achilles Tatius (II, 22), and similarly Lucian (*Dialogues of the Gods* XII, 1) says "he plays the pipe with the horn." A horn-player is a ***keraulēs***. Otherwise, Greek writers, unless they are describing Roman military affairs, rarely mention horns, so far as I know.
