they perform, as in Xenophon, *Anabasis* II, 2, 4: "whenever he gives the signal with the horn." <note>original: ἐπειδὰν σημήνῃ τῷ κέρατι</note> Giving signals is said in Greek either as *hyposēmainein* <note>original: ὑποσημαίνειν; literally "to signal from beneath" or give a cue</note> (Thucydides VI, 32: "silence was signaled by the trumpet") <note>original: τῇ σάλπιγγι σιωπὴ ὑπεσημάνθη</note> or "to give the starting note" <note>original: ἐνδόσιμον διδόναι</note> ("to give the watchword," Himerius, *Oration* III, 9; "to give the signal," ibid. XIV, 31). <margin>5</margin> This is also said of the leader of a chorus singing the opening. From the art of music, the expression was later transferred to other things and carries the force of inciting or urging on; see Gataker’s notes on Marcus Antoninus, p. 336, and Perizonius on Aelian’s *Varia Historia* XIV, 41. For example, Herodian III, 6, 10: "giving the signal <note>original: τὸ ἐνδόσιμον</note> for zeal and courage to the soldiers by his actions," and VI, 3: "those who incited him to cruelty <margin>10</margin> by giving the signal <note>original: τὸ ἐνδόσιμον</note> to his anger, he judged to be well-disposed and faithful friends." See also Dio Chrysostom, *Oration* LVI, 4: "Nor do I happen to learn the names of the leaders of the choruses, who are called the signal-givers <note>original: σημαίνοντας</note> and those who give the melody." <note>original: μέλος ἐνδίδοντας</note>

### §. 7.
### *On the Hydraulic and Pneumatic Organ.* <margin>15</margin>

The *Hydraulic Organ*, or *Hydraulus* <note>original: ὕδραυλις; a "water-pipe"</note>—a certain culmination and perfection of both the shepherd’s pipe <note>The Pan-pipe or syrinx</note> and the bagpipe—is commonly held to be the invention of Ctesibius, that most famous ancient engineer. According to Aristocles in Athenaeus, book IV, p. 174, <margin>20</margin> he lived during the reign of Ptolemy Euergetes II, though learned men have seen that Ptolemy Euergetes I should be substituted for him. See Buttmann in the *Commentaries of the Berlin Academy*, 1811, p. 169. There are, however, some among the ancients who attributed the invention of the hydraulic organ to Archimedes, such as Tertullian in *On the Soul*, ch. 14, and *On the Spectacles*, ch. 10; see also Claudian, *On the Consulship of Mallius <margin>25</margin> Theodorus*, v. 315. Indeed, Athenaeus mentions in the passage cited above that there were those who believed that the "all-harmonic" instrument <note>original: panarmonium</note> of Plato in *The Republic* (III, p. 404 D) was some species of hydraulic organ. Two descriptions of the hydraulus survive from antiquity: one by Hero in *Pneumatics*, p. 227 ff., and another by Vitruvius, book X, ch. 13; they differ little from one another and were <margin>30</margin> excellently illustrated by Buttmann. This hydraulus had only a few pipes, which were touched by certain wooden keys <note>original: pinnis; literally "fins" or "wings"</note>—much like in our modern organ, the front of which has the keys of a clavichord—although they were played with difficulty and great physical effort. By the compression of water enclosed in a bronze chest, which several organ-boys <margin>35</margin> (the *hydrauloi*) moved, the pipes were inflated with air. Whether we should agree with Drieberg (*On the Pneumatic Inventions of the Greeks*, Berlin, 1822, p. 58), who contends that Vitruvius’s organ had several "voices" <note>stops or ranks of pipes</note>—to use a more modern term for this matter—while Hero’s had only one (making the former "many-stringed" <note>poly-chord</note> and the latter "single-stringed" <note>mono-chord</note>), let others decide. For the <margin>40</margin>
<meta>catchword: For the</meta>

---

<page-num>151</page-num>
<header>SUPPLEMENT ON THE MUSIC OF THE ANCIENTS §. 6—7.</header>

Roman Emperors, hydraulic organs were a great delight, such as for Nero (Suetonius, *Life of Nero*, ch. 41 and 54), Elagabalus, and Alexander Severus (Aelius Lampridius, ch. 27).

Regarding the invention of the *Pneumatic Organ*, little is certain. However, <margin>5</margin> Hero, in the passage cited on pp. 229–230, already mentions one type of it; see Drieberg, p. 61. That species, whose pipes were no longer inflated by the pressure of water but by the help of bellows (as they are today), seems to have been accepted into use only in the fourth century after Christ. The Emperor Julian described it in these verses:
<margin>10</margin> > "I see a different nature of reeds; surely from another
> brazen field they have perhaps sprouted
> wildly; they are not shaken by our winds,
> but a blast leaping forth from a bull-hide cave <note>The bellows, made of ox-hide.</note>
> travels from below beneath the roots of well-bored reeds;
<margin>15</margin> > and some majestic man, having swift fingers of the hand,
> stands touching the coherent keys <note>original: κάνονας; the sliders or rulers that controlled the air flow</note> of the pipes;
> and they, skipping softly, squeeze out a song."
In this place, Heyler (p. 541) noted: "Since it is unclear in what age and by which mortal pneumatic organs were <margin>20</margin> invented, it is helpful to learn here that their use was already known in the fourth century. One might conjecture they were a fairly recent invention, as Julian deemed them worthy of both wonder and description." This opinion is favored by the first part of the passage, which Beckmann (*Contributions to the History of Inventions*, IV, p. 467) extracted from Alexander Sarpi's books on the inventors of things: <margin>25</margin> "Organs with lead pipes are said to have been sent from Byzantium in Gaul to King Pepin by the Emperor Constantine <note>original: Constantino Caesare</note> \*) <note>Constantine V, in the year 757 AD.</note>; others think their use was discovered in Rome by Pope Vitalian." Nevertheless, in the opinion of Barth (*Adversaria*, p. 956), who also mentions Julian's epigram, <margin>30</margin> "already in Tertullian (*On the Spectacles*, ch. 10), where organs are mentioned, one should understand them to be the pneumatic organs of the Christians received into the churches." But from Tertullian's words—"the things which are performed by voice and melodies and organs and lyres have as their masters the Muses, Apollos, Minervas, and Mercuries"—no such thing can <margin>35</margin> be gathered. Instead, we should compare Julian's verses to those of Augustine...

---

\*) In the year 757. See the *Lorsch Annals* in Pertz, *German Monuments*, Vol. I, p. 141, and the *Metz Annals*, p. 333, in which place it is said among other things: "an organ, which had not been seen before in Francia"—from which it <margin>40</margin> seems rightly concluded that the use of the instrument was more common in the East than in the West.

...words on Psalm 56, 16: "all instruments of musicians are called organs: not only is that large instrument called an organ which is inflated by bellows, but whatever is physical and adapted for a song, which the singer uses as an instrument, is called an organ." <note>original: "organa dicuntur omnia instrumenta musicorum: non solum illud organum dicitur, quod grande est et inflatur follibus, sed quidquid aptatur ad cantilenam et corporeum est, quo instrumento utitur, qui cantat, organum dicitur"</note>
<margin>5</margin> Compare Isidore, *Etymologies* III, 20 and Cassiodorus on Psalm 150: "the organ is like a certain tower built with various pipes, to which a very rich voice is sent by the breath of bellows; and so that a graceful melody may compose it, it is constructed with certain wooden tongues from the interior <note>read: anterior/front</note> part, which the fingers of the masters, pressing <margin>10</margin> down with skill, produce a loud-sounding and most sweet song." <note>original: "organum est quasi turris quaedam diversis fistulis fabricata, quibus flatu follium vox copiosissima destinatur, et ut eam modulatio decora componat, linguis quibusdam ligneis ab interiore (l. anteriore) parte construitur, quas disciplinabiliter magistrorum digiti reprimentes, grandisonam efficiunt et suavissimam cantilenam"</note> I think, however, that Cassiodorus only heard reports of this instrument; otherwise, Varro (II, 40) would not have praised the cithara <note>a professional version of the lyre</note> as the most perfect instrument. Among the Byzantines, organs were used not in churches, but in the circus and occasionally at the banquets of emperors. I <margin>15</margin> believe I have found the most recent mention of the <term>hydraulis</term> <note>a water-powered organ</note> in the verses of Leo the Great on the baths of the imperial court in Matranga, *Anecdota Romana* p. 567:
> A stream of boundless waters <note>original: ῥόος ὑδάτων ἀπείρων</note>

> unseen, without a controller <note>original: ἀφανῶς ἄνευ κρατοῦντος</note>
> weaving the age for the King. <note>original: βασιλεῖ πλέκων τὸν αἰῶνον</note>

Du Cange has collected some notes on organs in his *Greek Glossary* under the word *organa*. Add also Muratori, *Italian Antiquities*, Vol. II p. 536. Meister was the first to explain it more accurately in "On the Ancient Hydraulis" in *New Commentaries of the Royal Society of Science at Göttingen*, Vol. II p. 158. ### §. 8.
<margin>30</margin> ->On the perceptible kind of organs. On the Monochord and Helicon.<-

Perceptible <note>or "sensory"</note> organs seem to have been very common among the Greeks, and many kinds of them are named, although even the Alexandrian grammarians did not know much about them beyond their names. Especially, the number of strings was called into question. <margin>34</margin> For some convinced themselves that multi-stringed <note>original: polychorda</note> instruments were new, being surprised that Anacreon speaks of the <term>magadis</term> <note>a harp-like instrument</note> as being equipped with twenty strings. However, Euphorion judged otherwise, whose words I wrote above on p. 79, 3. And Artemon, in Athenaeus XIV p. 637 C, asserts that many instruments are so unknown that not even <margin>40</margin>

<column-break/>

<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>SUPPLEMENT ON ANCIENT MUSIC § 7—9.</header>

this can be known: whether they were truly in use at some time, or whether one and the same instrument lies hidden under different names. Therefore, we must first set aside those whose use pertained only to the theory <note>original: speculationem</note> of the art, for investigating the mathematical <margin>5</margin> ratios of sounds and intervals. To this class belongs, above all others, the *Monochord*. Ptolemy, who praises it in *Harmonics* II, 12, adds however: "and for the sake of its practical use" <note>original: καὶ τῆς χρήσεως δὲ ἕνεκεν</note> (Attic writers often used the adverb *heneka* <note>original: ἕνεκα; meaning "for the sake of"</note>, more recent ones rarely so. Dio Chrysostom, *Oration* XXXVII 32: "for for the sake of slander, even Socrates might be a corrupter of the young." <note>original: διαβολῆς μὲν γὰρ ἕνεκα καὶ Σωκράτης εἴη τῶν νέων διαφθορεύς</note> <margin>10</margin> Libanius, *Oration* III p. 176 R: "but if they did not report it, the victories would be unknown as far as I am concerned") <note>original: εἰ δ’ οὐκ ἀπήγγελον, ἡγνοεῖτ’ ἂν ἕνεκα γ’ ἐμοῦ τὰς νίκας</note> "this instrument would be the last of all and the weakest." Pythagoras, according to Diogenes Laertius, had invented the Monochord or *kanon* <note>meaning "rule" or "measuring rod"</note>, about the construction of which one should consult Nicomachus p. 79, Euclid p. 37, Aristides p. 116 ff., compare Boeckh on Pindar’s Meters p. 208 ff. <margin>15</margin> Subsequently, all harmonic investigations proceeded from this instrument, according to Ptolemy, *Harmonics* I, 2. However, Simus the Harmonic—perhaps not different from that Simus of Posidonia whom Iamblichus mentioned—claimed the invention of the monochord as his own, according to Porphyry’s *Life of Pythagoras* <margin>20</margin> ch. 3. Similar was the use in harmonics of the *Helicon*, a four-stringed instrument, of which Uhdolphus gave an accurate description in *Harmonics* p. 7 ff., following Ptolemy, *Harmonics* II, 2 and Aristides p. 117 ff.

<margin>25</margin> ### §. 9.
->On the Lyre and Cithara.<-

Of all instruments, however, the most common were the Lyre and the Cithara. Since these were as if the private property of the Greek nation, almost all authors attributed their invention to the gods, <margin>30</margin> until more recent writers—such as the followers of Euhemerus <note>a philosopher who believed gods were originally great men</note>—like Strabo in his tenth book, traced the origins of cithara-playing music back to Thrace and even to Asia. In truth, it cannot be denied that some distinction existed between the two instruments; this did not consist in the external shape <margin>35</margin> (about which Burette and Forkel, Vol. I p. 200, speculated without suitable authority from the ancients), but in the tension of the strings, which is clearly apparent from Ptolemy, *Harmonics* II, 16 (Aristides Quintilianus p. 101 compared with Boeckh on Pindar’s Meters p. 260). Those more skilled in music may consult this more difficult passage. Thus the difference perhaps comes down to that which <margin>40</margin> exists between two more recent instruments, one of which is called by the Italian name *viola*, the other...

<summary>This page discusses the historical definitions of "organs" (from general instruments to the bellows-driven pipe organ) and examines theoretical stringed instruments like the monochord and helicon used for mathematical musicology. It begins a comparison between the lyre and cithara, suggesting their difference lay in string tension rather than just physical shape.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical and historical analysis of ancient stringed instruments, specifically focusing on the terminological confusion between the lyre and the cithara, and the mythical origins and construction of the lyre from a tortoise shell.</meta>

# ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS

<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS</header>

...is called the *viola di braccio* <note>Italian: "arm viola," a member of the violin family held against the shoulder</note>, from which confusion—because the external appearance and form are nearly the same—no one except a trained musician is safe. Is it any wonder, then, that the names of the lyre and the cithara <note>The cithara was a larger, professional version of the lyre with a wooden soundbox</note> were used interchangeably hundreds of times by the ancients? <margin>5</margin> Thus, it was said "the lyre plays the cithara" <note>original Greek: λύρα κιθαρίζειν</note>, and Hesychius <note>original: λύρα· κιθάρα — "lyre: cithara"</note> and Suidas <note>original: κιθάρα· ὄργανον μουσικόν, ἢ λύρα — "cithara: a musical instrument, or a lyre"</note> seem to have established no distinction at all, though it is otherwise in the writings of Pollux. Nor is this confusion to be blamed only on more recent writers; even the ancient and esteemed authors erred in this. <margin>10</margin> For in the Homeric Hymn to Mercury <note>Hermes</note>, there is no difference between the lyre and the cithara, nor in Aristophanes’ *Clouds* (line 1360). Plato, however, says in the *Euthydemus*: "For the art of lyre-making <note>original: ἡ λυροποιϊκή</note> and the art of kithara-playing <note>original: ἡ κιθαριστική</note> differ greatly from one another." And in the *Laws*, it is ordered that the cithara-player perform the melody on a lyre for the students to sing along. <margin>15</margin> Xenophon says much the same in the *Symposium*: "With the lyre tuned to the flute, the boy played the cithara and sang." Likewise, Himerius—perhaps following the example of the lyric poets as he does in other matters—speaks in his fourth oration of the cithara of Orpheus and Amphion, while in another oration he attributes a lyre to the same Orpheus.

To begin with the lyre, we must first note the foolish <margin>20</margin> etymological guesses of certain grammarians, such as the author in Boissonade’s *Anecdota*: "The lyre was so named because the gore <note>original: λύθρον (lythron), meaning blood or filth</note> was given by Hermes after the theft of Apollo's cattle," just as Euripides says in the *Antiope*: "The lyre redeemed the ransom of the cattle." <note>This is a play on the word *lytra*, meaning ransom</note> There is also the commentary on Nicander: <margin>25</margin> "For Apollo, being a hired servant of Admetus, was herding his cattle which Hermes stole; when caught, Hermes gave him in exchange <note>original: ἀντίλυτρον (antilytron)</note> the cithara he had constructed from a tortoise, the so-called *chelys*, whence it was called a *lyra*, being as it were a 'ransom' <note>original: λύτρα (lytra)</note> for the cattle." Without doubt, the name of the lyre, like the names <margin>30</margin> of almost all instruments, is of Asiatic origin. Generally, however, the invention of the lyre—specifically the seven-stringed version—is attributed to Mercury. For Mercury found a tortoise in the Chelydorean mountains of Arcadia, from whose shell (according to the Homeric Hymn to Mercury) he fashioned the lyre as follows: <margin>35</margin>

> "He cut stalks of reed to measure and fixed them,
> piercing the back of the stone-skinned tortoise;
> he stretched ox-hide over it with his skill,
> and added arms, fitting a yoke across both,
> and stretched seven harmonious strings of sheep-gut." <margin>40</margin>

<column-break/>

<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>ADDENDUM ON ANCIENT MUSIC §. 9.</header>

Lucian, in his *Dialogues of the Gods*, rendered these same details in prose: "Somewhere finding a dead tortoise, he constructed an instrument from it. For having fitted arms and joined them with a yoke, then fixing pegs and placing a bridge beneath, he stretched seven strings and played a very sweet melody." Thus Mercury <margin>5</margin> ran reed sticks from the bottom to the top of the hollow shell, over which he stretched the ox-hide. Thus he created the soundboard or base <note>original: βατῆρα (batera)</note>. <margin>*</margin> Then, in the place where the little creature's front legs had been, he fixed two arms or horns <note>original: πήχεις (pecheis), κέρατα (kerata)</note>, which were joined at the far end by a transverse piece of wood, the yoke <note>original: ζυγῷ (zygo)</note>. <margin>10</margin> Conversely, in the place where the tortoise's tail had been, around the center point <note>original: χορδότονον (chordotonon) or χοινικίς (choinikis); in our language, a string-holder or tailpiece</note>, he stretched seven strings over the soundboard, extending between the horns up to the yoke, where they were fixed with tuning pegs <note>original: κόλλαβοι (kollaboi)</note>. Now the strings only produced a sound when a bridge <margin>15</margin> <note>original: μαγάς (magas); in German, "ein steg"</note> was placed beneath them—whether made of reed, which is why Aristophanes speaks of a "reed under the lyre," or of horn. If the bridge was removed, the harmony of the strings ceased, which is why Sophocles said of a man suddenly struck dumb: "Your reed has been taken away, just like a lyre's." Later, however, the structure of the lyre was changed so that, <margin>20</margin> once the large soundboard shell was removed, it resembled that piece of wood which is called a "pedal" in our clavichords. Its parts were two horns, a yoke, and the wooden base of the instrument, which was called the *bater* or *chordotonon* without distinction, clearly because it was hollowed out from the inside and served as the soundbox. <margin>25</margin>

It was mentioned above that the appearance of the cithara was not very different from the lyre. Its inventor was said to be either Apollo himself, or it was believed to have been invented by Mercury and given to Apollo. John Lydus says: "Mercury <margin>30</margin> mythically gives the cithara to Apollo, as the God of Eloquence gives the harmony of the universe to the Sun." Conversely, in a certain hymn of Alcaeus, Apollo receives the lyre from his father Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note>. As far as I know, Hesiod was the first to mention Apollo as a cithara-player in Olympus, surrounded by the Muses. "Apollo [is] not only..." <margin>35</margin>

---

<margin>*</margin> I call the *tympanum* a soundboard <note>original German: resonanzboden</note> according to the commentator on Germanicus in the description of the celestial lyre. Scaliger correctly notes: "Every hollow object that is round on one side is called a *tympanum* <note>original: ὄστρακον (ostrakon) — shell</note>. Thus pearls of that shape are called *tympana*, as Pliny and after him Isidore testify." <margin>40</margin>

<summary>This page analyzes the historical and mythological origins of the lyre and cithara, emphasizing that while ancient authors often confused the two, they were distinct in construction. It provides a detailed technical description of how Hermes allegedly fashioned the first lyre from a tortoise shell, ox-hide, and reeds.</summary>

# ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS

<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS</header>

...in a musical contest he defeated Marsyas <note>A satyr who challenged Apollo to a flute-playing contest and was flayed for his hubris</note> and, with Tmolus as judge, he defeated Pan. According to the testimony of Pausanias (IX. 30), he even surpassed Mercury himself. He also sang to the cithara at the wedding of Peleus and Thetis in Homer (*Iliad* 24.63), at the wedding of Cupid and Psyche in Apuleius (*Metamorphoses* VI, p. 183), and especially at
<margin>5</margin> the wedding of his favorite, Admetus, king of Pherae, to Alcestis, for whom he himself had acted as matchmaker, according to Apollodorus (I. 9, 15; see also Callimachus’ *Hymn to Apollo* line 47, Plutarch’s *Amatorius* p. 761, Lactantius I. 10, and the Scholia on Euripides’ *Alcestis* line 1). These are the words of Wernsdorf in his commentary on Himerius (p. 327). Even at the wedding of Liber <note>Dionysus</note> and Ariadne, celebrated on the island of Naxos, Apollo 
<margin>10</margin> sang to the lyre, as Seneca notes (*Oedipus* line 488). Finally, it should be noted that Bacchus himself, along with his companions, is not infrequently represented as a cithara-player <term>citharoedos: a professional singer who accompanies themselves on the cithara</term>; on this matter, consult Welcker’s *Ancient Monuments* (III, p. 130).

Regarding the striking of the cithara, a passage from Asconius (commenting on Cicero’s *Against Verres* II. 1. 20, 53) is particularly relevant: "When the cithara-players perform, 
<margin>15</margin> both hands perform their duty. The right hand uses the plectrum <note>a pick or quill</note>, and this is called *playing from the outside*. With the fingers of the left hand, they pluck the strings, and this is called *playing from the inside*. It is difficult, however, to do what the Aspendian <note>a musician from Aspendos in Pamphylia</note> cithara-player used to do: he did not use both hands for the song, but performed everything—that is, the entire melody—'inside' and with the left hand alone." 
<margin>20</margin> On this point, Scaliger erred remarkably in his notes on Manilius’ *Astronomy* (page 419, 1600 edition). The cithara could also be played by placing the instrument in the lap, where the strings were plucked with both hands, much like our modern harp. Homer was ignorant of the use of the comb or plectrum, as the ancient grammarians have already observed. 
<margin>25</margin> For it is first mentioned by the author of the Homeric *Hymn to Apollo* (line 7). Even on gems and other ancient monuments, the strings are usually plucked with the fingers alone without a plectrum (see Perizonius on Aelian’s *Various History* III. 32, p. 260). From Achilles Tatius (I. 8), I gather that an instrument struck with a plectrum produced a louder sound. In 
<margin>30</margin> tuning the lyre (<gloss>string-tuning: *chordologein* in Plutarch’s *On Benefiting from Enemies* ch. 3</gloss>), they began from the *mese* or middle string. Dio Chrysostom (Oration 68. 7) says: "Having established the middle tone on the lyre, they then tune the others to this; if they do not, they will never achieve any harmony." <note>original Greek: ἐν λύρᾳ τὸν μέσον φθόγγον καταστήσαντες ἔπειτα πρὸς τοῦτον ἁρμόζονται τοὺς ἄλλους...</note> The Greeks expressed the sound of the string by the 
<margin>35</margin> word *blinyri* <note>original: βλίνυρι — an onomatopoeic term for the sound of a vibrating string</note>, a term discussed by Lobeck in *Aglaophamus* (p. 1331) and collected by Leutsch in *Greek Proverbs* (I, p. 387).

---

<margin>*) Concerning the Aspendian cithara-player, see Schweighäuser’s notes on Athenaeus (IV, p. 174 D). This became a proverb used to describe a greedy person or a thief (Zenobius II. 30; Plutarch’s *Proverbs* II. 20): "For the Aspendian cithara-players do not bring any movement outside, but keep everything inside the instrument." <note>This refers to the "inside" playing technique mentioned in the main text, punning on the idea of a thief "keeping" everything for themselves.</note></margin>
<margin>40</margin>

<column-break/>

<page-num>157</page-num>
<header>SUPPLEMENT ON THE MUSIC OF THE ANCIENTS §. 9.</header>

Regarding the number of strings, there was the greatest disagreement among the Greeks. Thus, there were those who believed the lyre was originally equipped with three or four strings. Stephanus of Byzantium (under the entry *Asia*): "A city of Lydia near Mount Tmolus, where the three-stringed cithara was invented." 
<margin>5</margin> With the three sounds of the cithara, Apollo blends the three seasons of the year, according to the pseudo-Orpheus in the *Hymn to Apollo* (XXXIV). Diodorus (I. 16) records that the deepest sound is winter, the middle is spring, and the highest is summer. They attributed the fourth string to a certain Meline. Lysanias 
<margin>10</margin> (see Schmidt’s *Fragments of Didymus*, p. 396) in Aelius Festus Aphthonius (p. 241, Gaisford edition) says: "They think that the song <term>melos</term> which arises from these is named after Meline, daughter of Oceanus, whom Lysanias asserts was the first to use four strings." Especially relevant here is a fragment following Censorinus (ch. 12): "The instrument once had three tensions: low, middle, and high; hence there were also thought to be three Muses originally, 
<margin>15</margin> named *Hypate*, *Mese*, and *Nete*" <note>These are the Greek names for the highest, middle, and lowest strings in pitch</note>—and a little later: "They say Apollo noticed the sweetness of a pleasantly sounding string (so I read by conjecture) in his sister's bow and immediately stretched a cithara; then he noted that tighter threads of gut were raised to a high pitch, while loosened ones responded with low tones. From this, he made the first three, 
<margin>20</margin> which were mentioned above. This arrangement was taken up by Linus" (following the manuscript evidence, O. Jahn wrote *Lycorus*, referring the reader to Pausanias X. 6, 3: "Another story is told, different from the first, that Lycorus was born to Apollo by the nymph Corycia"; see also *Etymologicum Magnum* under *Lykoreia*), "whom they indeed say was the son of Apollo and 
<margin>25</margin> the nymph Paramese, and he left it to Chrysothemis. By him was added the mode called *synemmenos* <note>The "conjunct" tetrachord in the Greek musical system</note>. Terpander increased this number by the addition of the *diezeugmenon* <note>The "disjunct" tetrachord</note>, which first turns from the low tones toward the high. Then Timotheus added two more: the *paramese* and the *hyperbolaeon*." These are mere fictions of the Delphians, 
<margin>30</margin> as Jahn very correctly stated in his note (p. 91). Nor should we value more highly what Boethius (*On Music* I. 20, p. 1075) records, first from Nicomachus and then from some unknown ancient author: that until the time of Orpheus, the instrument was equipped with four strings: "Of which four-stringed instrument Mercury is said to be the inventor. Then 
<margin>35</margin> Torebus, son of Atys, added the fifth, who was king of the Lydians. Hyagnis the Phrygian added a sixth string to these. But the seventh string was added by Terpander of Lesbos, according to the likeness of the seven planets. To these, Lichaon of Samos added the eighth, and between the *paramese* (also called the *trite*) and the *paranete*, he fitted a middle string, so that he himself..."

<summary>This page discusses the performance techniques of the ancient cithara, specifically the "inside" (fingers) and "outside" (plectrum) methods, and explores the mythological evolution of the instrument's string count from three to eight.</summary>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of stringed instruments, specifically focusing on the increasing number of strings and the technical innovations of famous musicians like Terpander and Pythagoras, moving from mythological origins to physical construction and specific instrument types.</meta>

# ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS

<page-num>158</page-num>
<header>ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS</header>

...the third was from the highest note <note>original: "nete"</note>. Prophrastus Periotes, however, added one string to the lower register to create a nine-stringed instrument <note>original: "enneachordum"</note>, known as the *hyperhypate* <note>the note just above the lowest note</note>. Hestiaeus of Colophon fitted a tenth string to the lower register. Timotheus of Miletus, on the other hand, added an
<margin>5</margin>eleventh. — In general, the prevailing opinion was that the lyre or cithara had seven strings from its very beginning, as I explained above on page 79, line 10. However, the writer Eratosthenes, in his *Catasterisms* (Chapter 24), offers some unique details, stating that Orpheus, the son of Calliope, gave the lyre nine strings to match the number of the Muses.

<margin>10</margin>The merits of Terpander <note>a 7th-century BC Greek poet and musician</note> are not to be sought in increasing the number of strings, but in a new tuning of the seven strings and their division into two tetrachords <note>four-note scales</note>. For those seven strings did not originally produce the harmony of the *diapason* <term>diapason: an octave</term>, but only two tetrachords joined together so that the *mese* <note>the middle note</note> was both the 
<margin>15</margin>lowest sound of the upper tetrachord and the highest sound of the lower tetrachord; it truly held the middle position between the three strings on either side. Terpander is said to have removed the *trite* (the third string) and added the *neta* (the highest note) so that the harmony of the octave would result (see Aristotle, *Problems* XIX, 32). Thus, in this system, there was an uncomposed interval of 
<margin>20</margin>three semi-tones from the *paranete* <note>next-to-highest note</note> to the *paramese* <note>next-to-middle note</note>. Since the *paramese* was the third string of the system counting from the top, it too could bear the name *trita*, as seen in the fragments of Philolaus (see Boeckh, *Philolaus*, p. 70). This being the case, even those who contended that the *paramese*, rather than the *trite*, 
<margin>25</margin>was omitted in the most ancient system were not in error. Pythagoras was the first to introduce the *octachord* <note>eight-stringed scale</note>, which encompassed two perfect, separate tetrachords, although it is impossible that Terpander’s seven-stringed system did not remain in use even after his time. Finally, it should be added that one string of the cithara bore the name *iynx* <note>the wryneck bird, associated with magic and attraction</note>; the Scholiast on Aristides 
<margin>30</margin>writes (p. 318 A): <note>original: "ἴυγξ — τινὲς δὲ λέγουσι χορδήν τινα κιθάρας πάνυ ἐμμελῆ"</note> "The *iynx* — some say it is a certain very melodious string of the cithara."

The citharas were undoubtedly made of wood or bronze. The *phorminx* <note>an early, simpler version of the lyre</note> of Achilles in the *Iliad* (I, 187) was adorned with a silver yoke. The *phorminx*—which the ancients believe was named because it could be easily carried 
<margin>35</margin>around (for they interpret it as <note>original: "ἡ τοῖς ὤμοις φερομένη κιθάρα"</note> "the cithara carried on the shoulders")—differed either not at all or very little from the lyre or cithara. I do not quite follow why Boeckh (in *On the Meters of Pindar*, p. 260) called it a type of cithara, and a larger one at that. How elaborately citharas were sometimes decorated is shown by the cithara of Evangelus, which 
<margin>40</margin>Lucian described in *Against the Unlearned* (Chapter 8). And the cithara was not even

<column-break/>

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>ADDENDUM ON THE MUSIC OF THE ANCIENTS §. 9—10.</header>

unknown to the barbarians, as the Getae <note>a Thracian tribe</note> used it during peace embassies (Theopompus according to Athenaeus, XIV p. 627 D). A person who manufactured musical instruments of any kind was called a *lyropoios* <note>a lyre-maker</note> (Pollux, IV, 64). Finally, those who played the cithara without singing 
<margin>5</margin>were called *citharistae* or *psilocitharistae* <note>literally "bare-cithara players"</note>, while those who also added their voice were called *citharoedi* <note>citharoedes</note>; Menagius discusses this in his notes on Diogenes Laertius (III, 88). Ammonius says of the *citharoedos*: "A citharist is one who only plucks the strings, but a citharoede is one who both sings and plucks." The Scholiast on Aristides (Vol. III, p. 4 A) says: 
<margin>10</margin>"A citharoede differs from a citharist because the citharoede uses his voice, while the citharist only knows how to strike the strings." See also the *Prolegomena to Hermogenes* (Walz, *Rhetores Graeci*, IV p. 7). Citharoedes usually appeared in quite magnificent attire (see Bergk, *Commentary on Ancient Comedy*, p. 383, and Plutarch, *Symposium of the Seven Sages*, p. 161 B). The Muse Terpsichore was the patroness of the lyre (Arsenius, XXXII, 83).
<margin>15</margin>

---

### §. 10.
### Regarding the Barbiton, Trigonum, Magadis, Pectis, Sambuca, and Phoenix.

The *Barbiton* was usually called *hē barbitos* <note>feminine gender</note> by the Greeks (though sometimes *ho barbitos* <note>masculine</note>, 
<margin>20</margin>which Mehlhorn restored in Anacreon), and occasionally *to barbiton* <note>neuter</note>. According to Strabo, it was a foreign instrument, similar to the lyre but with many strings (see Theocritus 16, 45). However, a three-stringed barbiton is mentioned in a fragment of Anaxilas in Athenaeus (IV p. 183 B). It was especially common on the island of Lesbos, 
<margin>24</margin>which is why Horace calls it "the Lesbian" (Odes I, 1, 34). As for its inventor—that is, the one who first spread its use in Greece—Horace (Odes I, 32, 4) names Alcaeus; Athenaeus (XIV p. 635 D) names Terpander; and elsewhere (IV p. 175 E) names Anacreon (see Bergk’s *Anacreon*, p. 250 ff.). It was also called the *barmos* according to Athenaeus (XIV 
<margin>30</margin>p. 636 C), as well as the *barymiton* <note>deep-stringed</note> according to Pollux (IV, 59; who also lists *mitoi*, meaning strings, among the parts of instruments in IV, 62). Aristophanes used the verb *barbitizein* <note>to play the barbiton</note>, according to Pollux (IV, 63).

The *Trigonum* was an instrument very similar to our harp, except that the shortest strings were held against the chest of the player. 
<margin>35</margin>Aelius discusses it in Porphyry (p. 216): "The ancients made the *trigonum* with strings of unequal lengths; the longest was the outermost, the next was slightly shorter, and those further inward, sitting at the corner of the instrument, were the shortest in length." According to King Juba in his history of the 
<margin>40</margin>theater (in Athenaeus, IV p. 175 D), the *trigonum* was an invention of the Syrians.

<summary>This page catalogs the historical and technical development of Greek stringed instruments, detailing how musicians like Terpander and Pythagoras refined string counts and tuning systems (tetrachords and octaves). It also distinguishes between types of performers (citharists vs. citharoedes) and describes specific harp-like instruments such as the barbiton and trigonum.</summary>
<keywords>ancient Greek music, cithara, lyre, Terpander, Pythagoras, barbiton, trigonum, tetrachord, octave, organology, music history</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of ancient musical instruments, moving from the standard lyre and cithara discussed previously to more specialized and "exotic" stringed and wind instruments like the Magadis and the Nabla.</meta>

<page-num>160</page-num>
<header>ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS</header>

Alexander of Alexandria, a contemporary of Athenaeus, played this instrument excellently (Book IV, p. 183 E).

*Regarding the Magadis and the Pectis, there is little agreement among the ancients.* See above, page 80, line 25. Thus, Anacreon, in Athenaeus (Book XIV, p. 634 C), <margin>5</margin> called the Magadis an instrument of twenty strings. On the other hand, in a certain verse by Ion, according to the interpretation of Aristarchus, it was the name of a flute <note>original: "tibiae"</note>. Likewise, Trypho, in the second book of *On Names* <note>original: "περὶ ὀνομασιῶν"</note>, established that the Magadis was a flute that could produce both low and high sounds at once, and thus it exceeded the interval of an Octave. Many, <margin>10</margin> however, understood the word to refer to a stringed instrument. Indeed, Euphorio first claimed the Magadis was an ancient instrument of the Mitylenaeans, later called the *sambuca* <note>original: "σαμβύκη"</note> after its form was slightly changed. Menaechmus, however, held the Magadis to be an invention of Sappho, and not different from the Pectis. Aristoxenus <margin>15</margin> agreed on this point, asserting that both instruments were played without a plectrum <note>a small tool used to pluck strings</note>, by plucking with the fingers <note>original: "διὰ ψαλμοῦ"</note>. In contrast, others established a distinction between the two instruments. Apollodorus, in Athenaeus (p. 636 F), thought the Magadis was identical to the more recent psaltery. Furthermore, Telestes, in a dithyramb <note>a choral hymn</note> titled *Hymenaeus*, <margin>20</margin> attributed five strings to the Magadis. Then there is the fiction of Duris in Book 20 (p. 637 F), asserting that the instrument took its name from a certain Magdis, a Thracian. It is apparent from such diverse and conflicting testimonies that the true form and nature of the instrument cannot be determined. In Greek, to play the Magadis was called *magadizein* <note>original: "μαγαδίζειν"</note>. <margin>25</margin>

*The Sambuca* <note>original: "σαμβύκη"</note> was a four-stringed instrument with a very high-pitched sound, used by the Parthians, Syrians, and Troglodytes <note>an ancient group of people from the Red Sea coast</note>, about which Euphorio reported in *On the Isthmian Games* (from Pythagoras's book *On the Red Sea*) in Athenaeus (p. 633 F). The strings of this instrument were very small, according to Aristides Quintilianus (p. 101). There <margin>30</margin> were those who derived the name from the Sibyl Sambuca, or from some Sambyx or other (Athenaeus, p. 637 B). Philemon mentioned a female sambuca player <note>original: "sambucistriam"</note> in the same author (Book IV, p. 175 D), where it is added that a certain type of sambuca was called the *lyrophoenix* <note>original: "λυροφοῖνιξ", literally "lyre-date-palm"</note>. There was also the *Phoenix*, a stringed instrument, according to Ephorus in Athenaeus <margin>35</margin>

---

*) It happened by chance that the Italian name for a shepherd’s pipe, *sampogna*, bears some similarity to this. However, this is derived from *symphonia* (see Diez, *Etymological Dictionary of Romance Languages*, p. 303), which already in a verse by Venantius Fortunatus—"until the full symphony sang with its own breath"—designates a <margin>40</margin> type of flute.

<column-break/>

<page-num>161</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX ON ANCIENT MUSIC §. 10—11.</header>

(p. 637 B), so named because it was an invention of the Phoenicians; according to Semus, it was because its horns were made from the date palm <note>original: "phoenice"</note> of Delos.

### §. 11.

->*On the Nabla, Cinyra, Psaltery, and other obscure instruments.*<-

The *Nablas* or *Nabla*, according to Athenaeus (Book IV, p. 175 C), was <margin>5</margin> a stringed instrument invented by the Phoenicians, which seems to have been little used in Greece. It is the Hebrew *Nebel*, described by Josephus in *Antiquities of the Jews* (VII, 12, 3): "The nabla, having twelve notes <note>he means strings</note>, is struck with the fingers." <note>original: "ἡ δὲ νάβλα δώδεκα φθόγγους... ἔχουσα τοῖς δακτύλοις κρούεται"</note> That the Nabla was not different <margin>10</margin> from the *Psaltery* is perhaps to be inferred from the fact that *psaltery-player* <note>original: "ψάλτης"</note> is explained in the ancient Glosses by the word *nablium-player* <note>original: "Nablio"</note>. Certainly, in Lexicons, *psaltery-player* is explained as "a type of cithara player" <note>original: "εἶδος κιθάρας"</note>. It is also said "to pluck to the cithara" <note>original: "ψάλλειν πρὸς κιθάραν"</note> in Heliodorus (I, 11) or "to pluck with the cithara" <note>original: "ψάλλειν κιθάρᾳ"</note> in Achilles Tatius (II, 7). That the *psaltae* <note>psaltery players</note> were generally wretched <margin>15</margin> and very poor men, I gather from what Plutarch (*On Flattery and Friendship*, ch. 17) and Aelian (*Historical Miscellany*, IX, 36) have narrated. Nevertheless, Alexander Citherius made a name for himself by increasing the number of strings on the psaltery; he hung his instrument in the temple of Diana of Ephesus, according to Juba in Athenaeus (Book IV, <margin>20</margin> p. 183 C). But the most recent writer who mentioned the nabla, though only in poetic speech, is undoubtedly Leo the Master in *The Wedding of Emperor Leo* (v. 14), in Matranga's *Roman Anecdotes* (p. 562):

> "Give me a nabla to sound." <note>original: "δότε μοι νάβλαν δονῆσαι"</note>

<margin>25</margin> In Greek, the nabla player is called a *nablistes* <note>original: "ναβλιστής"</note>. The same Josephus, in the place mentioned above, described the *Cinyra* thus: "The cinyra, fitted with ten strings, is struck with a plectrum." <note>original: "ἡ μὲν κινύρα δέκα χορδαῖς ἐξημμένη τύπτεται πλήκτρῳ"</note> Furthermore, in Book VIII, 3, 8, he records that King Solomon had nablas and cinyras made of amber <note>original: "electro"</note>. This is the Hebrew *Kiniroth*, so the grammarians who derived <margin>30</margin> the name from a "complaining and mournful sound" <note>associating it with the Greek 'kinyros'</note> should now be dismissed.

There are also certain other very obscure instruments, of which almost only the names have been preserved for us by Athenaeus and others. For instance, the *Pandura* (see above in the section on flutes), a three-stringed <note>original: "trichordum"</note> instrument invented by the Assyrians, according to Pollux (IV, 60); see also <margin>35</margin> Salmasius, *Writers of Augustan History* (Vol. I, p. 874). Regarding the ancient use of the instrument in Greece—though the name *pandura-player* <note>original: "πανδουριστοῦ"</note> was more recent—Euphorio testifies in Athenaeus (Book IV, p. 182 E). Hesychius says: "*Pandoura* or *pandouris*, a musical instrument. *Pandouros* is the one who handles the instrument." <note>original: "πανδοῦρα ἢ πανδουρίς, ὄργανον μουσικόν. πάνδουρος δὲ ὁ μεταχειριζόμενος τὸ ὄργανον"</note> Nicomachus <margin>40</margin> (p. 8, compared with p. 67) preserved a third form of the word, *phandouros*, and says that the monochord <note>a single-stringed instrument</note> or "canon" of Pythagoras...

<page-num>162</page-num>
<header>ON ORGANS OR INSTRUMENTS</header>

...it is called so. Furthermore, the *Scindapsus* was a four-stringed instrument similar to a lyre; see Athenaeus, Book IV, page 183 A, and Juba in *Proverbs*, Book I, page 387. The *Pariambis* is mentioned in a verse by Epicharmus in the same passage of Athenaeus (page 183 C), which Santenius, in his work on Terentianus Maurus (page 60), believed accompanied "pyrrhic" <note>a fast, warlike dance rhythm</note> songs, since the "pariambus" <margin>5</margin> was called the same as the "pyrrhichius." There was also a type of song called *Pariambides*, according to Pollux (Book IV, 59): "the *pariambides* were melodies for the cithara <note>original: νόμοι κιθαριστήριοι</note>, which were also accompanied by the flute." The same ambiguity recurs regarding the *Clepsiambos*, which Aristoxenus, according to Hesychius, says were "certain songs in the works of Alcman" <note>original: μέλη τινὰ παρὰ Ἀλκμᾶνι</note>, whereas Pollux and Athenaeus (page 183 F), citing the same Aristoxenus, refer them to musical <margin>10</margin> instruments. Indeed, in Athenaeus, Aristoxenus lists the Phoenix, the Pectis, the Magadis, the Sambuca, the Trigonon, the Clepsiambos, the Scindapsus, and finally the Enneachord <note>a nine-stringed instrument</note> (which is therefore different from a standard lyre equipped with nine strings) among the "barbarian" and foreign musical instruments. Regarding the words of Phyllis of Delos *) from his se- <margin>15</margin> cond book *On Music*, cited in the same Athenaeus (Book XIV, page 636 B): "those [instruments] on which they sang iambics <note>a specific poetic meter</note> were called *iambykes*; those on which they played in a style that deviated from the strict meters were called *clepsiamboi*; the *magadides* were those tuned in octaves <note>original: τὰ διαπασῶν</note> and adjusted to the equal parts of those singing," <margin>20</margin> this is no clearer to me than it was to Welcker in his work on Alcman (page 9).

And there was no lack of instruments that exceeded the count of twenty strings, though they were little used. To this category belong the *Epigonium* and the *Simicium*, concerning which Pollux (Book IV, 59) says: "the *epigoneion* is named after its inventor; Epigonus was by birth an Ambracian, but a citizen of Sicyon by honor; he was the first <margin>25</margin> to pluck [the strings] without a plectrum <note>a small tool like a pick</note>. The *epigoneion* has forty strings, just as the *simikion* has thirty-five." Regarding the *Epigonium* as the invention of Epigonus, Juba also speaks in Athenaeus (Book IV, page 183 D), though omitting the number of strings: "that which is now remodeled into the 'upright psaltery' <note>original: ψαλτήριον ὄρθιον</note> preserves the name of the instrument it was modeled after," from which you might conclude, along with Boeckh in <margin>30</margin>

---

*) Phyllis of Delos, an author of uncertain age, wrote several books *On Music* and *On Flute Players*. The fragments were collected by Karl Müller, *Fragments of History*, Volume IV, page 476. — "Phyllis or Phyllis the Musician (Suda, under the entry 'Phyllis'), originally from Delos, is praised in Athenaeus (Book I, page 21 F; Book XIV, page 634 D, 636 B); <margin>35</margin> Suda, Photius, under the entry 'skolion'; Scholia on Plato, page 341 Bekker; Scholia on Lucian, Volume IV, page 116 Jacob (where he is called Philo). His book *On Times*, or by Nitzsch's conjecture (Melett. I, page 79 ff.) *On Choruses*, is cited in the *Anecdota Bekker*, page 783. Cf. Schneider on the sources of the Scholia to Aristophanes, page 109 ff.; Meineke, *Greek Anthology*, page 134 ff." — Jahn, in the *Fragments after Censorinus*, IX, page 87, where Phyllis was received into the catalog of famous musicians before Aristoxenus instead of "Follis." <margin>40</margin>

<page-num>163</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX TO THE MUSIC OF THE ANCIENTS § 11.</header>
<sig>11 *</sig>

*The Meters of Pindar* (page 260), that the instrument perhaps had fewer strings in the beginning. It seems that this Epigonus was a contemporary of Lasus, since Porphyry (in his commentary on Ptolemy, found in Meibom’s *Aristoxenus*, page 79) lists his school of music in first place among those that preceded Aristoxenus. Furthermore, Lasus and the followers of Epigonus maintained <margin>5</margin> that a sound has a "breadth" <note>a technical musical theory regarding the pitch of notes</note>, an opinion which Drieberg thought he had clarified in his *Musical Lexicon* (page 45), but which was little favored by the Pythagoreans and Aristoxenus. This same Epigonus was the first to practice "accompanied cithara playing" <note>original: ἔναυλον κιθάρισιν</note>, which is the accompaniment of the cithara with flutes (Athenaeus, Book XIV, page 637 F), and he discovered the chromatic genus <note>a scale using half-steps</note>, which Boethius <margin>10</margin> erroneously attributes to Timotheus of Miletus. Boeckh (in the place cited, page 261) does not differ much regarding the age of the man. Concerning another instrument whose name was the *Saccadium*, it has already been mentioned above on page 90, line 25; however, at that point, I could perhaps have added that there is only a coincidental similarity of name between this instrument (doubtless of foreign origin) and Sacadas <margin>15</margin> of Argos, the most famous flute player. Thus, only one instrument remains, which went out of use along with its inventor: the *Tripod* <note>original: Tripus</note> of Pythagoras of Zakynthos, concerning which Artemon may be consulted in Athenaeus (Book XIV, page 637 C).

But enough on these matters. For if nothing else, I have certainly learned this from Plutarch’s little book: *that the highest and most musical thing is to apply to everything its proper measure.* <margin>20</margin> <note>original: ὅτι ἀνώτατον καὶ μουσικώτατον, τὸ παντὶ τὸ προσῆκον μέτρον ἐπιτιθέναι</note>. I have set aside a more extensive catalog of ancient musicians for another place and time.

<summary>This page contains an alphabetical index for the editor's commentary on Plutarch’s musical treatise, listing significant historical figures, types of ancient instruments, and technical musical terms. <summary>This page concludes the alphabetical index of the commentary, providing a comprehensive list of musical terms, legendary inventors, and historical developments from Plato to Terpander. It emphasizes the classification of flutes, lyres, and the traditional melodic Laws (nomoi) of ancient Greece.</summary>
